DU LL.B. II Term · Paper LB-205 · Topic 5 · 9 Hours

Law of the Sea

The whole of the international law of the sea — every maritime zone from the baseline to the deep seabed, the law that divides the sea between neighbouring States, the idea of the common heritage of mankind, the parallel system of seabed mining, India's maritime interests, policy and statutes, and the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea — built article-by-article on the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea, 1982, on the four Geneva Conventions of 1958, on S.K. Verma's Chapter 14, and on the seven prescribed cases: Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries, Corfu Channel, North Sea Continental Shelf, Tunisia/Libya, Italy v. Union of India (2013), the Bay of Bengal Arbitration (2014) and Somalia v. Kenya (2021).

MAPHow to Use These Notes

What this topic is. Every other topic in this paper deals with States on land. Topic 5 deals with the 70.8 per cent of the earth's surface that is water, and with the oldest question in the law of nations after war: how much of the sea may a State call its own, and on what terms may it use the rest? The answer, today, is a single instrument — the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea, 1982 ("UNCLOS"), signed at Montego Bay on 10 December 1982, in force since 16 November 1994, with 320 articles, 17 Parts and 9 Annexes — read together with the four Geneva Conventions of 1958, the 1994 Implementing Agreement on Part XI, the 1995 Fish Stocks Agreement and the 2023 BBNJ Agreement. The whole topic is the working-out of one idea, expressed by the International Court in the North Sea Continental Shelf cases and repeated ever since: "the land dominates the sea" — the land territorial situation of a State is the starting point for determining its maritime rights.

What the syllabus requires. Six heads, in the order of the syllabus image: 5.1 maritime zones — territorial sea, contiguous zone, continental shelf, exclusive economic zone, high seas; 5.2 delimitation of adjacent and opposite maritime boundaries; 5.3 the concept of the "common heritage of mankind" relating to the resources of the international seabed Area; 5.4 international seabed mining — the parallel system of mining; 5.5 Indian maritime interests, policy and law; 5.6 the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea. Seven cases carry the topic: 30. Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries Case (ICJ Rep. 1951, p. 116); 31. Corfu Channel Case (ICJ Rep. 1949, p. 4); 32. North Sea Continental Shelf Cases (ICJ Rep. 1969, p. 3); 33. Libya v. Tunisia Continental Shelf Case (ICJ Rep. 1982, p. 17); 34. Republic of Italy v. Union of India (2013) 4 SCC 721; 35. In the Matter of the Bay of Bengal Maritime Boundary Arbitration (Bangladesh v. India), PCA, 2014; 36. Maritime Delimitation in the Indian Ocean (Somalia v. Kenya), ICJ Rep. 2021. All seven are set out below in the seven-part format required by the case material: (1) why the case is in the syllabus, (2) facts, (3) issues, (4) arguments of both sides, (5) decision, (6) principle evolved, (7) examination angle.

How each head is treated. Four layers, as in the earlier topics. (i) The text: the governing articles are quoted — UNCLOS articles first, then the corresponding 1958 articles, then the Indian statutory provision, because that is the order in which an examiner expects a coastal State's powers to be justified. (ii) The doctrine: S.K. Verma's Chapter 14 ("Law of the Sea") supplies the theory, history and structure; the DU case material supplies the cases and the examination vocabulary; the class notes and the exam capsule supply the Indian framing ("the land dominates the sea", the zone first-principles). (iii) The cases: the seven prescribed cases in full, plus thirty supporting authorities — Lotus, I'm Alone, Red Crusader, M/V Saiga, M/V Virginia G, Norstar, Bangladesh/Myanmar, Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire, the 2011 and 2024 Seabed Disputes Chamber opinions, and the rest. (iv) The material the syllabus does not print: every related order, rule, notification and statute is inserted at the point where it belongs — the 1958 Conventions, the 1994 Implementation Agreement, the Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act 1878, the Territorial Waters Act 1956, the Maritime Zones Act 1976 and the notification SO 67/E of 1981, the Maritime Zones of India (Regulation of Fishing by Foreign Vessels) Act 1981, the SUA Act 2002, the Coast Guard Act 1978, the Admiralty Act 2017, the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act 2022, the CLCS and the ISA Regulations — with real examples from Indian and world practice.

The two items the teacher highlighted. On the syllabus image two entries are marked in green, and both are treated at disproportionate length below: case 34, Republic of Italy v. Union of India (2013) 4 SCC 721 — the Enrica Lexie reference, the only Indian case in the syllabus and the meeting point of the contiguous zone, the EEZ, the Maritime Zones Act and the Constitution (§5.1.2, §5.1.4, case 34); and head 5.6, the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea — which is treated with the whole of its Statute, its jurisdiction, its case-law and its Indian dimension (§5.6). The handwritten notes on the image ("baseline → low tide line", "Sec-2 Art-3", "Sec-4 Art-1", "500 NM E.E.Z" style marks, "High Seas" underlined) are answered at the corresponding places, and Appendix F collects them in one table.

A note on sources and provenance. The DU LB-205 case material (2022) prints, in its Law-of-the-Sea block: Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries; Corfu Channel; North Sea Continental Shelf; Tunisia/Libya; Republic of Italy v. Union of India; and Somalia v. Kenya. The facts, pleadings, reasoning and operative paragraphs of those six are taken from the case material and from the judgments themselves. The Bay of Bengal award (case 35) is listed but not printed in the case material; it is set out below from the Award of 7 July 2014 and from the published commentary, and the provenance is stated at the head of the case. Verma's Chapter 14 is the doctrinal spine throughout. Where a figure, date or instrument has been verified against an external official source (the ICJ, ITLOS, the PCA, the ISA, the Ministry of Earth Sciences, the International Seabed Authority) rather than the course material, the text says so.

↑ top

5.0The Problem Stated

A. Why the Sea Needs Law

Begin, as the course material begins, with the physical fact. "The sea is a physical unit which covers the greatest part (70.8%) of the earth's solid crust." A single body of water, continuous around the globe, has historically performed two functions for humanity: "first, as a medium of communication; and secondly, as a vast reservoir of resources, both living and non-living. Both of these functions have stimulated the development of legal rules." Those two functions pull in opposite directions, and the whole law of the sea is the record of their conflict. As a medium of communication, the sea is more useful the freer it is: navigation, cables, overflight and trade all want open water. As a reservoir of resources — fish, oil, gas, nodules, sand, energy — the sea is more valuable the more of it a State can exclude others from: resources are depletable, and the State with the strongest incentive to conserve and exploit them is the coastal State.

From that tension follow the two structural principles of the modern law.

(1) The sea is divided into belts of decreasing State competence. Sovereignty is complete in internal waters; it is sovereignty subject to the right of innocent passage in the territorial sea; it is limited functional control in the contiguous zone; it is "sovereign rights" for economic purposes in the exclusive economic zone and on the continental shelf; and it is absent altogether on the high seas and in the Area, which are open to all States and, in the case of the Area, held in trust for mankind. Every question you will be asked is, at bottom, a question of which belt are we in, and what does the Convention allow there.

(2) "The land dominates the sea." This is the governing principle of the whole topic, and the class notes put it first: "the land territorial situation constitutes the starting point for the determination of the maritime rights of a coastal state." Two consequences are examinable. First, maritime entitlements are measured from the land — from the low-water line or from straight baselines drawn along the coast — so that the shape of the coast, its indentations, its islands and its fringing reefs determine the extent of the sea a State may claim. Second, because entitlements flow from land, a State with a deeply indented coast or a fringe of islands may "leap out" with straight baselines and thereby claim more sea than a State with a smooth coast of the same length; that asymmetry is the reason Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries (case 30) is the first case of the syllabus and the reason the concavity of the Bay of Bengal mattered so much in case 35.

Real examples — the two functions in collision
  • Communication. The Enrica Lexie (case 34) and Corfu Channel (case 31) are both navigation cases: a strait used for international navigation may not be closed in time of peace, and an Italian tanker's navigation on the high seas may not be interfered with by India even when a crime has been committed on an Indian fishing boat.
  • Resources. The Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case is a fisheries case; the North Sea Continental Shelf cases and Tunisia/Libya are oil and gas cases; the Bay of Bengal award is about gas blocks and the extended continental shelf; Somalia v. Kenya is about oil concessions in the Lamu Basin. In every one of them the boundary line is drawn not for its own sake but because of what lies on the seabed beneath it.
  • Both at once. The 2021 blockage of the Suez Canal by Ever Given, the attacks on shipping in the Red Sea after 2023, and the Somali piracy prosecutions in Mumbai (2026) show the two functions interacting: navigation security is what makes resource exploitation and trade possible, and both depend on the legal régime of the straits, the EEZ and the high seas.

B. The Vocabulary You Must Control

Maritime-law answers are lost more often through loose vocabulary than through ignorance of cases. Fix these terms before anything else.

Table 5.1 — The vocabulary of the law of the sea, defined
TermMeaning, and the provision that fixes it
BaselineThe line from which the breadth of every maritime zone is measured. Normally the low-water line along the coast (Art. 5 UNCLOS); exceptionally straight baselines joining points on the coast or on fringing islands (Art. 7); for archipelagic States, archipelagic baselines (Art. 47).
Internal watersWaters on the landward side of the baseline (Art. 8) — rivers, lakes, ports, harbours, bays. The coastal State has full sovereignty; there is no right of innocent passage (except where straight baselines have enclosed an area previously part of the territorial sea — Art. 8(2)).
Territorial seaThe belt measured from the baseline up to 12 nautical miles (Art. 3), over which the coastal State has sovereignty — over the water, the seabed, the subsoil and the superjacent airspace — subject to the right of innocent passage (Art. 17) and to other treaty limits (Arts. 24–32).
Contiguous zoneThe belt beyond the territorial sea up to 24 nm from the baseline, in which the coastal State may exercise the control necessary to prevent and punish infringement of customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary laws committed in its territory or territorial sea (Art. 33). Not sovereignty; not a resource zone.
Exclusive economic zone (EEZ)The belt beyond and adjacent to the territorial sea up to 200 nm from the baseline (Art. 57), in which the coastal State has sovereign rights over the exploration and exploitation, conservation and management of natural resources, and jurisdiction over artificial islands, marine scientific research and the marine environment (Art. 56). Not sovereignty; the high-seas freedoms of navigation, overflight and cables survive (Arts. 58, 87).
Continental shelfThe natural prolongation of the land territory to the outer edge of the continental margin, or to 200 nm from the baseline where the margin does not extend so far (Art. 76(1)); in which the coastal State has sovereign rights for exploring and exploiting natural resources, and jurisdiction over drilling, tunnels, installations and (in part) cables and pipelines (Art. 77 ff.).
High seasAll parts of the sea not included in the territorial sea, internal waters or EEZ of any State (Art. 86). Open to all States; not subject to the sovereignty of any State (Art. 89); six freedoms are guaranteed (Art. 87).
The AreaThe seabed and ocean floor and subsoil thereof beyond the limits of national jurisdiction (Art. 1(1)(i)); the common heritage of mankind (Art. 136), administered by the International Seabed Authority. The Area and the high seas are different spaces governed by different Parts of the Convention — a distinction examiners love.
Innocent passagePassage through the territorial sea that is continuous and expeditious and not prejudicial to the peace, good order or security of the coastal State (Art. 18, read with the list of non-innocent activities in Art. 19(2)(a)–(l)).
Transit passageThe right of unimpeded, continuous and expeditious passage (which cannot be suspended) through straits used for international navigation between one part of the high seas or EEZ and another (Arts. 37–44).
Archipelagic sea lanesRoutes and air routes designated by an archipelagic State through its archipelagic waters, through which all States enjoy archipelagic sea-lanes passage "in their normal mode" (Arts. 53, 54).
Historic waters / historic titleWaters over which a State has consolidated a title (as against general rules of delimitation) through continuous use, acquiescence and the passage of time — the second limb of Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries, and the basis of India's Palk Bay settlement in 1974.
Low-tide elevationA naturally formed area of land surrounded by water which is exposed at low tide but submerged at high tide (Art. 13). Its own low-water line may serve as the baseline where it lies wholly within the territorial sea; it cannot generate a territorial sea beyond 12 nm, and cannot be used as a base point in delimitation between States (case 36).
RockAn island that "cannot sustain human habitation or economic life of its own" generates no EEZ or continental shelf, only a 12 nm territorial sea (Art. 121(3)).
Straight baselinesBaselines joining points on the coast or fringing islands, permitted where the coastline is deeply indented and cut into, or where there is a fringe of islands along the coast in its immediate vicinity (Art. 7; Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries).
Median / equidistance lineIn the territorial sea, the line every point of which is equidistant from the nearest points on the baselines of the two States; the presumptive boundary under Art. 15, unless "special circumstances" require otherwise. In the EEZ and continental shelf (Arts. 74 and 83) it is the usual starting point of a three-stage exercise, but the governing standard is an equitable solution.
International Seabed Authority (ISA)The organisation through which States Parties organise and control activities in the Area on their behalf (Art. 156; Parts XI and XII and Annexes III–IV), seated at Kingston, Jamaica. Its mining arm is the Enterprise (Art. 170).
CLCSThe Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf, 21 members, which considers the scientific evidence supporting claims to a continental shelf beyond 200 nm and makes recommendations (Art. 76(8); Annex II). Recommendations are binding in the sense that the coastal State's outer limits "shall be final and binding".

C. Three Historical Moments

Three dates must be carried in the head, because each of them is a favourite opening line of an examiner.

Moment one — 1609: mare liberum defeats mare clausum. The course material records the starting point with precision: "The seas were at one time thought capable of subjection to national sovereignties. The Portuguese in particular in the seventeenth century proclaimed huge tracts of the high seas as part of their territorial domain, but these claims stimulated a response by Grotius who elaborated the doctrine of open seas, whereby the oceans as res communis (a thing of the entire community) international law were to be accessible to all nations but incapable of appropriation. This view prevailed, partly because it accorded with the interests of the North European States, which demanded freedom of the seas for the purposes of exploration and expanding commercial intercourse with the East." Add the counterpoint: the freedom Grotius won was never absolute — the territorial sea survived as the coastal State's belt, justified by cannon range (Bynkershoek's De dominio maris, 1702) and the "cannon-shot rule" of a marine league (three nautical miles) defended by the United States and Great Britain in the nineteenth century, and the whole of the twentieth century then spent enlarging it.

Moment two — 1945 to 1960: the sea is re-drawn in the coastal State's favour. The turning point is President Truman's Proclamation No. 2667 of 28 September 1945, which asserted United States jurisdiction over the natural resources of the continental shelf beneath the high seas contiguous to its coasts, while expressly describing the water above as high seas with free navigation. Within a decade, unilateral claims to fisheries jurisdiction and to the seabed had proliferated, and the First United Nations Conference on the Law of the Sea (Geneva, 1958) codified the position in four Conventions: on the Territorial Sea and Contiguous Zone; on the High Seas; on Fisheries and the Conservation of the Living Resources of the High Seas; and on the Continental Shelf. A Second Conference (Geneva, 1960) was convened exclusively on the breadth of the territorial sea and fishery limits, and failed by one vote to adopt a six-plus-six formula; "no convention was therefore adopted in this Conference". The 1958 régime left three gaps: the breadth of the territorial sea was unsettled; the continental shelf definition (200 m depth, plus exploitability) was technologically fragile; and the new States of Asia and Africa, the archipelagic, land-locked and geographically disadvantaged States, had had no voice in Geneva.

Moment three — 1967 to 1994: common heritage, and a constitution for the oceans. On 1 November 1967, Ambassador Arvid Pardo of Malta told the General Assembly that the seabed beyond national jurisdiction should be declared the "common heritage of mankind" — a claim the Assembly accepted by its Declaration of Principles Governing the Sea-Bed and Ocean Floor (resolution 2749 (XXV), 17 December 1970) and which became Part XI of the Convention. The Third United Nations Conference on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS III) began in 1973 with about 160 participating States, worked by "package deal" consensus across nine years, and produced the Convention adopted at Montego Bay on 10 December 1982 — 320 articles, 17 Parts and 9 Annexes — which entered into force on 16 November 1994 after the sixtieth ratification. The price of entry into force was the Agreement relating to the Implementation of Part XI of 28 July 1994, which cut back the Part XI régime; a further gap was closed by the 1995 Fish Stocks Agreement, and a further gap — biodiversity beyond national jurisdiction — by the BBNJ Agreement of 19 June 2023, in force since 17 January 2026 (§5.1.5(H)).

D. The Architecture of UNCLOS 1982

Table 5.2 — The Convention as an instrument: the parts you must know by Part number
PartSubjectArticles and examinable content
IIntroductionArts. 1–3: definitions (the Area, the Authority, "marine environment"); the 12-mile limitation on the territorial sea.
IITerritorial sea and contiguous zoneArts. 3–33: baselines, bays, straits, innocent passage, criminal and civil jurisdiction on board ships, duties of the flag State for warships; the contiguous zone (Art. 33).
IIIStraits used for international navigationArts. 34–45: transit passage; non-suspendable innocent passage in straits formed by an island and the mainland.
IVArchipelagic StatesArts. 46–54: archipelagic baselines, archipelagic waters, archipelagic sea-lanes passage.
VExclusive economic zoneArts. 55–75: sovereign rights, jurisdiction, fisheries conservation, surplus, land-locked and geographically disadvantaged States, enforcement, delimitation.
VIContinental shelfArts. 76–85: definition, outer limits beyond 200 nm, sovereign rights, drilling, tunnels, cables, delimitation, payments.
VIIHigh seasArts. 86–120: freedoms, flags, duties of the flag State, immunity, slavery, piracy, drugs, broadcasting, right of visit, hot pursuit, fisheries.
VIIIRegime of islandsArt. 121: the island / rock distinction.
IXEnclosed or semi-enclosed seasArts. 122–123: definition and duties of cooperation.
XRight of access of land-locked StatesArts. 124–132: access to the sea, freedom of transit, flags, cooperation.
XIThe AreaArts. 133–191: common heritage, ISA, Enterprise, transfer of technology, delimitation disputes, Seabed Disputes Chamber.
XIIProtection and preservation of the marine environmentArts. 192–237: general obligation, monitoring, global and regional cooperation, dumping, vessels, enforcement, sovereign immunity.
XIIIMarine scientific researchArts. 238–265: consent regimes in the territorial sea, EEZ and on the shelf; implied consent; suspension.
XIVDevelopment and transfer of marine technologyArts. 266–278: the forgotten Part, still examinable as the equitable dimension of the Convention.
XVSettlement of disputesArts. 279–299: the menu of courts (ITLOS, ICJ, arbitration), limitations and exceptions (Arts. 297–298), provisional measures (Art. 290), prompt release (Art. 292).
XVIGeneral provisionsArts. 300–304: good faith, abuse of rights, responsibility for damage, sovereign immunity.
XVIIFinal provisionsArts. 305–320: signature, ratification, accession, amendments, depositary.
AnnexesNine annexesI highly migratory species; II CLCS; III basic conditions of prospecting, exploration and exploitation in the Area; IV the Enterprise; V conciliation; VI ITLOS Statute; VII arbitration; VIII special arbitration; IX international organisations.

The Convention's relationship to the 1958 Conventions (Art. 311(1)). "As between States Parties, this Convention shall prevail over the Geneva Conventions of 1958 on the Law of the Sea." The 1958 texts do not disappear: for a State party to both, UNCLOS governs; for a State party only to a 1958 text, that text governs; and where the 1982 text is silent or identical, the 1958 jurisprudence (and the Convention's own borrowing of 1958 language) is the best evidence of the custom. This is why North Sea Continental Shelf (1969), decided thirteen years before UNCLOS, is still the starting point for a question on Article 83.

Constitutional character. Because amendments require an elaborate procedure (Arts. 312–316) and because the Convention is meant to be read with its implementing agreements, it is habitually described — in the words of the General Assembly's 1982 resolution endorsing it — as "a constitution for the oceans". For an answer, the description has legal consequences: it explains why the Convention, and not subsequent unilateral claims (the 200-mile claims of the 1970s, or the 2025 United States licensing initiative under the Deep Seabed Hard Mineral Resources Act 1980) supplies the framework against which new claims are tested.

E. Where the Law of the Sea Comes From

Use Article 38 of the ICJ Statute as the frame, because an examiner marking a "sources" question wants it visible even when the question is a maritime one.

  • Treaties. UNCLOS (1982) and its implementing agreements; the four Geneva Conventions (1958); the 1995 Fish Stocks Agreement; the 1926 Slavery Convention and the 1988 SUA Convention for piracy-related offences; the 1969 Civil Liability for Oil Pollution Damage, MARPOL 73/78, the OPRC 1990 and the Bunkers Convention 2001 for marine pollution; the 1957/1960 ILO conventions on merchant shipping; regional instruments such as the Djibouti Code of Conduct (2009) and the Indian Ocean MOU.
  • Custom. Freedom of the high seas; the exclusive flag-State jurisdiction (Lotus, 1927, and Art. 92 UNCLOS); the illegality of peacetime closure of international straits (Corfu Channel, case 31); the continental-shelf doctrine as it stood between the Truman Proclamation and Geneva (North Sea, case 32, on why Art. 6 of the 1958 Convention did not become custom for non-parties).
  • General principles. Good faith and abus de droit (Art. 300); estoppel and acquiescence (case 36); equity as a general principle expressed through law and not ex aequo et bono (Tunisia/Libya, case 33).
  • Judicial decisions. The seven prescribed cases; plus Lotus (1927), Barcelona Traction, Nicaragua, Bangladesh/Myanmar (ITLOS, 2012), Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (2017), the Seabed Disputes Chamber's advisory opinions of 2011 and the Tribunal's climate opinion of 2024. ICJ decisions are formally binding only between the parties (Art. 59 Statute), but in maritime delimitation the Court's own consistency is now a practical source of law: an answer that says "Article 83 has been applied as a three-stage methodology in Romania v. Ukraine (2009), Bangladesh/Myanmar (2012) and Somalia v. Kenya (2021)" is doing source-analysis, not just case-listing.
  • Resolutions and acts of international organisations. Assembly resolutions, though recommendatory, are evidence of opinio juris where they are declaratory of principles (the 1970 Declaration; the 1974 Charter of Economic Rights and Duties of States; the Security Council's piracy resolutions of 2008 onwards). The same is true of the ISA's regulations, adopted under Part XI and binding on contractors through their contracts.
  • Writings. Grotius, Bynkershoek and Vattel historically; Gidel, Colombos, O'Connell, Brownlie and Tanaka today; Shaw's chapter 9 and Verma's chapter 14 for the course; and the ILC's Articles and commentaries on the law of the sea questions it worked on in 1954–58.

F. The Zones on One Page

Fig. 5.1 — The zones, the distances, and the two different legal spaces (water column / seabed) that UNCLOS keeps apart

Keep the vertical and horizontal structure separate in your mind. Vertically, the Convention distributes competence over three layers — the airspace, the water column, and the seabed and subsoil — and the layers do not always belong to the same State, as the "grey area" of the Bay of Bengal award shows. Horizontally, it divides the sea by distance from the baseline, and the distances are cumulative (internal waters 0; territorial sea to 12 nm; contiguous zone to 24 nm; EEZ and continental shelf to 200 nm; extended shelf to 350 nm or 100 nm beyond the 2,500-metre isobath, whichever gives more).

G. Ten Sentences That Answer Any Question

The ten propositions — learn them verbatim
  1. The territorial sea extends up to 12 nautical miles measured from the baselines (Art. 3), and within it the coastal State exercises sovereignty over the water column, the seabed and subsoil and the airspace, subject to the right of innocent passage (Arts. 2, 17).
  2. The normal baseline is the low-water line along the coast (Art. 5); straight baselines are permissible where "the coastline is deeply indented and cut into, or if there is a fringe of islands along the coast in its immediate vicinity" (Art. 7), and the drawing of them must not depart appreciably from the general direction of the coast (Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries, case 30).
  3. The contiguous zone may not extend beyond 24 nautical miles from the baseline, and in it the coastal State may "prevent and punish" infringement of its customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary laws committed within its territory or territorial sea (Art. 33). It confers no resource rights.
  4. The EEZ may not extend beyond 200 nautical miles (Art. 57); in it the coastal State has sovereign rights for exploring and exploiting, conserving and managing natural resources, and jurisdiction over artificial islands, marine scientific research and protection of the marine environment (Art. 56) — not sovereignty, and not ownership of the sea.
  5. The continental shelf comprises the seabed and subsoil of the natural prolongation of the land territory to the outer edge of the continental margin, or to 200 nm where that margin does not extend so far (Art. 76(1)); the coastal State's rights are ipso jure, exclusive, and do not depend on occupation or proclamation (Art. 77; North Sea, case 32).
  6. The high seas are open to all States and subject to the sovereignty of none (Arts. 87, 89); ships on the high seas are subject to the exclusive jurisdiction of the flag State (Art. 92; Lotus, 1927) — and piracy is the exception, because every State may seize a pirate ship and try the pirates (Arts. 100, 105).
  7. The Area and its resources are the common heritage of mankind (Art. 136); no State may claim or exercise sovereignty over any part of the Area (Art. 137(1)); activities in the Area are organised and controlled by the ISA, on behalf of mankind as a whole (Arts. 153, 157; Part XI, as modified by the 1994 Agreement).
  8. Delimitation of the territorial sea between opposite or adjacent States is by agreement on the basis of international law, and, failing agreement, by the median line unless special circumstances require otherwise (Art. 15); delimitation of the EEZ and the continental shelf must "achieve an equitable solution" (Arts. 74(1), 83(1)), which the courts apply through the three-stage exercise of a provisional equidistance line, relevant circumstances, and a disproportionality check.
  9. Disputes are settled by the peaceful means of Part XV: in default of the parties' choice, by ITLOS, the ICJ, or arbitration under Annex VII (Art. 287), with prompt release of detained vessels available to the flag State (Art. 292) and provisional measures available to preserve the parties' rights (Art. 290).
  10. For India: Article 297 of the Constitution vests in the Union the territory and resources of the territorial sea, continental shelf and EEZ; the Maritime Zones Act, 1976 fixes the zones and (by the notification SO 67/E of 27 August 1981) extends the penal law of India to the EEJ, so that the Union — not a State — may prosecute offences committed in the contiguous zone and EEZ (Italy v. Union of India, case 34).

↑ top

5.1Maritime Zones

The first head of the syllabus is a study of belts. UNCLOS does not treat "the sea" as one thing: it slices it, from the coast outwards, into spaces in which the coastal State's competence decreases band by band, and alongside that horizontal slicing it separates the airspace, the water column and the seabed and subsoil, because different States may hold rights in different layers at the same spot. Two principles give the structure its rigour. First, everything is measured from the baseline (Art. 5, and exceptionally Arts. 7, 47); change the baseline and every zone moves. Second, the character of the coastal State's competence changes with the belt: sovereignty in internal waters and the territorial sea; control in the contiguous zone; sovereign rights and jurisdiction in the EEZ; sovereign rights over resources only on the continental shelf; and no State competence at all on the high seas and in the Area, where the Convention substitutes the flag State, the international community, or the Authority.

The table below is the single most useful object in this topic: an examiner's favourite question is a comparison, and this table contains every comparison worth writing.

Table 5.3 — The maritime zones compared: distance, legal character, resources, enforcement, and the governing articles
FeatureInternal watersTerritorial seaContiguous zoneEEZContinental shelf
Distance from baselineLandward of the baseline0–12 nm (Art. 3)12–24 nm (Art. 33(2))12–200 nm (Art. 57)To the outer edge of the margin, or 200 nm; up to 350 nm in Art. 76 terms
Legal characterSovereignty (Art. 8)Sovereignty (Art. 2(1))Control, not sovereignty (Art. 33)Sovereign rights + jurisdiction (Art. 56) — not sovereigntySovereign rights for resource purposes (Art. 77) — not sovereignty
Layers coveredWater, seabed, subsoil, airspaceWater, seabed, subsoil, airspace (Art. 2(2))Water column and seabed, but no resource rights; no airspace rightsWater column, seabed and subsoil for economic purposes; airspace not includedSeabed and subsoil only; the superjacent water is unaffected (Art. 78)
Right of innocent passageNone (Art. 8(1)); exception for straight-baseline enclosures (Art. 8(2))Yes (Art. 17)Passage is the high-seas freedom of navigation exercised under Art. 58Yes, as freedom of navigation under Art. 58(1); not "innocent passage" as suchNot applicable (the shelf is about the seabed; navigation is governed by the water-column régime)
Resource rightsCompleteComplete (subject to the passage right)None; only the four enforcement competencesAll natural resources, living and non-living; energy from water, currents and winds; Art. 56(1)(a)Mineral and other non-living resources of the seabed and subsoil, and sedentary living organisms (Art. 77(4))
Rights of the coastal State "whether claimed or not"Not applicableNot applicableExists only if declared (Art. 33)Exists only if declared (Arts. 55, 57) — but the shelf right exists ipso jureYes — inherent and automatically vested (Art. 77(3))
Enforcement powersAll lawsAll laws, with the Art. 27 and 28 limits for ships in passagePrevention and punishment of customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary breaches only (Art. 33(1))All laws adopted consistently with the Convention, including boarding, inspection, arrest and judicial proceedings (Art. 73), subject to prompt-release and bondAll laws for exploring and exploiting the shelf: drilling, installations, safety zones, and no interference with navigation (Arts. 60, 78, 80, 81)
Military activitiesRegulated by the coastal State, subject to treatiesInnocent passage cannot be used for any military activity listed in Art. 19(2), e.g. weapons exercise, intelligence gathering (19(2)(a)–(c)); submarines must navigate on the surface (Art. 20)No separate power; the Art. 58 freedoms apply beyond the territorial seaContested: the coastal State has no general power to restrict foreign naval activity; Art. 58 preserves high-seas freedoms, and Art. 88 confines the EEZ to peaceful purposesPeaceful purposes only in the Area beyond national jurisdiction; on the shelf, Art. 78 and installations rules
Delimitation provisionGoverned by the boundary of the land, unless historic titleArt. 15: median/equidistance unless special circumstances justify otherwiseFollows the territorial sea and EEZ delimitation (i.e. Art. 15 to 24 nm, then Arts. 74/83 beyond)Art. 74: agreement on the basis of international law to achieve an equitable solutionArt. 83: same standard; plus Art. 76 limits and Art. 76(10) (the shelf boundary does not affect the superjacent water boundary)
The distinction that decides cases

Sovereignty vs sovereign rights. In the territorial sea the coastal State has sovereignty — the verb is "sovereignty is exercised". In the EEZ and on the shelf it has sovereign rights — "rights" for defined purposes only, and the Convention's draftsman chose the words deliberately. In Italy v. Union of India (case 34) the Supreme Court drew exactly this distinction: the Union's power in the EEZ is a power to legislate for the "exclusive economic zone", not to exercise sovereignty, and the notification SO 67/E of 1981 extending the Indian Penal Code and the Code of Criminal Procedure to the EEZ therefore rests on Article 297 of the Constitution read with sections 6 and 7 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976 — not on ordinary territorial jurisdiction. Get the vocabulary wrong and the whole answer becomes wrong.

5.1.1 The Territorial Sea

A. Nature, legal status and content

The territorial sea is "the belt of sea adjacent to the coast over which the coastal State exercises sovereignty". Article 2 of UNCLOS states it with classical economy:

Article 2 — Legal status of the territorial sea, of the air space over the territorial sea and of its bed and subsoil. "1. The sovereignty of a coastal State extends, beyond its land territory and internal waters and, in the case of an archipelagic State, its archipelagic waters, to an adjacent belt of sea, described as the territorial sea. 2. This sovereignty extends to the air space over the territorial sea as well as to its bed and subsoil. 3. The sovereignty over the territorial sea is exercised subject to this Convention and to other rules of international law."

Four consequences are examinable.

  1. The territorial sea is territory for most purposes. Sovereignty over it is of the same quality as sovereignty over the land (1958 Geneva Convention, Art. 1: "The sovereignty of a State extends, beyond its land territory and its internal waters, to a belt of sea adjacent to its coast, described as the territorial sea"). That is why an invasion of the territorial sea is an invasion of the State for the purposes of the law of the use of force, why a warship there is a projection of the flag State's territory for immunity purposes, and why, in the Enrica Lexie dispute, the "territorial sea" and "contiguous zone" characterisation mattered so much to Kerala's claim.
  2. The belt is three-dimensional but not unlimited. Article 2(2) extends sovereignty upwards into the airspace and downwards to the bed and subsoil. It does not extend to the outer space (the boundary between airspace and outer space is still unsettled in treaty law) and it does not extend to the subsoil beneath the land territory's internal waters in any different way.
  3. Sovereignty is exercisable "subject to this Convention". The three great qualifications are: innocent passage (Arts. 17–32); transit passage through international straits (Arts. 37–44); and non-suspendable innocent passage in the two situations of Article 45. Add the treaty-based qualifications: the 1919/1921 conventions on the Åland Islands and the Turkish Straits; the 1888 Constantinople Convention on the Suez Canal and the 1903 Hay–Varilla Treaty on the Panama Canal (now the 1977 Torrijos–Carter Treaties); the 1971 Seabed Arms Control Treaty and the 1979 Moon Agreement (for the seabed beyond 12 nm); and the régime of "archipelagic waters" in Part IV.
  4. The territorial sea is not a zone of mere "police powers". In the Lotus case (PCIJ, Ser. A No. 10, 1927) the Court distinguished, in the classical passage, between the exercise of jurisdiction over acts performed on board a foreign ship in the territorial sea and the high seas: "it is certainly true that — apart from certain special cases which are defined by international law — vessels on the high seas are subject to no authority except that of the State whose flag they fly... The case is different with vessels in the territorial waters of a State, for these are subject to and within the jurisdiction of the coastal State." That distinction — territorial sea: coastal State jurisdiction; high seas: flag State — is the backbone of Part VII, and of case 34.

B. Breadth of the territorial sea: the three-mile quarrel and the twelve-mile settlement

(a) The cannon-shot rule. The territorial sea began as a security belt measured by the range of coastal artillery. Bynkershoek's principle — terrae potestas finitur ubi finitur armorum vis — was translated into distance by Galiani's suggestion of three miles, adopted by the United States (Jefferson's instructions of 1793) and Great Britain, and applied in the practice of the nineteenth century. The three-mile rule was never universal: Norway, Sweden and the then standard of the Scandinavian States claimed four miles, and the Mediterranean States claimed six.

(b) Codification fails. The Hague Codification Conference of 1930 came within one vote of adopting a three-mile territorial sea with a three-mile contiguous zone (the proposal failed because States insisted on the right to reserve the question of contiguous zones). The 1958 Geneva Convention on the Territorial Sea and Contiguous Zone said nothing about breadth, because the question was considered incapable of agreement; the 1960 Second Conference, convened solely on breadth and fisheries limits, failed by one vote on a "six plus six" formula (6 nm territorial sea plus a further 6 nm fishery zone). This is the point the DU course material makes: the four Geneva Conventions "made a good start, but they failed to respond to all the issues, most particularly the breadth of territorial waters".

(c) Unilateral enlargement and settlement at 12 nm. Between 1947 and 1974 the belt was widened by unilateral claim: Chile and Peru (1947), and the Santiago Declaration of Chile, Ecuador and Peru (1952), claimed 200 nm of "maritime zone"; Iceland extended from 3 to 4 nm (1952), to 12 nm (1958), to 50 nm (1972) and to 200 nm (1975), producing the three Fisheries Jurisdiction cases against the United Kingdom and the Federal Republic of Germany (ICJ 1974); Indonesia and the Philippines asserted archipelagic claims. State practice settled at 12 nm, and UNCLOS put it in Article 3: "Every State has the right to establish the breadth of its territorial sea up to a limit not exceeding 12 nautical miles, measured from baselines determined in accordance with this Convention." The 12-mile figure then became customary law for States not party to the Convention as well — a rare instance of a treaty provision achieving general acceptance before the treaty entered into force.

India's own progression — a favourite short question

3 nm (pre-1956) → 6 nm (1956) → 12 nm (1967). India inherited the British three-mile belt under the Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act, 1878 (which remains on the Indian statute book and still governs questions of jurisdiction over offences committed on board foreign ships within the belt). By a Proclamation of 22 March 1956, India adopted a six-mile territorial sea; by a Proclamation of 1967 the belt was extended to twelve nautical miles; the position was given statutory form by the Territorial Waters Act, 1956 and finally re-enacted in section 3 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976, which reads: "The sovereignty of India extends and has always extended to the territorial waters of India (hereinafter referred to as the territorial waters) and to the seabed and subsoil below, and the airspace above, the territorial waters." Section 3(2) fixes the limit of the territorial waters at twelve nautical miles from the nearest point of the appropriate baseline. The 1976 Act also declares, in sub-section (3), that the waters declared as historic waters (Palk Bay, Palk Strait and the Gulf of Mannar in relation to Sri Lanka) are historic waters and that the Union has, and has always had, full and exclusive sovereignty over them.

C. Baselines: how the belt is measured

The whole topic hangs on the baseline, and the handwritten note on the syllabus image ("baseline → low tide line") is the examiner's signal. UNCLOS devotes eight articles to the subject.

Table 5.4 — The baseline provisions of UNCLOS (with the corresponding 1958 articles)
SituationUNCLOS rule1958 analogue and notes
Normal baselineArt. 5: the normal baseline is the low-water line along the coast as marked on large-scale charts officially recognised by the coastal State. Art. 16: the baselines and the limits derived from them must be shown on charts or described in a list of coordinates, and due publicity given.1958 Convention, Art. 3 (low-water line, plus straight baselines); Art. 4 (straight baselines).
Straight baselinesArt. 7(1): where the coastline is deeply indented and cut into, or if there is a fringe of islands along the coast in its immediate vicinity, the method of straight baselines joining appropriate points may be employed. 7(2): the drawing of straight baselines must not depart to any appreciable extent from the general direction of the coast, and the sea areas lying within the lines must be sufficiently closely linked to the land domain. 7(3): baselines may not be drawn to and from low-tide elevations unless lighthouses or similar installations permanently above sea level have been built on them. 7(4)–(6): economic interests evidenced by long usage may be considered; the system may not cut off the territorial sea of another State from the high seas or an EEZ; and straight baselines may not be applied to produce a cut-off.1958 Art. 4 — the same language, which the Convention copied from Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries (case 30). The Stanzin class notes put it exactly: "Much of language from the case was codified in the Article 4 of the 1958 Convention... Almost the same language is in Article 7 of UNCLOS on straight baselines."
River mouthsArt. 9: if a river flows directly into the sea, the baseline is a straight line across the mouth of the river between points on the low-water line of its banks.1958 Art. 13.
BaysArt. 10: the 24 nm closing line and the semicircle test (below).1958 Art. 7.
Ports and roadsteadsArt. 11: the outermost permanent harbour works forming an integral part of the harbour system are regarded as part of the coast (artificial islands and off-shore installations are not). Art. 12: roadsteads normally used for loading, unloading and anchoring, which would otherwise be wholly or partly outside the outer limit of the territorial sea, are included in it.1958 Arts. 8, 9.
Low-tide elevationsArt. 13: a low-tide elevation is a naturally formed area of land surrounded by water which is above water at low tide but submerged at high tide. Where it lies wholly within 12 nm of the mainland or of an island, its low-water line may be used as a baseline; beyond that distance it has no territorial sea of its own.1958 Art. 11. In delimitation, a low-tide elevation cannot generate base points for an equidistance line — Bangladesh v. India (case 35) and Somalia v. Kenya (case 36) both say so expressly.
ReefsArt. 6: in the case of islands situated on atolls or of islands having fringing reefs, the baseline is the seaward low-water line of the reef, as shown by the appropriate symbol on charts.No 1958 equivalent.
CombinationArt. 14: the coastal State may determine baselines in turn by any of the methods provided, to suit different conditions.Not in 1958.
Archipelagic baselinesArt. 47: straight archipelagic baselines joining the outermost points of the outermost islands and drying reefs, the ratio of water to land being between 1:1 and 9:1, and the length of any baseline segment not exceeding 100 nm (with an exception for 3 per cent of segments up to 125 nm).New in 1982; the 1958 régime treated archipelagos as an aggregation of islands.
Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries mainland (deeply indented, cut into by fjords) fringe of islands (skjærgaard) straight baselines joining suitable points on the islands territorial sea now measured from the straight lines waters landward of the lines = internal waters (Art. 8)
Fig. 5.2 — The baseline determines everything: same coast, two methods, a materially larger territorial sea under the straight-line method
(i) The three tests of Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries

The Court's judgment of 18 December 1951 (case 30, below) is the source of the modern law, and it laid down the conditions in language that Article 7 now carries:

  1. The geographic test: the method of straight baselines is permissible where the coast is "deeply indented and cut into" or where there is "a fringe of islands along the coast in its immediate vicinity" — the Court's concern being the "danger of a state being able to extend its territorial sea to an unreasonable extent" if the method were used on smooth coasts.
  2. The general-direction test: "the drawing of baselines must not depart to any appreciable extent from the general direction of the coast". The Norwegian lines did not do so because they followed the general direction of the skjærgaard (the fringe of rocks and islands), not of the mainland.
  3. The economic and social test: the Court accepted that "the realities of the situation" may justify straight baselines where the "economic interests peculiar to a region, the reality and importance of which are clearly evidenced by a long usage", bind the coastal population to the waters concerned — an element now in Article 7(5), and the ancestor of the modern idea of "vital interests" in delimitation.

Add the fourth, unstated but decisive, factor: long usage, tolerance and acquiescence. Norway's delimitation had been "enjoyed by Norway for a long period" and the United Kingdom's protest came only in 1933 (formal protest) and 1948 (institution of proceedings); the Court held that where a delimitation has been tolerated by the international community over a long period, it becomes opposable to other States — the doctrine of historic consolidation, applied again in case 36 in a different form (acquiescence).

Cross-reference — the case in full

Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries Case (United Kingdom v. Norway), ICJ Reports 1951, p. 116, is set out in full at case 30, with the 1935 Royal Decree, the skjærgaard, the contentions of the United Kingdom and Norway, the judgment, the three tests, the principle evolved (including its relation to Article 7 UNCLOS) and the examination angle.

(ii) Indian baselines: what India has actually done

India follows the normal low-water line for most of its coast, with straight baselines in limited areas and a special declaration of historic waters. The essential points, all traceable to the 1976 Act and to the notifications made under it, are:

  • Section 3(2), Maritime Zones Act, 1976: the limit of the territorial waters is "twelve nautical miles from the nearest point of the appropriate baseline".
  • Section 3(3): the historic waters of India — the waters of the Gulf of Mannar, Palk Bay and Palk Strait between India and Sri Lanka — are declared historic, and "the Union has, and has always had, full and exclusive sovereignty and jurisdiction over such waters and the airspace thereabove, their bed and subsoil and all resources therein". This is India's statutory use of the doctrine of historic title accepted in case 30 and later settled by the India–Sri Lanka agreements of 26/28 June 1974 and 23 March 1976.
  • Archipelagic baselines: India is not an archipelagic State under Part IV in respect of its mainland, but the Andaman and Nicobar, and the Lakshadweep groups are treated as islands; India's Andaman and Nicobar Islands have been the subject of extended-shelf claims and of the India–Indonesia and India–Myanmar boundary agreements (§5.2 G).
  • Publication: consistent with Article 16, the Indian baseline and zone coordinates have been notified in the Gazette of India and lodged with the United Nations (the Ministry of External Affairs maintains the gazette notifications of 1976 onwards).
(iii) Bays, historic bays and the semicircle test

Article 10 is a careful compromise and is examinable as an arithmetic exercise. A "bay" is "a well-marked indentation whose penetration is in such proportion to the width of its mouth as to contain land-locked waters and constitute more than a mere curvature of the coast" (Art. 10(2)); an indentation is not a bay unless its area is as large as, or larger than, that of the semicircle whose diameter is the line drawn across the mouth of that indentation. Article 10(4) then permits a closing line of up to 24 nautical miles from the low-water line of the bay; where the mouth is wider than 24 nm, a straight baseline of 24 nm may be drawn within the bay so as to enclose as much water as possible. Article 10(6) excludes from the article's scope the "historic bay" and the "bays" of archipelagic States. Learn the classic examples: historic bays — Hudson Bay (Canada), Chesapeake and Delaware Bays (United States), the Bay of Fonseca (Nicaragua/Honduras/El Salvador, settled by the ICJ in the Land, Island and Maritime Frontier Dispute, 1992), the Gulf of Gabès, Peter the Great Bay (USSR declaration of 1957); non-historic — the Gulf of Sirte, whose closing by Libya in 1973 was not accepted and was tested in the Gulf of Sidra incidents of 1981 and 1986. India's Kutch and Khambhat are shallow gulfs treated as internal waters by the historic-waters notification (1854/1975 position, at times contested with Pakistan in the Sir Creek context).

D. Internal waters and the effect of drawing baselines

Article 8(1): "waters on the landward side of the baseline of the territorial sea form part of the internal waters of the State." Internal waters are treated as land: full sovereignty, and no right of innocent passage (a foreign ship may enter a port only with consent, subject to treaty obligations, force majeure and distress — see Art. 18(2), and the Nicaragua case's reference to entry in distress). Article 8(2) is the technical but important exception: where the establishment of a straight baseline under Article 7 has the effect of enclosing as internal waters areas which had not previously been considered as such, "a right of innocent passage as provided in this Convention shall exist in those waters". The provision protects the navigational rights of third States against the extension of baselines.

Two practical sub-rules complete the picture. Ports: the coastal State decides on admission; a ship voluntarily in port submits to the local criminal jurisdiction in matters affecting the peace of the port (the British and American practice crystallised in the R v. Cunningham and Wildenhus's Case line). Warships: a foreign warship that has not complied with the laws of the coastal State concerning passage may be required to leave immediately (Art. 30) — the narrow remedy for the assertion of that immunity.

Exam note — the "baseline → low tide line" annotation

The handwritten note on the syllabus image beside 5.1.1 points to Article 5 (normal baseline = low-water line), and secondarily to Article 7 (straight baselines) and Article 13 (low-tide elevations). When a question asks how the territorial sea is measured where the coastline is deeply indented and cut into and there is a fringe of islands in the immediate vicinity — the question actually set in 2025 (PYQ 6, §PYQ Answer 1) — the answer must move in this order: (1) the general rule (Art. 5); (2) the exception (Art. 7(1)) and the conditions in Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries; (3) the three limiting tests (general direction; close link with the land domain; economic interests evidenced by long usage); (4) the express prohibitions (Art. 7(3) on low-tide elevations; Art. 7(6) against cut-off of another State's zones); (5) the consequence for internal waters and Article 8(2); and (6) the Indian illustration in the 1976 Act.

↑ top

E. Innocent passage — the right that limits sovereignty

The right of innocent passage is the oldest and most valuable limitation on the coastal State's sovereignty over the territorial sea, and Part II, Section 3 of UNCLOS (Arts. 17–32) is a complete code of it. The structure is simple: who may pass (all States, Art. 17); what passage means (Art. 18); when it is innocent (Art. 19); what the coastal State may do about it (Arts. 21–28); and what the coastal State may not do (Arts. 24, 26, and the specific protections of Arts. 27–32).

Article 17 — Right of innocent passage. "Subject to this Convention, ships of all States, whether coastal or land-locked, enjoy the right of innocent passage through the territorial sea."

Article 18 — Meaning of passage. "1. Passage means navigation through the territorial sea for the purpose of: (a) traversing that sea without entering internal waters or calling at a roadstead or port facility outside internal waters; or (b) proceeding to or from internal waters or a call at such roadstead or port facility. 2. Passage shall be continuous and expeditious. However, passage includes stopping and anchoring, but only in so far as the same are incidental to ordinary navigation or are rendered necessary by force majeure or distress or for the purpose of rendering assistance to persons, ships or aircraft in danger or distress."

Article 19 — Meaning of innocent passage. "1. Passage is innocent so long as it is not prejudicial to the peace, good order or security of the coastal State. Such passage shall take place in conformity with this Convention and with other rules of international law. 2. Passage of a foreign ship shall be considered to be prejudicial to the peace, good order or security of the coastal State if in the territorial sea it engages in any of the following activities: (a) any threat or use of force against the sovereignty, territorial integrity or political independence of the coastal State, or in any other manner in violation of the principles of international law embodied in the Charter of the United Nations; (b) any exercise or practice with weapons of any kind; (c) any act aimed at collecting information to the prejudice of the defence or security of the coastal State; (d) any act of propaganda aimed at affecting the defence or security of the coastal State; (e) the launching, landing or taking on board of any aircraft; (f) the launching, landing or taking on board of any military device; (g) the loading or unloading of any commodity, currency or person contrary to the customs, fiscal, immigration or sanitary laws and regulations of the coastal State; (h) any act of wilful and serious pollution contrary to this Convention; (i) any fishing activities; (j) the carrying out of research or survey activities; (k) any act aimed at interfering with any systems of communication or any other facilities or installations of the coastal State; (l) any other activity not having a direct bearing on passage."

Four points about Article 19 deserve to be put in the answer as separate propositions.

  1. The test is objective and conduct-based, not subjective. Passages are innocent or not according to what the ship does in the territorial sea; the coastal State's perception of the flag State's intentions is not decisive, and the burden of showing non-innocence lies on the coastal State. This is precisely the reasoning of Corfu Channel (case 31), where the Court refused to treat the passage of four British warships, with crews at action stations, as abusive: "the Court cannot accept the view that these measures were contrary to international law" having regard to "all the circumstances".
  2. The list in Article 19(2) is exhaustive of the conduct that makes passage non-innocent, and sub-paragraph (l) closes it. This matters for submarines, nuclear-powered vessels and warships: those categories are dealt with by Articles 20, 22, 23 and 29–32, not by a general "military" exception.
  3. Submarines. Article 20: "In the territorial sea, submarines and other underwater vehicles are required to navigate on the surface and to show their flag." A submerged transit is by definition not innocent passage; the rule was applied in the famous Whiskey on the rocks incident (Swedish and Soviet practice, 1981) and is the reason submarine transits of straits are conducted under transit passage as "normal mode" navigation.
  4. Continuous and expeditious. A ship that anchors other than for the purposes of Article 18(2) — for example to wait for a favourable market or to transfer cargo — is not in passage, and the coastal State may act. The Saiga (No. 2) case (ITLOS, 1999) is the leading modern illustration: Guinea's arrest of the Saiga, a bunkering vessel, was held unlawful and the Tribunal found that Guinea had used excessive force and violated the Convention, awarding compensation — the Tribunal's approach being that enforcement must not be excessive.

F. What the coastal State may and may not do — Articles 21 to 32

Table 5.5 — The coastal State's powers and duties over ships in innocent passage
ArticlePower or dutyContent, and the cases or practice that illustrate it
Art. 21Laws and regulations on innocent passageThe coastal State may adopt laws on navigation safety, sea-lanes and traffic separation, protection of cables and pipelines, conservation of living resources (including the boarding of fishing vessels), fishing, the environment, marine scientific research and survey, customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary matters, and any other law not having the practical effect of denying or impairing innocent passage (with special regard to the design, construction, manning or equipment of foreign ships — such laws require a generally accepted international rule).
Art. 22Sea-lanes and traffic separation schemesMay be prescribed for the safety of navigation where necessary, taking into account the recommendations of the competent international organisation (IMO), particularly for tankers, nuclear-powered ships and ships carrying dangerous goods, which may be required to use designated sea-lanes. The standard examples are the traffic separation schemes in the Dover Strait and the North Sea and the schemes adopted through the IMO off the Indian coast.
Art. 23Foreign nuclear-powered ships and ships carrying dangerous cargoMust carry the documents and observe the special precautionary measures established by international agreements while passing (the IMO codes; the 1969 Civil Liability Convention and the 1971 Fund Convention).
Art. 24Duties of the coastal StateNot to hamper innocent passage (having regard to the safety of navigation), not to discriminate in form or fact between foreign ships, and in particular not to charge for the mere fact of passage.
Art. 25Rights of protectionMay take necessary steps to prevent non-innocent passage; may, without discrimination, temporarily suspend innocent passage in specified areas of its territorial sea when necessary for the protection of its security, including weapons exercises — after due publicity. Suspension is prohibited in straits under Art. 45. The temporary-suspension power was invoked by Turkey (Bosporus), by Bangladesh (2020, during the COVID-19 pandemic, in respect of certain vessel categories) and by India during the 1971 war and in the 1984–87 operations in the Palk Bay area.
Art. 26ChargesNo charge may be levied upon foreign ships by reason only of their passage; charges are permitted only as payment for specific services rendered (pilotage, towage, etc.), without discrimination.
Art. 27Criminal jurisdiction on board a foreign shipThe coastal State should not exercise criminal jurisdiction over persons on board, or arrest in connection with an offence committed on board during passage, except in four cases: (a) if the consequences of the crime extend to the coastal State; (b) if the crime is of a kind to disturb the peace of the country or the good order of the territorial sea; (c) if the assistance of the local authorities has been requested by the master, diplomatic agent or consul; or (d) if such measures are necessary for the suppression of illicit traffic in narcotic drugs or psychotropic substances. The coastal State may arrest where the ship is passing through from internal waters. And Art. 27(5) preserves the flag State's power to request the identity of the vessel and to take proceedings — the Lotus question, now answered by Art. 97 for collision cases.
Art. 28Civil jurisdictionNo arrest or execution against a foreign ship in passage for the sake of civil proceedings, except for obligations assumed or liabilities incurred for the purpose of the passage; a coastal State should not exercise civil jurisdiction for the purpose of proceedings in rem against a vessel lying in passage. The relaxation in Art. 28(3) for ships lying in internal waters is the source of the Indian admiralty jurisdiction, now codified in the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017.
Art. 29Definition of warship"a ship belonging to the armed forces of a State bearing the external marks distinguishing such ships of its nationality, under the command of an officer duly commissioned by the government of the State and whose name appears in the appropriate service list or its equivalent, and manned by a crew which is under regular armed forces discipline."
Art. 30Non-compliance by warshipsIf any warship does not comply with the laws of the coastal State concerning passage and disregards any request for compliance, the coastal State may require it to leave the territorial sea immediately. This is the whole of the coastal State's remedy: it may not arrest or use force against the warship.
Art. 31Responsibility of the flag State for damage"The flag State shall bear international responsibility for any loss or damage to the coastal State resulting from the non-compliance by a warship or other government ship operated for non-commercial purposes with the laws and regulations of the coastal State concerning passage through the territorial sea or with the provisions of this Convention or other rules of international law." The article is a direct application of the law of State responsibility (Topic 4) to the sea.
Art. 32ImmunitiesThe Articles on criminal and civil jurisdiction (27 and 28) do not apply to warships and other government ships operated for non-commercial purposes; the only rule that applies to them is Art. 30 plus the flag State's responsibility under Art. 31.
Fig. 5.3 — The Article 19(2) test as a decision tree, with the straits régime separated out

G. Warships and the right of innocent passage — the classic controversy

The question whether warships enjoy innocent passage at all, or only with prior notification or authorisation, is the oldest live dispute in the subject, and it is a set question in the 2025 paper as a short note (PYQ 4(1), §PYQ Answer 2). The position must be stated with its history.

  • Text. Article 17 speaks of "ships of all States", without distinguishing merchant from warship; Part II then treats warships separately in Articles 29–32 (definition, the "leave immediately" remedy, flag-State responsibility, immunity from the criminal and civil jurisdiction articles). Article 19(2) makes specific military conduct non-innocent, which presupposes that warships may pass. The better reading of UNCLOS, and the reading of the great majority of commentators and of State practice, is therefore that warships enjoy the right of innocent passage, and prior authorisation may not be required; but that the question of prior notification remains open, some States treating it as a condition of passage in their national law.
  • History. The 1958 Conference recorded the dispute in its records rather than in its text, and the International Law Commission's commentary noted that the question "must be left to States to decide" — the compromise that produced the ambiguity. The Soviet bloc and China insisted on prior authorisation; the Western States and most maritime States insisted on free passage. The Joint Statement by the United States and the USSR on the Uniform Interpretation of the Rules of International Law Governing Innocent Passage (1989) settled the point bilaterally for the two superpowers: "all ships, including warships, regardless of cargo, armament or means of propulsion, enjoy the right of innocent passage through the territorial sea in accordance with international law, for which neither prior notification nor authorization is required."
  • State practice and incidents. The dispute has been tested by incident after incident: the 1988 Black Sea incident, when Soviet warships physically bumped two United States warships (the Yorktown and CG-49 Caron) exercising innocent passage off the Crimea; the 2001 EP-3 incident; the 2018–2021 South China Sea transits by United States, British, French, Australian and Japanese warships through waters claimed by China; HMS Defender in the Black Sea in June 2021 (in waters claimed by Russia off Crimea); and INS transits under India's own notification regime. None of these has produced an authoritative ruling; the practice has hardened, however, in favour of the view that prior authorisation is not required, while notification where required by local law is a matter of prudence and comity.
  • India's position. Section 4(2) of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976 provides that "foreign warships, including submarines and other underwater vehicles, shall enter or pass through the territorial waters after giving prior notice to the Central Government", subject to the exceptions in sub-section (3) (ships forced by weather, damage or distress, and ships carrying Heads of State or Government of foreign States). Section 4(2) was considered by the Kerala High Court and referred to, without being resolved, in Italy v. Union of India: the notification regime was one of the reasons the Union argued that the States' police could not act in the maritime zones without central authority. The provision is India's legislative expression of the "notification", rather than "authorisation", school.
  • The modern risk. In the South China Sea and the Black Sea, the dispute about warship passage has become part of a larger dispute about maritime claims and about the freedom of navigation operations ("FONOPs") conducted by the United States and its partners. For an answer, the correct formulation is: warships enjoy innocent passage; enforcement against a non-complying warship is limited to requiring it to leave; the flag State bears responsibility for loss or damage; and whether prior notification may be demanded is a matter of continuing controversy, on which India's 1976 Act adopts the notification position.

H. Straits used for international navigation — Part III

Straits are the pressure point of the topic: they are the places where the coastal State's territorial sea covers what is otherwise the world's principal navigation route. Part III resolves the tension in favour of navigation, and Corfu Channel (case 31) is the reason.

Table 5.6 — The straits régime, article by article
ArticleRule
Art. 34(1)The régime of passage through straits used for international navigation is a separate régime from the legal status of the waters: "the régime of passage through straits used for international navigation established in this Part shall not in other respects affect the legal status of the waters forming such straits or the exercise by the States bordering the straits of their sovereignty or jurisdiction over such waters and their air space, bed and subsoil."
Art. 35The régime does not apply to: (a) straits where the waters are internal waters, or where a straight baseline has been drawn and the waters had not previously been considered internal waters only because the drawing of baselines had the effect of enclosing them; (b) straits where the high seas or an EEZ route of similar convenience exists; or (c) straits regulated in whole or in part by long-standing international conventions in force specifically relating to them.
Art. 36Where a high-seas or EEZ route of similar convenience exists, Part III does not apply to that strait.
Art. 37Part III applies to straits "which are used for international navigation between one part of the high seas or an exclusive economic zone and another part of the high seas or an exclusive economic zone".
Art. 38Transit passage: all ships and aircraft enjoy the right of transit passage, which "shall not be impeded"; transit passage is the exercise of the freedom of navigation and overflight solely for the purpose of continuous and expeditious transit; there shall be no suspension of transit passage. Where the strait is formed by an island of a State bordering the strait and its mainland, transit passage applies only seaward of the island, subject to Art. 38(2).
Art. 39Duties of ships and aircraft during transit passage: proceed without delay; refrain from any threat or use of force against the sovereignty, territorial integrity or political independence of States bordering the strait; refrain from any activities other than those incident to their normal modes of continuous and expeditious transit; and comply with generally accepted international regulations on safety at sea and prevention of pollution.
Art. 40No marine scientific research or hydrographic survey may be conducted without prior authorisation of the States bordering the strait.
Art. 41Sea lanes and traffic separation schemes may be designated by the States bordering straits, with adoption by the competent international organisation (IMO) — a power expressly allocated to the coastal State but internationalised through the IMO.
Art. 42Laws and regulations of States bordering straits relating to transit passage: safety of navigation and traffic regulation, pollution prevention, fishing vessels (forbidden to fish), loading and unloading, and embarkation or disembarkation of persons contrary to customs, fiscal, immigration or sanitary laws. Such laws must not discriminate and must not have the practical effect of denying, hampering or impairing transit passage.
Art. 43User States and States bordering a strait shall cooperate on navigational and safety aids and on the prevention, reduction and control of pollution — the "user State pays" principle, implemented through the IMO and the Co-operative Mechanism for the Straits of Malacca and Singapore (2007).
Art. 44States bordering straits shall not hamper transit passage; and there shall be no suspension of transit passage.
Art. 45Non-suspendable innocent passage applies to: (a) straits excluded from transit passage because they are formed by an island of a State bordering the strait and its mainland, where there is a high-seas route of similar convenience; and (b) straits between one part of the high seas or an EEZ and the territorial sea of a foreign State. In both, the régime is innocent passage with no possibility of suspension.
Art. 233Safeguards with respect to straits used for international navigation for Part XII (marine environment): a State bordering a strait may not enforce pollution laws against foreign ships inconsistently with Part III, and the enforcement powers of Arts. 218, 220 and 222 do not apply to the extent that they would have the practical effect of denying/hampering/impairing transit passage.
Corfu Channel as the source of the straits régime

Article 37, and the whole structure of Part III, are codifications of a single passage from the judgment in Corfu Channel (case 31): "It is generally recognised and in accordance with international custom that States in time of peace have a right to send their warships through straits used for international navigation between two parts of the high seas without the previous authorization of a coastal State, provided that the passage is innocent. Unless otherwise prescribed in an international convention, there is no right for a coastal State to prohibit such passage through straits in time of peace." The Court's test for "straits used for international navigation" was not how busy the strait is: "the decisive criterion is rather its geographical situation as connecting two parts of the high seas and the fact of its being used for international navigation". That test — geographical situation plus use — is what Part III now inherits in Article 37's reference to straits "used for international navigation".

The Corfu Channel also bequeathed the principle of no coercive self-help: the British minesweeping operation ("Operation Retail") of 12–13 November 1946 was held to be a violation of Albanian sovereignty, notwithstanding Albania's own failure to warn shipping, and the Court's declaration of that violation was accepted as "appropriate satisfaction". For Topic 4, the case is the authority on omission, circumstantial evidence and satisfaction; for Topic 5, it is the authority on straits, and on the proposition that even a wrongful coastal State conduct does not license the use of force in foreign waters.

Real examples — straits in the news
  • Straits of Malacca and Singapore. The busiest choke point in the world, governed by transit passage, with the cooperative mechanism under Article 43 for navigational safety and pollution control; Indonesia, Malaysia and Singapore are the coastal States, and Japan, China, the Republic of Korea and India are among the principal user States contributing to the aids-to-navigation fund.
  • Bab-el-Mandeb and the Strait of Hormuz. Since 2015 and especially after the Red Sea attacks of 2023–24, transit passage through Bab-el-Mandeb has been militarily contested (the Galaxy Leader hijacking of November 2023, the ITLOS award in the Galaxy Leader case of 2024 ordering the release of the vessel). The Strait of Hormuz has seen seizures and tanker attacks (2019) — a live test of Article 39(1)(b)'s prohibition of force by ships in transit, and of Art. 44's non-suspension rule.
  • The Turkish Straits. Governed by the long-standing 1936 Montreux Convention, which is protected by Article 35(c); hence the special régime limiting the passage of certain warships and the tonnage of non-Black Sea navies.
  • Indian waters. The Gulf of Mannar and Palk Bay settlements with Sri Lanka (1974/1976) are the illustration of a strait-like situation resolved by an historic-waters agreement rather than by a straits régime; the Andaman Sea and the Six Degree Channel (India–Thailand/Indonesia) are governed by transit passage and by the 1974/1977/1978 boundary agreements.

I. The territorial sea in Indian law: section by section

Table 5.7 — The territorial sea under the Maritime Zones Act, 1976 and related Indian law
ProvisionContent
Article 297, Constitution"All lands, minerals and other things of value underlying the ocean within the territorial waters, or the continental shelf, or the exclusive economic zone, of India shall vest in the Union and be held for the purposes of the Union"; and the maritime zones themselves (territorial waters, continental shelf, EEZ) are declared to be "the territory of India" for the purposes of Articles 260 and 297 and of legislation. This is the constitutional foundation for the Union's exclusive competence: the States have no jurisdiction over the maritime zones except as delegated.
s. 3, MZA 1976Sovereignty of India over the territorial waters, the seabed and subsoil below, and the airspace above; limit of 12 nm; historic waters declared.
s. 4(1)Notification of the limits of the territorial waters and other maritime zones; the Central Government may, by notification, alter them and prescribe baselines.
s. 4(2)Foreign warships, including submarines and other underwater vehicles, shall enter or pass through Indian territorial waters only after prior notice to the Central Government.
s. 4(3)Exceptions: a foreign warship compelled by weather, damage or distress; and ships carrying Heads of State or Government of foreign States, or of a State's armed forces on official duty, with prior notification.
s. 5Contiguous zone: the zone beyond the territorial sea extending to 24 nm from the baseline, in which India may "exercise such powers and take such measures as may be necessary" to prevent and punish infringements of its customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary laws within its territory or territorial sea (§5.1.2).
s. 6, 7Continental shelf and exclusive economic zone; sovereign rights over resources; jurisdiction for economic purposes; and by the Notification SO 67/E of 27 August 1981, the extension of the Indian Penal Code, the Code of Criminal Procedure, the Civil Procedure Code, the Indian Penal Code's maritime provisions and the Customs Act to the contiguous zone, continental shelf and EEZ, with new section 188A CrPC (now s. 208 BNSS) providing for a designated court in the coastal district — the provision considered at length in Italy v. Union of India (case 34).
