Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act, 1878The oldest Indian provision still in force on the subject: it declares the jurisdiction of the Crown over offences committed on board foreign ships within the territorial waters, and requires the consent of the Governor-General (now Central Government) for prosecutions of foreign officers — the statutory ancestor of the modern notification regime, and the reason a case about jurisdiction over foreign ships in Indian waters has English Victorian law behind it.
Coast Guard Act, 1978Establishes the Indian Coast Guard for the enforcement of maritime law in the territorial sea, contiguous zone and EEZ (with powers of boarding, search, arrest and seizure coextensive with those of a police officer and with the customs and other enforcement agencies). The Coast Guard is India's principal on-scene maritime law-enforcement agency, complemented by the Indian Navy for security operations and by the state marine police.
Fishing Regulation Act, 1981The Maritime Zones of India (Regulation of Fishing by Foreign Vessels) Act, 1981 prohibits fishing in the Indian maritime zones by foreign vessels without a licence, and provides for arrest, confiscation and penalties — the Indian implementation of Articles 61–62 and 73 of UNCLOS (§5.1.4).
Ten things to say about the territorial sea in any answer
  1. Breadth: up to 12 nm from the baseline (Art. 3) — the end of the 3-mile cannon-shot era and of the 1960 deadlock.
  2. Baseline: the low-water line (Art. 5), with the straight-baseline exception (Art. 7) governed by Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries.
  3. Sovereignty over the water, seabed, subsoil and airspace (Art. 2), qualified by the Convention.
  4. Innocent passage for all ships (Art. 17) — the balancing right.
  5. The objective innocence test with the Art. 19(2)(a)–(l) list, and the special rules for submarines (Art. 20).
  6. The coastal State's legislative (Art. 21), protective (Art. 25) and charging (Art. 26) powers and duties (Art. 24).
  7. The four heads of permitted criminal jurisdiction (Art. 27(1)) and the limitation of civil jurisdiction (Art. 28).
  8. Warships: immunity, the "leave immediately" remedy (Art. 30), flag-State responsibility (Art. 31) and the notification controversy.
  9. Internal waters landward of the baseline (Art. 8) with no passage right, subject to Art. 8(2); historic bays and historic waters.
  10. India: Article 297 of the Constitution and sections 3 and 4 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976, with the 12 nm limit, the foreign-warship notification rule and the declaration of historic waters.

↑ top

5.1.2 The Contiguous Zone

A. Text, origin and function

Between the 12-mile line and the 24-mile line lies the belt of "intermediate" or "contiguous" waters — in the words of the old English statutes, the coasts waters; in the language of UNCLOS, the contiguous zone. It exists not because the coastal State owns anything there, but because the coastal State needs a margin of sea in which to protect the interests it has on land and immediately offshore.

Article 33 — Contiguous zone. "1. In a zone contiguous to its territorial sea, described as the contiguous zone, the coastal State may exercise the control necessary to: (a) prevent infringement of its customs, fiscal, immigration or sanitary laws and regulations within its territory or territorial sea; (b) punish infringement of the above laws and regulations committed within its territory or territorial sea. 2. The contiguous zone may not extend beyond 24 nautical miles from the baselines from which the breadth of the territorial sea is measured."

Origin. The contiguous zone is older than the territorial sea in its modern sense. It grew out of the "hovering acts" — the British statutes of 1736 and the Customs Consolidation Act of 1876, the United States' revenue statutes of 1790 and 1799, and the "twelve-mile limit for customs" maintained by the United States after the Civil War — under which the coastal State asserted the right to board and search vessels hovering just outside the three-mile belt to prevent smuggling. The 1930 Hague Codification Conference first used the name "contiguous zone"; the 1958 Geneva Convention on the Territorial Sea and Contiguous Zone gave it its modern form in Article 24 (a belt of the high seas not extending beyond 12 nm from the baseline, in which the coastal State might prevent and punish infringements of customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary regulations); and UNCLOS simply doubled the permitted distance to 24 nm from the baseline (i.e., 12 nm measured from the outer edge of the territorial sea). The course material states the rule exactly: "The 1982 Convention states that contiguous zone may not extend beyond 24 nautical miles from the baselines from which the breadth of the territorial sea is measured... A coastal State thus, can exercise its jurisdiction up to 24 nautical miles."

B. Element-by-element analysis of Article 33

  1. "In a zone contiguous to its territorial sea." The contiguous zone begins where the territorial sea ends. Since the territorial sea may be narrower than 12 nm (a State may claim 3, 6 or 10 nm), the contiguous zone is defined by its inner edge (the outer limit of the territorial sea) and its outer edge (24 nm from the baseline) — not by a fixed width. Where a State claims a 12 nm territorial sea (as India does), the contiguous zone is the 12–24 nm belt.
  2. "May exercise the control necessary." "Control", not sovereignty, and "necessary", not unlimited. The power is functional: it exists for the four enumerated purposes and for nothing else. In the Saiga (No. 2) case (ITLOS, 1999) the Tribunal held that Guinea could not apply its customs legislation to a vessel in its EEZ, because the Convention's scheme allows customs enforcement outside the territorial sea only within the contiguous zone and only in the terms of Article 33; the same reasoning was applied in the M/V "Norstar" case (Panama v. Italy, ITLOS, Judgment of 10 April 2019), where the Tribunal held that Italy's arrest and prosecution of a vessel for bunkering activities carried out beyond the territorial sea could not be justified under Article 33, and that Italy's exercise of criminal jurisdiction infringed the freedom of navigation protected by Article 87(1)(a). Both cases are the authority for the proposition that the contiguous zone is a closed, limited régime and cannot be enlarged by analogy.
  3. "Prevent" and "punish". The two limbs correspond to two different situations. Prevention is prospective and may involve action against a ship before the infringement occurs — a warship or customs vessel may board, inspect, or order a vessel to stop if there is reasonable ground to suspect that the vessel is proceeding towards the territorial sea in order to break the law. Punishment is retrospective and requires an antecedent violation within the territory or territorial sea, since a State cannot punish an act which international law does not permit it to prevent. The distinction is drawn in the Harvard Research and in the ILC commentary of 1956, and it explains why the classic modern cases are about enforcement after the fact (Norstar) rather than about prevention.
  4. The four categories of laws — and the absence of a fifth. Customs (smuggling, evasion of duties); fiscal (taxes and revenue other than customs); immigration (entry, stowaways, trafficking in persons); sanitary (quarantine and public health). A "security" category is not in the article. This is the single most examinable point in the whole topic: several States (Bangladesh, India, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, Vietnam and others) have legislated for "security" in their contiguous-zone statutes, and the legality of such an extension is contested. The correct answer is that Article 33 does not authorise a security zone as such; security interests may however be protected by other lawful means — the coastal State's powers within the territorial sea, the specific competences conferred by other treaties (Art. 234 in ice-covered areas; Art. 108 on drug trafficking; the SUA Convention and the Security Council's piracy resolutions in the specific case of Somali piracy), and measures taken under the law of the sea's general provisions on piracy, hot pursuit and the use of force in self-defence.
  5. "Committed within its territory or territorial sea." This is a jurisdictional condition, and it is the reason the Enrica Lexie case turned out as it did. The classic contiguous-zone case is the one in which the crime has already been committed ashore or in the territorial sea and the offender flees seawards: the coastal State may then continue the pursuit and arrest the offender within the belt. In I'm Alone (1935) the United States coastguard chased a rum-runner from outside the three-mile limit for 200 miles into the Gulf of Mexico on the theory that the vessel was "constructively present" in the territory (its cargo was intended for the United States), and sank it; the Commission held that the sinking was unlawful and awarded compensation to the master and crew of the Canadian vessel, while accepting the general principle of the contiguous zone and of hot pursuit. In Italy v. Union of India the Court noted that the shooting took place about 20.5 nautical miles from the Kerala coast — inside India's contiguous zone, outside her territorial sea — and that the incident's legal consequences therefore had to be traced through section 5 of the Maritime Zones Act and the 1981 notification, which was why the Union, and not the State of Kerala, had the competence to investigate (case 34 below).
  6. No rights over resources, no rights in the airspace. The contiguous zone is not a resource zone: fish, oil, gas and minerals in it belong to the régime of the EEZ and the continental shelf, and where a State claims both, the two zones overlap and the EEZ régime governs the resources. Nor does Article 33 confer airspace rights: the airspace above the contiguous zone is, like the airspace above the EEZ, governed by the freedoms of Article 58 and Article 87, subject to the Chicago Convention's rules for civil aviation. The course material makes the second point expressly: "Unlike the territorial sea, the contiguous zone only gives jurisdiction to a State on the ocean's surface and floor. It does not provide air and space rights."
Saiga (No. 2) 1999 and Norstar 2019.
Fig. 5.4 — Article 33 as a functional régime: the zone is wide but the competence is narrow

C. Enforcement in the contiguous zone: the mechanics and the limits

The coastal State enforces in the contiguous zone through the ordinary instruments of maritime law enforcement: warships, coast-guard vessels, customs cruisers and aircraft; boarding and inspection; the right of visit under Article 110 (which applies to ships on the high seas, and therefore also to ships in the contiguous zone to the extent that the zone is beyond the territorial sea); the power to order a vessel to stop and to divert; and — where the conditions are met — hot pursuit under Article 111.

Article 111(1) — Right of hot pursuit. "The hot pursuit of a foreign ship may be undertaken when the competent authorities of the coastal State have good reason to believe that the ship has violated the laws and regulations of that State. Such pursuit must be commenced when the foreign ship or one of its boats is within the internal waters, the archipelagic waters, the territorial sea or the contiguous zone of the pursuing State, and may only be continued outside the territorial sea or the contiguous zone if the pursuit has not been interrupted. It is not necessary that, at the time when the foreign ship within the territorial sea or the contiguous zone receives the order to stop, the ship giving the order should likewise be within the territorial sea or the contiguous zone. If the foreign ship is within a contiguous zone, as defined in article 33, the pursuit may only be undertaken if there has been a violation of the rights for the protection of which the zone was established."

Article 111(3)–(8), in summary. The pursuit must be continuous and uninterrupted; it must begin after a visual or auditory signal to stop given at a distance at which it can be seen or heard; it ceases as soon as the pursued ship enters the territorial sea of its own State or of a third State; the right applies mutatis mutandis to violations in the EEZ or on the continental shelf; aerial pursuit is permitted where the aircraft has given the order and a ship or aircraft of the coastal State continues the pursuit without interruption; and the right may be exercised only by warships, military aircraft or other ships or aircraft clearly marked and identifiable as being on government service and authorised to that effect. Violence in the exercise of the right is governed by the general international law of the use of force — see I'm Alone (1935) and Red Crusader (1962), in which the fact-finding commission found that shots fired at a fleeing trawler and the subsequent boarding were excessive.

Three practical rules follow. First, hot pursuit from the contiguous zone can only be founded on a violation of the four interests protected by Article 33 — a pursuit based on, say, fisheries enforcement begun in the contiguous zone (rather than the EEZ) is a pursuit begun in the wrong place. This is the Saiga (No. 2) point again: Guinea's pursuit of the Saiga was unlawful because Guinea was trying to enforce customs law in the EEZ, where Article 33 authorises nothing; the Tribunal awarded compensation to Saint Vincent and the Grenadines and found that Guinea's use of force had been excessive. Second, the pursuit must be continuous: any interruption breaks the chain and the coastal State's enforcement powers end at the edge of its zones. Third, force must be necessary and reasonable: the standard is that of the general international law of law enforcement at sea, restated in the Basic Principles on the Use of Force by Officials, in Article 22 of the 1995 Fish Stocks Agreement (which codifies the minimal-use-of-force standard for fisheries enforcement) and applied by ITLOS in Saiga (No. 2) and in the M/V "Virginia G" case (Panama v. Guinea-Bissau, 2014), where the Tribunal held that the use of force by Guinea-Bissau in arresting a bunkering vessel was excessive and that the arrest itself had been unlawful.

D. The Indian provisions in full

Section 5, Maritime Zones Act, 1976 — Contiguous zone of India. "(1) The contiguous zone of India (hereinafter referred to as the contiguous zone) is an area beyond and adjacent to the territorial waters and the limit of the contiguous zone is the line every point of which is at a distance of twenty-four nautical miles from the nearest point of the baseline referred to in sub-section (2) of section 3. (2) Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, the Central Government may exercise such powers and take such measures in or in relation to the contiguous zone as it may deem necessary either by itself or through such authority, agency or person as may be authorised by it in this behalf, with respect to — (a) the security of India; and (b) immigration, sanitation, customs and other fiscal matters."

Four elements of section 5 must be noticed, because they show how India has dealt with the "security" problem identified above.

  • The zone is 24 nm wide in law and, in practice, the 12–24 nm belt, since section 3 fixes the territorial sea at 12 nm. The line is measured from the same baseline as every other zone.
  • India has legislated for security. Section 5(2)(a) expressly includes "the security of India" among the matters in respect of which the Central Government may exercise powers in the contiguous zone — the Indian version of the disputed "security zone". The justification is not Article 33 (which does not mention security) but the sovereign right of the State to protect itself, and the practice is shared with several neighbouring States; it is best defended as an exercise of the State's inherent right of self-preservation exercised consistently with its other treaty obligations, and as being in any event confined to prevention and punishment, not to resource appropriation.
  • The enforcement structure is central, not state. Section 5(2) vests the power in the Central Government, "either by itself or through such authority, agency or person as may be authorised by it". This is why the notification regime matters: SO 67/E of 27 August 1981 extended the Indian Penal Code, the Code of Criminal Procedure (with the new section 188A), the Civil Procedure Code and the Customs Act to the contiguous zone, the continental shelf and the EEZ, and designated a court in the coastal district as the special court. In Italy v. Union of India the Supreme Court held that section 5 and the notification, read with Article 297 of the Constitution, gave the Union the exclusive competence to investigate and prosecute offences committed in the contiguous zone, and that the State of Kerala could not exercise jurisdiction — a conclusion that the Court reached on the basis that sovereignty over the maritime zones vests in the Union and that the State's police powers end at the water's edge in the absence of delegation.
  • Related Indian legislation operating in the contiguous zone. The Customs Act, 1962 (definitions of "India" and "customs waters", and the powers of customs officers to board, search, seize and arrest in the "customs area" and within the territorial waters); the Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances Act, 1985 (with the presumptions that facilitate prosecution of drug trafficking discovered in the contiguous zone); the Foreigners Act, 1946 and the Passport Act, 1920 (immigration offences); the Epidemic Diseases Act, 1897 and the Indian Port Health Rules (sanitary control); the Coast Guard Act, 1978 (powers of interception, boarding, search, seizure and arrest); and the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 for security-related offences. The 2026 piracy convictions in Mumbai (§5.5) show how the enforcement chain crosses zones: interception on the high seas, delivery to the nearest Indian police station, and trial before a designated special court.
Real examples — the contiguous zone in action
  • Drug interdiction. India's drug seizures at sea — the largest being the 2023–24 operations in which the Indian Navy and the Narcotics Control Bureau intercepted vessels carrying thousands of kilograms of methamphetamine and heroin in the Arabian Sea and the Indian Ocean, one of which involved the boarding of a dhow 300 nm from the coast and another a fishing vessel inside the contiguous zone — are the standard modern illustration of contiguous-zone and Art. 108 enforcement working together with high-seas interdiction.
  • Migration. The 2015 Andaman Sea boat crisis and Australia's Tampa episode (2001) are the classic examples of the contested boundary between a coastal State's immigration powers in the contiguous zone and its obligations under the 1951 Refugee Convention and the SAR Convention.
  • Smuggling. The classic old cases — I'm Alone (1935), the Red Crusader (1962) — arose from customs enforcement (rum-running; illegal fishing). Their modern equivalents are the oil bunkering and diesel-smuggling cases: Saiga (No. 2) (1999) and M/V "Norstar" (2019).
  • Enrica Lexie. The shooting of two fishermen at about 20.5 nm off the Kerala coast — inside the contiguous zone — is the Indian case that put the whole régime to work; the Italian marines were arrested in India, and the legal foundation of India's competence was section 5 of the 1976 Act, not the law of the territorial sea (case 34).
Model answer structure — "Write a note on the contiguous zone" (10 marks)
  1. Definition and distance: Art. 33; a belt beyond the territorial sea, not beyond 24 nm from the baseline; India: s. 5, MZA 1976, 12–24 nm.
  2. Nature: not sovereignty, not a resource zone; "control necessary" for four purposes; no airspace rights.
  3. The four heads: customs, fiscal, immigration, sanitary; with the two limbs — prevention (prospective) and punishment (retrospective, and only for breaches committed in the territory or territorial sea).
  4. Origin: the hovering acts; the 1930 Hague Conference; 1958 Art. 24; the doubling to 24 nm in 1982.
  5. Enforcement and limits: boarding, inspection, hot pursuit (Art. 111(1) — the special condition for pursuits begun in the contiguous zone); the I'm Alone, Red Crusader, Saiga (No. 2) and Norstar cases; the minimal-force standard.
  6. The security question: not in Art. 33; India's s. 5(2)(a), and the debate; how the gap is filled (self-defence, SUA, piracy resolutions, Art. 108).
  7. Indian illustration and conclusion: Italy v. Union of India — 20.5 nm = contiguous zone = Union competence.

↑ top

5.1.3 The Continental Shelf

A. Definition: geology, law, and the two criteria

The continental shelf is the one zone in the Convention whose definition is borrowed from physical geography. Geologically, the "continental margin" comprises three provinces: the continental shelf proper — the gently sloping submarine extension of the continent, usually to a depth of about 130–200 metres and a breadth of 10–100 km; the continental slope — the steep descent from the shelf edge to the deep ocean floor; and the continental rise — the gentler apron of sediment at the foot of the slope. Shelf, slope and rise together make up the margin, which constitutes about 23 per cent of the ocean floor but contains most of its recoverable oil and gas. The legal definition, however, is not geological but juridical, and it has passed through three formulations.

Table 5.10 — The three definitions of the continental shelf
InstrumentDefinitionDefect
Truman Proclamation, 28 September 1945 (Proclamation No. 2667)The United States "regards the natural resources of the subsoil and seabed of the continental shelf beneath the high seas but contiguous to the coasts of the United States as appertaining to the United States, subject to its jurisdiction and control" — a unilateral assertion, coupled with the statement that the waters above remained high seas and that the freedom of navigation was unaffected.Unilateral; no definition of the shelf's seaward limit; but it triggered the worldwide process of claims and the 1958 codification.
Geneva Convention on the Continental Shelf, 1958, Article 1"The term 'continental shelf' is used to refer to (a) the seabed and subsoil of the submarine areas adjacent to the coast but outside the area of the territorial sea, to a depth of 200 metres or, beyond that limit, to where the depth of the superjacent waters admits of the exploitation of the natural resources of the said areas; (b) to the seabed and subsoil of similar submarine areas adjacent to the coasts of islands."The exploitability clause made the shelf's limit depend on technology, and therefore expandable without limit; Article 12 permitted reservations to Articles 1–3, with the consequence that the definition and the equidistance rule were not, as such, customary law — the finding in North Sea Continental Shelf (case 32) which the course material highlights: "The Geneva Convention on Continental Shelf allowed States parties to make reservation with respect to certain important provisions. This meant that those provisions would never be transformed into customary law."
UNCLOS 1982, Article 76(1)"The continental shelf of a coastal State comprises the seabed and subsoil of the submarine areas that extend beyond its territorial sea throughout the natural prolongation of its land territory to the outer edge of the continental margin, or to a distance of 200 nautical miles from the baselines from which the breadth of the territorial sea is measured where the outer edge of the continental margin does not extend up to that distance."None for practical purposes: the definition is now dual — natural prolongation to the outer edge of the margin, or a guaranteed 200 nm, whichever is greater — and the outer limits on broad-margin coasts are disciplined by the formulae and constraints of Article 76(4)–(7) and by the CLCS.

Article 76 therefore has two limbs, and the examiner's favourite question — "what is the continental shelf: a geological or a legal concept?" — is answered by separating them. Limb one: where the continental margin extends beyond 200 nm (a "broad-margin" or "marginal" State: Canada, Brazil, Australia, Norway, India, Sri Lanka, Ireland, Russia, New Zealand), the shelf extends to the "outer edge of the continental margin", subject to the formulae and constraints in Article 76(4)–(6). Limb two: where the margin does not extend to 200 nm (a "narrow-margin" State: most of the Pacific coast of South America, West Africa, Pakistan, Bangladesh), the shelf nevertheless extends to 200 nm by the distance criterion — a rule which makes the shelf and the EEZ coincident in width for such States. For States within 400 nm of each other, the distance criterion effectively determines both the shelf and the EEZ; the International Court said exactly that in Libya/Malta (1985): where the distance between the coasts is less than 400 nm, "the distance criterion must now apply to the continental shelf as well as to the exclusive economic zone".

B. The outer limits on a broad-margin coast — Article 76(4) to (7)

Article 76(4) gives the coastal State two alternative ways to fix the outer edge of the margin — the "Heezen–Tharp" line-and-formula method and the "Gardiner" sediment-thickness method — and Article 76(5) then imposes two constraints. The architecture is best learned as an exercise in applied geometry.

Fig. 5.5 — Article 76(4)–(7): two formulae, two constraints, and the CLCS to check them

Read the article in four steps.

  1. Article 76(3) — the continental margin and the foot of the slope. "The continental margin comprises the submerged prolongation of the land mass of the coastal State, and consists of the seabed and subsoil of the shelf, the slope and the rise. It does not include the deep ocean floor with its oceanic ridges or the subsoil thereof." The "foot of the continental slope" is defined in Article 76(4)(b) as "the point of maximum change in the gradient at its base".
  2. Article 76(4)(a) — the two formulae. (i) The sediment-thickness or Gardiner formula: a line drawn through the outermost points at each of which the thickness of sedimentary rocks is at least 1 per cent of the shortest distance from the point to the foot of the continental slope; or (ii) a line drawn 60 nautical miles from the foot of the continental slope. The coastal State may use either and is not obliged to choose the more conservative; the CLCS's practice is to accept the more favourable line where the geology supports it.
  3. Article 76(5) — the two constraints. The fixed points comprising the line drawn under paragraph 4 shall not exceed 350 nm from the baselines, or 100 nm from the 2,500-metre isobath (the line joining points of 2,500 m water depth). The two are alternatives: the coastal State may use whichever is further seaward (so a State whose margin is very broad may claim beyond 350 nm if the 2,500 m isobath lies far enough out). The 350 nm limit is the single most frequently examined number after 200 nm — it is the maximum outer limit of the continental shelf anywhere in the world.
  4. Article 76(6) — ridges. "Notwithstanding the provisions of paragraph 5, on submarine ridges, the outer limit of the continental shelf shall not exceed 350 nautical miles from the baselines... This paragraph does not apply to submarine elevations that are natural components of the continental margin, such as its plateaux, rises, caps, banks and spurs." The distinction between a submarine ridge (limited to 350 nm) and a submarine elevation (unlimited, subject to Art. 76(5)) has generated the largest disputes in the CLCS's practice — most famously in the Russian Federation's claim to the Mendeleev and Lomonosov Ridges in the Arctic, in Australia's Kerguelen Plateau claim, and in the Japanese claim around the Ogasawara Plateau. Article 76(7) adds the technical rule that the fixed points of the outer limit line, when drawn, shall be joined by straight lines not exceeding 60 nm in length.

C. The rights of the coastal State over the shelf — Articles 77 to 85

Table 5.11 — The continental-shelf régime article by article
ArticleRule and its practical meaning
Art. 76(8)–(9)Information on the limits of the shelf beyond 200 nm, supported by scientific and technical data, is to be submitted by the coastal State to the Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf, which makes recommendations; "the limits of the shelf established by a coastal State on the basis of these recommendations shall be final and binding". The coastal State is to deposit charts and relevant information, including geodetic data, with the Secretary-General of the United Nations, who is to give due publicity.
Art. 77Sovereign rights. "1. The coastal State exercises over the continental shelf sovereign rights for the purpose of exploring it and exploiting its natural resources. 2. The rights referred to in paragraph 1 are exclusive in the sense that if the coastal State does not explore the continental shelf or exploit its natural resources, no one may undertake these activities without the express consent of the coastal State. 3. The rights of the coastal State over the continental shelf do not depend on occupation, effective or notional, or on any express proclamation. 4. The natural resources referred to... consist of the mineral and other non-living resources of the seabed and subsoil together with living organisms belonging to sedentary species, that is to say, organisms which, at the harvestable stage, either are immobile on or under the seabed or are unable to move except in constant physical contact with the seabed or the subsoil."
Art. 78Legal status of the superjacent waters and air space. "1. The rights of the coastal State over the continental shelf do not affect the legal status of the superjacent waters or of the air space above those waters. 2. The exercise of the rights of the coastal State over the continental shelf must not infringe or result in any unjustifiable interference with navigation and other rights and freedoms of other States as provided for in this Convention."
Art. 79Submarine cables and pipelines on the shelf. All States are entitled to lay submarine cables and pipelines on the shelf; the coastal State may not impede their laying or maintenance, but may take reasonable measures for the exploration of the shelf, the exploitation of its resources and the prevention, reduction and control of pollution; the route of a pipeline and the consent of the coastal State for entry into its territory are preserved; and in laying cables or pipelines States must have due regard to those already in place.
Art. 80Article 60 (artificial islands, installations and structures, safety zones) applies mutatis mutandis to the continental shelf.
Art. 81Drilling on the continental shelf. "The coastal State shall have the exclusive right to authorize and regulate drilling on the continental shelf for all purposes." The words "for all purposes" are deliberate: they cover scientific drilling as well as oil and gas, and they are the reason a foreign research vessel may not drill on a shelf without consent (see Art. 246(5)(b)).
Art. 82Payments and contributions with respect to the exploitation of the continental shelf beyond 200 nm. The coastal State "shall make payments or contributions in kind in respect of the exploitation of the non-living resources of the continental shelf beyond 200 nm": at 1 per cent of the value or volume of production at the site for the sixth year of production, rising by 1 per cent each year to 7 per cent from the twelfth year onward. Payments are made through the ISA, which distributes them to States Parties taking into account the interests of developing States, particularly least developed and land-locked States. Article 82(3) exempts a developing State which is a net importer of a mineral resource produced from its own shelf. This is the "revenue-sharing" innovation of 1982, and the only provision of the shelf régime with direct distributive content.
Art. 83Delimitation of the shelf between opposite or adjacent States is by agreement on the basis of international law, as appropriate on the basis of the equidistance line, in order to achieve an equitable solution; failing agreement within a reasonable period, the States resort to the procedures of Part XV. Article 83(3) requires the parties, pending agreement, to make every effort to enter into provisional arrangements of a practical nature and not to hamper the reaching of a final agreement.
Art. 85Tunnelling. The right of the coastal State to exploit the subsoil by means of tunnelling, irrespective of the depth of the water above, is not affected by the Convention — the provision behind the Japanese Seikan Tunnel, the Channel Tunnel and any future sub-seabed road or rail link; in India, it is the provision that would govern any sub-sea tunnel to Lakshadweep or across the Palk Strait.

D. Why the shelf still matters — the case law and the doctrines

Three doctrines from the case law must be carried into any answer on this head.

(1) The shelf right is ipso jure and independent of occupation. Article 77(3) is the codification of the International Court's holding in North Sea Continental Shelf (case 32) that the rights of the coastal State over its shelf "exist ipso jure and from the outset" by virtue of its sovereignty over the land, because the shelf is the natural prolongation of the land territory. Two consequences: a State cannot lose its shelf by failing to claim or use it, and a State that has proclaimed a shelf which international law does not recognise acquires nothing.

(2) Natural prolongation and the distance criterion. In 1969 the Court treated natural prolongation as the "most fundamental" of the principles of the shelf doctrine; by 1982, Article 76(1) had added the distance criterion; and in Tunisia/Libya (case 33) the Court, reviewing the new Convention, held that "natural prolongation... is not the only criterion", and that in the modern law "the principle is that the shelf is the natural prolongation of the land territory... up to the outer edge of the continental margin or 200 nautical miles, whichever is greater", and that where the distance between the coasts is less than 400 nm, distance becomes the controlling criterion. In Libya/Malta (1985) the Court held that the distance criterion "must now apply to the continental shelf as well as to the exclusive economic zone" in such geographical situations. The lesson for an answer: natural prolongation explains why the shelf belongs to the coastal State; distance fixes where its limits are in most disputes; and beyond 200 nm, geology returns, which is precisely why the CLCS exists.

(3) The shelf and the EEZ are separate but interlocking. The shelf can exist without an EEZ, and vice versa; the seabed rights in the EEZ are in substance the shelf rights (Art. 56(1)(a) covers "the seabed and its subsoil"), but the two are delimited by the same single maritime boundary in practice, and the Convention's Article 76(10) insists that the shelf's outer limits "do not prejudice the question of the delimitation of the continental shelf between States with opposite or adjacent coasts". Where the two diverge is precisely at the outer limit: beyond 200 nm a State may have shelf but no EEZ, which is the situation of the "grey area" (case 35) and, for India, of the Central Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal claims.

E. The Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf

The CLCS is not a court but a scientific-technical body. It was established by Article 76(8) and Annex II: 21 members, experts in geology, geophysics or hydrography, elected by the Meeting of States Parties from among the candidates nominated by States Parties, serving in their personal capacity for five-year terms, with one member from each of the five regional groups at least. The applicable procedure, in outline:

  • Submission. A coastal State intending to establish the outer limits of its shelf beyond 200 nm shall submit particulars of those limits to the CLCS within ten years of the entry into force of the Convention for that State; the Meeting of States Parties decided in 2001 that the deadline could be satisfied by a preliminary submission, and subsequently extended the deadline for several States.
  • Examination. The Commission's subcommission examines the data, may seek clarifications, and prepares recommendations; the Commission adopts them, and the coastal State then "establishes" its outer limits "on the basis of" the recommendations, which are "final and binding" as between that State and other States Parties.
  • The overlapping-claims problem. Because the Commission is not competent to decide disputes between States, its Rules provide that, where a submission involves areas in dispute, it will not consider the submission in respect of those areas unless the States concerned have given their prior consent; and Annex I to the Rules obliges States with overlapping claims to "refrain from making any submission... which may prejudice the final delimitation of the continental shelf", and to make every effort to resolve their disputes (pending delimitation, they may make joint or separate submissions without prejudice to delimitation).
  • What the Commission does not do. It does not delimit boundaries between States, does not decide legal questions of entitlement, and does not create the shelf; the shelf exists by law, and the Commission's function is to verify the evidence supporting the outer limit. That is why the Bay of Bengal Tribunal (case 35) could delimit a shelf beyond 200 nm without waiting for the CLCS, holding that it had jurisdiction because both parties were parties to the Convention and the delimitation of a shelf beyond 200 nm is a question of entitlement, not of the Commission's procedure.

F. India and the continental shelf

  • Section 6, Maritime Zones Act, 1976. The continental shelf of India comprises the seabed and subsoil of the submarine areas that extend beyond the territorial waters throughout the natural prolongation of the land territory to the outer edge of the continental margin, or to a distance of 200 nm from the baselines where the outer edge does not extend that far; and India has sovereign rights for the purpose of exploring, exploiting, conserving and managing the natural resources, and jurisdiction over the establishment of artificial islands, installations and structures, marine scientific research, and the protection of the marine environment. Section 6 also declares the shelf to be part of the "territory of India" for constitutional purposes, following Article 297 of the Constitution.
  • The oldest Indian resource case. Production from the Bombay High field began in 1976, following the 1974 discovery in the Mumbai Offshore Basin, and it was the first major demonstration that a continental-shelf claim could be made to yield revenue; the Bassein, Heera, Neelam and Panna–Mukta fields followed, and the Krishna–Godavari Basin (discoveries from 2002, the KG-D6 field) and the Cauvery and Mahanadi basins consolidated the shelf as India's principal domestic hydrocarbon source. The Oilfields (Regulation and Development) Act, 1948, the Petroleum and Natural Gas Act, 1948, the Oil and Natural Gas Commission Act, 1959, the Petroleum and Natural Gas Regulatory Board Act, 2006 and the newer Oilfields (Regulation and Development) Amendment Act, 2025 are the statutory chain, and the New Exploration Licensing Policy (1997-99) and its successor Hydrocarbon Exploration and Licensing Policy (2016) the licensing chain.
  • Extended continental shelf claim. India made partial submissions to the CLCS in 2009 in respect of areas in the Bay of Bengal, the Indian Ocean and the Arabian Sea, claiming an additional area of about 1.2 million sq km of seabed beyond its EEZ (the precise areas are those defined in the submissions lodged with the Commission, prepared by the National Centre for Polar and Ocean Research and the Ministry of Earth Sciences). Reported developments since then: India has revised its claim in respect of the Central Arabian Sea — a modified submission was reported in April 2025, adding some 10,000 sq km in areas free of overlap — while its claims in the Bay of Bengal and the Indian Ocean are affected by overlapping interests and objections from Sri Lanka and Myanmar (reported), and the position regarding Oman is that the two States agreed in 2010 that the overlapping area is not in dispute. These are matters of diplomatic practice rather than decided law; for an answer, cite them as illustrations of the Article 76(8) process and note that until the Commission's recommendations are made, the outer limits are provisional.
  • What is at stake. An area of shelf beyond 200 nm carries with it exclusive rights to explore and exploit resources — the outer shelf off the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, the Bay of Bengal and the Central Arabian Sea is prospected for gas hydrates, polymetallic sulphides and cobalt crusts as well as hydrocarbons. Since Article 82 would require payments from the twelfth year of production, India's diplomatic interest in an early and favourable recommendation is doubly economic: the resources, and the avoidance of surprise in the Article 82 accounts.
Real examples — the shelf in the world
  • The Arctic. Russia's 2001, 2015 and 2021 submissions (the last significantly expanded, claiming the Mendeleev and Lomonosov Ridges), Canada's 2019 submission, Denmark's (Greenland) claims, and Norway's 2006 submission and 2009 recommendations — the largest Article 76 exercise ever conducted, and the reason the "submarine ridge" question in Article 76(6) is so politically charged.
  • Australia's 2004 submission and 2012 recommendations extended its shelf beyond 200 nm in nine regions, adding about 2.5 million sq km of seabed — including areas off the Kerguelen and Heard–McDonald Islands.
  • Brazil's 2004/2007 submissions in respect of the Amazon Fan, and the 2015 partial recommendations — the first major southern-hemisphere claim, and the largest.
  • The Bay of Bengal. Both Bangladesh and Myanmar made submissions to the CLCS, and the ITLOS judgment of 14 March 2012 in Bangladesh/Myanmar delimited the shelf beyond 200 nm before the Commission had completed its work — the case that established that the CLCS's process does not bar judicial delimitation.
  • India's neighbourhood. Sri Lanka's extended-shelf claim in the Gulf of Mannar and the Bay of Bengal overlaps India's; Myanmar's claim overlaps both India's and Bangladesh's; Pakistan protested India's western Arabian Sea claim in the Sir Creek area — which is why India's 2025 modification concentrated on uncontested Central Arabian Sea acreage.
Four-point skeleton for "Discuss the concept of the continental shelf with reference to recent developments"
  1. Definition and criteria: Art. 76(1)'s two limbs; the 1958 definition and its exploitability clause; the dual criterion of natural prolongation and distance.
  2. Rights: Art. 77 (ipso jure, exclusive, sedentary species); Art. 78 (superjacent waters unaffected); Arts. 79–81 (cables, installations, drilling); Art. 82 (payments beyond 200 nm).
  3. Recent developments: the CLCS and the Article 76(8) process; the extended-shelf claims of Russia, Australia, Brazil, India, Sri Lanka and others; the distinction between submarine ridges and elevations; the 2012 ITLOS judgment and the 2014 PCA award in the Bay of Bengal, including the "grey area"; and the tension between CLCS competence and delimitation by courts.
  4. India: Art. 297 of the Constitution, s. 6 of the MZA 1976, the Bombay High and KG basin, the 2009 submissions and later modifications, and the strategic significance of the extended shelf for India's blue economy.

↑ top

5.1.4 The Exclusive Economic Zone

A. What the zone is, and how it was born

Article 55 — Specific legal régime of the exclusive economic zone. "The exclusive economic zone is an area beyond and adjacent to the territorial sea, subject to the specific legal régime established in this Part, under which the rights and jurisdiction of the coastal State and the rights and freedoms of other States are governed by the relevant provisions of this Convention."

Article 57 — Breadth of the exclusive economic zone. "The exclusive economic zone shall not extend beyond 200 nautical miles from the baselines from which the breadth of the territorial sea is measured."

Article 55 is carefully drafted to avoid saying what kind of zone the EEZ is. It is not the territorial sea (the coastal State has no sovereignty there), and it is not the high seas (the high-seas freedoms are not exercisable there as of right against the coastal State's resource competences). The Convention calls it a "specific legal régime" — the sui generis zone — and the whole of Part V is the specification of that régime. The course material describes the zone in the terms you should use in the answer: "The exclusive economic zone (EEZ) is an area beyond and adjacent to the territorial sea which may extend up to 200 nautical miles from the baselines from which the breadth of the territorial sea is measured. In the exclusive economic zone, the coastal State has the sovereign rights for the purpose of exploring and exploiting, conserving and managing the natural resources, whether living or non-living, of the waters superjacent to the seabed and of the seabed and its subsoil."

History matters here, because the examiners ask for it. Three stages.

  1. 1945–1952: the unilateral extension of resource jurisdiction. The Truman Proclamations of 28 September 1945 claimed for the United States jurisdiction over the resources of the continental shelf (Proclamation 2667) and the right to establish conservation zones in the high seas adjacent to its coast for fisheries (Proclamation 2668). Chile and Peru followed in 1947 by claiming sovereignty and jurisdiction over the sea within 200 nm; the Santiago Declaration of 1952 (Chile, Ecuador and Peru) declared the 200-mile "maritime zone"; Iceland, Canada, Australia, New Zealand and others created fisheries zones of varying widths; the European States created the 12-mile fisheries zone after the 1964 London Fisheries Convention.
  2. 1972–1974: the African and Asian initiative that produced the name. The concept of an "exclusive economic zone" — as distinct from a territorial claim — was placed on the agenda by the Asian–African Legal Consultative Committee and by the OAU's 1973 Declaration on the Law of the Sea. The Kenyan delegation's proposal of 1972, prepared for the AALCC session in Colombo and refined at Yaoundé, is conventionally credited with the term and the design: a 200-mile zone of sovereign rights over resources, coupled with the preservation of navigation freedoms — the compromise on which the Conference eventually settled. The UNCLOS III conference (Caracas, 1974 onwards) adopted the zone in the Informal Composite Negotiating Text, and Part V emerged substantially intact.
  3. 1982 to the present: from treaty to custom. Some 120–150 States now claim an EEZ or an exclusive fisheries zone. The International Court held in Tunisia/Libya (1982) that the concept "may be regarded as part of modern international law", and in Libya/Malta (1985) that the institution of the EEZ "is shown by the practice of States to have become a part of customary law". In the Nicaragua v. United States case (1986) the Court applied the customary EEZ to a State (the United States) that had neither signed nor ratified UNCLOS. Once custom, the zone binds even non-parties in its broad outlines — which is why the United States, not a party, polices the EEZs of others and asserts its own rights to navigate within them.

B. The rights and jurisdiction of the coastal State — Article 56

Article 56(1). "In the exclusive economic zone, the coastal State has: (a) sovereign rights for the purpose of exploring and exploiting, conserving and managing the natural resources, whether living or non-living, of the waters superjacent to the seabed and of the seabed and its subsoil, and with regard to other activities for the economic exploitation and exploration of the zone, such as the production of energy from the water, currents and winds; (b) jurisdiction as provided for in the relevant provisions of this Convention with regard to: (i) the establishment and use of artificial islands, installations and structures; (ii) marine scientific research; (iii) the protection and preservation of the marine environment; (c) other rights and duties provided for in this Convention."

Article 56(2). "In exercising its rights and performing its duties under this Convention in the exclusive economic zone, the coastal State shall have due regard to the rights and duties of other States and shall act in a manner compatible with the provisions of this Convention."

Analyse Article 56(1) in four moves, because each move is a different examination point.

(1) "Sovereign rights", not sovereignty. The phrase was chosen precisely to mark the difference from Article 2. The rights are "sovereign" in that they are exclusive — no other State may exercise them without the coastal State's consent — and in that they are inherent in the coastal State, not dependent on any proclamation. But they are "rights" — limited to the purposes specified — and they do not carry the presumption of unlimited competence which sovereignty carries. This is the point the Supreme Court made in Italy v. Union of India when it held that in the EEZ the Union exercises "sovereign rights" for specified purposes, which is why the extension of the Indian Penal Code to the EEZ by notification had to be grounded in the constitutional power to legislate for the Union's maritime zones, and not in the ordinary criminal jurisdiction of a State.

(2) The natural resources covered. All living and non-living resources of the water column, seabed and subsoil: fish, crustaceans, molluscs other than sedentary species (which belong to the continental-shelf régime), oil, gas, sand, gravel, polymetallic deposits within the EEZ seabed, and the genetic resources of the water column (the subject of the BBNJ Agreement's negotiation insofar as marine genetic resources lie in Areas Beyond National Jurisdiction). To this Article 56 adds "other activities for the economic exploitation and exploration of the zone, such as the production of energy from the water, currents and winds" — the textual basis of every modern offshore-wind and ocean-energy licence, and the provision India cites for its offshore wind policy (§5.5).

(3) Jurisdiction, not rights, over three matters. Artificial islands, installations and structures (Art. 60: the coastal State has the exclusive right to construct, authorise and regulate them, with safety zones up to 500 m, and has jurisdiction over customs, fiscal, health, safety and immigration laws in them); marine scientific research (Art. 246: consent regime, with implied consent in defined circumstances and four exceptional categories in which consent may be withheld); and the protection and preservation of the marine environment (Part XII, including the coastal State's enforcement powers under Art. 220, which allow proceedings, including detention, against vessels in the EEZ which have violated pollution laws).

(4) The residuum — Article 59. For rights and jurisdictions not attributed by the Convention, "the conflict should be resolved on the basis of equity and in the light of all the relevant circumstances, having regard to the respective importance of the interests involved to the parties as well as to the international community as a whole". Article 59 is the Convention's own admission that the EEZ's boundaries are unfinished — and it is the article to cite in an answer on military activities, hydrographic survey, or unforeseen uses such as floating data centres and ocean carbon removal.

C. The rights and freedoms of other States — Article 58

Article 58(1)–(2). "1. In the exclusive economic zone, all States, whether coastal or land-locked, enjoy, subject to the relevant provisions of this Convention, the freedoms referred to in article 87 of navigation and overflight and of the laying of submarine cables and pipelines, and other internationally lawful uses of the sea related to these freedoms, such as those associated with the operation of ships, aircraft and submarine cables and pipelines, and compatible with the other provisions of this Convention. 2. Articles 88 to 115 and other pertinent rules of international law apply to the exclusive economic zone in so far as they are not incompatible with this Part."

Article 58(3). "In exercising their rights and performing their duties under this Convention in the exclusive economic zone, States shall have due regard to the rights and duties of the coastal State and shall comply with the laws and regulations adopted by the coastal State in accordance with the provisions of this Convention and other rules of international law in so far as they are not incompatible with this Part."

The surviving freedoms. Navigation; overflight; the laying of submarine cables and pipelines; and "other internationally lawful uses of the sea related to these freedoms". Note what is not there: the freedoms of fishing and of scientific research are excluded, because those are subject to the coastal State's competences. Note also the phrase "subject to the relevant provisions of this Convention", which is the textual hook for the coastal State's right to regulate, for instance, the routeing of vessels carrying dangerous cargo or the installation of safety zones around its structures.

The application of Articles 88 to 115. By Article 58(2), the high-seas rules travel into the EEZ "in so far as they are not incompatible with this Part". Hence: the EEZ is reserved for peaceful purposes (Art. 88); piracy and the right of visit apply in the EEZ (Arts. 100–107, 110); the flag State's exclusive jurisdiction applies (Art. 92, with the exception of the coastal State's enforcement under Art. 73); the rules on drugs (Art. 108) and unauthorised broadcasting (Art. 109) apply in the EEZ, with the coastal State having the power to arrest persons broadcasting without authority from the EEZ; and hot pursuit under Article 111 may begin in the EEZ for violations of the coastal State's EEZ laws.

The military-activities controversy. Do warships and military aircraft enjoy the same navigational freedoms in a foreign EEZ as on the high seas? The coastal State's answer is that they do not: the EEZ is a zone of "functionally limited sovereignty", and Article 88's reservation of the EEZ for "peaceful purposes" excludes military exercises, intelligence gathering, hydrographic survey and weapons testing without consent. The maritime States' answer is that Article 58(1) expressly imports the freedom of navigation and overflight and "other internationally lawful uses", that warships are ships, and that Article 88's "peaceful purposes" means only what Article 88 says — no aggressive use of force — not a licence to exclude naval activity that is otherwise lawful. The correct examination answer is that the text supports the maritime States' reading, but that State practice — the Chinese restrictions in the South China Sea, the 1985 Soviet declaration, the "exclusive economic zone military activities" incidents, and the 2001 EP-3 collision over Hainan — shows continuing disagreement, and that Article 59 is the only textual tie-breaker. In the Enrica Lexie arbitration, this debate was one of the issues argued: India relied on its EEZ laws and the 1981 notification; Italy relied on the freedoms of the high seas and, in part, on the proposition that the Indian criminal-law extension to the EEZ could not bind it. The Tribunal found that Article 97 did not apply, and held (2020) that the incident occurred on the high seas and that Italy's marines were entitled to immunity — while finding that Italy had violated India's rights under Articles 87(1)(a) and 90 in relation to the St. Antony (case 34, postscript).

D. Living resources: Articles 61 to 68

Table 5.8 — Fisheries under Part V: article, obligation and Indian practice
ArticleRuleIllustration and related instruments
Art. 61Conservation: the coastal State determines the allowable catch (TAC), on the basis of the best scientific evidence available, taking into account the effects on species associated with or dependent upon harvested species, and must take such measures as may be necessary to maintain or restore populations at levels which can produce the maximum sustainable yield (MSY), qualified by relevant environmental and economic factors. It must also contribute and exchange scientific information through the competent international organisations.The FAO Code of Conduct for Responsible Fisheries (1995); the UN Fish Stocks Agreement (1995); the FAO Port State Measures Agreement (2009), which India ratified; the WTO Agreement on Fisheries Subsidies adopted at the Twelfth Ministerial Conference (June 2022), where India pressed successfully for the "special and differential treatment" carve-out and for discipline on distant-water subsidies.
Art. 62Utilisation: the coastal State promotes the objective of optimum utilisation, determines its own capacity to harvest, and where it does not have the capacity, "shall... give other States access to the surplus". Article 62(4) sets out a long list of laws the coastal State may adopt: licensing, fees, catch reporting, landing requirements, observers, fitness of vessels, and — the ancestor of national protectionism — local crewing.India's surplus-access regime: the Maritime Zones of India (Regulation of Fishing by Foreign Vessels) Act, 1981, which prohibits all foreign fishing other than under licence and permits joint ventures on central-government terms; the deep-sea fishing policy of 1977 and the subsequent joint-venture and charter arrangements (the controversial 2015–16 "deep sea fishing" proposal, withdrawn in 2017 after the fishermen's protests).
Art. 63Stocks occurring within the EEZs of two or more States, and straddling stocks (occurring both within and beyond the EEZ): the States concerned "shall seek, either directly or through appropriate sub-regional or regional organizations, to agree upon the measures necessary" for conservation and development.India's fisheries cooperation in the Bay of Bengal through BOBP-IGO (Bay of Bengal Programme Inter-Governmental Organisation, 2003) and IOTC (Indian Ocean Tuna Commission) — the regional mechanisms that give Article 63 effect in the Indian Ocean.
Art. 64Highly migratory species (tuna, marlin, swordfish, etc.): the coastal State and other States fishing in the region "shall cooperate, directly or through appropriate international organizations, with a view to ensuring conservation and promoting the objective of optimum utilisation of such species throughout the region, both within and beyond the exclusive economic zone."IOTC, and the Indian Ocean's yellowfin tuna stock, whose conservation status has produced recurring disputes between coastal States and distant-water fleets (the 2020s debate over catch limits). The 1995 Fish Stocks Agreement implements Articles 63 and 64 with a precautionary approach and binding dispute settlement.
Art. 65–67Marine mammals (States may prohibit, limit or regulate their exploitation); anadromous species (salmon: the State of origin of the rivers has the primary interest and responsibility, and fishing for them may be prohibited beyond 200 nm — the "salmon principle"); catadromous species (eels: the coastal State manages them in whose waters they spend most of their life cycle).The Behring Sea Fur Seal Arbitration (1893) and the 1911 North Pacific Fur Seal Convention; the 1992 Convention for the Conservation of Anadromous Stocks in the North Pacific Ocean; the Pacific Fur Seals case is the ancestor of Article 66. Japan's whaling programme under the guise of scientific research, criticised for decades, ended in Japan's withdrawal from the IWC (2019) — the reason Article 65 matters.
Art. 68"This Part does not apply to sedentary species as defined in article 77, paragraph 4" — i.e. organisms that at the harvestable stage are immobile on or under the seabed, or unable to move except in constant physical contact with it. They belong to the continental shelf régime, not the EEZ.Sedentary species: crab, lobster, prawn, abalone, oysters, coral, sponges, sea cucumbers. The distinction produced an international dispute between Japan and China (the Okinotorishima case, 2004) and between Australia and Indonesia over the Timor and Arafura Sea fisheries.

E. Enforcement: Article 73 and prompt release

Article 73 — Enforcement of laws and regulations of the coastal State. "1. The coastal State may, in the exercise of its sovereign rights to explore, exploit, conserve and manage the living resources in the exclusive economic zone, take such measures, including boarding, inspection, arrest and judicial proceedings, as may be necessary to ensure compliance with the laws and regulations adopted by it in conformity with this Convention. 2. Arrested vessels and their crews shall be promptly released upon the posting of reasonable bond or other security. 3. Coastal State penalties for violations of fisheries laws and regulations in the exclusive economic zone may not include imprisonment, in the absence of agreements to the contrary by the States concerned, or any other form of corporal punishment. 4. In cases of arrest or detention of foreign vessels the coastal State shall promptly notify the flag State, through appropriate channels, of the action taken and of any penalties subsequently imposed."

Article 73 is the most litigated enforcement provision in the Convention, and it comes with its own remedy: Article 292, under which the flag State may apply to ITLOS (or to a court or tribunal agreed by the parties) for the prompt release of a detained vessel and its crew. Four propositions answer almost every question on this subject.

  1. Article 73 powers are real and broad, extending to boarding, inspection, arrest and judicial proceedings, and applying not only to fishing but to the coastal State's laws for the conservation and management of resources generally. In M/V "Virginia G" (Panama v. Guinea-Bissau, ITLOS, 2014) the Tribunal held that the coastal State may regulate bunkering of fishing vessels in its EEZ — the regulation was connected to the conservation and management of living resources, and thus within Art. 56 and Art. 73 — but that the arrest had been effected with excessive force and that damages were payable: the State's right does not carry a right to use disproportionate force.
  2. Prompt release on bond is mandatory. Article 73(2) is a self-executing obligation; the amount and form of the bond are for the detaining State, but the reasonableness of the bond is reviewable by the tribunal. The criteria were stated in Camouco (Panama v. France, ITLOS, 2000) and Monte Confurco (Seychelles v. France, ITLOS, 2000): the gravity of the alleged offences, the penalties imposed or imposable under the laws of the detaining State, the value of the detained vessel and of the cargo, and the amount and form of the bond imposed. The result is a jurisprudence of bonds — FF 20 million in Camouco, FF 56 million in Monte Confurco, 5.5 million marks in Volga (2002), 10 million yen in Hoshinmaru (2007) — which is astonishingly practical for an international tribunal and is the reason ITLOS matters to fishermen.
  3. No imprisonment for fisheries offences. Article 73(3) forbids imprisonment or corporal punishment for fisheries violations in the EEZ, absent agreement between the States concerned. This is the provision that explains India's practice of detaining foreign fishing vessels while releasing crews, and it is why the seizure of foreign trawlers off Kerala, Tamil Nadu and Gujarat is usually resolved by bond and confiscation rather than by the imprisonment of the crew.
  4. Notification and the flag State. Article 73(4) requires prompt notification to the flag State. Practice shows that failure to notify is itself a violation: in Duzgit Integrity (Malta v. São Tomé and Príncipe, ITLOS, 2016) the Tribunal found violations of Article 73(2) and 73(4) and awarded compensation for the unlawful boarding and detention of the vessel. The prompt-release cases have become the busiest part of ITLOS's docket and are studied at §5.6.

F. Marine scientific research, installations and the environment in the EEZ

Marine scientific research (Arts. 245–247, 252–255). In the territorial sea the coastal State has the exclusive right to regulate and conduct MSR (Art. 245). In the EEZ and on the continental shelf the rule is consent: "States and competent international organizations which intend to conduct marine scientific research in the exclusive economic zone or on the continental shelf of a coastal State shall seek the consent of the coastal State" (Art. 246(2)) — but consent is normally to be given, and there is implied consent where the research is to be conducted by a State or organisation more than six months after the request, provided the coastal State has not notified a specified area in which consent is required (Art. 246(3)–(6)); consent may be withheld in four cases (direct significance for the exploration and exploitation of natural resources; involving drilling or explosives; involving construction of artificial islands or installations; or misleading information about the project — Art. 246(5)). Research may be suspended or halted for non-compliance (Art. 253), and Part XIII's other provisions regulate the rights of the researching State, the participation of the coastal State, and the publication of results.

Installations, structures and safety zones (Art. 60). The coastal State has the exclusive right to construct and authorise artificial islands, installations and structures in the EEZ, the exclusive jurisdiction over them, the right to establish safety zones up to 500 m around them (Art. 60(5)), and the duty to give due notice of their construction, removal and abandonment; nothing may be installed where it may interfere with the use of recognised sea lanes essential to international navigation (Art. 60(7)), and abandoned or disused installations must be removed with due regard to fishing, the environment and the rights of other States (Art. 60(3)). India's oil platforms in the Bombay High and its KG-basin installations are the standard examples; so are the Chinese installations in the South China Sea, which are the subject of a separate dispute about whether they are "artificial islands" under Article 60 or "islands" under Article 121.

Protection of the marine environment (Part XII, especially Arts. 192, 194, 220). The EEZ is where the coastal State's environmental enforcement bite is strongest: Article 220 permits it to institute proceedings, including detention of the vessel, where there are clear grounds for believing that a vessel navigating in its EEZ has, in the EEZ, committed a violation of applicable international rules on pollution, or that the violation has caused or threatens significant pollution; Article 211(6) allows special mandatory measures in defined areas of the EEZ; and Article 234 gives the coastal State extensive rights of non-discriminatory enforcement in ice-covered areas. India's use of Article 220 powers, its MARPOL obligations through the DG Shipping, and the creation of marine protected areas in the Gulf of Mannar and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, all rest on this Part.

G. The Indian EEZ: statute, notification and practice

  • Section 7, Maritime Zones Act, 1976. The EEZ of India is the area beyond and adjacent to the territorial waters, the limit of which is 200 nm from the baseline. India has "sovereign rights" for exploring, exploiting, conserving and managing the natural resources of the seabed, its subsoil and the superjacent waters, "and other activities for the economic exploitation and exploration of the zone such as the production of energy from the water, currents and winds"; and jurisdiction over the establishment and use of artificial islands, installations and structures, marine scientific research, and the protection and preservation of the marine environment. Section 7(4)–(6) add powers to make rules, to give effect to international agreements, and to provide for the conduct of any activity prohibited without authorisation.
  • The notification of 27 August 1981 (SO 67/E). Extends the Indian Penal Code, the Code of Criminal Procedure (with new s. 188A, now s. 208 BNSS), the Civil Procedure Code, the Bombay High Court jurisdiction provisions and the Customs Act to the EEZ and the continental shelf; designates a Court of Session in the coastal district as the special court; and requires the prior approval of the Central Government for any investigation or proceeding by any authority other than a sanctioned one. This is the notification on which the Supreme Court relied in Italy v. Union of India.
  • Fishing enactments. The Maritime Zones of India (Regulation of Fishing by Foreign Vessels) Act, 1981; the Marine Products Export Development Authority Act, 1972; the state marine fisheries acts (for example the Kerala Marine Fishing Regulation Act, 1980, and the Tamil Nadu Marine Fishing Regulation Act, 1983); and the Deep Sea Fishing Policy, 1991, which vests the regulation of fishing beyond territorial waters in the Union. The union–state division of fisheries competence is one of the live questions in Indian federalism, and it surfaced in the Enrica Lexie litigation.
  • Dimensions. India's EEZ covers about 2.02 million sq km around the mainland and the island groups (the Andaman and Nicobar Islands add a substantial area in the Bay of Bengal; Lakshadweep in the Arabian Sea). That is more than half of India's land area, and it explains why the Union's maritime bureaucracy — the Ministry of Defence and the Navy, the Ministry of Earth Sciences, the Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways, the Coast Guard, the DG Shipping, the DG Hydrography and the state fisheries departments — is so crowded.
Table 5.9 — Who may do what in the EEZ: a two-column comparison worth memorising
The coastal State may…The coastal State may not…
Explore, exploit, conserve and manage all natural resources of the water column, seabed and subsoil (Art. 56(1)(a)).Prohibit or hamper navigation, overflight, or the laying of cables and pipelines by other States (Art. 58(1)).
Regulate fishing, licence foreign fishing vessels, and grant access to any surplus (Arts. 61–62).Convert the EEZ into territory: no sovereignty, no right to expel foreign ships for mere presence, no territorial-sea powers (Arts. 55–58).
Construct and authorise artificial islands, installations and structures with safety zones (Art. 60).Deny transit through the EEZ to ships of third States, or claim historic rights over the waters as such.
Regulate marine scientific research through the consent regime (Art. 246).Prevent the establishment of submarine cables or pipelines, though their route may be regulated "for the prevention, reduction and control of pollution" (Art. 79(3) applies to the shelf; Art. 58(1) preserves the freedom in the EEZ).
Enforce its laws by boarding, inspection, arrest and judicial proceedings, subject to prompt release on bond and no imprisonment for fisheries offences (Art. 73).Use excessive force in enforcement; the standard is necessity and reasonableness (Saiga (No. 2); Virginia G; Duzgit Integrity).
Protect and preserve the marine environment, including by detention of polluting vessels (Arts. 192, 211, 220).Apply its customs, fiscal, immigration or sanitary laws beyond the 24 nm limit through the contiguous-zone ruse (Saiga (No. 2); Norstar).
Exercise rights over the continental shelf beneath the EEZ, including sedentary species (Arts. 68, 77).Interfere with the laying of cables and pipelines laid by other States on the shelf (Art. 79(2)–(3)) or with navigation above them (Art. 78(1)).
Exam note — the "500 NM" annotation and the 200-mile rule

Whatever the handwritten marks on the syllabus image mean, the only distances that exist in UNCLOS are these: 12 nm (territorial sea, Art. 3); 24 nm (contiguous zone, Art. 33(2)); 200 nm (EEZ, Art. 57; and the guaranteed width of the continental shelf, Art. 76(1)); 350 nm or 100 nm beyond the 2,500 m isobath (the outer limits of the continental shelf on a broad-margin coast, Art. 76(5)–(6)); and 60 nm / 100 nm constraints in the Gardiner formula (Art. 76(4)). There is no 500-mile zone in the Convention. Candidates who write "500 NM EEZ" lose marks; candidates who explain that no such zone exists, and why, gain them.

Real examples — the EEZ in dispute and in use
  • Canada v. Spain, 1995 ("the Turbot War"). Canada arrested the Spanish trawler Estai under its Coastal Fisheries Protection Act, invoking jurisdiction over straddling stocks beyond its EEZ; the case is the classic illustration of Article 63(2)'s inadequacy and of the need for the 1995 Fish Stocks Agreement, and it was later arbitrated (Canada–Spain, 1998) and reflected in the Northwest Atlantic Fisheries Organization's enforcement scheme.
  • India: Enrica Lexie. The incident occurred about 20.5 nm off the Kerala coast, in the contiguous zone; the Indian position was that the penal laws applied to the EEZ under the 1981 notification and that the St. Antony's flag State interest justified Indian action. The PCA (2020) found the incident to be an "incident of navigation" outside Article 97, held that immunity attached to the marines as State officials, and found Italy in breach of Articles 87(1)(a) and 90 in relation to the St. Antony — a finding which acknowledges India's high-seas navigation rights even while denying its jurisdiction.
  • Prompt-release practice. Over twenty cases have come to ITLOS under Article 292 since 1997, involving vessels detained by France, Guinea, Guinea-Bissau, Spain, Belize, Seychelles, Yemen and others. The doctrine is now settled and is examined in every advanced course: bond, not bail; release, not trial; and reasonableness reviewed by the Tribunal.
  • Distant-water fishing and IUU. The Chinese distant-water fleet's operations off West Africa and in the Indian Ocean, and its 2021 Coast Guard Law authorising the use of force within claimed waters, are the modern flash-point; India's response has been to strengthen the Navy's and Coast Guard's patrolling, to press for regional cooperation through the IOTC and BOBP-IGO, and to negotiate fisheries agreements with Sri Lanka and the Maldives (the 2024–25 Sri Lankan and Maldivian arrangements on fishing access).

↑ top

5.1.5 The High Seas

A. Definition, scope and the six freedoms

Article 86 — Application of the provisions of this Part. "The provisions of this Part apply to all parts of the sea that are not included in the exclusive economic zone, in the territorial sea or in the internal waters of a State, or in the archipelagic waters of an archipelagic State. This article does not entail any abridgement of the freedoms enjoyed by all States in the exclusive economic zone in accordance with article 58."

Article 87 — Freedom of the high seas. "1. The high seas are open to all States, whether coastal or land-locked. Freedom of the high seas is exercised under the conditions laid down by this Convention and by other rules of international law. It comprises, inter alia, both for coastal and land-locked States: (a) freedom of navigation; (b) freedom of overflight; (c) freedom to lay submarine cables and pipelines, subject to Part VI; (d) freedom to construct artificial islands and other installations permitted under international law, subject to Part VI; (e) freedom of fishing, subject to the conditions laid down in section 2; (f) freedom of scientific research, subject to Parts VI and XIII. 2. These freedoms shall be exercised by all States with due regard for the interests of other States in their exercise of the freedom of the high seas, and also with due regard for the rights under this Convention with respect to activities in the Area."

Four points of definition and scope.

  1. The high seas are defined negatively. Everything that is not internal waters, territorial sea, archipelagic waters or EEZ is high seas. The seabed beneath the high seas may be part of a continental shelf (not the "Area") — which is why Article 78's rule that shelf rights do not affect the superjacent waters matters, and why the "high seas" of the water column and the "Area" of the seabed are two different legal spaces.
  2. Article 86 is not an abridgement of EEZ freedoms. The freedoms of navigation, overflight, and cables inside the EEZ are the same freedoms of the high seas in substance (Art. 58(1) imports Article 87); but inside the EEZ they operate subject to the coastal State's resource rights (Art. 56) and the coastal State's laws adopted consistently with the Convention (Art. 58(3)). The conceptual difficulty is stated in the textbooks as the contrast between "high seas freedoms" and "high seas rights": the freedoms exist in both spaces, but the exclusive flag-State jurisdiction of Article 92 is qualified in the EEZ by the coastal State's enforcement powers under Article 73.
  3. The six freedoms are illustrative, not exhaustive. "Inter alia" means the list is open, and the history of the law of the sea since 1958 has been the history of adding to it (underwater cultural heritage — now the 2001 UNESCO Convention, a separate instrument; marine scientific research; the laying of submarine cables for telecommunications and electricity; ocean carbon dioxide removal; space activities).
  4. The freedoms are subject to the "due regard" standard. Article 87(2) and Article 300 (good faith and abuse of rights) mean that the freedoms are not licences to injure. The MOX Plant arbitration (2003), the ITLOS orders on provisional measures in the MOX Plant and Land Reclamation cases (both 2001–2003), and the general environmental cases show the limits.

B. Legal status: non-appropriation, peace, and the flag State

Table 5.12 — The high-seas régime article by article: status, ships, and duties
ArticleRule
Art. 88"The high seas shall be reserved for peaceful purposes." The provision does not disarm the high seas — navies operate there lawfully — but it excludes the use of force inconsistent with the Charter, and it is the textual basis for the argument that the deployment of weapons of mass destruction on or above the high seas is unlawful (with the 1971 Seabed Arms Control Treaty prohibiting nuclear weapons on the seabed beyond 12 nm).
Art. 89"No State may validly purport to subject any part of the high seas to its sovereignty." This is the legal expression of Grotius's mare liberum: non-appropriation. Historically, the article closes the era of the Portuguese and Spanish mare clausum; operationally, it makes unilateral claims to enclosed or historic oceans — the USSR's 1957 "Peter the Great Bay" declaration, Libya's 1973 Gulf of Sidra claim, and the more recent "nine-dash line" claims in the South China Sea — opposable to no one.
Art. 90Every State, coastal or land-locked, has the right to sail ships flying its flag on the high seas.
