In the Enrica Lexie arbitration the Tribunal applied this article, together with Article 87(1)(a), to hold that Italy had breached India's rights — the Indian fishing boat St. Antony having been a vessel exercising the freedom of navigation on the high seas, which the Italian marines' firing and the subsequent order to the vessel to turn back had interfered with (case 34, postscript). The point to keep straight is that Article 90 protects the ship of whichever flag State it belongs to, and it is reciprocal.
Art. 91Nationality of ships: each State fixes the conditions for granting its nationality to ships, for registration and for the right to fly its flag; "there must exist a genuine link between the State and the ship"; and States must issue documents to ships they register. The genuine link requirement, taken from the Nottebohm logic, has been applied chiefly through the 1986 UN Convention on Conditions for Registration of Ships, which never entered into force, and through practice (open registries: Panama, Liberia, the Marshall Islands, Malta).
Art. 92Status of ships: ships shall sail under the flag of one State only and, save in exceptional cases expressly provided for in international treaties or in the Convention, "shall be subject to its exclusive jurisdiction on the high seas". A ship which sails under the flags of two or more States, using them according to convenience, may not claim any of those nationalities and may be assimilated to a ship without nationality — the "flag of convenience" problem. The rule is the modern codification of the Lotus holding.
Art. 94Duties of the flag State: effectively exercise jurisdiction and control in administrative, technical and social matters over its ships; maintain a register; assume jurisdiction over each ship and its master, officers and crew; ensure safety at sea (construction, equipment, seaworthiness, manning, labour conditions, training, communications, collision prevention); and investigate casualties. Partly implemented through the IMO instruments (SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, the 2006 Maritime Labour Convention) and India's Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.
Art. 95"Warships on the high seas have complete immunity from the jurisdiction of any State other than the flag State." The immunity is absolute and is unaffected by any enforcement power under Part VII; a pursuing coastal State may not arrest a warship, and the remedy is diplomatic.
Art. 96Government ships operated for non-commercial purposes also enjoy complete immunity; the "non-commercial" qualification is what distinguishes a naval auxiliary from a state-owned cargo ship.
Art. 97Penal jurisdiction in matters of collision or any other incident of navigation: no penal or disciplinary proceedings may be instituted against the master or crew except before the judicial or administrative authorities of the flag State or of the State of which the person is a national, and no arrest or detention of the vessel may be ordered by any authority other than the flag State. This article was the centrepiece of the Enrica Lexie litigation — the Supreme Court held in 2013 that Article 97 had no application to the EEZ because Part VII applies only to the high seas (Art. 86), and the arbitral tribunal in 2020 held that Article 97 did not apply to the shooting because it was not an "incident of navigation" of the Enrica Lexie, while holding that immunity attached to the marines (case 34, postscript).
Art. 98Duty to render assistance: the master of a ship must render assistance to any person found in danger of being lost at sea, proceed with all speed to the assistance of persons in distress, and render assistance after a collision; coastal States are to promote the establishment of an adequate and effective search-and-rescue service, cooperating through regional organisations (the 1979 SAR Convention, the IMO's Global SAR Plan, and India's SAR service under the Coast Guard).
Art. 99Prohibition of the transport of slaves: every State shall take effective measures to prevent and punish the transport of slaves in ships authorised to fly its flag and to prevent the unlawful use of its flag for that purpose; and "any slave taking refuge on board any ship, whatever its flag, shall ipso facto be free." The provision descends from the 1815 Congress of Vienna declaration, the 1841 Treaty of London (the Antelope and Le Louis cases), the 1926 Slavery Convention and the 1956 Supplementary Convention, and is part of the jus cogens prohibition of slavery.
Art. 100Duty to cooperate in the repression of piracy: "All States shall cooperate to the fullest possible extent in the repression of piracy on the high seas or in any other place outside the jurisdiction of any State."
Art. 101Definition of piracy (set out in full below).
Art. 102–104Piracy by a warship, government ship or government aircraft whose crew has mutinied and taken control is assimilated to piracy committed by a private ship; a ship or aircraft is considered a pirate ship or aircraft if it is intended by the persons in dominant control to be used for a piratical purpose; and a ship may retain its pirate character even after it has become a pirate ship, so long as it remains under the control of the persons guilty of that act. Article 104: the ship or aircraft may retain its nationality although it has become a pirate ship or aircraft; retention or loss of nationality is determined by the law of the State from which such nationality was derived.
Art. 105Seizure of a pirate ship or aircraft. "On the high seas, or in any other place outside the jurisdiction of any State, every State may seize a pirate ship or aircraft, or a ship or aircraft taken by piracy and under the control of pirates, and arrest the persons and seize the property on board. The courts of the State which carried out the seizure may decide upon the penalties to be imposed, and may also determine the action to be taken with regard to the ships, aircraft or property, subject to the rights of third parties acting in good faith." This is the classic example of universal jurisdiction: the seizing State need have no link with the vessel, the victims or the offence.
Art. 106Liability for seizure without adequate grounds: where a ship has been seized on suspicion of piracy without adequate grounds, the State making the seizure is liable to the State the nationality of which is possessed by the ship or aircraft for any loss or damage caused by the seizure.
Art. 107Ships and aircraft which are entitled to seize on account of piracy: only warships or military aircraft, or other ships or aircraft clearly marked and identifiable as being on government service and authorised to that effect.
Art. 108Illicit traffic in narcotic drugs or psychotropic substances: States shall cooperate in its suppression; any State which has reasonable grounds for believing that a ship flying its flag is engaged in such traffic may request the cooperation of other States; and a State may, with the authorisation of the flag State, take appropriate action including boarding, search and, if evidence of involvement is found, arrest of the persons and the ship.
Art. 109Unauthorised broadcasting from the high seas: the offence, and the jurisdiction of the flag State, the State of registry of the installation, the State of which the person is a national, the State where the transmissions can be received and any State where authorised radio communication is suffering harmful interference — the answer to the pirate radio stations of the 1960s, and the ancestor of the modern regulation of satellite and internet broadcasting.
Art. 110Right of visit. A warship which encounters a foreign ship on the high seas is justified in boarding it if there is reasonable ground for suspecting that the ship is engaged in piracy, the slave trade or unauthorised broadcasting, or is without nationality, or is of the same nationality as the warship though flying a foreign flag or refusing to show its flag. The boarding party may verify the ship's right to fly its flag, but the ship may be visited only in these cases; if the suspicions prove unfounded and the ship has committed no act justifying them, it shall be compensated for any loss or damage sustained. In the Lotus case the PCIJ had already recognised the limited nature of the power; Article 110's compensation clause is its modern corrective.
Art. 111Right of hot pursuit (set out at §5.1.2 C). Note the extension in Article 111(2)–(4): pursuit may begin in the territorial sea, contiguous zone, EEZ or on the continental shelf, and pursuit begun in the EEZ or on the shelf continues as if it had begun in the territorial sea, provided the pursuit is continuous and no force inconsistent with the Charter is used.
Art. 112–115Submarine cables and pipelines: the duty to punish breaking or injuring cables and pipelines on the high seas; the duty to pay for damage caused by cables; indemnity for loss of gear sacrificed in avoiding injury to a cable; and the exemption from liability for damage caused by a person who, in order to avoid injuring a cable, sacrifices fishing gear.
Art. 116–120Fisheries on the high seas: the right of all States for their nationals to engage in fishing on the high seas, subject to their treaty obligations, to the interests and rights of coastal States in coastal stocks and to Articles 63–67 (Art. 116); the duty of each State to adopt measures for the conservation of the living resources of the high seas for its nationals (Art. 117); the duty to cooperate with other States in the conservation and management of those resources, including by joint scientific research and the establishment of sub-regional or regional fisheries organisations (Art. 118); the requirement that conservation measures produce the maximum sustainable yield qualified by relevant environmental and economic factors, that associated and dependent species be maintained above levels at which they become seriously threatened, and that data and statistics be contributed (Art. 119); and the special protection of marine mammals (Art. 120).

C. Piracy — the definition, element by element, and its modern practice

Article 101 — Definition of piracy. "Piracy consists of any of the following acts: (a) any illegal acts of violence or detention, or any act of depredation, committed for private ends by the crew or the passengers of a private ship or a private aircraft, and directed: (i) on the high seas, against another ship or aircraft, or against persons or property on board such ship or aircraft; (ii) against a ship, aircraft, persons or property in a place outside the jurisdiction of any State; (b) any act of voluntary participation in the operation of a ship or of an aircraft with knowledge of facts making it a pirate ship or aircraft; (c) any act of inciting or of intentionally facilitating an act described in subparagraph (a) or (b)."

private ship → mutiny alone is not piracy 4. Against another ship or persons or property on board it (101(a)(i)) → the "second ship" rule 5. On the high seas or "outside the jurisdiction of any State" Arts. 58(2) → applies in the EEZ Gap 1 — politically motivated seizure The Achille Lauro (1985): the PLO hijackers acted for political ends, so the act was not piracy under Art. 101; hence the 1988 SUA Convention (as amended in 2005), which criminalises the unlawful seizure of ships and the use of a ship as a weapon. Gap 2 — armed robbery within the territorial sea Attacks on ships at anchor in ports and internal waters (the pattern in the Gulf of Guinea and off Somalia after 2012) are not piracy but "armed robbery at sea", governed by coastal-State law, the SUA Convention and the IMO Code of Practice. Consequences once the definition is satisfied — Arts. 100, 105, 106, 107 and 110 Duty of all States to cooperate (Art. 100) · universal jurisdiction of the seizing State over the ship and persons (Art. 105) · seizure only by warships or authorised government ships (Art. 107) · compensation if seizure was without adequate grounds (Art. 106) · right of visit on suspicion of piracy (Art. 110) Modern practice: UN Security Council resolutions from 2008 onwards on Somali piracy, the 2009 Djibouti Code of Conduct, the Contact Group on Piracy off the Coast of Somalia, and national statutes (India's Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022).
Fig. 5.6 — Piracy under Article 101: five elements, two gaps, and the consequences that follow

The five elements in detail.

  1. Illegal acts of violence, detention or depredation. The offence is conduct-based; there is no requirement of robbery or of gain. The Privy Council so held in Re Piracy Jure Gentium (1934) — an appeal from Hong Kong in which it was held that "an act of piracy jure gentium may be committed although no robbery has been accomplished", and that a frustrated attempt at robbery committed on the high seas is piracy. The judgment also restated the classical position that the offence is against the law of nations and triable by any State. The same point appears in the Harvard Research's definition (1932) which Article 101 substantially adopts.
  2. For private ends. The restriction is deliberate and is the reason international law's piracy definition is narrower than the popular use of the word. In Re Piracy Jure Gentium and in the older authorities (the Magellan Pirates (1853); United States v. Smith (1820)), piracy is described as robbery or violence upon the sea by persons acting for their own gain; the 1958 High Seas Convention and UNCLOS kept the private-ends requirement. The result is that acts of maritime terrorism for political ends, and acts of violence committed by insurgents against government forces (Sri Lanka's LTTE "Sea Tigers", the Houthi attacks in the Red Sea since 2023), fall outside Article 101 and must be dealt with by the SUA Convention, the Terrorist Bombings Convention, the Hostages Convention, the law of armed conflict and the Security Council's counter-terrorism resolutions.
  3. By the crew or passengers of a private ship or aircraft. This produces the "two-ship" rule: piracy requires a pirate vessel (or aircraft) attacking another vessel. A mutiny on board a single ship (the crew seizing their own ship) is not piracy under Article 101 unless the act is against another ship or persons on board it; Article 102 covers mutinied warships only by assimilating them to pirate ships. The classic illustration is the Santa Maria (1961), a Portuguese liner seized by political insurgents — the incident that led to the 1963 Tokyo Convention on Offences and Certain Other Acts Committed on Board Aircraft, an instrument concerned with a single aircraft rather than with piracy.
  4. Directed against another ship, or persons or property on board it. The "two-ship" consequence of element 3. The attack must be directed from the pirate ship against the victim ship; a single-vessel attack by crew on their own ship cannot be piracy.
  5. On the high seas or in a place outside the jurisdiction of any State. Article 101(a)(ii) covers the "place outside the jurisdiction of any State" — which includes the Area and, on the accepted reading, the EEZ by virtue of Article 58(2) (the piracy provisions apply there so far as not incompatible with Part V). Attacks within the territorial sea of a State are "armed robbery at sea", a matter of coastal-State jurisdiction, which is why the international legal response to Somali attacks on ships in port was the IMO's Code of Practice on the Prevention and Suppression of Piracy and Armed Robbery against Ships (2011) and the Security Council's authorisation of counter-piracy operations in Somali territorial waters by resolution.

Modern piracy and India. Somali piracy, which began in 2005, produced a complete international response: Security Council resolutions from 2008 onwards authorising naval operations in Somali territory (including, exceptionally, in Somali territorial waters) and extending the mandate year by year; the 2009 Djibouti Code of Conduct; the Contact Group on Piracy off the Coast of Somalia (2009) and its Working Group 2 on legal issues; the "piracy ransom payments" debate and the shift to shipboard security (BMP and the IMO's Best Management Practices); and the prosecution of over a thousand pirates in more than twenty States, including Kenya, Seychelles, Mauritius, the Netherlands, Germany, the United States and India. The piracy "gap years" of 2017–2022 ended with a resurgence from December 2023, and India's response has been the most visible in the Indian Ocean: the Indian Navy's Operation Sankalp deployments in the Gulf of Aden from 2019, the anti-piracy patrols in the Arabian Sea, and the operations of March 2024 in which the Navy rescued MV Ruen (35 pirates surrendered after the interception of the hijacked vessel about 260 nautical miles east of Somalia, the crew being rescued unharmed) and FV Al-Kambar 786 (nine pirates taken after the rescue of 23 crew). The prosecuted accused were brought to Mumbai, tried before a Special Court designated under the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022 — India's implementing statute for the UNCLOS piracy provisions — and in July 2026 the Special Court sentenced 44 Somali nationals to life imprisonment in the two cases, the first-ever convictions under the 2022 Act. India's naval and legal response to Somali piracy is the best modern illustration of Articles 100 to 107 in operation: universal jurisdiction exercised by the seizing State, prosecution under national law, and the flag State's (and the victims' State's) cooperation through the counter-piracy contact group.

D. The erosion of the freedom of the seas — the great theme

The examiners frequently set the essay "Write an essay on the freedom of the open sea" (it appears in the older papers, PYQ B below), and the answer must be a structure with a thesis: the freedom of the high seas survives as the default rule, but it has been progressively qualified by the growth of coastal-State zones, by the regulation of fisheries, by environmental law, and by security law. Five qualifications should be identified.

  1. Zone-based qualification. The EEZ, the contiguous zone and the continental shelf have removed from the "free" sea the oil, gas, fish and seabed minerals within 200 nm of the coast — roughly 36 per cent of the ocean surface and, on some estimates, more than 90 per cent of the fish catch and virtually all known offshore hydrocarbons. Every new claim to a maritime zone is an inroad on the classical freedom.
  2. Resource-based qualification. Articles 116–120 convert the freedom of fishing into a regulated activity; the 1993 Compliance Agreement and the 1995 Fish Stocks Agreement make the right conditional on flag-State control and cooperation with RFMOs; and the FAO's Port State Measures Agreement (2009) and the WTO's fisheries-subsidies disciplines (2022) extended the regulation from the sea to ports and subsidies.
  3. Environment-based qualification. Part XII applies to the high seas as much as to any other zone: the duty to protect and preserve the marine environment (Art. 192), the duty not to transfer damage or hazards (Art. 195), the duty to prevent, reduce and control pollution from all sources (Art. 194), dumping controls (Art. 210), vessel-source pollution standards (Art. 211), and the enforcement powers of the flag State (Art. 217) and port State (Art. 218). The climate advisory opinion of the Seabed Disputes Chamber/ITLOS of 21 May 2024 went further still: greenhouse-gas emissions are marine pollution within Article 1(4), and States are under a stringent due-diligence obligation to take all necessary measures to prevent, reduce and control it (§5.4, §5.6).
  4. Security-based qualification. The Security Council's counter-piracy and counter-terrorism resolutions, the Proliferation Security Initiative (2003), the SUA Convention and its 2005 Protocol, and the bilateral ship-boarding agreements (the United States' agreements with Panama, Liberia, the Marshall Islands and others) have created powers that the 1958 régime would have regarded as unthinkable: the boarding of foreign-flagged vessels on the high seas with the flag State's standing consent.
  5. Governance-based qualification. The Area's régime under Part XI removed the deep seabed from the realm of free appropriation; the BBNJ Agreement (adopted 19 June 2023, in force 17 January 2026, with its first Conference of the Parties due in 2027) now adds area-based management tools, including marine protected areas, and environmental impact assessment to the high seas, alongside a benefit-sharing régime for marine genetic resources. The oceans beyond national jurisdiction are, in short, no longer ungoverned — they are governed by an increasingly dense network of international institutions.
Real examples — the high seas at work
  • Flag-State jurisdiction and its gaps. The Lotus (1927) collision between the Boz-Kourt and the Lotus; the Red Crusader (1962) and I'm Alone (1935) on the limits of enforcement; and the ITLOS prompt-release jurisprudence on the practical effect of Article 292.
  • The slave trade and trafficking. The Antelope (1825) and the freedom clause of Article 99 have a modern successor in the trafficking protocols and the 2000 Palermo Convention; Indonesia's and Thailand's Andaman Sea practices are the recurring regional flash-point.
  • Cables and pipelines. The 2024 damage to the Baltic Sea telecom cables (the Yi Peng 3 incident), the 2024 Red Sea cable damage, and the Nord Stream sabotage (2022) have made Article 112–115 a live security as well as civil topic, and have revived the debate about the adequacy of the 1884 Paris Convention for the Protection of Submarine Telegraph Cables.
  • Seabed and biodiversity. The ISA's exploration contracts, the Nauru two-year rule (2021), the 2025 United States licensing initiative outside the ISA framework, the ISA compliance inquiry into NORI/TOML and the ITLOS provisional-measures orders of 18 July 2026 (§5.4); and the entry into force of the BBNJ Agreement on 17 January 2026.
  • India on the high seas. Operation Sankalp; the Ruen and Al-Kambar rescues; the 2026 piracy convictions in Mumbai; the Gulf of Aden escort operations since 2008 and the Combined Maritime Forces' participation (India has been a partner but not a member); the Indian Ocean Rim Association and the Indian Ocean Naval Symposium; and India's ratification of the SUA Convention (2002) and the establishment of the Information Fusion Centre – Indian Ocean Region (IFC-IOR at Gurugram, 2018) for maritime domain awareness.

↑ top

5.1.6 Islands, Archipelagos, Enclosed Seas and Land-locked States — the Four Special Regimes

The Convention's system of zones is completed by four special régimes that cut across the boundaries between them. They are examinable as separate short questions, but they matter more than that: the geographical features they regulate — islands, reefs, archipelagic baselines, semi-enclosed seas and land-locked access — are the raw material from which maritime entitlements are built, and they are the reason two States with similar populations can have very different amounts of sea.

A. Islands and rocks — Article 121

Article 121 — Regime of islands. "1. An island is a naturally formed area of land, surrounded by water, which is above water at high tide. 2. Except as provided for in paragraph 3, the territorial sea, the contiguous zone, the exclusive economic zone and the continental shelf of an island are determined in accordance with the provisions of this Convention applicable to other land territory. 3. Rocks which cannot sustain human habitation or economic life of their own shall have no exclusive economic zone or continental shelf."

Three questions arise on the text, and each has been litigated.

(1) "Naturally formed area of land, surrounded by water, above water at high tide." The definition excludes man-made structures: an artificial island or installation under Article 60 has no territorial sea of its own and generates no EEZ or shelf (Art. 60(8)). It also excludes low-tide elevations (Art. 13), which are not islands but may, if within 12 nm of land, contribute their low-water line to the baseline. The requirement that the feature be "above water at high tide" means that features which disappear at high tide cannot generate zones, however elaborate the construction placed upon them — the decisive point in the South China Sea disputes over Mischief Reef and Subi Reef.

(2) Full entitlement for islands. Article 121(2) gives islands the same entitlement as any other land territory: 12 nm territorial sea, 24 nm contiguous zone, 200 nm EEZ and continental shelf. Hence the enormous significance of small features: Rockall, Clipperton, Okinotorishima, Jan Mayen, Aves Island, Serranilla and Bajo Nuevo, and in India's neighbourhood, the Andaman and Nicobar Islands and Lakshadweep, each of which generates a full set of zones. New Moore/South Talpatti in the Bay of Bengal (the island that emerged in the mouth of the Haribhanga in the 1970s and has since disappeared) is the Indian subcontinent's own illustration: when it existed it was claimed by both India and Bangladesh; when it disappeared, the boundary question had to be resolved without reference to it — and the 2014 Tribunal held that India's claim to it did not affect the delimitation, while awarding India the area around it (case 35).

(3) The "rock" clause — Article 121(3). Rocks which cannot sustain human habitation or economic life of their own have a 12 nm territorial sea but no EEZ and no continental shelf. Four points must be made about this much-disputed provision:

  • The terms are cumulative and objective. The test is capacity — "cannot sustain" — not current use; and the words "of their own" exclude dependence on outside subsidy, which is why the practice of building barracks, lighthouses or weather stations on a rock does not convert it into an island generating an EEZ.
  • State practice is self-serving on both sides. Japan treats Okinotorishima (roughly the size of two tennis courts at high tide) as an island generating a 200 nm EEZ and has spent hundreds of millions of dollars on concrete reinforcement; China has protested; the dispute reached the CLCS and the Japanese extended-shelf submission. Similarly, China, Taiwan and Vietnam have built on features in the Spratlys.
  • The 2016 South China Sea arbitration (Philippines v. China, PCA, Award of 12 July 2016). The Tribunal held, on Article 121(3), that it must be interpreted by reference to the object and purpose of the Convention — the preservation of the interests of coastal populations — and that a feature satisfies the test only if it has the capacity to sustain a stable community of people, with the resources to do so without external support. It found that none of the Spratly features generated an EEZ or continental shelf, that all of the features claimed by China (including Itu Aba, the largest) were rocks or low-tide elevations, and that China's assertion of historic rights within the "nine-dash line" was incompatible with the Convention. China has rejected the award; it has nonetheless become the most detailed judicial treatment of Article 121(3) in existence.
  • The Indian position. India's islands — the Andaman and Nicobar chain (over 500 islands, the largest being North, Middle and South Andaman, Great Nicobar, and Car Nicobar) and Lakshadweep (36 islands, 11 inhabited) — are unquestionably islands with full entitlements; India has claimed an extended continental shelf off the Andamans, and the Lakshadweep group affects the India–Maldives boundary. India has consistently supported the full-entitlement reading of Article 121(2) and has not relied on Article 121(3) in any dispute.
Fig. 5.7 — One coastline, four kinds of feature, four different legal yields

B. Archipelagic States and archipelagic waters — Part IV (Arts. 46–54)

Definition. Article 46(a): an "archipelagic State" is "a State constituted wholly by one or more archipelagos and may include other islands"; Article 46(b) defines an "archipelago" as "a group of islands, including parts of islands, interconnecting waters and other natural features which are so closely interrelated that such islands, waters and other natural features form an intrinsic geographical, economic and political entity, or which historically have been regarded as such". The régime is available only to States that are wholly archipelagic — Indonesia, the Philippines, Fiji, Papua New Guinea, the Bahamas, Solomon Islands, Maldives (which uses the régime? the Maldives' claim to archipelagic baselines was included in the 2010s), Trinidad and Tobago, Vanuatu, Kiribati and Tuvalu. The attempt by States with mid-ocean outlying archipelagos (Ecuador with the Galápagos, India with the Andamans and Lakshadweep, Norway with Svalbard, Spain with the Canaries, Portugal with the Azores) to obtain the same régime failed at UNCLOS III: Article 47 permits the drawing of straight archipelagic baselines only by an archipelagic State, and the islands of a continental State are treated as islands under Article 121.

Table 5.13 — The archipelagic régime in operation
ArticleRule
Art. 47Archipelagic baselines joining the outermost points of the outermost islands and drying reefs, provided that the ratio of the area of the water to the area of the land, including atolls, is between 1:1 and 9:1; the length of any segment must not exceed 100 nm, except that up to 3 per cent of the total number of segments may exceed that length up to a maximum of 125 nm; such baselines "shall not depart to any appreciable extent from the general configuration of the archipelago"; they shall not be drawn in such a way as to cut off from the high seas or the EEZ the territorial sea of another State; and the system may not be applied in a manner which does so (Art. 47(2), (3), (4), (5)).
Art. 48The breadth of the territorial sea, contiguous zone, EEZ and continental shelf is measured from the archipelagic baselines.
Art. 49Sovereignty of the archipelagic State extends to the waters so enclosed ("archipelagic waters"), the airspace above, the seabed and subsoil, and the resources contained therein — subject to Part IV.
Art. 50Internal waters: the archipelagic State may draw closing lines for internal waters within archipelagic waters, including for ports, rivers and bays.
Art. 51Existing agreements, traditional fishing rights and existing submarine cables are respected, and the archipelagic State must respect such rights; new routes and cables are subject to notification.
Art. 52Innocent passage through archipelagic waters, which may be suspended temporarily and without discrimination after due publicity — the mirror of Article 25(3).
Art. 53Archipelagic sea lanes passage: the archipelagic State may designate sea lanes and air routes suitable for continuous and expeditious passage through and over its archipelagic waters and the adjacent territorial sea, using existing routes; all ships and aircraft enjoy the right of archipelagic sea-lanes passage "in their normal mode" for continuous, expeditious and unobstructed transit; there is no suspension.
Art. 54Articles 39, 40, 42 and 44 (duties of ships and aircraft, no research or survey, laws, and non-hampering) apply mutatis mutandis.

Three practical consequences. First, an archipelagic State can "leap out" with baselines even more dramatically than a State with a deeply indented coast: Indonesia's archipelagic baselines (declared in 1960, notified in 2009 after years of international criticism, and now partly revised) enclose an area of straits and seas that would otherwise be high seas, and Indonesia's designation of three archipelagic sea lanes through the Malacca, Sunda, Lombok, Ombai and Wetar passages (submitted to the IMO, adopted in 2017) is the world's first such designation. Second, the régime is exercised subject to the navigation rights of others — archipelagic sea-lanes passage in "normal mode" means that submarines may transit submerged, which is the main practical difference between it and innocent passage. Third, in India's neighbourhood the régime matters because the Malacca and Singapore Straits, the Sunda and Lombok Straits, and the sea lanes through the Indonesian archipelago are the lifelines of Indian trade to East Asia: any interruption of archipelagic sea-lanes passage, or any dispute about the legality of Indonesia's baselines or sea lanes, has direct consequences for India's energy and trade security.

C. Enclosed or semi-enclosed seas — Articles 122 and 123

Article 122 — Definition. "'Enclosed or semi-enclosed sea' means a gulf, basin or sea surrounded by two or more States and connected to another sea or the ocean by a narrow outlet or consisting entirely or primarily of the territorial seas and exclusive economic zones of two or more coastal States."

Article 123 — Cooperation of States bordering enclosed or semi-enclosed seas. States bordering such a sea "should co-operate with each other in the exercise of their rights and in the performance of their duties under this Convention", and to that end shall endeavour, directly or through an appropriate regional organisation: (a) to coordinate the management, conservation, exploration and exploitation of the living resources of the sea; (b) to coordinate the implementation of their rights and duties with respect to the protection and preservation of the marine environment; (c) to coordinate their scientific research policies and undertake where appropriate joint programmes of scientific research in the sea; and (d) to invite, as appropriate, other interested States or international organisations to cooperate with them.

The classical examples are the Baltic Sea (with the 1974/1992 Helsinki Convention and HELCOM), the Mediterranean (the 1976 Barcelona Convention and UNEP/MAP, the first regional seas programme), the Black Sea (the 1992 Bucharest Convention, and the MOX Plant and Land Reclamation litigation around it), the Caribbean (the 1983 Cartagena Convention), the Persian Gulf (the 1978 Kuwait Convention, and the Iran–United States litigation in Oil Platforms (2003)), and the Red Sea and Gulf of Aden (the 1982 Jeddah Convention, with PERSGA). The Bay of Bengal is a semi-enclosed sea within Article 122 — a basin surrounded by India, Bangladesh, Myanmar, Sri Lanka and (by the Andaman Sea) Thailand, Malaysia, Indonesia and Singapore, connected to the ocean through the channels of the Andaman and Nicobar Islands and the straits at the southern end. That characterisation matters twice: it is the framework within which the 2014 Bay of Bengal award was decided (case 35 — the concavity of the coast of a semi-enclosed bay is a "relevant circumstance" in delimitation), and it is the legal basis of the regional cooperation architecture — the Bay of Bengal Programme Inter-Governmental Organisation (BOBP-IGO, 2003), BIMSTEC's fisheries and maritime cooperation, the Bay of Bengal Large Marine Ecosystem project (BOBLME), the Indian Ocean Tuna Commission, and the annual India–Bangladesh–Myanmar–Sri Lanka naval and coast guard interactions.

D. Land-locked and geographically disadvantaged States — Part X

Definitions (Art. 124). A "land-locked State" is a State with no sea coast; a "transit State" is a State with or without a sea coast situated between a land-locked State and the sea, through whose territory traffic in transit passes; a "geographically disadvantaged State" is a coastal State whose geographical situation makes it dependent on the exploitation of the living resources of the EEZs of other States in the sub-region for adequate supplies of fish, or which cannot claim an EEZ of its own.

Table 5.14 — The rights of land-locked States under Part X and Part V
ArticleRule
Art. 125Land-locked States have the right of access to and from the sea for the purpose of exercising the rights provided for in the Convention, including the rights relating to the freedom of the high seas, the common heritage of mankind and the right of innocent passage; the terms and modalities are agreed with the transit States by common agreement, "on an equitable basis"; transit States, "in the exercise of their full sovereignty over their territory", have the right to take all measures necessary to ensure that the rights and facilities provided "shall in no way infringe their legitimate interests".
Art. 126Most-favoured-nation clause: the provisions of the Convention and of special agreements on the exercise of the right of access do not involve the abolition of rights already enjoyed by particular land-locked States.
Art. 127Customs duties, taxes and other charges on traffic in transit to or from land-locked States shall not be levied on such traffic; transit transport is to be free of customs duties (with the exception of charges for specific services).
Art. 128Free zones or other customs facilities may be provided in the ports of transit States by agreement between the land-locked State and the transit State.
Art. 129Cooperation in the construction and improvement of means of transport, and the provision of greater facilities in the ports.
Art. 130Measures to avoid or eliminate delays and other difficulties of a technical or administrative character in traffic in transit.
Art. 131Equal treatment in maritime ports: ships flying the flag of land-locked States shall enjoy treatment equal to that accorded to other foreign ships.
Art. 132Grant of greater transit facilities: transit States may grant greater facilities than the Convention requires; such grants are permanent.
Art. 69Land-locked States' right to participate, on an equitable basis, in the exploitation of the surplus of the living resources of the EEZs of the coastal States of the same sub-region or region, on agreed terms.
Art. 70The same right for geographically disadvantaged States, with the qualification in Article 70(4) for coastal States with a narrow EEZ and heavy dependence on living resources.

India and the land-locked neighbours. India is the transit State for Nepal, Bhutan and, in part, Afghanistan (historically, through the Attari–Wagah and Chabahar routes) and Mongolia (through Kolkata and Haldia). The governing instruments are the Indo-Nepal Treaty of Peace and Friendship, 1950 (Article V of which gives Nepal the right to import and export goods through Indian territory), the Treaty of Transit, 1991 between India and Nepal (with the 2006 and 2016 renewals and the 2016 Agreement on Trade and Transit), the India–Bhutan Treaty of Friendship, 1949 (as revised in 2007) and the transit arrangements through the Kolkata port and the Radhikapur and Phulbari corridors, and the Bangladesh–India Protocol on Inland Water Transit and Trade (1972) with the renewal and coastal shipping agreements of 2018 and 2020. The 2015 Nepal blockade and the 2020 India–Nepal dispute over the Lipulekh pass show the political sensitivity of transit rights: the legal framework of Part X gives the land-locked State a right of access, but the modalities are matters of agreement, and Article 125(3) preserves the transit State's sovereignty over its territory — which means, in practice, that a land-locked State's access depends as much on politics as on law. In the WTO regime, the same problem is addressed by Article V of GATT 1994 (freedom of transit) and by the 2013 Bali Trade Facilitation Agreement, and the UN Convention on the Law of the Sea's Part X remains the only multilateral instrument devoted to it.

E. Sea-level rise: the newest problem

The one problem that the 1982 Convention does not answer is what happens to maritime zones when the coast itself disappears. If baselines are ambulatory — measured from the low-water line as it exists from time to time — then a State whose land sinks, or whose reef is submerged, loses zones; and if baselines and boundaries are fixed at the moment of delimitation, the State keeps them even after its land is submerged. Four strands of practice have developed.

  • The doctrinal default: baselines move. Article 5's reference to the "low-water line along the coast" suggests that the baseline follows the coast as it is. The International Law Association's 2018 Sydney Report on sea-level rise and the International Law Commission's study (commenced in 2018, with the appointment of a Study Group and the first issues paper in 2020 by Bogdan Aurescu and Nilüfer Oral, and a series of reports and draft conclusions up to 2025) examine whether the law should instead be understood to stabilise baselines and entitlements. The Commission's work distinguishes three questions: the law of the sea, statehood, and the protection of persons affected — and its provisional conclusions are cautious.
  • The political claim: zones should be preserved. The 2021 Declaration of the Pacific Islands Forum on Preserving Maritime Zones in the Face of Climate Change-related Sea-Level Rise asserts that maritime zones, once established in accordance with the Convention, should continue to apply without reduction notwithstanding physical changes to the coastline, and that this is consistent with the Convention; the 2023 Falepili Union Treaty between Australia and Tuvalu gives effect to the same idea in a bilateral instrument. The 2021 Declaration has been supported by other groupings and has been placed before the Meeting of States Parties.
  • The judicial treatment of coastline change in delimitation. In the Bay of Bengal award (case 35) Bangladesh argued that the instability of its deltaic coastline — the constant shifting of the Ganges–Brahmaputra delta and the disappearance of South Talpatti — was a relevant circumstance justifying an adjustment; the Tribunal refused, holding that "what matters is the coast line at the time of delimitation" and that future changes in the coastline cannot alter a boundary once established. The point is a useful one for the exam: delimitation freezes the line; what a rising sea does to the line once frozen is the unresolved question.
  • Climate change before the international courts. The ITLOS advisory opinion of 21 May 2024 on climate change and the marine environment (requested by the Commission of Small Island States on Climate Change and International Law) held, unanimously, that anthropogenic greenhouse-gas emissions constitute "pollution of the marine environment" within Article 1(4) of the Convention, that States must take all necessary measures to prevent, reduce and control it (Art. 194(1)), that the standard of due diligence is stringent, and that complying with the Paris Agreement is not by itself sufficient compliance with Part XII. The ICJ's advisory opinion on climate change (delivered in 2025) and the Inter-American Court's opinion followed; together with the Urgenda, Shell and KlimaSeniorinnen cases, they have moved the law from the question whether climate change is a legal problem to the question of the content of States' obligations. For a law-of-the-sea answer, the ITLOS opinion is now the leading authority that Part XII of the Convention is not confined to ship-source and dumping pollution, but reaches the atmosphere's contribution to the ocean.
When to use these four régimes in an answer
  • Island/rock question: Art. 121 in three paragraphs; the 2016 South China Sea arbitration on 121(3); India's island chains; and the effect of islands on delimitation (case 35 — St Martin's Island given no effect; case 36 — islets and a low-tide elevation not used as base points).
  • Archipelago question: Art. 46 definitions; Art. 47 conditions (the 1:1 to 9:1 ratio; the 100/125 nm rule); archipelagic waters and the two navigation régimes (innocent passage, Art. 52; sea-lanes passage, Art. 53); Indonesia's sea lanes; and the exclusion of continental States with outlying archipelagos.
  • Enclosed-sea question: Art. 122's two-limb definition; Art. 123's four heads of cooperation; the Baltic, Mediterranean and Black Sea régimes; and the Bay of Bengal as a semi-enclosed sea — the key to the concavity argument in case 35.
  • Land-locked question: Art. 124 definitions; Art. 125 right of access and freedom of transit; Articles 126–132; Articles 69–70 on fisheries; and India's 1950 and 1991 treaties with Nepal as the living example.

↑ top

5.2Delimitation of Adjacent and Opposite Maritime Boundaries

Head 5.2 is the most heavily litigated part of the whole law of the sea, and the part of the syllabus to which the four delimitation cases (32 to 36) belong. The problem it addresses is simple to state: a coastal State's zones are measured from its own coast, and the coast of the State opposite or adjacent is close enough that the two sets of zones overlap. Who draws the line? The answer, in the Convention, is a three-step hierarchy: first, the parties should agree; second, if they cannot agree, the boundary is drawn in accordance with the standards of Articles 15, 74 and 83; and third, if they still cannot agree, either party may submit the dispute to the compulsory jurisdiction of Part XV — overwhelmingly to the ICJ, ITLOS, or an Annex VII arbitral tribunal, which is what happened in three of the four cases prescribed in this syllabus.

A. The Three Articles That Govern

Article 15 — Delimitation of the territorial sea between States with opposite or adjacent coasts. "Where the coasts of two States are opposite or adjacent to each other, neither of the two States is entitled, failing agreement between them to the contrary, to extend its territorial sea beyond the median line every point of which is equidistant from the nearest points on the baselines from which the breadth of the territorial seas of each of the two States is measured. The above provision does not apply, however, where it is necessary by reason of historic title or other special circumstances to delimit the territorial seas of the two States in a way which is at variance therewith."

Article 74 — Delimitation of the exclusive economic zone between States with opposite or adjacent coasts. "1. The delimitation of the exclusive economic zone between States with opposite or adjacent coasts shall be effected by agreement on the basis of international law, as referred to in Article 38 of the Statute of the International Court of Justice, in order to achieve an equitable solution. 2. If no agreement can be reached within a reasonable period of time, the States concerned shall resort to the procedures provided for in Part XV. 3. Pending agreement as provided for in paragraph 1, the States concerned, in a spirit of understanding and cooperation, shall make every effort to enter into provisional arrangements of a practical nature and, during this transitional period, not to jeopardize or hamper the reaching of the final agreement. Such arrangements shall be without prejudice to the final delimitation."

Article 83 — Delimitation of the continental shelf between States with opposite or adjacent coasts. "1. The delimitation of the continental shelf between States with opposite or adjacent coasts shall be effected by agreement on the basis of international law, as referred to in Article 38 of the Statute of the International Court of Justice, in order to achieve an equitable solution. 2. If no agreement can be reached within a reasonable period of time, the States concerned shall resort to the procedures provided for in Part XV. 3. Pending agreement as provided for in paragraph 1, the States concerned, in a spirit of understanding and cooperation, shall make every effort to enter into provisional arrangements of a practical nature and, during this transitional period, not to jeopardize or hamper the reaching of the final agreement. Such arrangements shall be without prejudice to the final delimitation."

The differences between the three provisions are the whole story of the modern law.

  1. Article 15 is a rule of result; Articles 74 and 83 are rules of standard. Article 15 tells the States what the line is when they cannot agree — the median line, absent historic title or special circumstances. Articles 74(1) and 83(1) tell them only what the line must achieve — an equitable solution — and leave the method to be worked out by the parties, and failing that, by the court or tribunal seised of the dispute. Articles 74 and 83 are therefore not self-executing and do not themselves supply a method; they supply a standard.
  2. The drafting of the standard was a hard-fought compromise. The competing texts at UNCLOS III were an "equidistance" school (which wanted the 1958 formula of equidistance with special circumstances extended to the EEZ and the shelf) and an "equitable principles" school (which wanted the North Sea formula). The final text — "agreement on the basis of international law... in order to achieve an equitable solution" — is deliberately method-neutral, and the negotiating history records that the choice was made so that neither school could claim victory. The consequence, as every textbook notes, is that the content of Articles 74 and 83 has had to be supplied by the courts — and they have supplied it, over fifty years, in a consistent jurisprudence that now dominates the subject.
  3. Article 15's special-circumstances clause is a safety valve, not a licence. "Historic title" and "other special circumstances" — the classic example being the presence of islands in the median line, as in the Anglo-French Arbitration (1977) and Qatar v. Bahrain (2001), or the historic waters of the type India and Sri Lanka resolved by agreement in 1974.
  4. All three articles are subject to the other parts of the Convention. Article 76(10) provides that the provisions on the outer limits of the shelf "are without prejudice to the question of delimitation of the continental shelf between States with opposite or adjacent coasts". Article 15's opening words ("failing agreement") and Article 74(3)/83(3)'s interim duties complete the scheme.

B. Equidistance, Equity and the Fifty-Year Argument

The intellectual history of this head is a single argument in four movements, and every one of the four cases in the syllabus belongs to one of them.

Movement one — 1958: equidistance as a treaty rule. Article 6 of the 1958 Geneva Convention on the Continental Shelf said that where the same shelf lies between two or more States whose coasts are opposite, the boundary is the median line, and where adjacent, the line of equidistance, "unless another boundary line is justified by special circumstances"; and Article 6(2) required that in determining boundary lines, principles of equity be applied to the extent that special circumstances permit. The provision applied only between parties, and it was reservation-eligible. This is the world that North Sea Continental Shelf (case 32) had to interpret.

Movement two — 1969: equity as the foundation. In the North Sea cases the Court held (at the instance of the Federal Republic of Germany, which by reason of the concavity of its coast would have been confined to a small triangle by a strict equidistance line) that Article 6 was not opposable to a non-party, and then that equidistance was not a rule of customary international law. It held further that delimitation was to be effected by agreement "in accordance with equitable principles", taking account of all the relevant circumstances, so as to leave to each party "all those portions of the continental shelf that constitute a natural prolongation of its land territory into and under the sea", without encroaching on the natural prolongation of the other; and that the "just and equitable share" of a party was not the test. The dispositif listed the factors: the general configuration of the coasts and the "element of a reasonable degree of proportionality", and the "unity of any deposits". This is the birth-certificate of the equitable-principles school.

Movement three — 1977 to 1985: the two schools in opposition. The Anglo-French Arbitration (1977) applied equidistance with special circumstances in the Channel and Atlantic; Tunisia/Libya (1982, case 33) spoke the language of equity as a general principle and held that equity is applied "in the light of the relevant circumstances", with the result — not the principles — being predominant; Gulf of Maine (1984) and Libya/Malta (1985) refined and moderated both positions; the Court in Libya/Malta emphasised the "fundamental norm" of equidistance and the need for a "reasonable and practical" solution. Meanwhile some arbitral tribunals and the Federal Republic's advocates argued for a "principle of proportionality" as a freestanding rule.

Movement four — 1992 to the present: convergence. Since the Jan Mayen case (1993) and especially since Qatar v. Bahrain (2001), Cameroon v. Nigeria (2002), Barbados/Trinidad and Tobago (2006), Romania v. Ukraine (2009), Bangladesh/Myanmar (ITLOS, 2012), Nicaragua v. Colombia (2012), Peru v. Chile (2014), Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (ITLOS, 2017) and Somalia v. Kenya (2021), the courts have settled on a single uniform methodology: construct a provisional equidistance line, adjust it if special or relevant circumstances require, and then test the result for gross disproportionality. The two schools have been fused: equidistance is the method, equity is the standard, and the disproportionality test. The result is that in the great majority of decided cases, the final boundary is either the strict equidistance line or a modest, calibrated adjustment to it. The syllabus's own four delimitation cases trace precisely this arc — North Sea (movement two), Tunisia/Libya (movement three), and the two modern awards (movement four).

C. The Three-Stage Methodology, Stage by Stage

North Sea (1969): base points on the coast; Tunisia/Libya (1982): the most equitable line; Bangladesh/Myanmar (2012): base points on the coast, not on a low-tide elevation. Stage 2 — North Sea: concavity and proportionality; Tunisia/Libya: historic rights, economic factors (excluded save oil wells); Bangladesh/Myanmar and Bangladesh v. India: concave bay → cut-off → adjusted line. Stage 3 — Romania v. Ukraine: no gross disproportion; Somalia v. Kenya: coasts 733 km : 511 km → 1:1.43; areas 92,389 : 120,455 sq km → 1:1.30 — no gross disproportion. What may not be a relevant circumstance Economic dependence or poverty of a party; oil concessions and licensing practice; fisheries practice (unless catastrophic consequences); security or defence needs as such; conduct of the parties unless it is a quasi-agreement (Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire, 2017). What may be a relevant circumstance Geographical: concavity of a coast; seaward cut-off; presence and position of islands; marked change in the direction of the coast; length of the relevant coasts; broad regional configuration (Somalia v. Kenya: Tanzania and Pemba); natural prolongation on the shelf.
Fig. 5.8 — The three stages, the authorities, and the closed list of circumstances

Stage 1 — the provisional equidistance or median line

The first stage is a construction, and it is now highly technical. Four sub-steps are visible in the practice since Romania v. Ukraine.

  1. Identify the relevant coasts. Only coastlines whose projections overlap with those of the other party count; "relevant coasts" exclude the parts facing away from the area of overlap and the coasts of third States (though the effects of third-State geography may enter at Stage 2 as part of the broader configuration — as Tanzania and Pemba Island did in Somalia v. Kenya).
  2. Select the base points. Base points must be on the coast or on islands that generate entitlements; low-tide elevations cannot be used unless they fall within 12 nm and have lighthouses, and isolated islets are frequently disregarded. In Bangladesh v. India (case 35) the Tribunal refused to use base points on a low-tide elevation, and in Somalia v. Kenya (case 36) the Court took its base points on the solid mainland, refusing to treat either the Diua Damasciaca islets or the low-tide elevation at Ras Kaambooni as base points, and declining to use the tiny islets as points of departure for the median line.
  3. Construct the line. The line is drawn from the base points on each side, by the standard geodetic methods, until it reaches the area where the interests of third States arise, or the outer limit of the zones claimed. Where a single boundary is to serve for the territorial sea, the EEZ and the shelf, the line must be constructed so as to be technically defensible for all three (what is called the single maritime boundary — the practice since Gulf of Maine in 1984).
  4. Note the alternative methods. Where equidistance is impractical — because of the configuration of coastlines, as in Bangladesh/Myanmar and Bangladesh v. India — the tribunal may use another method, in particular a "bisector" or "angle-bisector" line, which may then be adjusted for relevant circumstances. In the Bay of Bengal cases both tribunals preferred to construct an adjusted equidistance line rather than an angle bisector, in order not to give the geometric approach too much weight.

Stage 2 — relevant circumstances and adjustment

The second stage is the equity stage, and the list of circumstances that have been accepted is shorter than students expect. Learn it as three tiers.

Table 5.15 — Relevant and irrelevant circumstances, with authority
CircumstanceStatusAuthority and reasoning
Concavity / cut-off of a coastAcceptedNorth Sea Continental Shelf (1969), ¶¶ 8, 89–91 (the concavity of the German coast); Bangladesh/Myanmar (ITLOS, 2012), ¶¶ 390–407; Bangladesh v. India (2014), ¶¶ 397–413 — the Bay of Bengal's concavity produces a cut-off effect on Bangladesh's seaward projection, which requires adjustment of the provisional line at and beyond 200 nm.
Presence and position of islandsAccepted, but calibrations varyAnglo-French Arbitration (1977) (Channel Islands given a 12 nm enclave); Qatar v. Bahrain (2001); Nicaragua v. Honduras (2007); Bangladesh v. India (2014) — St Martin's Island given no effect on the line, but its presence acknowledged; Somalia v. Kenya (2021) — islets given no effect; Note the modern tendency: islands at the landward end of the line are given effect as base points only if they do not distort the delimitation.
Length of the relevant coasts / proportionalityAcceptedNorth Sea ¶¶ 91, 98; Libya/Malta (1985); Romania v. Ukraine (2009) ¶¶ 110–122; Somalia v. Kenya (2021) ¶¶ 178–185 (the disproportionality test as the third stage).
Marked change in the direction of the coast / regional configurationAcceptedTunisia/Libya (1982) — the change in direction of the Tunisian coast; Cameroon v. Nigeria (2002); Romania v. Ukraine (2009) — the "Serpents' Island" effect; Somalia v. Kenya (2021) — the Court took into account the broader geographical configuration of the East African coast, including the presence of Tanzania and Pemba Island, in deciding whether an adjustment was required.
Historic rights or title in the disputed areaAccepted in the territorial sea; rarely elsewhereArticle 15 expressly saves historic title; Tunisia/Libya (1982) ¶ 100 — historic rights may be a relevant circumstance; but Qatar v. Bahrain (2001) and Eritrea/Yemen (1998–99) treated historic fishing rights as not sufficient to shift a boundary.
Natural prolongation / geomorphologyAccepted for the shelf; largely subsumed by distanceNorth Sea ¶ 43 (the "most fundamental" principle); Tunisia/Libya ¶ 44 (natural prolongation must be distinguished from the "precise determination" of a boundary); Libya/Malta (1985) — the distance criterion now applies within 400 nm.
Unity of depositsAccepted in a limited wayNorth Sea ¶ 97; the practice of "unitisation" agreements: India's own Delhi-Abu? The classic Indian illustration is the practice of unitisation for cross-boundary reservoirs in the Persian Gulf (Saudi Arabia–Kuwait) and the North Sea (the Frigg field agreement). For an examination, cite the principle and the practice of unitisation as the practical answer.
Security and defence interestsWeak — normally disregardedAnglo-French Arbitration (1977) rejected navigational and defence interests as such; Nicaragua v. Honduras (2007) and Somalia v. Kenya (2021) — Kenya's security concerns about piracy and terrorism were not accepted as circumstances justifying an adjustment.
Economic dependence, oil concessions, licensing practiceDisregarded, with narrow exceptionsTunisia/Libya ¶ 107 — economic factors are not relevant, "except perhaps" where a boundary would have "catastrophic repercussions" for the livelihood of the population; concession lines are not determinative but may be evidence of acquiescence (Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire, 2017, on oil practice).
Fisheries practiceDisregarded save in extreme casesAnglo-French Arbitration; Barbados/Trinidad (2006) ¶¶ 372–384; Somalia v. Kenya ¶¶ 189–196 — Kenya's fisheries arguments were rejected on the basis that the line did not have the effect of depriving the population of a livelihood.
Conduct of the parties / oil concessions as evidence of a tacit agreementRelevant, with a high thresholdGhana/Côte d'Ivoire (ITLOS Special Chamber, 2017): the Chamber considered evidence of both States' oil practice and held that there was no tacit agreement — the threshold is high and requires consistency and acceptance; the same analysis of acquiescence was applied in Somalia v. Kenya (2021), where Kenya's claim of a boundary at the parallel was rejected.

Two observations about stage 2 make an examiner's ears prick up. First, the courts insist that the exercise is not a redistribution of maritime space on the basis of need: it is a delimitation of legal entitlements, and the "equitable solution" is an equitable line, not an equitable sharing. The intellectual centre of the point is Tunisia/Libya's distinction between the "principle of equity as a general concept" and the application of "equity as a method of law"; and the Court's warning that "the result is predominant, the principles subordinate". Second, the courts have been careful to keep the list of relevant circumstances manageable, precisely because an unbounded list converts delimitation into a debate about the parties' relative wealth, which the law does not countenance. The most frequent instruction in the modern cases is therefore corrective rather than creative: the line is adjusted by a modest, reasoned amount, rather than redrawn.

Stage 3 — the disproportionality test

The third stage is a negative check: the result of stages 1 and 2 must not be "grossly disproportionate". The technique is arithmetic and is worth reproducing in an answer. The tribunal measures (i) the lengths of the relevant coasts — not the entire coastline of each State, but the coastlines whose projections overlap; and (ii) the areas of the maritime zones conferred on each party by the boundary. It then compares the two ratios. If the ratio of the areas is markedly out of line with the ratio of the coastal lengths, the line is adjusted; otherwise it stands. The leading example is Romania v. Ukraine (2009), ¶¶ 110–122: the relevant coastlines were in a ratio of about 1:2.8, and the areas produced by the final line about 1:2.1 — no gross disproportion. Somalia v. Kenya (2021) applied the same technique: the relevant coasts were 733 km (Somalia) : 511 km (Kenya), a ratio of 1:1.43; the maritime areas produced by the adjusted line were 92,389 : 120,455 sq km, a ratio of 1:1.30 — again, no gross disproportion. In the Bay of Bengal award the Tribunal, having adjusted the line substantially in Bangladesh's favour because of the concavity, found the result equitable without a separate arithmetic correction, holding that the disproportion between the parties' coastal lengths (about 1:1.5) and the areas awarded was not gross.

D. Delimiting the Territorial Sea — Article 15 in Practice

Territorial-sea delimitation is the narrowest and most rule-bound part of the exercise, and modern tribunals treat it in a single short section of their judgments. The steps are:

  1. Identify the land boundary terminus. The starting point of the maritime boundary is the point at which the land boundary between the two States meets the sea. Where the land boundary is disputed, the maritime tribunal must first construe the instrument that fixed it — which is exactly what the Bay of Bengal Tribunal did with the Radcliffe Award of 1947 (case 35): it held that the Radcliffe Award's reference to the Haribhanga was alone relevant, that the midstream of the main channel of that river had to be located as it was in 1947, that a low-tide elevation in the estuary could not be used as a base point, and that the terminus so fixed did not lie on the median line — a circumstance requiring the drawing of a short geodetic line from the terminus to the median line.
  2. Draw the median line from appropriate base points. Article 15's line is "every point of which is equidistant from the nearest points on the baselines". Lawful straight baselines and the coastlines of islands are relevant; low-tide elevations and artificial structures are not. In Somalia v. Kenya the Court drew a median line for the territorial sea using base points on the mainland of each State and terminated it at Point A, 12 nm from the coast on the boundary line.
  3. Consider historic title and special circumstances. Islands are the classic special circumstance; historic waters are the other. India's Palk Bay settlement with Sri Lanka in 1974 is an illustration of the second: rather than litigating, the two States agreed a boundary in historic waters and preserved Indian fishing rights by an exchange of letters — and the resulting dispute over Kachchatheevu and the treatment of Indian fishermen remains a live political issue on the Indian side (see §5.2 G).
  4. Terminate the territorial-sea line where it meets the outer limit (12 nm) and continue the boundary for the EEZ and shelf under Articles 74 and 83. The two operations are separate: a single line may be arrived at, but the competent provision changes at the 12 nm point — a distinction the Bay of Bengal Tribunal expressly made in its dispositif, treating the territorial sea boundary and the EEZ/shelf boundary as separately delimited.

E. Beyond 200 nm: the Shelf, the CLCS, and the "Grey Area"

Three questions arise once the shelf extends beyond 200 nm.

(1) May a court or tribunal delimit the shelf beyond 200 nm at all? Yes — and the point is now settled after an evolution of four cases. In Barbados/Trinidad and Tobago (2006) the Annex VII tribunal held that it could not delimit the shelf beyond 200 nm unless the parties had established their entitlements through the CLCS process, because until then the existence and extent of the entitlement were speculative. In Nicaragua v. Honduras (2007) the ICJ said the same. In Romania v. Ukraine (2009) the Court accepted that it had jurisdiction in principle but concluded that it lacked sufficient information about the entitlement. In Bangladesh/Myanmar (ITLOS, 2012), however, the Tribunal held that it could exercise its jurisdiction to delimit the shelf beyond 200 nm where the entitlements of the parties were established by the scientific evidence before it and where the parties had agreed that the Tribunal had jurisdiction — with the important qualification that the CLCS retains competence to consider the scientific validity of the outer limits, and that the Tribunal's delimitation was without prejudice to the Commission's work. The Bay of Bengal award (2014, case 35) followed that approach and delimited the shelf between India and Bangladesh out to the outer limits, and the Somalia v. Kenya judgment (2021, case 36) extended the boundary line beyond 200 nm "until it reaches the outer limits of the Parties' continental shelves, which are to be delineated on the basis of the recommendations to be made by the Commission", or until it reaches the area where the rights of third States may be affected.

(2) How does the methodology change beyond 200 nm? In principle not at all: the same three stages are applied. In practice, two adjustments appear. First, the base points and the relevant coasts are the same, but the geometry of the extended shelf may mean that the projection lines cross differently, so that an equidistance line constructed for the shelf beyond 200 nm may be a continuation of the EEZ line or may be adjusted separately. Second, the disproportionality test is harder to apply, because the area to be divided is defined by the geology rather than by the distance criterion. The Bay of Bengal Tribunal handled the problem by extending the same adjusted line to the outer limits of the shelf, thereby avoiding a discontinuity.

(3) What is a "grey area" and what law governs it? A grey area arises whenever the boundary of the EEZ and the boundary of the continental shelf are not identical in a region where one State's shelf entitlement (based on natural prolongation) extends beyond 200 nm and the other State's EEZ extends over the same area. The result is a zone in which the two States have rights in different layers: the seabed and subsoil belong (for exploitation purposes) to one State, while the water column and its living resources belong to the other. The Bay of Bengal award (case 35) is the leading example: the Tribunal held that part of the area awarded to Bangladesh as extended continental shelf lies within 200 nm of India, so that "within the grey area Bangladesh has sovereign rights with respect to the continental shelf, including the seabed and subsoil, while India has sovereign rights with respect to the exclusive economic zone, notably its living resources and other resources of the water column". The Tribunal held that this result was a legitimate consequence of the applicable law and did not require it to alter its boundary; it left the practical administration of the grey area to the parties (and required consultations). In Somalia v. Kenya (2021) the Court noted that only a "possible grey area of limited size" would arise and declined to pronounce on the applicable régime, on the basis that the question was hypothetical. In Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (2017) the Special Chamber, faced with a similar problem, used its equitable authority differently — deciding that where the areas of the EEZ and the shelf would diverge, the boundary should be drawn so as to avoid the grey area altogether by departing from the equidistance line. The lesson for an answer: the grey area is a consequence of the Convention's own architecture — the shelf is not limited by 200 nm, the EEZ is, and the two are delimited by different standards — and the courts have not yet settled on a uniform technique for dealing with it.

F. Governing the Undelimited Area — Article 74(3) and 83(3)

Between the dispute and the judgment lies the transitional period, and Articles 74(3) and 83(3) impose duties during it. The leading pronouncement is Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (ITLOS Special Chamber, 2017), which identified three obligations: (i) the duty to negotiate in good faith with a view to reaching an agreement; (ii) the duty to make every effort to enter into provisional arrangements of a practical nature, which is an obligation of conduct and not of result; and (iii) the duty not to jeopardise or hamper the reaching of a final agreement — which prohibits unilateral activities and unilateral acts that would prejudice the final delimitation, but which does not prohibit all activity. The Chamber also held that the obligations in Articles 74(3) and 83(3) continue to apply after the judgment, as a matter of customary law, where the parties have to administer the area in ways the judgment does not fully cover. In Somalia v. Kenya (case 36) the Court applied the same test to Kenya's activities in the disputed area and found: that Kenya's claims had been made in good faith; that activities in an area subsequently attributed to another State are not wrongful if carried out before the judgment in good faith; that the transitional-period obligation ran from 2009 (when the dispute crystallised) and not from 1979; that Kenya's transitory activities — naval patrols, fisheries enforcement, seismic surveys, the collection of data — did not jeopardise or hamper the reaching of a final agreement; and that the alleged post-2009 drilling in the disputed area had not been proven. The claim for reparation therefore failed. The judgment is the modern authority for the proposition that a State may act in an undelimited area, but not in a way that forecloses the final agreement.

G. India's Maritime Boundaries

Table 5.16 — India's maritime boundaries: instrument by instrument
NeighbourInstrument and dateSubject matter and current state
Sri LankaAgreement on the Boundary in Historic Waters between the Two Countries and Related Matters, signed 26/28 June 1974; Agreement on the Maritime Boundary in the Gulf of Mannar and the Bay of Bengal, signed 23 March 1976, in force 10 May 1976; trijunction point with Maldives fixed on 31 July 1976.The 1974 agreement settled the Palk Strait/Palk Bay boundary in historic waters and delivered Kachchatheevu to Sri Lanka; the 1976 agreement drew the Gulf of Mannar boundary (13 turning points) and the Bay of Bengal boundary (8 points, about 214 nautical miles). The 1974 agreement's exchange of letters preserved the traditional fishing rights of Indian fishermen in the waters around Kachchatheevu — the source of the continuing dispute over arrests and the periodic diplomatic friction.
MaldivesMaritime boundary agreement, 1976.Divides the Arabian Sea/Indian Ocean boundary between Lakshadweep and the Maldives; the trijunction with Sri Lanka was fixed in 1976. India and the Maldives have since added coast-guard cooperation (2016, 2019) and the 2024–25 defence and development agreements.
Indonesia1974 (Great Channel, between the Nicobar Islands and Sumatra) and 1977 (Andaman Sea and the straits between the Andaman and Nicobar Islands and Sumatra).Two of India's earliest boundary agreements, delimiting the channel that carries traffic between the Indian Ocean and the Malacca Strait. The Six Degree Channel is the principal route of Indian Ocean traffic toward the Pacific; Indonesia's archipelagic sea lanes and the Andaman and Nicobar trijunctions are the modern issues.
Thailand1978 (Andaman Sea).Delimits the Andaman Sea boundary between the Nicobar Islands and Thailand; the area is also relevant to the India–Indonesia and India–Myanmar trijunctions.
Myanmar23 December 1986 (Andaman Sea, Coco Channel and Bay of Bengal).Delimits India's eastern boundary as against Myanmar, closing the gap between India's Andaman claims and Myanmar's coast; the northern end of the boundary interacts with Bangladesh's claims and with the 2014 award's tripoint.
BangladeshIn the Matter of the Bay of Bengal Maritime Boundary Arbitration (Bangladesh v. India), PCA, Award of 7 July 2014 (case 35).The only Indian maritime boundary fixed by adjudication. Bangladesh was awarded about 19,467 sq km of the 25,602 sq km in dispute; India retained about 6,135 sq km, including the area of the former New Moore/South Talpatti Island. The delimitation covers the territorial sea, the EEZ and the continental shelf within and beyond 200 nm, and it created a "grey area" in which the seabed belongs to Bangladesh while the superjacent water column belongs to India. The Award was accepted by both States and is studied in full at case 35 and at §5.5.
PakistanUnresolved — the Sir Creek dispute.Sir Creek is a 96-km tidal estuary in the Rann of Kutch; India claims the boundary runs along the eastern bank and Pakistan along the western bank (or, on Pakistan's reading, along the middle of the channel), with consequences for the maritime boundary in the Arabian Sea and for the area of the continental shelf off Gujarat and Sindh. The dispute also affects India's 2009 extended-shelf submission; India's 2025 modified submission was, on the reported account, deliberately directed to the uncontested Central Arabian Sea.

Two lessons from the Indian table. First, India has been a consistent user of the treaty method: five of India's six settled boundaries are bilateral agreements, several of them agreed before UNCLOS III concluded, and each of them shows the classic pattern of the territorial sea, the EEZ and the shelf being fixed by a single set of coordinates in a single instrument. Second, the two boundaries that were not settled — the Bay of Bengal and Sir Creek — produced India's two most instructive legal events: the 2014 award, in which India accepted and implemented an award that went against it in large part, and the unresolved Sir Creek question, which continues to affect both India's maritime delimitation and its extended continental-shelf submission.

The two questions this head produces, and how to answer them
  1. "How is a maritime boundary between adjacent or opposite States delimited today?" — Answer in the order: Articles 15, 74 and 83 (with the text); the equidistance/equitable-principles history (1958 Art. 6; North Sea 1969; Tunisia/Libya 1982; the convergence after 1993); the three-stage methodology (with the authorities for each stage); the categories of relevant circumstance (with the authorities); the disproportionality test (with the arithmetic of Romania v. Ukraine and Somalia v. Kenya); and the beyond-200-nm jurisprudence and the grey area.
  2. "Discuss the contribution of the case law to the delimitation of maritime boundaries." — Answer case by case, but group the cases by contribution: North Sea (equitable principles, natural prolongation, concavity, proportionality, unity of deposits); Tunisia/Libya (equity as law, the subordination of principles to result, the exclusion of economic factors); Libya/Malta (distance criterion within 400 nm); Gulf of Maine (single maritime boundary); Anglo-French Arbitration (islands and enclaves); Qatar/Bahrain and Cameroon/Nigeria (base points, islands); Romania v. Ukraine (the three stages); Bangladesh/Myanmar and Bangladesh v. India (concavity, the shelf beyond 200 nm, the grey area); Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (tacit agreement and Art. 74(3) obligations); Somalia v. Kenya (acquiescence, base points, regional configuration, disproportionality, the transitional-period duties).
