CASESCase Laws in Full Detail — Cases 30 to 36

Cases 30 to 33 are the classical judgments of the International Court; cases 34 to 36 are the three modern decisions with an Indian connection — the Indian Supreme Court judgment in the Enrica Lexie reference; the PCA award that fixed India's maritime boundary with Bangladesh; and the ICJ judgment that settled the Indian Ocean boundary between Somalia and Kenya. Each case is set out in the seven-part form required by the DU case material: (1) why the case is in the syllabus; (2) the facts; (3) the issues; (4) the arguments of both sides; (5) the decision; (6) the principle evolved; and (7) the examination angle, with cross-references to the heads of the syllabus. Where a case is listed in the syllabus but not printed in the case material, the provenance is stated at the head of the case.

Case 30 · Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries Case (United Kingdom v. Norway)

Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries Case (United Kingdom v. Norway) — "the Fisheries case"

International Court of Justice, Judgment of 18 December 1951, I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 116 — the first case listed under head 5.1.1 of the syllabus, and the leading authority on baselines and on the drawing of straight baselines · Prescribed in the DU case material, Law of the Sea, Topic 5

Provenance. The case material prints the judgment with the facts, the pleadings and the judgment's reasoning; the account below follows the case material and the judgment itself, and adds the codification history (Article 4 of the 1958 Geneva Convention on the Territorial Sea and Contiguous Zone; Article 7 of UNCLOS 1982) and the doctrine from S.K. Verma, Chapter 14.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Every maritime zone is measured from a baseline; the baseline is normally the low-water line; and the exception to that rule — the straight baseline — is the law's concession to geography. Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries is the case that created the exception, and it is in the syllabus for four reasons. First, it establishes the two methods of drawing baselines and the geographical conditions for the exceptional one. Second, it supplies the three tests — general direction of the coast, close link with the land domain, and long usage evidenced by economic dependence — which Article 7 of UNCLOS now carries in its own language, so that the case is the best guide to the meaning of a treaty provision. Third, it is the authority for the proposition that delimitation is a unilateral act with international legal consequences: the coastal State draws the line, but the validity of the line depends upon international law and not on the State's own decree. Fourth, and most usefully for the student, it is the case in which the Court rejected the "ten-mile rule" for bays and the argument from long practice of other States, showing how custom, usage and acquiescence work in this field. The class notes put the point that examiners want: "In this case the court clarified the law relating to the determination of fisheries zone by the states... Much of the language from the case was codified in Article 4 of the 1958 Convention... Almost the same language is in Article 7 of UNCLOS on straight baselines."

2. Facts

(a) The coast. The Norwegian coast, from the frontier with the Soviet Union in the north-east to the Skagerrak in the south, is one of the most singular coastlines in the world. Along the mainland runs the skjærgaard — a fringe of some 120,000 islands, islets, rocks and reefs, separated from the mainland by a series of sounds and fjords, and sheltering a coastal passage (the indrelidsleia, "the inner lead") which navigators use in preference to the open sea. The mainland itself is deeply indented: the fjords cut into it, and the mountains fall into the sea. Norwegian fishing communities live not on the mainland but on the islands of the fringe, and their livelihood depended on the waters immediately to the seaward of the fringe.

(b) The decrees. Norway had long claimed a belt of sea measured not from the mainland but from the skjærgaard. Royal Decrees of 1812 and 1869 and a Resolution of 1889 had fixed a one-mile limit measured from the low-water mark of the outermost islands; the system was applied by the Norwegian authorities over decades. On 12 July 1935 a Royal Decree delimited the Norwegian fisheries zone — the belt reserved for Norwegian fishermen — by reference to 48 selected points, most of them on the islands and rocks of the skjærgaard and some at a considerable distance from the mainland; the lines joining them were straight baselines from which the breadth of the fisheries zone (four nautical miles at the material time) was measured. The Decree was not a delimitation of the territorial sea; the territorial sea remained four miles wide, but it was measured from lines drawn much further out to sea than the low-water mark of the mainland.

(c) The dispute. The waters enclosed by the 1935 lines were exactly the waters where British trawlers fished. Arrests of British vessels followed — the case material records the sequence of incidents which produced the litigation, including the arrest and condemnation of trawlers within the lines in 1934 and 1935 and the later incident which finally drove the United Kingdom to court. The United Kingdom protested — in a note of 27 July 1933 in respect of an earlier delimitation, and again in 1948 — and on 28 September 1949 instituted proceedings by Application, asking the Court to declare that the Norwegian system of delimitation was contrary to international law and that the United Kingdom was entitled to compensation for the arrests.

(d) The system as it stood in 1951. Norway had applied the straight-line method since 1869; the Kingdom of Denmark and Sweden had applied the same method for parts of their coasts; several other States, including France, Spain and Portugal, had asserted a "bays and headlands" position; and the practice of States since the nineteenth century had been, at the least, inconsistent with any absolute rule of the low-water line. That background of practice and tolerance became one of Norway's principal arguments.

3. The issues

  1. Whether the method of straight baselines — a line drawn between points on the coast or on the islands of the skjærgaard, from which the belt of sea is measured — is permissible in international law.
  2. Whether, if the method is permissible, the conditions for its use were satisfied on the Norwegian coast; and what those conditions are.
  3. Whether the 1935 Decree, and the selection of the 48 points, were consistent with those conditions — that is, whether the Decree was a lawful application of a lawful method.
  4. Whether the United Kingdom's delay in protesting (the years 1869 to 1933) and the general tolerance of Norway's system affected the United Kingdom's position — the question of historic consolidation or prescription.
  5. Whether any absolute rule of international law — in particular the "ten-mile rule" for bays, and the contention that the territorial sea must follow the sinuosities of the coast — bound Norway.

4. The arguments of the parties

(a) The United Kingdom
  • The ordinary (low-water mark) rule is the rule of international law. The territorial sea is a belt of sea adjacent to the coast measured from the low-water mark along the whole length of the coast; the coast is followed in all its sinuosities; and this is a rule of general customary law from which a State may not depart by unilateral decree.
  • The straight-line method is a local aberration. Such a system can be justified only within very narrow limits — the closing of bays of modest width, and, possibly, where there is a genuine fringe of islands ("welts") immediately along the coast on a limited stretch; the Norwegian system applied straight lines on stretches of coast where there is no such fringe, and in some places the lines were drawn to points 20 or 30 or even more miles from the mainland.
  • The 1935 Decree is not justified by prescription or usage. A fisheries zone is not a territorial sea; the earlier decrees never purported to fix a boundary opposable to foreign States; the United Kingdom protested as soon as the Decree affected its fishermen; and the Court is not entitled to legitimise a claim merely because Norway has repeated it.
  • Any absolute rule of the low-water line is also supported by practice. The ten-mile rule for bays, the practice of the United States and Great Britain, and the rules adopted in the 1930 Hague Codification Conference all pointed the same way.
  • Subsidiary submission. Even if the method were lawful in principle, the specific lines chosen were so drawn as to close off large areas of the sea which cannot be regarded as internal waters, and the United Kingdom was entitled to a declaration to that effect and to compensation for the arrests.
(b) Norway
  • The skjærgaard is a unit with the mainland. The fringe of islands is not a series of detached features but an organic part of the Norwegian coast; Norwegian law and Norwegian geography treat mainland and skjærgaard as one; the real coast for the purposes of the law of the sea is the outer edge of the skjærgaard, and the waters inside are not, and never have been, high seas.
  • The straight-line method is a rule of general customary law, at least for coasts of this type. Norway relied on a documented practice from 1869, on the practice of other States (Sweden, Finland, Spain, Portugal, Ecuador, Italy), and on the absence of any general protest.
  • The decree is an application of long-established usage. The system had been applied "quietly and consistently"; the arrests of foreign trawlers had been accepted without protest for decades; the United Kingdom's protests were late and, when made, were directed to particular incidents rather than to the system as a whole; and by 1951 the system had ripened into a title opposable to other States — what the Court would call a "consolidated" delimitation.
  • Economic and social realities. The Norwegian coastal population depended on the fisheries of the waters enclosed by the lines, as was shown by the reports of the Norwegian authorities; the Court was entitled to treat this dependence as supporting the reasonableness of the delimitation.
  • The ten-mile rule is not part of general international law. Norway had always opposed it; a rule to which a State has persistently objected cannot be invoked against that State.

5. The decision

The Court held, by ten votes to two, that the method of straight baselines employed by the Norwegian Government in its 1935 Decree was not contrary to international law, and rejected the United Kingdom's submissions. Its reasoning proceeded in five steps.

(a) The delimitation is a question of international law, not of the decree's own validity. The Court's central statement:

"The delimitation of sea areas has always an international aspect; it cannot be dependent merely upon the will of the coastal State as expressed in its municipal law. Although it is true that the act of delimitation is necessarily a unilateral act, because only the coastal State is competent to undertake it, the validity of the delimitation with regard to other States depends upon international law." (p. 132)

(b) The low-water mark rule is the general rule, but it is not absolute. "The rule that the territorial sea must be measured from the low-water mark is not the only rule of international law that is applicable"; the Court held that where the coast is of the type of the Norwegian skjærgaard — deeply indented and fringed with islands — the belt of sea must be measured from the outer line of the skjærgaard, because "if the territorial sea is not measured from the line of the skjærgaard, the Norwegian coast would be robbed of its natural character and the belt would be an artificial construction".

(c) The three conditions (the tests that Article 7 now carries). The Court said that the straight-line system may be applied "only when a coast is deeply indented and cut into" or where there is "a fringe of islands along the coast in its immediate vicinity", and then laid down three limits on the drawing of the lines:

  1. The general direction of the coast. "The drawing of baselines must not depart to any appreciable extent from the general direction of the coast." The Court accepted that the Norwegian lines satisfied this test because they followed the general direction of the skjærgaard, which is itself the coast in question.
  2. The close link with the land domain. "The real question raised by the choice of the straight baselines... is whether the sea areas lying within these lines are sufficiently closely linked to the land domain to be subject to the régime of internal waters." The Court stressed that the waters between the mainland and the skjærgaard "have always... been... closely linked to the land domain" and are used by the coastal population.
  3. Economic interests evidenced by long usage. "Certain economic interests peculiar to a region, the reality and importance of which are clearly evidenced by a long usage" may be taken into account in deciding the reasonableness of a delimitation — the ancestor of Article 7(5) of UNCLOS ("where the method of straight baselines is applicable... account may be taken... of economic interests peculiar to the region concerned, the reality and the importance of which are clearly evidenced by long usage").

(d) The rejection of the ten-mile rule and of the argument from the practice of other States. The Court examined the United Kingdom's contention that there is a rule of international law forbidding the drawing of straight lines longer than ten nautical miles across the mouth of a bay, and held that it had not been established: "the ten-mile rule has been adopted by certain States both in their national law and in their treaties and conventions, and although certain arbitral decisions have applied it as between these States, other States have adopted a different limit. Consequently, the ten-mile rule has not acquired the authority of a general rule of international law." Norway, moreover, "has always opposed any attempt to apply it to the Norwegian coast".

(e) Tolerance, usage and consolidation. Finally, and importantly for later cases, the Court took into account the fact that the Norwegian system had been applied to an area that other States had not treated as high seas: "the Norwegian Government... has... proceeded... in a manner... The Court notes... that the... delimitation... has been enjoyed by Norway... for a long period... and that, since 1869... the... system... has been applied... without... The Court considers it... necessary... to... take... account... of... the... general toleration of the international community" — the proposition now conventionally cited as the doctrine of historic consolidation or of the opposability of a long-tolerated delimitation, and the reason the case is paired with the acquiescence issue in Somalia v. Kenya (case 36) and with the historic-waters provision of India's Maritime Zones Act, 1976.

(f) What the Court did not decide. The Court did not hold that the straight-line method may be used at the coastal State's unfettered discretion on any coast: the method is confined to coasts of the described type, and each line must satisfy the tests. Nor did the Court hold that the Norwegian lines themselves were beyond scrutiny: it examined them individually against the tests, and its conclusion was that the system, as applied, was not contrary to international law. Nor did the Court decide anything about the outer limit of the Norwegian fisheries zone or the areas of sea lying to the north of the disputed lines; with the judgment of 18 December 1951 the case was concluded, the United Kingdom did not pursue further proceedings, and the Decree of 1935 remained in force — its method later codified in Article 7 of UNCLOS.

6. The principles evolved

  1. Two methods of drawing baselines. The normal baseline is the low-water line along the coast (now Art. 5 UNCLOS; Art. 3 of the 1958 Convention); the exceptional method is the straight baseline joining appropriate points on the coast or on fringing islands (now Art. 7 UNCLOS; Art. 4 of 1958). The two conditions for the exceptional method: a coast "deeply indented and cut into", or "a fringe of islands along the coast in its immediate vicinity".
  2. Three tests on the drawing of straight baselines. No appreciable departure from the general direction of the coast; a sufficiently close link between the enclosed sea areas and the land domain; and, where applicable, economic interests evidenced by long usage.
  3. Delimitation is a unilateral act with international consequences. "The validity of the delimitation with regard to other States depends upon international law" — hence the publication requirement (now Art. 16) and the practice of lodging baselines with the United Nations.
  4. Custom must be proved, and the burden lies on the State asserting it. The ten-mile rule failed for want of uniformity and because of Norway's persistent objection; the Court's method — examining State practice, treaty practice, arbitral decisions and protests — is the classic application of the requirements of custom.
  5. Acquiescence and consolidation. A delimitation tolerated over a long period may become opposable to other States even if it was originally novel; the corollary is that a State which wishes to contest a baseline must protest in time, as the United Kingdom's late protest was held not to have preserved its position.
  6. Codification. The case's language was carried into Article 4 of the 1958 Convention and then into Article 7 of UNCLOS, and the resulting article is a treaty rule binding on States Parties regardless of the case; but the case remains the principal aid to the interpretation of the article's three conditions, and the principal authority on the limits of the method.
  7. Islands, bays and historic waters. The judgment is the source of the modern treatment of fringing islands (which may be joined by straight baselines) and of the distinction between lawful bay-closing lines (now Art. 10's 24 nm and semicircle rules) and unlawful encroachment on the high seas.

7. The examination angle

How the case is used in papers
  • Direct question: "Discuss the law relating to the delimitation of the territorial sea with special reference to the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case" — the answer is the two methods, the conditions, the three tests, the ten-mile rule's rejection, and the codification in Articles 5, 7 and 16 of UNCLOS.
  • As part of the 2025 question on measurement of territorial waters where the coastline is deeply indented with a fringe of islands (PYQ 6, §PYQ Answer 1): the case supplies the entire answer to the second half of that question, and the syllabus's handwritten annotation "baseline → low tide line" beside 5.1.1 points to the same material.
  • As authority for historic waters and India's section 3(3): the Court's treatment of long usage and tolerance is the source of the doctrine of historic waters which India invoked in 1974 for Palk Bay and Palk Strait, and which is now written into section 3(3) of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976.
  • As a contrast with the modern cases: compare the Court's flexible "realities of the situation" approach with the strict construction of Article 7 in the subsequent practice (the Qatar v. Bahrain and Bangladesh/Myanmar base-point decisions), and with the refusal of the 2014 and 2021 tribunals to allow small islands and low-tide elevations to distort a boundary (cases 35 and 36).
  • The sentence to quote: "the validity of the delimitation with regard to other States depends upon international law" — the single most useful line in the judgment, and one that links the case to the whole of Article 7 and to the good-faith obligation of Article 300.

Case 31 · Corfu Channel Case (United Kingdom v. Albania)

Corfu Channel Case (United Kingdom v. Albania)

I.C.J. Reports 1949, p. 4 (Merits, 9 April 1949) · I.C.J. Reports 1949, p. 244 (Assessment of Compensation, 15 December 1949) · Bench: President Basdevant; Judges Guerrero, Alvarez, Hackworth, Winiarski, Zoričić, De Visscher, Badawi Pasha, Krylov, Read, Hsu Mo, Azevedo, McNair; Judges ad hoc Daxner (Albania) and Eerola (United Kingdom)

Provenance. The case material prints the judgment in the law-of-the-sea chapter, where it is prescribed for the straits and innocent-passage questions; in this syllabus it is case 31 under §5.1.1. The account below follows the judgment and the case material, and states separately the portions relevant to Topic 4 (State responsibility), which is where the same case appears as case 23.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Corfu Channel is the foundation of the modern law of straits, and it is the only case in the syllabus that deals simultaneously with the two great functions of the sea — navigation and security. It is in the syllabus because it decides four matters: (i) that a strait connecting two parts of the high seas through the territorial sea of a coastal State is a strait used for international navigation, defined by geography and use rather than by traffic density, and that in time of peace the coastal State may not prohibit the passage of warships through it; (ii) that the coastal State's sovereignty over such a strait is not extinguished but qualified: it may issue regulations, but not prohibit or require special authorisation; (iii) that a warship's passage is not rendered non-innocent by the mere fact that it is a warship, that its crew is at action stations, or that the passage has a demonstrative purpose — the coastal State bears the burden of showing abuse; and (iv) that even a seriously injured State may not take the law into its own hands: the minesweeping operation ("Operation Retail") was an unlawful violation of Albanian sovereignty, and the Court's declaration of that violation was itself "appropriate satisfaction". The fourth proposition carries the case into the law of State responsibility (Topic 4).

2. Facts

(a) The Channel. The North Corfu Channel separates the Greek island of Corfu from the coast of Albania. It is a narrow strait, about two and a half nautical miles wide at its narrowest point, joining two parts of the high seas — the Adriatic and the Aegean/Ionian — and lying partly in Albanian and partly in Greek territorial waters. The channel had long been used for international navigation, and its freedom was of particular concern to Greece, whose shipping used it, and to the maritime Powers, which regarded it as one of the straits of the world.

(b) The antecedents (1944–46). In October 1944, during the war, the North Corfu Channel was swept by the British Navy and no mines were found; in November 1944 the existence of a safe route through the Channel was announced internationally; in January and February 1945 the Channel was check-swept, again with negative results. The British Admiralty's confidence is shown by the fact that on 15 May 1946 two cruisers passed through the Channel without special precautions against moored mines.

(c) 15 May 1946. The British cruisers Orion and Superb, passing southward, were fired at by an Albanian battery near Saranda; twelve to twenty rounds were fired over twelve minutes, and no ship was hit. Albania's note of 21 May 1946 complained of the passage of foreign warships without permission; a second note of 19 June 1946 repeated that the passage required the authorisation of the Albanian Government and refused to allow passage.

(d) 22 October 1946. A squadron of four British warships — the cruisers Mauritius and Leander and the destroyers Saumarez and Volage — proceeded northward through the swept channel, crews at action stations. Outside the Bay of Saranda the Saumarez struck a mine and was severely damaged; while attempting to assist, the Volage also struck a mine. Forty-four British officers and men were killed and forty-two injured. The Court found that the mines had been laid recently, that they could not have been laid without the knowledge of the Albanian Government, and that Albania had failed to notify the existence of the minefield although it had ample opportunity to do so; those findings found Albania's responsibility for the explosions (the Topic-4 half of the case).

(e) Operation Retail (12–13 November 1946). After the explosions, the United Kingdom notified Albania that it would sweep the channel, and on 12–13 November 1946, with twenty-eight minesweepers and supporting forces, cleared the channel against Albania's express objection: on 12 November the Albanian Government sent a protest, complaining that such a sweeping operation was a violation of Albanian sovereignty. The mines found were of a type manufactured in Germany, and the United Kingdom claimed that their presence, together with the finding of a mine cable in the Bay of Saranda and the refusal of Albania to permit any inquiry, established responsibility. Albania denied any knowledge of the mines and refused to permit an examination of its archives or of the region.

(f) The Special Agreement. The two States agreed by Special Agreement of 25 March 1948 to submit their dispute to the Court; the Agreement put two questions: whether Albania was responsible for the explosions and the damage, and whether the United Kingdom had violated Albanian sovereignty by its passage of 22 October 1946 and by the minesweeping of November 1946.

3. The issues

  1. Whether the North Corfu Channel was a strait used for international navigation, and if so on what test.
  2. Whether a coastal State may prohibit or subject to authorisation the passage of foreign warships through such a strait in time of peace.
  3. Whether the passage of 22 October 1946 — four warships, at action stations, in a narrow channel close to the Albanian coast, at a time of tension — was an abuse of the right of innocent passage, and whether the passage was rendered non-innocent by the "purpose of demonstrating force".
  4. Whether the United Kingdom's minesweeping of 12–13 November 1946 was a violation of Albanian sovereignty, and if so what remedy was appropriate, Albania having asked for a declaration of violation rather than compensation.
  5. Whether Albania's failure to warn of the mines made it internationally responsible for the explosions and damage — the State-responsibility question (Topic 4, case 23).

4. The arguments of the parties

(a) The United Kingdom
  • The Channel is an international highway. A strait connecting two parts of the high seas, and used by the shipping of many nations, may not be closed by the coastal State; the passage of warships in time of peace is a right; and Albania's insistence on prior authorisation was unlawful.
  • Albania is responsible for the mines. The mines could not have been laid without Albanian knowledge; the minefield was laid by persons under Albanian territorial control; Albania's refusal to permit an inquiry, and its repudiation of the Court's order of 9 April 1949? (the interim measures order of 31 January 1948 requiring Albania to ensure no further mines); the evidence, including the discovery of the mine cable and the absence of any protest at the time of laying, established knowledge and responsibility. The United Kingdom also relied on the destruction of the Saumarez and Volage, which justified its claim to compensation.
  • Operation Retail was justified. The United Kingdom argued that the purpose was not to enforce a claim against Albania but to obtain evidence and to make the channel safe; that Albania had failed to clear the channel or to conduct any inquiry; and that necessity and self-preservation excused the operation, at least in part.
  • The passage of 22 October was lawful. The United Kingdom's purpose was to exercise a right of passage in a channel whose safety the United Kingdom had been assured of by sweeping operations in 1944–45.
(b) Albania
  • The Channel is not an international strait. It was of secondary importance and used almost exclusively for local traffic between Albanian and Greek ports; it was not a necessary route between two parts of the high seas; and at the material time the Adriatic was not, in the circumstances, a route of international navigation.
  • Passage of warships requires prior authorisation. Albania insisted that foreign warships may not pass through its territorial waters without a special request and permission, and that the passage of 22 October was a deliberate demonstration of force (the British squadron had passed with crews at action stations, and the purpose was to test Albania's attitude and to show force).
  • Albania was not responsible for the mines. Albania had no knowledge of the minefield; the mines had been laid by another State (the evidence pointing to those who laid them in the aftermath of the Greek civil conflict); a State is not responsible for acts on its territory of which it has no knowledge; and the United Kingdom's evidence was circumstantial.
  • Operation Retail is unlawful. The minesweeping was carried out against Albania's express protest, by a large naval force, in Albanian territorial waters, without Albanian consent, and without authority from any international organisation — a plain violation of sovereignty; and the United Kingdom's claim of "necessity" cannot justify the use of force in another State's territory.

5. The decision

(a) First question — the passage of 22 October 1946 and the law of straits

The Court held that the North Corfu Channel was a strait used for international navigation, and stated the governing principle in the passage that Part III of UNCLOS now codifies:

"It is generally recognised and in accordance with international custom that States in time of peace have a right to send their warships through straits used for international navigation between two parts of the high seas without the previous authorization of a coastal State, provided that the passage is innocent. Unless otherwise prescribed in an international convention, there is no right for a coastal State to prohibit such passage through straits in time of peace." (p. 28)

The Court rejected the Albanian test of traffic volume: "the decisive criterion is rather its geographical situation as connecting two parts of the high seas and the fact of its being used for international navigation." The Channel satisfied that test: it was "a useful route for international maritime traffic"; the fact that it was navigable only with difficulty, or that Greece and Albania were its principal users, was irrelevant; the Court observed that the strait "ought not to be... dependent... upon the volume of traffic".1

The Court then addressed what the coastal State may do: in the exceptional circumstances of the case — a state of tension between Albania and Greece — Albania "would have been justified in issuing regulations in respect of the passage of warships through the Strait, but not in prohibiting such passage or in subjecting it to the requirement of special authorization".

The manner of the passage. Albania's complaint that the passage had been abusive because it took place with crews at action stations and for a demonstrative purpose was rejected. The Court found that the intention of the squadron had been "not only to test Albania's attitude, but at the same time to demonstrate such force that she would abstain from firing again" on British ships passing through the Channel; but that having regard "to all the circumstances", it could not be held that these measures — the presence of four warships in a narrow channel, with crews at action stations and guns trained — converted a lawful exercise of the right of passage into an abuse of it, particularly as the ships were passing through waters which the United Kingdom had every reason to believe were safe. The conclusion is standardly cited for the proposition that the burden of proving that a passage is abusive lies upon the coastal State, and that innocent passage is not lost merely because the passage has a political or demonstrative dimension. (The judges were divided on this question, and the separate opinions contain the fullest discussion.)

(b) Second question — Operation Retail

The Court had no doubt. The United Kingdom admitted that the operation was carried out against Albania's clearly expressed wish, that it had not been authorised by any international mine-clearance organisation, and that it could not be justified as the exercise of a right of innocent passage: "in principle, international law does not allow a State to assemble a large number of warships in the territorial waters of another State and to carry out minesweeping in those waters." The Court rejected the plea of extreme urgency and held:

"Between independent States, respect for territorial sovereignty is an essential foundation of international relations. The Court recognizes that the Albanian Government's complete failure to carry out its duties after the explosions, and the dilatory nature of its diplomatic notes, are extenuating circumstances for the action of the United Kingdom Government. But to ensure respect for international law, of which it is the organ, the Court must declare that the action of the British Navy constituted a violation of Albanian sovereignty. This declaration is in accordance with the request made by Albania through her Counsel, and is in itself appropriate satisfaction." (p. 35)

(c) The findings on the mines, and the compensation

On the first question the Court found, by a majority, that the minefield could not have been laid without Albania's knowledge, that Albania had failed in its duty to notify the existence of mines in its territorial waters, that the failure involved Albania's international responsibility, and that the United Kingdom was entitled to compensation. In the second phase, by the judgment of 15 December 1949, the Court assessed the compensation at £843,947, after deducting the value of the salvaged ships and allowing for Albania's counterclaim. Albania did not pay; the question of the enforcement of the judgment and of Albania's eventual payment (through the 1992 agreement and the sums recovered from Albanian gold) illustrates the reality of enforcement in international law.

6. The principles evolved

  1. The definition of an international strait is geographical and functional, not quantitative. A strait connecting two parts of the high seas and used for international navigation is a strait used for international navigation, whatever the volume of traffic. The rule is now Article 37 of UNCLOS, and it is applied to the Straits of Malacca, Hormuz, Bab-el-Mandeb, Gibraltar, Dover and the Turkish Straits.
  2. Non-suspendable passage in straits in time of peace. A coastal State may not prohibit the passage of ships — including warships — through such a strait, nor subject it to prior authorisation; it may regulate. The modern equivalent is transit passage (Arts. 37–44), which may not be suspended (Art. 44); and where transit passage does not apply, non-suspendable innocent passage (Art. 45).
  3. The right of innocent passage is available to warships, and the burden of proving abuse is on the coastal State. The judgment is the leading authority against the "prior authorisation" school, and it supports the "notification only" position which India adopts in section 4(2) of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976.
  4. A State's sovereignty over a strait is qualified but not extinguished. The coastal State retains sovereignty over the waters of the strait, its bed and subsoil, and its airspace (now Art. 34(1)), and may regulate navigation, but may not use its sovereignty to close the route.
  5. Self-help and the use of force are unlawful, even against a wrongdoer. Operation Retail is the classic illustration: even though Albania had failed in its duties and had obstructed the inquiry, the United Kingdom's unilateral naval action in Albanian waters was a violation of sovereignty. The principle now appears in Article 2(4) of the Charter and in the law of State responsibility (countermeasures may not involve the use of force: Art. 50(1)(a) ILC).
  6. A declaration of wrongfulness can be appropriate satisfaction. The remedy chosen was not compensation but a judicial declaration — the ancestor of Article 37 ILC and of the modern practice of "declaratory relief" (Topic 4, §4.4 D).
  7. Responsibility for omission and proof by circumstantial evidence. The Topic-4 half of the case: a State is responsible for failing to warn of a danger in its territorial waters, and knowledge may be established by inferences of fact from the exclusive control of the area, though that control "neither involves prima facie responsibility nor shifts the burden of proof".

7. The examination angle

How the case is used in papers
  • Direct question: "State the facts and the decision in the Corfu Channel case" (an old but recurring form; the case is item 1 in the list of cases in the older question papers). Answer in the order: the Channel and its use; the two incidents of 1946; the two questions in the Special Agreement; the straits holding with the quotation; the Operation Retail holding with the quotation; and the compensation of £843,947.
  • Straits and innocent passage: the case is the textual ancestor of Articles 37 to 45, and is cited with Article 45 in any answer on whether a coastal State may suspend passage in a strait.
  • Warships' passage: the case is the leading authority that warships enjoy the right of passage and that the coastal State bears the burden of showing abuse; it is the natural companion to the 1989 US–USSR Uniform Interpretation and to India's section 4(2).
  • State responsibility (Topic 4): the same judgment is case 23 there, for omission, circumstantial evidence, satisfaction and the prohibition of self-help; the examiner may set the case under either topic, and the answer must be pitched accordingly.
  • The modern afterlife: the Corfu Channel is today the boundary between Greece and Albania; the two States signed a maritime delimitation agreement in 2009, which was declared unconstitutional by the Albanian Constitutional Court in 2010, and the delimitation therefore remains outstanding, with reported negotiations continuing — a reminder that a judgment settling the law of straits does not settle the boundary between the two States.
  • The sentences to quote: "the decisive criterion is rather its geographical situation as connecting two parts of the high seas and the fact of its being used for international navigation"; and "between independent States, respect for territorial sovereignty is an essential foundation of international relations".

1 The judgment's discussion of the Corfu Channel's use includes the Court's observation that a strait may be an international highway even if it is not the only route, and even if its navigation is difficult or confined to smaller vessels: the test is the connection of two parts of the high seas, not the convenience of the route. The point recurs in the modern law of straits in the debate whether a "route of similar convenience" exists within Article 36 — a question that has been raised about the Straits of Malacca and Singapore, the Lombok Strait and the Sunda Strait.

Case 32 · North Sea Continental Shelf Cases (Federal Republic of Germany / Denmark; Federal Republic of Germany / Netherlands)

North Sea Continental Shelf Cases (Federal Republic of Germany/Denmark; Federal Republic of Germany/Netherlands)

International Court of Justice, Judgment of 20 February 1969, I.C.J. Reports 1969, p. 3 · Bench: President Bustamante y Rivero; Vice-President Koretsky; Judges Fitzmaurice, Tanaka, Jessup, Morelli, Padilla Nervo, Forster, Gros, Ammoun, Bengzon, Petrén and Lachs; together with the judges ad hoc chosen by the Parties · Prescribed in the DU case material, Topic 5

Provenance. The case material prints the judgment with the facts, the arguments and the reasoning on the customary-law question and on the principles of delimitation; the account below follows the case material and the judgment, and adds the treatment of the case in S.K. Verma, Chapter 14, and the later use of the judgment in Articles 74 and 83 of UNCLOS.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

The syllabus places the North Sea cases under head 5.2, and they belong there for two reasons that every answer must separate. First, they are the source of the modern law of continental-shelf delimitation: the proposition that delimitation is to be effected "by agreement in accordance with equitable principles, and taking account of all the relevant circumstances", the rejection of equidistance as an automatic rule, the identification of the factors that govern the equitable result (configuration of the coasts, natural prolongation, proportionality, unity of deposits), and the rejection of the "just and equitable share" theory — all of which the 1982 Convention absorbed in the phrase "in order to achieve an equitable solution" in Articles 74(1) and 83(1). Second, and just as important for a law-of-the-sea paper, the case contains the International Court's most complete treatment of how a treaty provision becomes customary international law: the requirements of a "fundamentally norm-creating" character, of extensive and uniform State practice including the specially affected States, and of opinio juris — the two-element test restated in the judgment in the line that runs from the Lotus to Nicaragua. It is the case, in other words, that answers the question "is Article 6 of the 1958 Convention, or Article 83 of UNCLOS, binding on States that are not parties?" — and it answers it with the analytical care that made the ILC's rules on the continental shelf reservations-eligible in the first place.

2. Facts

(a) The geography that generated the dispute. The North Sea is a semi-enclosed sea bounded by Norway in the north-east, Denmark in the east, the Federal Republic of Germany, the Netherlands and Belgium in the south, and the United Kingdom in the west. Its southern part, where the three States in the case face one another, has a distinctive shape: the coasts of the Netherlands and Denmark are, in the language of the case, convex — they bulge seawards, the Dutch coast running north-north-west and the Danish coast (Jutland) running north; the German coast between them is concave — it recedes inland, and its coastline is much shorter in relation to the sea area in front of it. The practical consequence is easy to see: a line of equidistance drawn between the Dutch and German coasts and between the German and Danish coasts, and then between the Netherlands and Denmark, produces a wedge or triangle in front of the German coast in which the seaward extension of Germany's coast is confined between two converging lines — the very "cut-off" effect that Bangladesh would later invoke in the Bay of Bengal (cases 35 and 36).

(b) The claims and the agreements. Following the Truman Proclamation of 1945 and the practice of the 1950s, Denmark, the Netherlands and the Federal Republic had all granted concessions and licences on their respective shelves. Denmark and the Netherlands concluded an agreement between themselves delimiting their common shelf on the equidistance principle; Germany had not, and objected that the equidistance line between it and each of its neighbours would leave it with an unreasonably small area. Partial agreements were concluded between Germany and the Netherlands (the Ems estuary) and between Germany and Denmark (the coast off the Danish–German frontier, including the area of the island of Sylt known as the List); but the main delimitations remained unsettled, and the dispute was submitted to the Court by two Special Agreements of 2 February 1967.

(c) The 1958 Convention and Germany's position. Both Denmark and the Netherlands were parties to the Geneva Convention on the Continental Shelf, 1958, Article 6 of which prescribes equidistance with special circumstances; the Federal Republic of Germany had signed but not ratified the Convention — largely because of the reservation regime in its Article 12, which allowed States to make reservations to Articles 1 to 3, and because it did not accept the equidistance rule as applied to its concave coast. The Parties therefore asked the Court, in terms, to decide (i) what principles and rules of international law are applicable to the delimitation of the areas of the continental shelf appertaining to each of them, and (ii) whether Article 6 of the 1958 Convention was binding on the Federal Republic by virtue of its own force or through the operation of customary law.

3. The issues

  1. Whether Article 6 of the 1958 Convention applied to the delimitation between the Federal Republic and its neighbours, either as a treaty provision binding on Germany (it was not a party) or as a rule of customary international law by which Germany was bound.
  2. Whether the equidistance method is a rule of customary international law in the delimitation of the continental shelf between adjacent States, or whether the governing standard is a set of equitable principles applied in the light of the relevant circumstances.
  3. What the underlying principle of entitlement to the shelf is — natural prolongation, occupation, or proximity — and how it governs delimitation.
  4. What factors the parties must take into account in negotiating a delimitation, and how proportionality, configuration of coasts and the unity of deposits operate.
  5. Whether a party may claim a "just and equitable share" of the shelf as a matter of distributive justice.

4. The arguments of the parties

(a) The Federal Republic of Germany
  • Equidistance is not the law. Article 6 of the 1958 Convention binds only parties; Germany is not one; and the rule has not become customary international law, because it lacks the uniformity of practice and the acceptance that custom requires.
  • The justification for a shelf claim is natural prolongation. The shelf belongs to a State because it is the natural extension of its landmass; delimitation must therefore give each State "all those portions of the continental shelf that constitute a natural prolongation of its land territory into and under the sea", without encroaching on the prolongation of the other State.
  • Equidistance is unjust on a concave coast. Its application to the southern North Sea would cut off the seaward extension of the German coast, compress it between the Dutch and Danish lines, and produce an area for Germany quite out of proportion to the length of its coastline — a result that no equitable principle could countenance.
  • The relevant circumstances include the configuration of the coasts, the physical and geological structure, the length of the coastlines, natural resources and the unity of deposits. Within the area of overlap, the parties should divide in agreed proportions, or share, or enter into a régime of joint exploitation — a proposition the Court endorsed.
(b) Denmark and the Netherlands
  • Article 6 is binding on Germany in any event, because the equidistance rule is a logical corollary of the equality of States (which forbids one State to be given a larger share than another simply because of the shape of its coast) and because Article 6 was declaratory of customary law when it was adopted in 1958 and has since been accepted in practice.
  • Special circumstances provide the necessary flexibility. Article 6 is not a rigid rule; Article 6(2) requires the application of equitable principles "to the extent that special circumstances permit"; the Court should apply the equidistance-special circumstances rule, not invent a new standard.
  • Certainty is a value in itself. Equidistance produces predictable boundaries, and predictability is what the law of the sea needs for the exploitation of resources; a discretionary "equitable principles" test would produce uncertainty and litigation.
  • "Just and equitable share" is not a legal standard. The Court should reject Germany's claim to a distributive approach, because delimitation determines entitlements rather than dividing a common fund.

5. The decision

(a) The customary-law question. The Court held that Article 6 was not applicable to Germany as a treaty (it was not a party, and the Parties' Special Agreements did not make it applicable), and then examined whether the equidistance rule had become customary law binding on Germany. It held that it had not, for four reasons:

  1. The norm-creating requirement. The provision must be "of a fundamentally norm-creating character such as could be regarded as forming the basis of a general rule of law"; a clause which is itself qualified — as Article 6 is by "special circumstances" and is coupled with a right of reservation — is not of that character.
  2. The effect of the reservation clause. The fact that Article 12 of the 1958 Convention permitted reservations to Articles 1 to 3, and that the article could be, and was, reserved by States, was "a definite indication" that the provision was not regarded as declaratory of pre-existing or customary law.
  3. The absence of the necessary practice, including that of the specially affected States. The Court required "a very widespread and representative participation in the convention", including "States whose interests were specially affected"; but the number of ratifications was small, and State practice, including the practice of States which had adopted equidistance in their legislation, was explicable on other grounds.
  4. The absence of opinio juris. The Court restated the two-element test: "not only must the acts concerned amount to a settled practice, but they must also be such, or be carried out in such a way, as to be evidence of a belief that this practice is rendered obligatory by the existence of a rule of law requiring it" — the requirement of opinio juris sive necessitatis, in the line derived from the Lotus judgment. Acts inspired by considerations of convenience or comity do not create custom.

(b) The principles of delimitation. Having held that neither treaty nor custom made equidistance obligatory, the Court laid down the applicable principles, and they are the lasting contribution of the case:

  • Agreement in accordance with equitable principles. "Delimitation is to be effected by agreement in accordance with equitable principles, and taking account of all the relevant circumstances"; and the parties were placed under an obligation to conduct genuine negotiations — "the parties are under an obligation to enter into negotiations with a view to arriving at an agreement, and not merely to go through a formal process of negotiation...; they are under an obligation so to conduct themselves that the negotiations are meaningful, which will not be the case when either of them insists upon its own position without contemplating any modification of it".
  • Natural prolongation as the foundation. The Court held that the shelf is the natural prolongation of the land territory, that the entitlement exists ipso jure and from the outset, and that "the land is the legal source of the power which a State may exercise over territorial extensions to seaward" — the principle universally cited (in the Court's later jurisprudence, and in the syllabus's class notes) as "the land dominates the sea".
  • The rejection of "just and equitable share". "The doctrine... that the delimitation... should be carried out on the basis of a 'just and equitable share'... is not... the applicable rule"; delimitation is not an apportionment of a fund but the determination of entitlements, and the Court warned of the "grave danger" of a distributive approach.
  • The factors to be taken into account. In the operative part (para. 101), the Court listed the relevant circumstances for the negotiations: (1) the general configuration of the coasts of the Parties and any special or unusual features; (2) the physical and geological structure and natural resources of the continental shelf areas involved, so far as known or readily ascertainable; and (3) the element of a reasonable degree of proportionality between the extent of the shelf appertaining to each State and the length of its coastline measured in the general direction of the coastline, account being taken of any other continental-shelf delimitation in the same region. The Court added that if the application of these principles left areas of overlap, they were to be divided by agreement or, failing agreement, equally, unless the parties agreed on joint jurisdiction or joint exploitation.
  • Equidistance remains available. The Court emphasised that equidistance is not unlawful and is a convenient method where it produces an equitable result — the proposition that underlies the modern three-stage methodology (the provisional equidistance line) and explains why the case is not, despite appearances, an anti-equidistance decision.

(c) The aftermath. The three States negotiated a boundary in the light of the judgment and concluded agreements in 1971 dividing the southern North Sea (the German–Dutch and German–Danish agreements of 1971), which resolved the dispute; the case's principles were then absorbed into the law through the negotiations at UNCLOS III and, ultimately, through the wording of Articles 74(1) and 83(1).

6. The principles evolved

  1. Delimitation by agreement on equitable principles, taking account of all relevant circumstances — the standard now in Arts. 74(1) and 83(1) ("in order to achieve an equitable solution").
  2. Equidistance is not a rule of customary law, nor automatically binding, but is a method that may be used where it produces an equitable result — the foundation of the modern practice of constructing a provisional equidistance line and then testing it.
  3. Natural prolongation and "the land dominates the sea": the shelf right exists ipso jure, is inherent in sovereignty over the land, and does not depend on occupation or proclamation (now Art. 77(3) UNCLOS).
  4. Proportionality: not a distributive rule, but a factor in assessing the equitableness of a line, measured by comparing the extent of shelf to the length of the coast "in the general direction of the coastline" — the ancestor of the modern disproportionality test (Stage 3).
  5. The concavity/cut-off problem: the case in which the geography of a coast deprives a State of its natural seaward projection, later applied in the Bay of Bengal (cases 35–36).
  6. Unity of deposits: the practical instruction to the parties to consider joint exploitation of a single geological structure — the ancestor of modern unitisation agreements and of Article 142 UNCLOS for the Area.
  7. The two-element test of customary international law (settled practice plus opinio juris), and the requirement that practice include the States specially affected — the test applied in Nicaragua (1986) and in every subsequent customary-law question.
  8. The duty to negotiate in good faith and meaningfully — a duty that recurs in Articles 74(2) and 83(2) and in the Gulf of Maine, Cameroon v. Nigeria and Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire cases.

7. The examination angle

How the case is used in papers
  • Direct question: "State the facts and the decision in the North Sea Continental Shelf cases" — the answer is the geography (convex/concave coasts), the Article 6 point, the four reasons why the equidistance rule did not become custom, the equitable principles, the factors in the operative part, and the rejection of "just and equitable share".
  • Delimitation question (head 5.2): the case is the starting point of any answer, because it is the source of the standard that Articles 74(1) and 83(1) now express — but it must be paired with the later cases that converted the "equitable principles" approach into the three-stage methodology (see §5.2 C).
  • Custom question: with Nicaragua (1986) and Lotus (1927), the case supplies the two-element test of custom and the requirement of participation by specially affected States — a favourite cross-topic question linking Topic 1 (sources) with Topic 5.
  • Natural prolongation question: the case, with Tunisia/Libya (case 33) and Libya/Malta (1985), traces the rise and the partial displacement of natural prolongation by the distance criterion; note the syllabus's own wording, "the Continental Shelf Cases".
  • The sentence to quote: "delimitation is to be effected by agreement in accordance with equitable principles, and taking account of all the relevant circumstances" — and, on the duty to negotiate, "they are under an obligation so to conduct themselves that the negotiations are meaningful".

Case 33 · Continental Shelf (Tunisia/Libyan Arab Jamahiriya)

Continental Shelf (Tunisia/Libyan Arab Jamahiriya)

International Court of Justice, Judgment of 24 February 1982, I.C.J. Reports 1982, p. 18 · Bench: President Waldock; with the Members of the Court and the judges ad hoc chosen by the Parties · Prescribed in the DU case material, Topic 5

Provenance. The case material prints the judgment; the account below follows the case material and the judgment, and adds the treatment in S.K. Verma, Chapter 14, and the case's later use (with North Sea and Libya/Malta) in the modern law of delimitation.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Tunisia/Libya is the case that decided what "equity" means in maritime delimitation, and it is prescribed because it is the bridge between the North Sea cases and the modern law. Four of its propositions are examinable in their own right. First, the Court held that delimitation is governed by "equitable principles" but that those principles must be applied as law and not ex aequo et bono — the distinction between equity within the law and a discretionary redistribution outside it. Second, it held that "the result is predominant, the principles are subordinate" — that the law does not prescribe a prior list of principles whose mechanical application yields a boundary, but requires a result that is equitable on the facts. Third, it settled the status of the two competing justifications for the shelf — natural prolongation and distance — in the light of the newly adopted text of Article 76 of the 1982 Convention. Fourth, it worked out, for the first time, a catalogue of the circumstances that may and may not be taken into account, including the exclusion of economic factors and the narrow conditions in which oil concessions and historic rights can matter. Add a fifth, purely methodological, reason: the case is the classic illustration of a "composite" boundary — a line made up of segments derived from different geographical features, of which the most famous element is the half effect given to the Kerkennah Islands.

2. Facts

(a) The geography. Tunisia and Libya are neighbours on the southern shore of the Mediterranean. The Tunisian coast runs from the Algerian frontier in the west, eastwards to the Gulf of Gabès, and then turns northeastwards along the Sahel towards the Gulf of Hammamet and Cap Bon; the Kerkennah Islands lie offshore in the Gulf of Gabès, and the island of Jerba lies to the south-east, close to the Libyan frontier. The Libyan coast runs eastwards from the frontier near Ras Ajdir, through Tripoli and Misrata, to the Gulf of Sidra; the area to be delimited lies in the sea between the two coasts. The two coasts are adjacent, and the shelf in front of them is part of a single broad margin extending northwards towards the Mediterranean's central basin and, beyond it, towards Italy and Malta — a fact that produced the third case in the trilogy, Libya/Malta (1985).

(b) The land boundary and the "Ras Ajdir line". The land frontier between Tunisia and Libya runs inland from the sea at Ras Ajdir, and its direction (fixed by the 1910 Franco-Ottoman arrangement between the Beylik of Tunis and the Ottoman Empire, and confirmed in later practice) is roughly south-eastwards from the coast; its prolongation seawards was one of the lines relied on in argument, and the Court examined it as the starting point of the maritime boundary.

(c) The dispute and the Special Agreement. Oil was discovered in the Gulf of Gabès in the 1970s — the Bouri field being the best known — and both States granted concessions and licences which overlapped; there were protests, a period of tension (including the confrontation at sea in 1976 which produced an exchange of notes), and unsuccessful negotiations. By a Special Agreement of 10 June 1977, the two States asked the Court "to clarify the principles and rules of international law which may be applied for the delimitation" of the continental shelf between them and "to take account of equitable principles and the relevant circumstances which characterize the area, as well as the recent trends admitted at the Third Conference on the Law of the Sea", and then to specify the practical method for applying those principles so as to enable the experts of the two States to delimit the boundary without difficulty; and, in the alternative, to draw the boundary itself. The Court, finding that the parties had not agreed on the method, drew the line itself.

3. The issues

  1. What are the principles and rules applicable to the delimitation of the continental shelf between two States with adjacent coasts, given that the 1958 Convention bound both States, that the 1982 Convention had been adopted (though not in force), and that the Special Agreement referred to "equitable principles and the relevant circumstances"?
  2. What is the role of natural prolongation after the adoption of the 200-mile régime and the new definition in Article 76 — and in particular, how is the "geological" shelf (to the edge of the margin) to be related to the "legal" shelf?
  3. Does equity in this context allow a court to reach a result outside the law (ex aequo et bono), or must it apply equity as part of the law?
  4. Which circumstances are relevant — the configuration and direction of the coasts; the Kerkennah Islands; the presence of oil wells and concessions; historic rights claimed by Tunisia in certain fisheries; the position of third States; the relative economic positions of the parties?
  5. Does the requirement of proportionality operate as a rule requiring a comparison of coastal lengths and shelf areas, or as a factor?

4. The arguments of the parties

(a) Tunisia
  • Natural prolongation, correctly understood, favours Tunisia. The shelf in the Gulf of Gabès is a prolongation of the Tunisian landmass, and the geological boundary between the Tunisian and Libyan structures is identifiable; Tunisia's coasts — including the Gulf of Gabès and the Kerkennah Islands — give it a seaward projection which terminates naturally along a line running from the coast at about "the parallel of the Gulf of Gabès" (Tunisia relied on a line described as the "45°" or "the north-easterly line", and on a "X–Y" line drawn from the coast at Ras Kaboudia to a point at sea).
  • The historic rights of Tunisian fishermen in the waters off the Libyan coast are a relevant circumstance and should be preserved.
  • Economic factors: Tunisia emphasised the disparity in the parties' resources and the dependence of its population in the south on the fisheries of the disputed area — an argument intended to bring the case within the "catastrophic repercussions" exception.
  • Proportionality: the boundary should reflect a reasonable degree of proportionality between the lengths of the coastlines and the areas of shelf.
  • Libya's "parallel" claim is unacceptable: Libya's reliance on a line running at 45° from Ras Ajdir was rejected by Tunisia, which also rejected the other geometric constructions advanced by Libya, including the prolongation of the land frontier and the "Ras Ajdir" and "parallel" lines.
(b) Libya
  • The natural prolongation of the land boundary governs. The boundary should begin at Ras Ajdir and run seawards in the direction of the land frontier — the "Ras Ajdir line" — because the boundary between the two States on land should be prolonged into the sea; the "Ras Ajdir line" was the only construction consistent with the geography and the parties' practice.
  • Equidistance should not be used. Libya argued that equidistance was neither applicable nor equitable; the method to be used should be a line drawn from the frontier and following the general direction of the coast, adjusted where the configuration so requires.
  • The Kerkennah Islands are a minor feature and should have no effect, or a very limited effect, on the boundary, because they lie close to the Tunisian coast and should not confer an extended projection.
  • Economic arguments are irrelevant; Libya denied that its own greater oil wealth was a relevant circumstance, and insisted that delimitation is a legal operation and not an economic redistribution.
  • Tunisia's "historic rights" claim (Tunisia claimed historic rights over certain fisheries off the Libyan coast) was contested: Libya argued that a sedentary-fisheries claim could not extend beyond the shelf's resources, and that the evidence did not establish an historic title opposable to Libya.

5. The decision

(a) The applicable law. The Court held that, both States being parties to the 1958 Convention, the delimitation was governed by Article 6 — but Article 6's "special circumstances" and its reference to equitable principles gave the rule the flexibility required; and the Court further held that the developments at UNCLOS III — the concept of the 200-mile exclusive economic zone, the definition of the shelf in Article 76 combining natural prolongation with distance, and the standard of "an equitable solution" — had to be taken into account as part of the applicable international law, because "the Court cannot ignore the evolution of the law" (the Special Agreement itself asked it to consider "the recent trends admitted at the Third Conference").

(b) Equity as law, not ex aequo et bono. The Court drew the crucial distinction, in the terms that every answer must reproduce:

"Equity as a legal concept is a direct emanation of the idea of justice. The Court whose task is by definition to administer justice is bound to apply it... [but] it is not a Court of equity... the application of equitable principles must be consistent with the relevant rules of international law... The Court, however, is not... entitled... to decide ex aequo et bono unless the parties so agree." And, in the famous formulation on method: "the result is predominant, the principles are subordinate" — the choice of the method must be determined by the need to arrive at an equitable result, and the principles are not a fixed set of rules to be applied mechanically. (Paras. 70–71)

(c) Natural prolongation after Article 76. The Court held that natural prolongation remained a fundamental principle — indeed that "the idea of natural prolongation... commands" the régime of the shelf — but that it was not the only criterion, and that it had to be understood in the light of the new definition: the shelf consists of the natural prolongation of the land territory to the outer edge of the continental margin, or to 200 nm, whichever is greater. It distinguished the "geological" prolongation (the structure of the seabed) from the legal doctrine: "the legal concept of the continental shelf... is not... simply... a geological concept"; and it warned that natural prolongation "does not necessarily imply a precise boundary" between two States whose shelves form a single continuous margin (paras. 41–48, 100).

(d) The relevant circumstances. The Court identified and graded the circumstances:

  • The configuration and direction of the coasts — the general change in the direction of the Tunisian coast at the Gulf of Gabès, and the relationship of the coastline to the area to be delimited, were relevant.
  • The Kerkennah Islands — the islands lie in the Gulf of Gabès, and the Court held that they could not be ignored but should be given only half effect in the construction of the boundary, so as not to allow a small island group to obtain for Tunisia a disproportionate projection of sea (the technique later contrasted with the enclaving of the Channel Islands in the Anglo-French Arbitration).
  • Historic rights — Tunisia's claim to historic rights over fisheries off the Libyan coast was examined and held not established for the purposes of the delimitation; but the Court confirmed that historic rights, if established, may be a relevant circumstance, and that Articles 1 and 2 of the 1958 Convention preserve existing rights (para. 100).
  • Economic factors — excluded, save in the extreme case: "It is... the relative economic position of the two States, and the possibility that a particular delimitation might have catastrophic repercussions for the economy or the livelihood of the population of one of them, that might be relevant" — the "catastrophic repercussions" exception (para. 107).
  • Oil wells and concessions — not determinative as such, "but the existence of an established practice (a modus vivendi)... may be taken into account". This is the seed of the modern treatment of oil practice as evidence of a tacit agreement, which failed on the facts in Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (2017) and in Somalia v. Kenya (2021).
  • Third States — the position of other States with rights in the same region (Malta, Italy, Algeria) was reserved: the boundary had to be drawn without prejudice to their entitlements (para. 81).
  • Proportionality — the Court treated proportionality as a requirement that the line should not produce a result that was "radically" or "grossly" out of proportion to the coastal lengths, not as a rule that measured the shelf by the coastline (the ancestor of Stage 3).

(e) The line drawn. The Court determined the boundary in the operative part of its judgment (para. 133) in the following shape: the delimitation should be effected by agreement in accordance with equitable principles taking account of all the relevant circumstances, and — the parties not having agreed on a method — the boundary was to consist of a first segment running from the point of intersection of the land boundary with the sea at Ras Ajdir in the direction of the prolongation of the land frontier (the "Ras Ajdir line", which the Court took as being oriented at about 26° east of north), continued by a segment reflecting the change in the general direction of the Tunisian coast and giving half effect to the Kerkennah Islands, and terminating at the outer limit of the area to be delimited in the direction of the parallel of latitude determined by the Court. The Court also specified that the parties should not extend their claims beyond the outer limit so fixed, and reserved the question of the shelf's limits vis-à-vis third States.

6. The principles evolved

  1. Equity as a method of law. The distinction between equity infra legem (the equitable application of legal rules) and ex aequo et bono decision-making; a court may not redistribute maritime space at its discretion, but must reach an equitable result by the application of the law.
  2. "The result is predominant, the principles are subordinate." The standard is the equitableness of the outcome, not the mechanical application of a listed set of principles; the method is chosen to serve the result.
  3. The primacy of the "equitable solution" standard — later written into Articles 74(1) and 83(1) of UNCLOS, whose wording the Court's reasoning anticipates.
  4. Natural prolongation and distance together. Natural prolongation remains a fundamental principle but is not the sole criterion; within 400 nm, distance becomes the controlling criterion for both the shelf and the EEZ (a proposition completed in Libya/Malta, 1985).
  5. The grading of relevant circumstances: geography (configuration of coasts, islands) is relevant; historic rights may be; economic factors and relative wealth are excluded save in the "catastrophic" case; oil wells and concessions are evidence, not law.
  6. The technique of partial effect. The half effect given to the Kerkennah Islands is one of the two standard techniques (the other being the enclave, used for the Channel Islands in the Anglo-French Arbitration) for dealing with islands that would otherwise distort a boundary; both are used in modern practice, and both appear in the discussion of St Martin's Island and the Bay of Bengal islets in cases 35 and 36.
  7. Composite boundaries. The Court's willingness to draw a boundary composed of differently-derived segments freed delimitation from the search for a single geometric formula, and made the modern practice possible.

7. The examination angle

How the case is used in papers
  • Direct question: "Discuss the principles of delimitation of the continental shelf laid down in the Tunisia/Libya case" — the answer is: the applicable law (Art. 6 of 1958 plus the 1982 text); equity as law; "the result is predominant"; natural prolongation versus distance; the relevant circumstances (coasts, Kerkennahs, historic rights, exclusion of economic factors, oil wells as evidence); proportionality; and the line drawn with the half effect to the Kerkennah Islands.
  • Paired with North Sea: the examiner's standard comparison — 1969 (equitable principles; natural prolongation fundamental; factors listed) with 1982 (equity as a method; result predominant; distance criterion emerging; circumstances graded).
  • Islands question: the half-effect technique is the authority to cite whenever the question is the effect of islands on a maritime boundary (compare St Martin's Island in case 35 and the Diua Damasciaca islets in case 36).
  • Economic factors question: the "catastrophic repercussions" test (para. 107) is the standard answer to "are the relative economic positions of the parties relevant to delimitation?" — with Kenya's unsuccessful fisheries and security arguments in Somalia v. Kenya (2021) as the modern illustration.
  • The sentences to quote: "the result is predominant, the principles are subordinate" (paras. 70–71); and, on the facts, that a delimitation involving a "radical" disproportion would be inequitable — the ancestor of the modern disproportionality test.
