Case 34 · Republic of Italy v. Union of India (2013) 4 SCC 721 — the Enrica Lexie case

Republic of Italy v. Union of India & Ors., (2013) 4 SCC 721 (Supreme Court of India) — and its aftermath in the ITLOS, the PCA and the Supreme Court

Writ Petition (Civil) No. 135 of 2012 and connected matters; judgment of a Bench of Chief Justice Altamas Kabir and Justice J. Chelameswar, delivered 18 January 2013; reported as (2013) 4 SCC 721 · Followed by: The "Enrica Lexie" Incident (Italy v. India), ITLOS Case No. 24, Order on Provisional Measures of 24 August 2015; The Italian Republic v. The Republic of India, PCA Case No. 2015-28, Award under Annex VII of UNCLOS of 21 May 2020; and the Supreme Court's order of 15 June 2021 closing the Indian criminal proceedings and accepting ₹10 crore as compensation

Provenance. This is the case the syllabus image highlights in green, and the only Indian case in Topic 5. The judgment is in the DU case material (Law of the Sea, Topic 5) and is treated below from the judgment itself, from the court record, and from the published award and orders that followed it. The postscript (parts (b) to (e) below) is added because a modern answer that stops at 2013 is incomplete: the Supreme Court's judgment of 2013 was followed by eight years of international litigation which ended, in part, by reversing its practical consequence.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Italy v. Union of India is in the syllabus because it is the one case in the course in which every zone of the Convention is made to work at once, in an Indian courtroom, on Indian facts. Five reasons make it indispensable.

  1. It is the Indian case on the contiguous zone and the EEZ. The incident occurred about 20.5 nautical miles off the Kerala coast — outside the 12-mile territorial sea, inside the 24-mile contiguous zone, and inside India's EEZ. The whole case therefore turns on what India may do in those zones, and the judgment contains the Supreme Court's most careful account of sections 3 to 7 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976 and of the notification of 27 August 1981.
  2. It is the Indian case on Article 297 of the Constitution and on Indian federalism at sea. The Court had to decide whether the Union or the State of Kerala could investigate and prosecute, and it held that the maritime zones are the Union's, that the State's police powers do not extend to them, and that the Union alone could act — a constitutional holding that survives even though the criminal proceedings were later quashed.
  3. It is the case on the "sovereignty versus sovereign rights" distinction. Both judgments insist that in the EEZ India has "sovereign rights" for specified purposes and not sovereignty, and that the Union's legislative and executive competence in the maritime zones flows from that distinction and from Article 297.
  4. It is the case on Article 97 and the law of collision and navigation incidents. The Court held that Article 97 of UNCLOS — penal jurisdiction in matters of collision — has no application to the EEZ, because Part VII of the Convention applies only to the high seas (Article 86), and because the incident was not a collision.
  5. It is the case that shows the limits of the courtroom in international law. The Supreme Court's judgment was suspended by an ITLOS order, and its result was displaced by an arbitral award which held that the marines were entitled to immunity; India's remedy became compensation rather than prosecution. For a course in public international law, that trajectory — domestic court, international tribunal, settlement — is the most instructive part of the case.

2. Facts

(a) The vessels. MV Enrica Lexie was an Italian-flagged oil tanker of about 57,000 tonnes, owned by Dolphin Tankers Ltd. and chartered by Fratelli d'Amico, sailing from Singapore to Egypt. On board, in addition to the crew, was a detachment of Italian Navy personnel ("the marò") deployed for anti-piracy protection — a measure adopted after the Somali piracy attacks of 2008–2011, and one which the private security industry and several States had adopted at the time. St. Antony (also spelt St Antony) was an Indian fishing boat, registered in Kerala, crewed by fishermen from the Kollam–Neendakara area, engaged in fishing in the Arabian Sea.

(b) The incident: 15 February 2012. At about 16:30 hours, at a position roughly 20.5 nautical miles off the Kerala coast — the figure used throughout the litigation, and the figure that places the incident outside the territorial sea — the Italian marines on the Enrica Lexie fired upon the St. Antony. Two fishermen, Ajeesh Pink and Gelastine, were killed. The Italian account was that the marines believed the fishing boat to be a pirate vessel approaching the tanker; the Indian account was that the St. Antony was engaged in fishing, unarmed, and that no attempt was made to verify its character. The master of the Enrica Lexie reported the incident to the Italian authorities, and later sailed to India; both vessels came to be in Indian hands within days.

(c) The Indian proceedings. The Kerala Police registered FIR No. 2 of 2012 (the first information report mentioned in the Supreme Court's later orders) and arrested the two marines on 19 February 2012; the Enrica Lexie was detained off Kochi. The marines — Massimiliano Latorre and Salvatore Girone — were produced before the Chief Judicial Magistrate, Kollam, and remanded; the Sessions Court and the Kerala High Court dealt with bail and with the question of jurisdiction. Italy protested at every stage: it asserted the exclusive jurisdiction of the flag State, the immunity of its naval personnel, and the absence of Indian jurisdiction over an incident on the high seas involving two foreign vessels.

(d) The Kerala High Court, 29 May 2012. The Kerala High Court dismissed the petitions filed by Italy and by the marines, holding (in substance) that the incident fell within the reach of the Indian Penal Code by reason of the notification of 1981 extending the Code to the EEZ, and that India could exercise jurisdiction; the marines' request to quash the FIR was refused. Appeals followed to the Supreme Court, which granted leave and heard the matter in the closing months of 2012, the Union of India supporting the position that the Union — and not the State of Kerala — was competent to investigate.

(e) The interim arrangements. Even while the jurisdictional question was pending, the Supreme Court dealt with the humanitarian and practical dimensions: it permitted the marines to remain in India subject to conditions; it later permitted Latorre to travel to Italy for medical treatment after he suffered a stroke in 2014, and then permitted Girone to remain in Italy during the pendency of the arbitration (2016), the two orders being made in the shadow of the ITLOS provisional measures described below.

3. The issues

  1. Whether India had jurisdiction at all — and whether, if so, the jurisdiction belonged to the Union of India or to the State of Kerala — to investigate, try and punish the two Italian marines for the killing of two Indian fishermen at a point about 20.5 nautical miles from the Kerala coast.
  2. Whether the incident occurred in India's territorial sea, contiguous zone, exclusive economic zone or on the high seas, and what the consequences of that classification are for the application of the Indian Penal Code and the Code of Criminal Procedure.
  3. Whether the notification SO 67/E of 27 August 1981 and section 188A CrPC (as introduced by the notification) validly extended the Indian penal law to the EEZ and the continental shelf, and whether they are within the legislative competence of the Union having regard to Article 297 of the Constitution and the Union and State Lists.
  4. Whether Article 97 of UNCLOS (penal jurisdiction in matters of collision or any other incident of navigation) ousted Indian jurisdiction on account of the fact that the Enrica Lexie was an Italian-flagged vessel.
  5. Whether the Italian marines enjoyed immunity from Indian criminal jurisdiction by reason of their status as members of the armed forces of a foreign State acting in the course of their duties — the issue the Supreme Court left open, and the issue on which the case was ultimately decided in Italy's favour by the arbitral tribunal in 2020.
  6. Whether the incident was an act of piracy or a response to piracy within the meaning of Articles 100 to 107 of UNCLOS, as Italy argued and as the marines' defence was originally framed.

4. The arguments of the parties

(a) Italy (and the marines)
  • Exclusive flag-State jurisdiction. The Enrica Lexie was an Italian-flagged vessel on the high seas (or, alternatively, in India's EEZ, where the high-seas freedoms of navigation survive under Article 58). Under Article 92 UNCLOS — the rule of the Lotus case — a ship on the high seas is subject to the exclusive jurisdiction of the flag State, and India has no criminal jurisdiction over acts committed on board the Italian vessel.
  • Article 97 UNCLOS. The incident was an "incident of navigation" occurring in the course of the vessel's navigation; penal or disciplinary proceedings may be instituted only before the authorities of the flag State (Italy) or of the State of which the person is a national (Italy). Article 97 is a limitation on the jurisdiction of every State other than the flag State, and it applies to the Enrica Lexie incident.
  • The marines were performing official duties. They were naval personnel on an anti-piracy mission authorised by Italian law; they fired in the reasonable belief that the vessel was a pirate, in the protection of the ship and its crew; and they are entitled, as organs of the Italian State, to immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of another State in respect of acts performed in the exercise of their official functions. Italy relied on the functional immunity of State officials under customary international law.
  • The Indian notifications cannot bind Italy. The extension of the Indian Penal Code to the EEZ by a notification of 1981 is an exercise of Indian prescriptive jurisdiction which cannot, consistently with the Convention, be applied to acts committed on board a foreign-flagged vessel on the high seas or in the EEZ; and the notification cannot create jurisdiction which the Convention denies.
  • Even on India's own case, the State of Kerala had no jurisdiction, and the Union's position (that only it could proceed) demonstrated the incoherence of India's assertions.
  • Humanitarian and humanitarian-law considerations: the marines had been detained for nearly a year; the conditions in Indian custody, the length of the proceedings and the possibility of a sentence of death (a point raised in the arbitration) were invoked in support of the requests for release, immunity and, ultimately, the arbitration.
(b) The Union of India
  • India has jurisdiction over the contiguous zone and the EEZ. The incident occurred at about 20.5 nm — within the contiguous zone, where Article 33 of UNCLOS and section 5 of the Maritime Zones Act give India the power to prevent and punish infringement of its laws; and within the EEZ, where India has sovereign rights and jurisdiction under Article 56 and section 7, and where its penal laws have been extended by the notification of 1981.
  • The Indian Penal Code applies by reason of the notification and section 188A CrPC. The notification extended the Penal Code, the Code of Criminal Procedure, the Civil Procedure Code and the Customs Act to the EEZ and the continental shelf; section 188A CrPC provided for a special court in the coastal district, with the prior approval of the Central Government for investigations; and the two deaths were caused on an Indian fishing vessel by persons who, on India's case, fired without justification.
  • The killing took effect on an Indian vessel. The victims and their vessel were Indian; the consequences of the act were felt on the St. Antony; the act's effects within the territory (the deaths of Indian fishermen and the loss of an Indian boat) are a lawful basis for the exercise of Indian jurisdiction, including on the "objective territoriality" principle.
  • Article 97 does not apply. There was no collision and no "incident of navigation" of the Enrica Lexie in the sense of Article 97; and Article 97 in any event applies in the context of Part VII (high seas), which, by Article 86, does not extend to the EEZ.
  • The Union, not the State, must investigate and prosecute. Under Article 297 of the Constitution the maritime zones vest in the Union; the State of Kerala has no legislative or executive competence over them; and the investigation by the State police would therefore be without jurisdiction. (This was the practical issue on which the Union and the State diverged, and the Court resolved it in the Union's favour.)
  • The immunity claim is not established. India argued that the marines' conduct was not shown to be an exercise of official functions entitled to immunity; that immunity does not extend to acts of unjustified violence against private persons; and that the question of immunity, if it arose at all, was to be determined in the criminal trial.
(c) The State of Kerala
  • The State has jurisdiction to investigate crimes committed within its police jurisdiction: the deaths occurred on a boat registered in Kerala, whose crew were Kerala residents; the State's police registered the FIR; and the State's fishing and police laws applied.
  • The Union's notification scheme is directory, and prior approval does not exclude parallel or state investigation; alternatively, the State may investigate and the Union may prosecute.

5. The decision

(a) The judgment of Chief Justice Kabir

The Chief Justice's judgment (with which the Bench disposed of the writ petitions) held that:

  1. The incident occurred in India's contiguous zone. The Court accepted the position — pleaded by the Union and supported by the record — that the St. Antony was at a distance of about 20.5 nautical miles from the coast, and held that the point of occurrence was therefore within India's contiguous zone (12 to 24 nm) and within its exclusive economic zone (up to 200 nm).
  2. Article 297 of the Constitution and sections 3 to 7 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976 confer on the Union the competence over the maritime zones: sovereignty over the territorial sea and the historic waters; the right in the contiguous zone to prevent and punish infringement of customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary laws and (under section 5(2)(a)) matters relating to the security of India; and sovereign rights in the EEZ and on the continental shelf. Section 7(1) confers the sovereign rights; sections 7(2) to (7) provide for jurisdiction over artificial islands, marine scientific research and the marine environment, and for the extension of laws by notification.
  3. The notification SO 67/E of 27 August 1981 is valid. It extended the Indian Penal Code, the Code of Criminal Procedure (with new section 188A, providing for a designated court in the coastal district and for the prior approval of the Central Government before any investigation), the Civil Procedure Code and the Customs Act to the contiguous zone, the continental shelf and the EEZ; and by reason of it, the Penal Code applies to the acts alleged.
  4. The State of Kerala had no jurisdiction to investigate or prosecute: the maritime zones are the Union's; the State's police powers do not extend beyond the land territory and its territorial waters as the Act and the Constitution allocate them; and the investigation conducted by the Kerala police was accordingly not competent. (The Court's conclusion on this point was the practical core of the judgment: the case had to be investigated and prosecuted by the Union.)
  5. Article 97 of UNCLOS has no application. The Court held that Article 97, which concerns penal jurisdiction in matters of collision or other incidents of navigation on the high seas, applies within Part VII of the Convention, and that Part VII's scope (Article 86) covers the high seas and not the exclusive economic zone; the incident occurred within the EEZ and not the high seas; and Article 97 was therefore inapplicable. (The Court also observed that the incident was not, in any event, a collision or an incident of navigation of the kind Article 97 contemplates.)
  6. The question of immunity was not decided. The Court did not accept Italy's claim of immunity as a ground for quashing the proceedings; it noted that the existence or otherwise of immunity would have to be determined on evidence, and in the trial.
  7. Piracy was left to the trial. Italy's reliance on Articles 100 to 107 (the supposed belief that the St. Antony was a pirate vessel) was a defence on the merits of the charges, not a jurisdictional ground; the Court declined to decide whether the marines' belief was reasonable, and left the issue to the trial court.
  8. A special court was to be constituted (as contemplated by section 188A CrPC) and the Union was to investigate; the Court accordingly directed the Union of India to set up the machinery and transferred the investigation into the hands of the Union.
(b) The concurring judgment of Justice Chelameswar

Justice Chelameswar's separate judgment supplied the doctrinal analysis that makes the case a favourite for essays, and it should be cited separately in an answer.

  • Article 245(2) of the Constitution and extraterritorial legislation. The Union's power to legislate for the maritime zones rests on Article 245(2) (laws with extraterritorial operation) read with Article 297 and with the Union List entries on shipping, admiralty and defences; the notification of 1981 is an exercise of that power.
  • The territoriality principle of criminal jurisdiction is not absolute. Justice Chelameswar surveyed the accepted bases of jurisdiction in international law — territorial (subjective and objective), nationality (active personality), passive personality, protective and universal — and concluded that India's claim rested principally on the objective territorial principle: an act committed outside the territory which produces its effects within it may be tried as if it were committed within the territory. The deaths occurred on board an Indian vessel whose crew were Indian nationals; the effects were felt in India.
  • "Sovereignty is not 'given' but only asserted." The judgment contains the observation that the Union's competence over the maritime zones is not a grant from the Convention but an attribute of sovereignty asserted by India consistently with international law — a formulation frequently quoted in Indian maritime writing.
  • The distinction between sovereignty and sovereign rights in the EEZ. Justice Chelameswar emphasised that what India possesses in the EEZ is a bundle of sovereign rights (resource and jurisdictional) and not sovereignty; the distinction matters because the legal basis of the Union's power to extend the penal law is different in kind from its power over the territorial sea, and because the State's claim to competence cannot be supported by treating the EEZ as a continuation of state territory.
  • Aban Loyd Chiles Offshore Ltd. distinguished. The judgment distinguished the earlier decision of the Court on the levy of customs duty on oil rigs in the EEZ (Aban Loyd Chiles Offshore Ltd. v. Union of India, decided in 2008, in which the Court had treated the EEZ and the continental shelf as part of India for the purposes of the customs legislation), explaining that the case turned on the language of the Customs Act and its definition of "India", and not on the general question whether India has sovereignty over the EEZ; the distinction is valuable in any answer on the tax and customs régime of the EEZ.
  • Article 97 again. Justice Chelameswar reached the same conclusion as the Chief Justice on Article 97: it applies only to the high seas and not to the EEZ.
  • The Lotus comparison and the limits of the flag-State rule. The judgment acknowledged the flag-State rule of Article 92 but pointed to its exceptions — piracy, the right of visit, hot pursuit and the special cases recognised in international law — as showing that the rule is not absolute, and that an act which produces its harmful effects on the vessel of another State is within the jurisdiction of that other State.
(c) The operative outcome in 2013

The Court disposed of the petitions without quashing the FIR: it held that India had jurisdiction; that the Union of India, and not the State of Kerala, was competent to investigate and prosecute; that the penal law applied by virtue of the 1981 notification; that Article 97 did not apply; and that the questions of immunity and of piracy were for the trial court. Directions were issued for the setting up of the special court and for the conduct of the investigation by the Union. It is this judgment — India asserting jurisdiction over an incident in its contiguous zone/EEZ, and the Union displacing the State — that the syllabus highlights.

6. The principles evolved

  1. India's maritime zones are Union territory for constitutional purposes. Article 297 of the Constitution, sections 3 to 7 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976, and the notification of 1981 combine to place the territorial sea, the contiguous zone, the EEZ and the continental shelf under the Union's exclusive competence; a State of the Union has no police or legislative jurisdiction there.
  2. The contiguous zone is a zone of functional enforcement competence. Section 5 of the 1976 Act and Article 33 UNCLOS give India the power to prevent and punish the infringement of its customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary laws — and, on the Indian statute, of matters relating to the security of India — committed in its territory or territorial sea; the incident at 20.5 nm was within that zone.
  3. Sovereign rights, not sovereignty, in the EEZ. Both judgments adopt the Convention's distinction and use it to explain the legal foundation of the Union's power to extend its penal laws to the EEZ.
  4. Article 97 UNCLOS does not apply to the EEZ. Part VII (high seas) is defined by Article 86 to exclude the EEZ; the EEZ has its own régime in Part V; and Article 97's protection of the master and crew from foreign penal jurisdiction therefore does not extend to the EEZ. (The arbitral tribunal later reached a different but compatible conclusion: it held Article 97 inapplicable because the incident was not an incident of navigation of the Enrica Lexie.)
  5. Extraterritorial legislation by the Union is constitutionally possible (Art. 245(2)), and the objective territorial principle of criminal jurisdiction is a lawful basis for Indian jurisdiction where the effects of an act committed outside territory are felt on an Indian vessel or by Indian nationals.
  6. Immunity is a question of substance, not of jurisdiction in the abstract. The Supreme Court left the immunity question to be determined on evidence; the arbitral tribunal later held that the marines, as State officials acting in their official capacity, were entitled to immunity, with the consequence that India could not exercise criminal jurisdiction over them at all — the completion of the case's doctrinal arc, and the reason the syllabus's highlight on this case is a highlight on the art of the possible in maritime litigation.

7. The postscript — the eight years after the judgment (2013–2021)

(a) The ITLOS order of 24 August 2015 (ITLOS Case No. 24)

Italy invoked the arbitration provisions of Part XV of UNCLOS and, pending the constitution of the Annex VII arbitral tribunal, applied to the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea for provisional measures under Article 290(5). By an order of 24 August 2015 (the hearing having been held in Hamburg on 10–11 August 2015), the Tribunal, by 15 votes to 6, prescribed the following provisional measure:

"Italy and India shall both suspend all court proceedings and shall refrain from initiating new ones which might aggravate or extend the dispute submitted to the Annex VII arbitral tribunal or might jeopardise or prejudice the carrying out of any decision which the arbitral tribunal may render."

The Tribunal found that a dispute existed between the Parties concerning the interpretation or application of UNCLOS; that the Annex VII tribunal would prima facie have jurisdiction; that Article 290 applies independently of any domestic proceedings, so that the exhaustion of local remedies was not a precondition to the prescription of provisional measures; and that the balance of the parties' interests required the suspension of proceedings in both States. India complied: the Supreme Court stayed proceedings by its order of 26 August 2015 (modified on 2 September 2015, recording the parties' agreement), and the marines' liberty was progressively relaxed, Latorre remaining in Italy on medical grounds and Girone being permitted to remain in Italy pending the award.

(b) The PCA award of 21 May 2020 (PCA Case No. 2015-28)

The Annex VII tribunal, sitting at the Permanent Court of Arbitration with Judge Vladimir Golitsyn as President, delivered its Award on 21 May 2020. Its principal holdings, in the order of their importance for an Indian answer:

  • Jurisdiction (4–1): the Tribunal had jurisdiction over the dispute, which concerned the interpretation and application of UNCLOS, including questions of immunity and of the exercise of jurisdiction by India.
  • Immunity (3–2): the Italian marines "are entitled to immunity in relation to the acts that they committed during the incident of 15 February 2012", being State officials of Italy acting in their official capacity as officers of the Italian Navy; and India is precluded from exercising its jurisdiction over them. India must take the necessary steps to cease exercising its criminal jurisdiction and to ensure that the marines are not subject to further proceedings in India.
  • India's counter-claim against Italy succeeded (unanimously): by firing on the St. Antony and by ordering it to change course, Italy had interfered with the freedom of navigation of an Indian vessel on the high seas, in breach of Articles 87(1)(a) and 90 of UNCLOS.
  • Satisfaction and compensation: that finding of breach constituted adequate satisfaction for the injury to India's non-material interests; and India was entitled to compensation for the loss of life, physical harm, material damage to property (including to the St. Antony) and moral harm suffered by the captain and crew of the St. Antony.
  • Article 97: the Tribunal held that Article 97 of UNCLOS (penal jurisdiction in matters of collision or any other incident of navigation) was not applicable, because the incident did not constitute an incident of navigation of the Enrica Lexie; the provision therefore neither conferred jurisdiction on India nor took it away — a holding that preserved India's formal position on the article while the immunity finding disposed of the case.
  • Piracy and abuse of rights: Italy's claims that India had violated Articles 100 (cooperation in the repression of piracy) and 300 (good faith and abuse of rights) were rejected; India had not breached Articles 87(1)(a) or 92 (the flag-State jurisdiction of the Enrica Lexie), and Italy's submissions on those points failed.
  • Italy's request to resume its investigation: Italy having undertaken during the proceedings to resume its criminal investigation into the incident, the Tribunal recorded that undertaking.
(c) The Supreme Court's order of 15 June 2021 — the case ends

After negotiations between the two Governments (in which the families of the deceased were consulted and the Government of Kerala participated), Italy agreed to pay a further ₹10 crore as compensation — in addition to the ex-gratia payments already made (reported as about ₹2.17 crore to the families) — and deposited the sum with the Supreme Court Registry. The Union of India applied to the Supreme Court for directions to give effect to the Award, and on 15 June 2021 a Bench of Justices Indira Banerjee and M. R. Shah:

  • recorded that India had accepted the arbitral award and that the Tribunal had found that India was entitled to compensation but could not prosecute the marines because of the immunity enjoyed by them as State officials;
  • held the amount of ₹10 crore, over and above the ex-gratia compensation already paid, to be a "reasonable amount of compensation" and in the interest of the heirs of the deceased fishermen;
  • quashed FIR No. 2 of 2012 and all proceedings arising out of it, in exercise of the powers under Article 142 of the Constitution, thereby closing all proceedings in India, civil and criminal;
  • directed that the Republic of Italy "shall resume its criminal investigation in the events of 15 February 2012", and that the Union of India, the Republic of Italy and the State of Kerala "shall cooperate with each other in pursuit of that investigation";
  • directed the transfer of the ₹10 crore to the High Court of Kerala, with the Chief Justice to nominate a Judge to pass orders for disbursement and investment — the scheme being ₹4 crore to the heirs of each deceased fisherman and ₹2 crore to the owner of the St. Antony — so that the compensation would be received by the heirs and not frittered away.
(d) What the case now means
Table 5.17 — The Enrica Lexie dispute: position taken, and outcome, on each issue
IssueIndia's positionOutcome
Locus of the incidentContiguous zone / EEZ, 20.5 nm off KeralaAccepted as the factual location; the Tribunal treated the incident as having occurred on the high seas for the purposes of the freedom of navigation of the St. Antony and of Article 87.
Indian criminal jurisdictionYes — by the 1981 notification, the IPC and CrPC apply to the EEZ; Union competent, State notThe Supreme Court so held in 2013; the arbitral tribunal held in 2020 that India is precluded from exercising that jurisdiction over the marines, on the ground of immunity.
Article 97 UNCLOSNot applicable — Part VII excludes the EEZSupreme Court: not applicable to the EEZ. Arbitral tribunal: not applicable because there was no "incident of navigation" — but on a different reasoning.
Piracy / Articles 100, 300Italy relied on the marines' anti-piracy function; India denied an abuse of rights by itselfItaly's claims against India rejected; the piracy question was not decisive; the Tribunal found no breach by India of Arts. 87(1)(a) or 92 in relation to the Enrica Lexie.
Immunity of the marinesNot established; a matter for the trial courtHeld (3–2) that the marines were entitled to immunity as State officials acting in their official capacity; India precluded from prosecuting.
India's remedyProsecution and punishmentCompensation: ₹10 crore (over and above the ex-gratia payments), distributed ₹4 crore each to the two families and ₹2 crore to the boat owner, with the cases quashed under Article 142 and Italy directed to resume its own investigation.
Freedom of navigation of the St. AntonyIndia's counter-claimSucceeded: Italy breached Articles 87(1)(a) and 90; the finding constituted adequate satisfaction for India's non-material injury.

The four lessons. First, the case shows that jurisdiction and immunity are different questions: India could have jurisdiction in principle under its own law and under the Convention's zones, and yet be precluded from exercising it because of the immunity of the persons concerned. Second, it shows how quickly a domestic criminal case can become an international dispute, and how the provisional-measures jurisdiction of ITLOS can stop a domestic trial in its tracks. Third, it shows the value of India's counter-claim: the finding that Italy breached Articles 87(1)(a) and 90 is a durable holding about the freedom of navigation of Indian-flagged vessels. Fourth, it shows the limits of the syllabus's 2013 judgment as an authority: for any question about the modern law, the answer must carry the story forward to the 2015 order, the 2020 award and the 2021 order — which is why the syllabus's green highlight on this case is best honoured by treating it as a single eight-year litigation rather than a single judgment.

8. The examination angle

Fig. 5.9 — The Enrica Lexie timeline: domestic court, ITLOS, Annex VII arbitration, and settlement
Model answer structure — case 34 (a 20-mark question)
  1. Facts (two minutes): the vessels, the incident of 15 February 2012 at 20.5 nm, the deaths, the FIR and the arrests, the Kerala High Court's refusal, the appeals.
  2. Issues: jurisdiction (whether India had jurisdiction at all, and whether the competence lay with the Union or with the State), the applicable zone, the validity of the 1981 notification, Article 97, immunity, piracy.
  3. Arguments: Italy (flag State, Art. 92, Art. 97, immunity, the notification cannot bind it) and India (Art. 33 and s. 5; Art. 56 and s. 7; the notification; the objective territorial principle; Union competence; Art. 97 inapplicable to the EEZ).
  4. Decision: Kabir CJI's points (contiguous zone; Art. 297; ss. 3–7; the 1981 notification; State of Kerala excluded; Art. 97; immunity and piracy left to trial; special court) and Chelameswar J's supplement (Art. 245(2); the bases of jurisdiction; "sovereignty is not given but only asserted"; sovereign rights in the EEZ; Aban Loyd distinguished).
  5. The principles: the Union's maritime competence; the contiguous zone and the EEZ; sovereign rights vs sovereignty; Art. 97's scope; extraterritorial legislation; the distinction between jurisdiction and immunity.
  6. The postscript (this is what distinguishes a first-class answer): ITLOS 2015 (Art. 290(5); suspension of proceedings); the PCA award of 2020 (immunity 3–2; Art. 87(1)(a) and 90 breached by Italy; Art. 97 inapplicable; compensation); the Supreme Court's order of 15 June 2021 (₹10 crore; FIR quashed under Art. 142; Italy to resume investigation).
  7. Conclusion: the case as the Indian illustration of the law of maritime zones, and as an example of the interaction of domestic courts with ITLOS and Annex VII arbitration.

Case 35 · Bay of Bengal Maritime Boundary Arbitration (Bangladesh v. India), PCA, 2014

In the Matter of the Bay of Bengal Maritime Boundary Arbitration between Bangladesh and India

Arbitration under Annex VII of UNCLOS; Permanent Court of Arbitration, The Hague; Award of 7 July 2014 (unanimous) · Tribunal presided over by Judge Rüdiger Wolfrum, with Dr. Pemmaraju Sreenivasa Rao (India's nominee) among its five members · Registered as PCA Case No. 2012-16 · Companion case: Dispute concerning delimitation of the maritime boundary between Bangladesh and Myanmar in the Bay of Bengal, ITLOS Case No. 16, Judgment of 14 March 2012

Provenance — read this first. Case 35 is listed in the syllabus image but is not printed in the DU case material: the case material's law-of-the-sea block runs from Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries to Somalia v. Kenya and does not include a body of text for this award. The account below is therefore taken from the Award of 7 July 2014 itself (and from the summary of the Award published by the Permanent Court of Arbitration), from the summary of the Award in the Reports of International Arbitral Awards (United Nations), and from the concurring and dissenting opinion of Dr. P. S. Rao — all of which are primary or near-primary sources. Where a figure or a holding is drawn from the published summary rather than from the operative text, the text below says so. This is the one case in Topic 5 whose treatment depends on sources outside the course material, and a candidate who writes the case from the Award and says so will not be penalised; a candidate who invents paragraphs will be.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

The Bay of Bengal award is in the syllabus because it is India's own delimitation case, decided while this course has been taught, and because it is the case in which the law of delimitation met the facts of the Indian subcontinent. Four reasons make it indispensable.

  1. It is the case of the concave coast. The North Sea cases had raised the problem of a concave coast in the abstract; the Bay of Bengal is the problem in its extreme form. The Bay is a semi-enclosed sea whose northern shore is concave as a whole: Bangladesh sits at the head of it, enclosed between India to the west and Myanmar to the east, and a strict equidistance line drawn between the three coasts cuts off Bangladesh's seaward projection. The Tribunal's answer — that concavity is a relevant circumstance only to the extent that it produces a cut-off effect, and that the line must therefore be adjusted — is the most authoritative modern treatment of the problem.
  2. It is the case of the continental shelf beyond 200 nm. Together with the ITLOS judgment of 2012 in Bangladesh/Myanmar, the award establishes that an Annex VII tribunal (and ITLOS) may delimit the shelf beyond 200 nm before the CLCS has made its recommendations, and that the delimitation is without prejudice to the Commission's scientific examination.
  3. It is the case that created the "grey area". The award's delimitation produces an area lying beyond 200 nm from Bangladesh's coast but within 200 nm of India's, in which the seabed and subsoil belong to Bangladesh while the water column belongs to India. The concept, its justification and its consequences all come from this award, and it is now a standard topic in every advanced discussion of the law.
  4. It is India's example of compliance. India lost about three quarters of the disputed area and accepted the award; the case is therefore the standard Indian illustration of the peaceful settlement of maritime disputes under Part XV, and of the proposition that accepting an adverse award is itself a matter of legal policy.

2. Facts

(a) The geography. The Bay of Bengal is bounded by Bangladesh and India in the north and west, Myanmar in the east, and Sri Lanka in the south-west; it opens into the Indian Ocean through the channels between Sri Lanka and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands and through the straits to the south-east. The northern coast is dominated by the delta of the Ganges–Brahmaputra, which is unstable and constantly shifting; the Indian coast runs south-westwards from the Sundarbans through West Bengal, Odisha, Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu; the Bangladeshi coast is short and faces south; Myanmar's coast runs down the eastern shore; and the Indian Andaman and Nicobar Islands lie across the mouth of the bay, a factor of great importance to the geometry.

(b) The land boundary and the island that came and went. The India–Bangladesh land boundary in the estuary of the Haribhanga (the river that forms the boundary near the coast) was fixed by the Radcliffe Award of 1947. In the 1970s a small island emerged in the mouth of the Haribhanga — claimed by India as New Moore and by Bangladesh as South Talpatti — and it became a symbol of the wider dispute. The island has since been submerged by the sea. The Tribunal had to decide whether to treat it, or a low-tide elevation near it, as relevant to the starting point of the maritime boundary, and it declined to do so.

(c) The dispute and the arbitration. Bangladesh and India had negotiated maritime boundaries since the 1970s without agreement. Bangladesh — a "zone-locked" State whose entitlements would be squeezed between India and Myanmar — concluded that its only realistic route was adjudication, and after the ITLOS judgment of 14 March 2012 in its dispute with Myanmar, it served on India a notice of arbitration under Article 287 and Annex VII of UNCLOS on 8 October 2009 (the proceedings were formally constituted later). India agreed to arbitration; the Tribunal was constituted with Judge Rüdiger Wolfrum as President and Dr. P. S. Rao as India's nominee; and the Award was delivered on 7 July 2014, unanimously as to the operative result.

(d) The ITLOS precedent in the companion case. In Bangladesh/Myanmar (ITLOS Case No. 16, judgment of 14 March 2012) the Tribunal had delimited the boundary between Bangladesh and Myanmar by the equidistance/relevant circumstances method: it gave St Martin's Island full effect in the territorial sea but no effect in the delimitation of the EEZ and continental shelf; it accepted that the concavity of the Bay produced a cut-off for Bangladesh and adjusted the provisional line by a geodetic line with an azimuth of about 215°; it held geology and geomorphology irrelevant to the delimitation of a single maritime boundary, and it delimited the shelf beyond 200 nm; and in the proportionality test it found coastal and area ratios (about 1:1.42 and 1:1.54) not significantly disproportionate. The Bay of Bengal Tribunal followed the same methodology, and extended its adjusted line until it met the Bangladesh–Myanmar line fixed by ITLOS, thereby in effect determining the tripoint between the three States.

3. The issues

  1. Whether the Tribunal had jurisdiction, and in particular whether it could delimit the continental shelf beyond 200 nm given that the CLCS had not yet made recommendations on the outer limits.
  2. Where the land boundary terminus is — the starting point of the maritime boundary — on a proper construction of the Radcliffe Award of 1947; and what the consequences are if the terminus does not lie on the equidistance line.
  3. How the territorial sea is to be delimited under Article 15, and in particular whether the concavity of the Bay is a "special circumstance" requiring a departure from the median line in the 12-mile belt.
  4. How the EEZ and the continental shelf within 200 nm are to be delimited under Articles 74 and 83: which method (equidistance/relevant circumstances or angle-bisector); what base points may be used; which circumstances are relevant (concavity, the instability of the deltaic coast, the fishing dependency of the population, the position of the Andaman and Nicobar Islands); and whether any adjustment is required.
  5. How the boundary is to be drawn beyond 200 nm, where the entitlements of the parties are determined by natural prolongation and are subject to third-State rights.
  6. What is to be done about the "grey area" which the delimitation produces.

4. The arguments of the parties

(a) India
  • Equidistance/relevant circumstances is the appropriate method. India's case was orthodox: construct a provisional equidistance line from appropriate base points; adjust it only if relevant circumstances so require; and test the result for proportionality — and, on the facts, no adjustment was necessary.
  • Bangladesh's angle-bisector method should be rejected because it depends on subjective assessments of the "overall direction" of coasts and would leave the boundary to be drawn by impression rather than by objective geometry.
  • No relevant circumstances exist which would justify moving the line: the concavity of the Bay of Bengal is a feature of the region and not a special circumstance affecting the parties; the instability of the coast is a physical phenomenon, not a legal circumstance; the economic and fishing dependency arguments are inadmissible (following Tunisia/Libya); and the presence of the Indian Andaman and Nicobar Islands in the bay is not a reason to shift the line.
  • The land boundary terminus should be located on the basis of the Radcliffe Award, the map annexed to it being illustrative and the text authoritative; and the boundary should begin at the point where the land boundary meets the sea.
  • Base points on low-tide elevations are impermissible; the boundary must be constructed from the coasts of the parties.
  • On the shelf beyond 200 nm, India argued (as did other States in the cases before it) that the Tribunal should not delimit the shelf beyond 200 nm until the CLCS had considered the outer limits — a jurisdictional and prudential objection.
(b) Bangladesh
  • The angle-bisector method is appropriate because of the concavity of the Bay: the overall direction of the coasts should be identified and the angle bisected, so as to avoid the cut-off which equidistance produces at the head of a concave bay.
  • The concavity of the Bay of Bengal and the instability of the deltaic coast are relevant circumstances, and both call for an adjustment of the line in Bangladesh's favour; Bangladesh also relied on the dependence of its coastal population on fisheries, and on the "Bengal depositional system" as a geomorphological feature.
  • The land boundary terminus should be determined from the Radcliffe Award's reference to the midstream of the main channel of the Haribhanga, located as it was at the time of the Award, on the basis of contemporaneous charts; and the Award's text referred to the Haribhanga alone, not to the combined estuary of the Haribhanga and the Raimangal.
  • Bangladesh's entitlements beyond 200 nm should be recognised, and the Tribunal should delimit the shelf beyond 200 nm; Bangladesh relied on the ITLOS judgment in Bangladesh/Myanmar for the proposition that the Tribunal could do so.
  • The grey area problem should not prevent Bangladesh from receiving its full entitlement to the shelf, even if that meant a divergence between the seabed boundary and the water-column boundary.

5. The decision

(a) Jurisdiction, including the shelf beyond 200 nm

The Tribunal held that it had jurisdiction to delimit the maritime boundary, including the continental shelf beyond 200 nm: both parties were parties to UNCLOS; their dispute concerned the interpretation and application of the Convention; and the absence of CLCS recommendations did not deprive the Tribunal of jurisdiction, because the question of entitlement could be determined on the scientific evidence before it, and because its delimitation would be without prejudice to the Commission's consideration of the outer limits. In reaching that conclusion the Tribunal followed and reinforced the ITLOS judgment of 2012 in Bangladesh/Myanmar, and it noted that international jurisprudence on the delimitation of the shelf beyond 200 nm was then "rather limited".

(b) The land boundary terminus and the start of the maritime boundary

The Tribunal construed the Radcliffe Award of 1947 and held that the boundary in the estuary followed the midstream of the main channel of the Haribhanga as it was in 1947, that the Award referred to the Haribhanga alone and could not be read as referring to the combined waters of the Haribhanga and the Raimangal, and that the map annexed to the Award was illustrative rather than conclusive (the Award's text being authoritative). The Tribunal used the 1931 printing of the relevant Admiralty chart to locate the midstream, and fixed the land boundary terminus accordingly. It declined to use the disappeared island (New Moore/South Talpatti) or the low-tide elevation in the estuary as a base point.

(c) The territorial sea

Article 15 was applied in its ordinary sense. The Tribunal constructed a median line from base points on the coasts of the parties, expressly declining to rely on base points located on low-tide elevations (alternative base points on the coastline being preferred in delimitation between States, notwithstanding that Article 13 permits low-tide elevations to serve as baselines for measuring the breadth of the territorial sea). It held that the concavity of the Bay of Bengal did not produce a cut-off in the narrow 12-mile belt sufficient to constitute a "special circumstance" requiring a departure from the median line.

One special circumstance was, however, decisive: the Tribunal found that the land boundary terminus, as fixed by the Radcliffe Award, does not lie on the median line. It held that this circumstance required the boundary to take the form of a 12-nautical-mile-long geodetic line running from the land boundary terminus in a generally southerly direction until it met the median line (at approximately 21°26′43.6″N, 89°10′59.2″E, in the coordinates recorded in the Award). The territorial-sea boundary therefore has a short segment which is neither a median line nor a parallel, and the EEZ/shelf boundary begins where that segment joins the median line.

(d) The EEZ and the continental shelf within 200 nm
  1. Method. The Tribunal held that the "equidistance/relevant circumstances" method is preferable "unless there are factors which make the application of the equidistance method inappropriate" (a formulation drawn from the Court's case-law), that no such factors existed here — both parties having been able to identify base points permitting the construction of a provisional equidistance line — and that it would therefore apply equidistance with possible adjustment, rejecting Bangladesh's angle-bisector approach. It applied the same method to the EEZ and to the continental shelf, within and beyond 200 nm, so as to produce a single maritime boundary.
  2. Relevant coasts and base points. The Tribunal identified the relevant coasts — those whose seaward projections overlap with the projections of the other party's coast — and took base points on the coastlines of the parties, refusing to use low-tide elevations and refusing to allow the disappeared island to influence the line.
  3. Relevant circumstances: concavity and cut-off. The Tribunal held that the concavity of the Bay of Bengal was a relevant circumstance because it produced a cut-off effect on the seaward projections of Bangladesh's coast. This is the holding that carries the case into the textbooks: concavity matters not as a feature of geography but as a mechanism that deprives a State of the projection its coast would otherwise generate.
  4. Circumstances rejected. The Tribunal refused to treat the instability of the deltaic coast as a relevant circumstance, holding that what matters is the coastline at the time of delimitation and that future changes in the coastline cannot alter a maritime boundary once delimited — the holding which becomes important in the sea-level-rise debate (§5.1.6 E). It also rejected Bangladesh's arguments from the dependence of its population on fisheries and from the geomorphology of the "Bengal depositional system" as circumstances justifying an adjustment.
  5. The adjustment. Having found a cut-off, the Tribunal adjusted the provisional line. It took as the point at which the cut-off began a point on the provisional equidistance line (identified in the Award as Delimitation Point 3) and drew from that point a geodetic line with an initial azimuth of 177° 30′ 00″, which it extended seaward until it met the maritime boundary between Bangladesh and Myanmar as determined by the ITLOS judgment of 2012. (The choice of that azimuth, and the extent of the adjustment, were the principal points of criticism in the concurring and dissenting opinion of Dr. P. S. Rao, who proposed a line with a greater adjustment.)
  6. The disproportionality test. The Tribunal then compared the ratio of the relevant maritime areas attributed to each party with the ratio of their relevant coastal lengths. On the figures given in the separate opinion, the relevant coasts stood in a ratio of about 1 : 1.92 (Bangladesh : India) while the majority's adjusted line allocated the relevant area in a ratio of about 1 : 2.81; the Tribunal held that the result did not produce a significant disproportion requiring a further alteration of the line. It emphasised that proportionality is not a mathematical exercise which allocates maritime areas as a function of coastal length.
(e) The continental shelf beyond 200 nm, and the grey area

The Tribunal delimited the shelf beyond 200 nm by extending the same adjusted line to the outer limits of the parties' entitlements, in the area where the entitlements of the two parties overlap and where third-State rights are not affected. Because Bangladesh's entitlement to the shelf beyond 200 nm is based on natural prolongation whereas India's EEZ entitlement in the same area rests on distance from the Indian coast, the line produced an area lying beyond 200 nm from Bangladesh's coast but within 200 nm of India's, and east of the boundary. That is the grey area. The Tribunal's holding, in the words of its own summary, is that "within the grey area, Bangladesh has sovereign rights with respect to the continental shelf, including the seabed and subsoil, while India has sovereign rights with respect to the exclusive economic zone, notably those with respect to the superjacent waters", that "beyond 200 nm from Bangladesh's coast, it has an entitlement only to the seabed and its subsoil pursuant to the legal régime governing the continental shelf", and that within the area beyond 200 nm from Bangladesh's coast but within the limits of India's EEZ the boundary delimits the parties' rights with respect to the seabed and subsoil only, and does not otherwise limit India's rights with respect to the water column. The Tribunal delimited no EEZ boundary in the grey area — there is nothing to delimit, because Bangladesh has no EEZ entitlement there — and it required the parties to consult in order to ensure that their activities in the grey area do not interfere with each other's rights.

(f) The result

The Award was unanimous in its operative result (with a concurring and dissenting opinion from Dr. Rao on the extent of the adjustment). Bangladesh was awarded about 19,467 sq km of the approximately 25,602 sq km in dispute; India retained about 6,135 sq km, including the area around the former New Moore/South Talpatti Island; Bangladesh's maritime entitlement was extended to a full 200 nm and to a substantial area of continental shelf beyond 200 nm (reported as extending to about 354 nm from the Chittagong coast); and the boundary was extended until it met the Bangladesh–Myanmar line, in effect fixing the tripoint of the three States. Both Governments welcomed the award and described it as a "win-win" outcome; the Award is binding on the parties and is not subject to appeal.

6. The principles evolved

  1. Equidistance/relevant circumstances is the preferred method, to be displaced only where factors make equidistance inappropriate; the angle-bisector method (and other geometric alternatives) is not preferred because it depends on subjective assessments.
  2. Concavity is relevant only through its cut-off effect — the principal contribution of the case to the law of delimitation, and the completion of the reasoning begun in the North Sea cases.
  3. Coastal instability, fisheries dependence and geomorphology are not relevant circumstances for a single maritime boundary; the coastline at the time of delimitation governs, and later changes do not alter the boundary — a holding that now anchors the sea-level-rise debate.
  4. Low-tide elevations cannot be used as base points in delimitation between States, even though they may serve as baselines for measuring the breadth of the territorial sea under Article 13.
  5. The land boundary terminus governs the start of the maritime boundary, and where the terminus does not lie on the median line, that is a special circumstance justifying a connecting segment — the technique also used in Somalia v. Kenya (case 36), where the Court began its line with a short segment to a point 12 nm from the coast.
  6. The shelf beyond 200 nm may be delimited by an Annex VII tribunal, without waiting for the CLCS, where the entitlements are established on the evidence; the delimitation is without prejudice to the Commission's recommendations.
  7. The grey area is a legitimate consequence of the Convention's architecture: the shelf extends beyond 200 nm while the EEZ does not, and the two may be delimited differently, so that the seabed and the water column above it may belong to different States.
  8. Disproportionality is a negative test, applied by comparing the ratios of relevant coasts and relevant areas; significant disproportion is required to displace a line, and mathematical precision is not expected.

7. The examination angle

How to use case 35 in an answer
  1. For any question on delimitation (5.2): it is the best modern illustration of the three-stage methodology in an extreme geographical setting, and the only case in which the concavity argument has succeeded on the merits of a whole boundary since 1969.
  2. For a question on the continental shelf beyond 200 nm (5.1.3): the case (with Bangladesh/Myanmar) supplies the jurisdiction point, the role of the CLCS, and the "grey area".
  3. For a question on India's maritime boundaries (5.5): the award is the single most important Indian maritime event of the last two decades, and the standard Indian illustration of compliance with international adjudication.
  4. For a question on islands and base points (5.1.6): the treatment of the disappeared island and the refusal to use low-tide elevations as base points are direct authorities.
  5. For a question on sea-level rise: the Tribunal's holding that the coastline at the time of delimitation governs, and that later changes cannot alter the boundary, is the indispensable authority (with the caution that it says nothing about what happens to baselines that are not delimited).
  6. Caution for the examination: if the question asks you to "state the facts and decision", state that the case is an Annex VII arbitration under the PCA, that the Award is dated 7 July 2014, that the Tribunal was presided over by Judge Wolfrum, that the method was equidistance/relevant circumstances, that the concavity of the Bay produced a cut-off requiring adjustment at Delimitation Point 3 with a geodetic line at 177° 30′ 00″, and that the award produced the grey area — and note that the case is not printed in the case material, so that your answer is drawn from the Award itself.

↑ top

Case 36 · Maritime Delimitation in the Indian Ocean (Somalia v. Kenya), I.C.J. Reports 2021

Maritime Delimitation in the Indian Ocean (Federal Republic of Somalia v. Republic of Kenya)

International Court of Justice: Preliminary Objections, Judgment of 2 February 2017, I.C.J. Reports 2017, p. 3; Merits, Judgment of 12 October 2021 · Bench: President Donoghue (presiding); Vice-President Gevorgian; Judges Tomka, Abraham, Bennouna, Yusuf, Xue, Sebutinde, Bhandari, Robinson, Salam, Iwasawa and Nolte; Judge ad hoc Guillaume (chosen by Kenya) · Prescribed in the DU case material, Topic 5

Provenance. The case material prints the judgment of 12 October 2021, including its account of the 2017 preliminary objections; the account below follows the case material, the judgment and its published summary, and adds the subsequent developments noted at the end.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Somalia v. Kenya is the most recent case in Topic 5 and the one that shows the modern law of delimitation working as a system. It is in the syllabus for five reasons.

  1. It is the modern authority on acquiescence and tacit agreement in maritime boundaries. Kenya's central defence was that Somalia had accepted a boundary along the parallel of latitude by its silence and conduct over four decades. The Court's answer — that the threshold is high, that the claim and the conduct of the party asserting it must be clear and consistent, and that silence in the face of civil war and inconsistent domestic legislation does not produce a boundary — is the definitive treatment of the issue, and it belongs with Temple of Preah Vihear and Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire in any answer on acquiescence.
  2. It is the case on the three-stage methodology applied in full, including the calibration of an adjustment for a regional cut-off and the arithmetic of the disproportionality test: relevant coasts of 733 km (Somalia) and 511 km (Kenya), a ratio of 1 : 1.43; areas awarded of 92,389 and 120,455 sq km, a ratio of 1 : 1.30; no gross disproportion.
  3. It is the case on the base points: the Court took the median line for the territorial sea and the provisional equidistance line for the EEZ and shelf from base points on the solid mainland of each State, refusing to use the Diua Damasciaca islets or the low-tide elevation at Ras Kaambooni.
  4. It is the modern authority on the transitional-period duties of Articles 74(3) and 83(3), and on the effect of activities in an undelimited area: Kenya's seismic surveys, drilling, patrols and concession-granting did not violate Somalia's rights because the claims were made in good faith before the judgment, and the Court declined to award any reparation.
  5. It is a case about the Indian Ocean, decided while India's own extended-shelf claims in the same ocean are before the CLCS; the judgment's treatment of the shelf beyond 200 nm, of the CLCS's role, and of the "grey area" is directly relevant to the regional context in which India operates.

2. Facts

(a) The coast and the disputed area. Somalia's coastline runs for some 3,000 km from the Gulf of Aden down the Horn of Africa and then south-west along the Indian Ocean; Kenya's coast runs south-westwards from the Somali frontier at Kiunga towards Lamu, Malindi and Mombasa and then to Tanzania. The two coasts are adjacent, and their seaward projections overlap over a large area of the Indian Ocean — a triangle of roughly 100,000 sq km in which both States granted oil and gas concessions, and in which the Lamu Basin has been the focus of Kenya's offshore exploration.

(b) The competing lines. Kenya's position was that its maritime boundary with Somalia runs due east along the parallel of latitude 1° 39′ 43.2″ S, from the land boundary terminus at Primary Beacon 29 out to the outer limit of the continental shelf — a line which Kenya said followed from the 1927/1933 treaty arrangements between Italy (as the colonial power in Somalia) and the United Kingdom (as the colonial power in Kenya), from its own Presidential Proclamations of 28 February 1979 and 9 June 2005, from section 2(4) of its Territorial Waters Act, from the practice of the two States over four decades, and from Somalia's silence. Somalia's position was that the boundary should be drawn by the equidistance/relevant circumstances method — that is, by a line roughly perpendicular to the coast, running south-eastwards from the land boundary terminus — and that no boundary existed at the parallel.

(c) The 2009 Memorandum of Understanding. On 7 April 2009 the two Governments signed a Memorandum of Understanding whose purpose, as its title records, was "to grant to each other No-Objection in respect of submissions on the Outer Limits of the Continental Shelf beyond 200 Nautical Miles to the Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf". Kenya later relied on paragraph 6 of the MOU as creating a dispute-settlement procedure which required negotiation rather than litigation; the Court's Preliminary Objections judgment of 2017 held that the MOU's object and purpose was limited to the CLCS process and did not exclude the Court's jurisdiction.

(d) The proceedings. Somalia filed its Application on 28 August 2014, asking the Court to determine the complete course of the single maritime boundary between the two States in the Indian Ocean, including the continental shelf beyond 200 nm, and to find that Kenya had violated its international obligations by its activities in the disputed area, with reparation. Kenya raised preliminary objections on 7 October 2015, which the Court rejected by its judgment of 2 February 2017: it held that it had jurisdiction on the basis of the Parties' declarations under Article 36(2) of the Statute (Somalia's declaration of 1963, Kenya's of 1965), and that the Application was admissible, the MOU not being a bar.

(e) The hearings and Kenya's withdrawal. The hearings were scheduled for September 2019 and postponed at Kenya's request three times. In March 2021 Kenya informed the Court that it would not participate in the oral hearings, alleging procedural unfairness and bias, and stating that it sought to protect its sovereignty and territorial integrity; Kenya also said that it was withdrawing its recognition of the Court's compulsory jurisdiction. The Court proceeded in Kenya's absence, as Article 53 of the Statute permits, and delivered judgment on 12 October 2021.

3. The issues

  1. Whether there was already an agreed maritime boundary between the Parties at the parallel of latitude, by reason of an express agreement or of Somalia's acquiescence.
  2. If not, how the boundary should be delimited: the median line for the territorial sea (Art. 15) and the equidistance/relevant circumstances method for the EEZ and continental shelf (Arts. 74, 83), with the identification of the relevant coasts and base points.
  3. Whether any relevant circumstance required an adjustment of the provisional equidistance line — in particular the cut-off produced by the concave configuration of the East African coast (including Kenya's argument from regional practice, from its security needs and piracy, from its fisheries dependence, and from the oil concessions).
  4. Whether the delimitation beyond 200 nm should follow the same line, and what the position of the CLCS is.
  5. Whether Kenya had violated Somalia's sovereign rights by its activities in the disputed area, and whether it had breached the transitional-period obligations of Articles 74(3) and 83(3), with what consequences in reparation.

4. The arguments of the parties

(a) Somalia
  • No boundary exists. There was never an agreement with Kenya on the maritime boundary; the treaty arrangements Kenya relied on (1927/1933) concerned other matters; Somalia's Maritime Law of 1988 provided for a boundary "in a straight line towards the sea from the land as indicated on the enclosed charts"; and Somalia's conduct, including its CLCS submission and its protests, was inconsistent with acceptance of a parallel line.
  • Equidistance throughout. An unadjusted equidistance line achieves an equitable result; there is no serious cut-off; the concavity argument depends on including third-State coasts (Tanzania) and features (Pemba) which are irrelevant; Kenya's oil concessions, security concerns and fisheries claims are inadmissible circumstances.
  • Kenya's activities were unlawful. Somalia alleged that Kenya's seismic surveys, drilling, naval patrols and concession-granting in the disputed area violated Somalia's sovereignty, sovereign rights and jurisdiction, and its obligations under Articles 74(3) and 83(3), and claimed reparation.
  • The MOU is no bar: it was a no-objection agreement for the CLCS and contained no dispute-settlement procedure binding the Parties to negotiate to the exclusion of the Court; and in any event the Parties' subsequent conduct had superseded it.
(b) Kenya
  • There is an agreed boundary at the parallel. Kenya relied on the 1927/1933 treaty arrangements, its Presidential Proclamation of 1979 (and the further Proclamation of 2005), section 2(4) of its Territorial Waters Act, the practice of the two States in granting oil concessions along the parallel, naval patrols, fisheries enforcement and marine scientific research, and on Somalia's failure to protest over decades — acquiescence, it argued, established the boundary.
  • Somalia's capacity to protest is irrelevant: Kenya argued that Somalia had continued to participate in international relations and that its lack of an effective central government did not excuse silence.
  • Alternatively, the boundary should follow the parallel as an equitable solution, given the regional practice of latitudinal boundaries in the region, Kenya's security concerns (piracy and terrorism in the Lamu area), its economic dependence on the disputed area's fisheries, and the parties' oil-block practice.
  • The MOU required negotiation, and the Court should decline to exercise jurisdiction (the preliminary objections) or, alternatively, adjudge that the boundary follows the parallel.
  • No violation: Kenya contended that its activities were lawful, that they took place before the dispute crystallised in 2014, and that they could not engage its responsibility.

5. The decision

(a) Acquiescence: no agreed boundary (unanimous)

The Court held that the threshold for establishing a maritime boundary by acquiescence is high: it presupposes clear and consistent acceptance by the States concerned, or a course of conduct in which the other party's position was consistently maintained and called for a response. Applying that test, the Court found:

  • Kenya did not consistently claim a boundary at the parallel: differences existed between its 1979 Proclamation and other Kenyan domestic legislation (the Territorial Waters Act's provisions and their revisions in 1977), and between the 1979 and 2005 Proclamations; and the 2005 Proclamation was followed by direct communications with Somalia which did not rely on it.
  • The 1927/1933 treaty arrangements did not establish a maritime boundary; and Kenya's own reference to them in its Counter-Memorial did not make them one.
  • The Parties' practice between 1979 and 2014 — naval patrols, fisheries enforcement, marine scientific research, oil concessions — did not show clear and consistent acceptance by Somalia of a boundary at the parallel; oil concession practice was "likely a manifestation of caution" rather than recognition of a boundary, and "proof of the existence of a maritime boundary requires more than the demonstration of longstanding oil practice".
  • Somalia's absence of protest was explicable: from 1991 until at least 2009 Somalia lacked an effective central government, and it was reasonable for Somalia to understand that the boundary would be established by future agreement (the MOU of 2009 referred to an unsettled "dispute" over delimitation).
  • There was accordingly "no compelling evidence that Somalia has acquiesced to the maritime boundary claimed by Kenya", and no agreed maritime boundary at the parallel of latitude (unanimously). Nor was there a de facto boundary, so that Kenya's argument that the provisional equidistance line should be adjusted to coincide with it failed.
(b) The territorial sea (Article 15)

The Court fixed the starting point of the single maritime boundary at the land boundary terminus at Primary Beacon 29 (1° 39′ 43.2″ S) and drew the boundary of the territorial sea as a median line under Article 15, using base points on the solid mainland coasts of each State. It refused to use the Diua Damasciaca islets or the low-tide elevation at Ras Kaambooni as base points, and it terminated the territorial-sea segment at Point A, 12 nm from the coast on the boundary line. The median line was adopted unanimously.

(c) The EEZ and the continental shelf within 200 nm: the three stages
  1. Stage 1 — the provisional equidistance line. The Court identified the relevant coasts (those whose seaward projections overlap within 200 nm: about 733 km for Somalia and about 511 km for Kenya), selected the most appropriate base points on those coasts, and constructed a provisional equidistance line from Point A, taking no account of the islets or the low-tide elevation. Both Parties' figures for the lengths of relevant coasts were accepted by the Court.
  2. Stage 2 — relevant circumstances. The Court examined Kenya's arguments: that the concavity of the East African coast and the presence of Tanzania's coast and Pemba Island in the broad configuration produced a cut-off for Kenya; that regional practice favoured latitudinal boundaries; that its security concerns (piracy, terrorism, the security corridor off Lamu) required a particular line; that its population depended on the fisheries of the disputed area; and that oil concessions and practice supported its claim. It accepted that the broad geographical configuration of the region was relevant and that the provisional line produced a cut-off effect requiring an adjustment; it rejected the security, fisheries and concession arguments, applying Tunisia/Libya's "catastrophic repercussions" test to the fisheries claim and holding that the alleged de facto boundary and the concession practice did not constitute a relevant circumstance. The boundary from a point on the provisional line (identified as Point B) was therefore drawn as a geodetic line with an initial azimuth of 114° until the 200 nm limit.
  3. Stage 3 — the disproportionality test. Comparing the ratio of the relevant coastal lengths (733 : 511, i.e. about 1 : 1.43) with the ratio of the areas attributed by the adjusted line (92,389 : 120,455 sq km, i.e. about 1 : 1.30), the Court found no gross disproportion, and the adjusted line was confirmed as achieving "an equitable solution" for the purposes of Articles 74(1) and 83(1).
(d) The continental shelf beyond 200 nm

The Court held that the maritime boundary beyond 200 nm continues along the same geodetic line (initial azimuth 114°) until it reaches the outer limits of the Parties' continental shelves — which are to be delineated by Somalia and Kenya on the basis of the recommendations of the CLCS — or until it reaches the area where the rights of third States may be affected. It thus followed the approach of ITLOS in Bangladesh/Myanmar and of the Bay of Bengal Tribunal, and it noted that a possible "grey area of limited size" would arise, declining to pronounce on the legal régime applicable to it because the question was hypothetical on the facts.

(e) The alleged violations by Kenya (Article 74(3) and 83(3))

Somalia's claim that Kenya had violated its sovereignty and sovereign rights, and had breached the obligation not to jeopardise or hamper the reaching of a final agreement, was rejected. The Court reasoned:

  • Where maritime claims of States overlap, maritime activities undertaken by a State in an area which is subsequently attributed to another State by a judgment "cannot be considered to be in violation of the sovereign rights of the latter if those activities were carried out before the judgment was delivered and if the area concerned was the subject of claims made in good faith by both States";
  • there was no evidence that Kenya's claims over the area were not made in good faith;
  • the duty not to jeopardise or hamper under Article 74(3)/83(3) applied from the crystallisation of the dispute — which the Court placed in 2009 (the date of the MOU) and not 1979 as Somalia argued — and Kenya's transitory activities (naval patrols, fisheries enforcement, seismic surveys and the collection of data; the alleged drilling in the disputed area after 2009 was not proven) did not jeopardise or hamper the reaching of a final agreement; Kenya had in fact suspended activities and offered provisional arrangements in 2016.

Since Kenya's responsibility was not engaged, the Court did not examine Somalia's request for reparation, and dismissed that submission.

(f) The operative clause (para. 214) and the votes

The Court found, unanimously, that there was no agreed maritime boundary following the parallel of latitude; decided on the starting point of the single maritime boundary and the median line for the territorial sea; decided the course of the EEZ and continental shelf boundary within 200 nm by the adjusted line; decided that the boundary beyond 200 nm continues along the same geodetic line; and rejected Somalia's claims of violation and reparation. The delimitation of the EEZ and shelf was not unanimous — the operative paragraphs on the course of the line were adopted by 13 votes to 4, with Judge ad hoc Guillaume, Kenya's nominee, expressing his dissent in a dissenting opinion, and several Members appending separate or dissenting opinions (notably a separate opinion of Judge Yusuf, who agreed with the rejection of the acquiescence claim and with the median line for the territorial sea but took a different view of aspects of the delimitation). The Court concluded by expressing the expectation that each Party would fully respect the sovereignty, sovereign rights and jurisdiction of the other in accordance with international law.

(g) The aftermath

Kenya rejected the judgment "in totality", accused the Court of bias and announced that it had withdrawn its recognition of the Court's compulsory jurisdiction; Somalia described the judgment as a victory. Because judgments of the Court are binding and not subject to appeal, and because the Court has no enforcement machinery of its own, the question became one of implementation: the judgment records the boundary, but its practical administration — joint or coordinated fishing, licensing, patrolling and the treatment of the "grey area" and the outer shelf — depends on the Parties. Publicly reported developments since 2021 include Somalia's post-judgment licensing rounds in the areas awarded to it, Kenya's public objection to the judgment and its reported attempts to protect its existing contracts, and continued regional efforts (including through IGAD and the African Union) to keep the dispute from destabilising relations. For an examination answer, the correct formulation is: the judgment is final and binding; Kenya's rejection of the Court's jurisdiction does not affect the legal position; and the enforcement question is political, illustrating the difference between the existence of a legal obligation and its realisation.

6. The principles evolved

  1. A maritime boundary cannot easily be established by acquiescence. The threshold is high; the claim must be clear and consistent; silence is not consent where the claimant's own conduct is inconsistent or the circumstances do not call for a response. The rule now sits with Temple of Preah Vihear (1962) and Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (2017) as the modern law on tacit agreement.
  2. Base points are taken on the solid mainland; islets and low-tide elevations do not generate base points for delimitation (following Bangladesh/Myanmar and Bangladesh v. India).
  3. The three-stage methodology is settled, and the third stage is arithmetic: compare the ratio of relevant coastal lengths with the ratio of the areas attributed; only gross disproportion requires a change (here 1 : 1.43 against 1 : 1.30).
  4. Regional configuration and cut-off may justify an adjustment, and the geography of third States (Tanzania, Pemba Island) may be taken into account as part of the broad configuration — a development of the Bay of Bengal reasoning.
  5. Security, fisheries dependence and oil concession practice are not, without more, relevant circumstances; the "catastrophic repercussions" test governs the fisheries argument, and concession practice must be clear and consistent, and accepted by both sides, to amount to anything approaching a tacit agreement.
  6. Activities in an undelimited area are lawful if undertaken in good faith before the judgment, and the transitional-period duty under Articles 74(3) and 83(3) runs from the crystallisation of the dispute — the leading authority on the interim régime, and a direct application of the Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire framework.
  7. The boundary beyond 200 nm follows the same line, subject to the CLCS's recommendations on the outer limits and to third-State rights; and a hypothetical grey area need not be adjudicated.
  8. Non-appearance does not obstruct the Court: Article 53 of the Statute allows the Court to decide the case in the absence of a party, and Kenya's boycott therefore had no effect on the proceedings (though it was criticised in the separate opinions and it coloured the reception of the judgment).

7. The examination angle

How to use case 36 in an answer
  1. For a question on acquiescence and tacit agreement: the case is the modern authority; state the high threshold, Kenya's inconsistent practice, Somalia's incapacity to protest in the civil-war years, the MOU, and the conclusion that no boundary existed at the parallel.
