  2. For a question on delimitation methodology: the case is the fullest modern application of the three stages, with base points, relevant coasts, adjustment and the arithmetic of the disproportionality test; use its figures.
  3. For a question on Articles 74(3) and 83(3): the case supplies the test — good faith, crystallisation of the dispute (2009), transitory activities permitted, drilling not proven — and the outcome (no violation, no reparation).
  4. For a question on the shelf beyond 200 nm: with Bangladesh/Myanmar and Bangladesh v. India, it completes the trilogy on the tribunal's competence, the CLCS's role and the grey area.
  5. For a question on the enforcement of international judgments: Kenya's rejection and its withdrawal of its Article 36(2) declaration make the case the standard modern illustration of the gap between res judicata and compliance.
  6. For an Indian answer: the case matters regionally — the Indian Ocean's outer shelf, the CLCS queue, and the possibility that India's claims and those of its neighbours will be affected by the same jurisprudence. It also shows the value of the Bay of Bengal's approach, since the Court followed it.
  7. The sentence to quote: that activities in an area later attributed to the other State "cannot be considered to be in violation of the sovereign rights of the latter if those activities were carried out before the judgment was delivered and if the area concerned was the subject of claims made in good faith by both States".

↑ top

AUTHSupporting Authorities — the Cases Behind the Cases

The seven prescribed cases decide this topic; these are the authorities that make up the rest of it. Every one of them is used somewhere in the sections above, and each is stated with the single proposition for which it should be cited. Read this table twice before the examination: it converts a list of names into a set of answers.

Table 5.31 · Supporting authorities in the law of the sea, with the proposition for which each is cited
AuthorityCitationThe proposition
The Lotus (France v. Turkey)P.C.I.J., Series A, No. 10 (1927)A State may not exercise its enforcement jurisdiction over a foreign ship on the high seas; jurisdiction belongs to the flag State. The "effects doctrine" exception which the Court allowed for a collision is now excluded by Article 97 of UNCLOS, which gives competence to the flag State and the State of nationality of the offender.
Nottebohm (Liechtenstein v. Guatemala)ICJ Rep. 1955, p. 4Nationality requires a "genuine connection"; the analogy is often drawn with ships' nationality, but the ITLOS judgment in the Saiga (No. 2) made clear that the absence of a genuine link does not entitle another State to refuse to recognise the flag State's rights (Arts 91–92).
The "I'm Alone" (Canada/United States)3 R.I.A.A. 1609 (1935)The United States had no right to sink a rum-running vessel on the high seas, and reparation was payable; the leading authority on the limits of hot pursuit and on the duty to use no more force than is necessary.
Red Crusader (United Kingdom v. Denmark)29 I.L.R. 521; Commission of Inquiry (1962)The arrest of a fishing vessel may be lawful, but the firing of shots at it and the manner of arrest were unjustified; the classical authority on the use of force in fisheries enforcement, applied in Saiga (No. 2) and Virginia G.
Naim Molvan v. Attorney-General for Palestine[1948] A.C. 351 (P.C.)A vessel without nationality on the high seas is not entitled to the protection of any State; the foundation of the right of visit over ships "without nationality" under Article 110(1)(d).
In re Piracy Jure Gentium[1934] A.C. 586 (P.C.)A mere attempt at piracy is piracy; actual robbery is not an essential ingredient of the offence (Article 101).
Fisheries Jurisdiction (United Kingdom v. Iceland; Federal Republic of Germany v. Iceland)ICJ Rep. 1974, pp. 3 and 175The unilateral extension of fisheries jurisdiction is not opposable to a State whose historic rights are thereby affected; the Court's recognition of preferential rights and equitable sharing prepared the ground for the compromise in Articles 61–62 and for the "equitable solution" language of Articles 74 and 83.
Aegean Sea Continental Shelf (Greece v. Turkey), Interim ProtectionICJ Rep. 1976, p. 3Provisional measures were refused because the alleged breach of rights over the shelf did not appear capable of causing irreparable prejudice — the standard of urgency that Article 290 now embodies.
Delimitation of the Maritime Boundary in the Gulf of Maine Area (Canada/United States)ICJ Rep. 1984, p. 246The first delimitation by a special chamber and the first single maritime boundary for the shelf and the water column; the Court applied equitable criteria and practical methods rather than a single geometric rule.
Continental Shelf (Libyan Arab Jamahiriya/Malta)ICJ Rep. 1985, p. 13Proportionality is a test of the equitableness of a line rather than an independent method of drawing it; relative coastal lengths are a relevant circumstance, and a small island may be given reduced effect.
Maritime Delimitation in the Area between Greenland and Jan Mayen (Denmark v. Norway)ICJ Rep. 1993, p. 38The classic modern authority on the effect of a small island in a large area: Jan Mayen was given partial effect and the line was adjusted so as to achieve an equitable result; equitable access to the capelin fishery was taken into account — the exception to the exclusion of economic factors.
Maritime Delimitation and Territorial Questions between Qatar and BahrainICJ Rep. 2001, p. 40Low-tide elevations and small features do not generate entitlements of their own for delimitation purposes; the Court drew a line which gave no effect to Fasht al Jarim because of its location — the authority applied by the Bay of Bengal Tribunal (case 35).
Land and Maritime Boundary between Cameroon and NigeriaICJ Rep. 2002, p. 303The Court delimited the Bakassi peninsula land boundary and the maritime zones in the Gulf of Guinea, applying the equidistance/relevant circumstances method and dealing with the role of third-State (Equatorial Guinea) interests.
Barbados/Trinidad and TobagoPCA, Award of 11 April 2006The "equidistance/equitable principles" method applied to a single maritime boundary, with the fishing practices of the two States treated as a relevant circumstance in the light of the history of the dispute.
Guyana v. SurinamePCA, Award of 17 September 2007Suriname's use of force in ordering a rig to leave a disputed area was unlawful; the Award is the leading modern decision on the duties of Articles 74(3) and 83(3) before delimitation, together with the obligation to negotiate in good faith.
Territorial and Maritime Dispute between Nicaragua and Honduras in the Caribbean SeaICJ Rep. 2007, p. 659Where the geography makes an equidistance line impracticable (the river-mouth situation), the angle-bisector method may be used — the case on which Bangladesh relied, unsuccessfully, in the Bay of Bengal arbitration.
Maritime Delimitation in the Black Sea (Romania v. Ukraine)ICJ Rep. 2009, p. 61The three-stage methodology — provisional equidistance line, adjustment for relevant circumstances, disproportionality check (paras 115–122) — stated in its canonical modern form and now the standard method.
Territorial and Maritime Dispute (Nicaragua v. Colombia)ICJ Rep. 2012, p. 624Islands may generate base points but the line must avoid an inequitable cut-off; the treatment of the Colombian islands in drawing a line through a sea otherwise far from the mainland coasts.
Chagos Marine Protected Area Arbitration (Mauritius v. United Kingdom)PCA, Award of 18 March 2015The Tribunal held that the United Kingdom's declaration of a marine protected area around the Chagos Archipelago was incompatible with its obligations under the Convention, while deciding that it had no jurisdiction over the underlying sovereignty dispute — an illustration of the careful line between law-of-the-sea and territorial questions.
Legal Consequences of the Separation of the Chagos Archipelago from Mauritius in 1969 (Advisory Opinion)ICJ Rep. 2019, p. 95The decolonisation and self-determination question underlying the Indian Ocean's delimitation disputes; the Mauritius–Maldives Special Chamber had to decide how far it could proceed without deciding sovereignty (Case No. 28, 2021 and 2023).
Nicaragua v. United States of America, MeritsICJ Rep. 1986, p. 14State practice did not establish a customary rule requiring prior notification or authorisation for the passage of warships; and the mining of Nicaragua's ports and interference with its maritime commerce violated its sovereignty — a use made of the case in the discussion of naval activities and the freedom of navigation.
Philippines v. China (South China Sea Arbitration)PCA Case No. 2013-19, Award of 12 July 2016Article 121(3) analysed feature by feature: rocks that cannot sustain human habitation or economic life generate no EEZ or shelf; claims to historic rights in the sea beyond the Convention's limits have no basis; and the environmental obligations of Part XII apply to the disputed features.
The M/V "Saiga" (No. 1) and (No. 2) (Saint Vincent and the Grenadines v. Guinea)ITLOS Cases Nos. 1 and 2 (1997; 1999)The bond under Article 292 must be reasonable and must relate to the offence under the Convention; and enforcement in the EEZ must comply with Article 73, with force used only as a last resort and no more than reasonably necessary.
The prompt-release line: "Camouco", "Monte Confurco", "Grand Prince", "Volga", "Juno Trader", "Hoshinmaru", "Tomimaru"ITLOS, 2000–2007The reasonableness of a bond is assessed by reference to the gravity of the offence, the penalties, the value of the vessel and cargo, and the amount demanded; the nationality of the vessel must be proved; and release does not render the application moot.
"ARA Libertad" (Argentina v. Ghana)ITLOS Case No. 20, Order of 15 December 2012A warship enjoys immunity and must be released; a provisional measures order can produce immediate practical compliance.
"Arctic Sunrise" (Netherlands v. Russian Federation)ITLOS Case No. 22, Order of 22 November 2013; Annex VII Award of 14 August 2015Provisional measures may be prescribed under Article 290(5) before the arbitral tribunal is constituted; non-appearance does not prevent the proceedings; the boarding and detention of the vessel in the EEZ were unlawful.
"M/V Virginia G" (Panama v. Guinea-Bissau)ITLOS Case No. 19, Judgment of 14 April 2014The coastal State may prohibit the bunkering of foreign fishing vessels in its EEZ as an aspect of its rights over the living resources; but the confiscation of the vessel and the failure to inform the flag State violated Article 73, and compensation was awarded.
M/T "Norstar" (Panama v. Italy)ITLOS Case No. 25, Judgment of 10 April 2019Italy's seizure of a foreign tanker for bunkering offences committed on the high seas violated Panama's rights; the grounds on which a State may exercise criminal jurisdiction over a foreign ship on the high seas are exhaustively regulated by the Convention.
Delimitation of the Maritime Boundary in the Bay of Bengal (Bangladesh/Myanmar)ITLOS Case No. 16, Judgment of 14 March 2012The first international judgment to delimit the continental shelf beyond 200 nm, and the first to apply the cut-off analysis to a concave coast — the direct precursor of the Bay of Bengal award (case 35).
Ghana/Côte d'IvoireITLOS Special Chamber, Case No. 23, Judgment of 23 September 2017The definitive treatment of Articles 74(3)/83(3): the duties to seek provisional arrangements, to refrain from jeopardising the final agreement and to avoid activities which might aggravate the dispute; and the rejection of a boundary by tacit agreement.
Mauritius/MaldivesITLOS Special Chamber, Case No. 28, Judgment of 28 January 2023Delimitation in the Indian Ocean: the Chamber declined to determine sovereignty over the Chagos Archipelago but proceeded to delimit, and applied equidistance with a careful treatment of base points.
Responsibilities and Obligations of States Sponsoring Persons and Entities with respect to Activities in the Area (Advisory Opinion)Seabed Disputes Chamber, Case No. 17, 1 February 2011Sponsoring States owe an obligation of due diligence, not of result; the precautionary approach, best environmental practices and environmental impact assessment are part of that obligation; and the obligations concerning the Area are owed erga omnes.
Request for an Advisory Opinion submitted by the Sub-Regional Fisheries Commission (Advisory Opinion)ITLOS Case No. 21, 2 April 2015Flag States must exercise due diligence to prevent illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing by their vessels in the EEZs of other States, and are liable for breach; the Tribunal's advisory jurisdiction under Annex VI, article 21 confirmed.
Request for an Advisory Opinion on Climate Change and International Law (Advisory Opinion)ITLOS Case No. 31, 21 May 2024Anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions are pollution of the marine environment; States must take measures with "stringent due diligence" and on the precautionary approach, and the Paris Agreement does not by itself discharge their obligations under Part XII.
Kerala High Court, Italia v. Union of India and connected mattersJudgment of 29 May 2012 (Kerala High Court); writ proceedings in the Supreme CourtThe Indian stage of the Enrica Lexie litigation before the Supreme Court's reference — the decision which held that India had jurisdiction and which led to the constitutional reference answered in case 34.
Aban Loyd Chiles Offshore Ltd. v. Union of IndiaSupreme Court of India, 2008The EEZ and the continental shelf were treated as part of India for the purposes of customs legislation, on the language of the Customs Act — distinguished in case 34, where the Supreme Court explained that the question there was statutory and not the general question of sovereignty in the EEZ.
How to use the supporting authorities
  1. One authority per proposition. Learn the table as pairs — Red Crusader for the use of force; Guyana/Suriname for Articles 74(3)/83(3); Jan Mayen for the effect of a small island; Romania/Ukraine for the three stages; Mauritius/Maldives for the Indian Ocean; South China Sea for Article 121(3).
  2. Never cite a case without a proposition, and never invent a paragraph number. If you recall the case but not the paragraph, write "the Court held…" and give the proposition.
  3. Prefer the prescribed cases where they overlap. The seven cases carry the marks; the supporting authorities are for the propositions the seven do not supply.

↑ top

5.3 · The Concept of the Common Heritage of Mankind Relating to the Resources of the International Seabed Area

Of all the ideas that the law of the sea has produced, the common heritage of mankind is the boldest. It says that a part of the planet — the deep ocean floor and its mineral wealth, an area larger than all the continents combined — belongs to no State, may be appropriated by no State, and must be used for the benefit of every human being, including those not yet born. It is the legal expression of a moral idea, and it is the one subject in Topic 5 where the question is not merely "what does the law permit?" but "whose law is the sea?"

A. The problem: three theories of the deep seabed

Before 1967 the deep seabed — the ocean floor beyond the limits of national jurisdiction — had an uncertain legal status, and international lawyers described it in terms of three rival theories, all borrowed from the law of property.

Table 5.18 · Three theories of the status of the deep seabed
TheoryMeaningConsequence for the deep seabedChampion and status
Res nulliusThings belonging to no one, but capable of being owned by the first taker (occupatio).Any State could occupy and mine portions of the seabed; ownership would follow the taking, as with newly discovered territory.The implicit view of early industrial interests; never accepted in the deep seabed, but it is the theory behind any unilateral exploitation of the Area.
Res communisThings belonging to everyone and to no one, incapable of appropriation, open to use by all.The high seas model: nobody owns the seabed, but anybody may use it — a rule of liberty, not of sharing.Grotius's Mare Liberum (1609); the inherited framework of the 1958 Geneva Conventions.
Common heritage of mankindThings which belong to all humanity and must be administered on its behalf by an institution, with benefits shared.No appropriation; use for peaceful purposes; international management; equitable sharing, including with developing countries and future generations.Arvid Pardo's proposal of 1967; adopted in UNCLOS Part XI and in General Assembly resolution 2749 (XXV) of 1970.

"The difference between common heritage of mankind and res communis is not that common heritage implies everyone owns the area or has equal access to it, but rather that it must be jointly administered and jointly managed."

De Soto, cited in the DU course material (Exam Capsule, Topic 5) — the course material's own formulation of the distinction, and the sentence to quote when the question asks you to distinguish the two concepts

The distinction between res communis and the common heritage of mankind is the crux of the topic. Res communis is a rule of non-appropriation combined with freedom: the high seas are free to all, and the freedom is exercised by each State for its own national advantage. The common heritage principle adds two further layers: management by an international authority on behalf of mankind, and the equitable sharing of the benefits of use. Freedom for all becomes the sharing of the proceeds of use by all. That is why Part XI is not an extension of the high seas régime but a distinct régime, and why Article 137 begins with a rule of non-appropriation and ends with a rule about how minerals may be alienated.

B. Origins: Arvid Pardo, 1967, and the ten-year courtship of a principle

(i) The trigger. On 1 November 1967 the delegation of Malta, led by Ambassador Arvid Pardo, asked the General Assembly to place on its agenda the question of the peaceful uses of the seabed beyond national jurisdiction. Pardo's speech — one of the most influential in the history of the United Nations — described the seabed's resources as the "common heritage of mankind" and urged the international community to take collective action before technology allowed individual States to occupy the ocean floor. Two forces gave the speech its effect: the collapse of the colonial empires had produced a large bloc of newly independent States determined to secure a share of the world's resources, and the technological capacity to mine the deep seabed then lay with a handful of industrialised States, so that a "first-come-first-served" régime would have transferred a global resource into a few national hands. Pardo's proposal was, in substance, that the deep seabed should be treated not as a res nullius waiting for the flag but as an inherited estate whose trustees were all the nations.

(ii) The instruments before the Convention. The idea was carried forward through a series of steps, each of which remains quotable:

  • 1967 — General Assembly resolution 2340 (XXII) established an Ad Hoc Committee to Study the Peaceful Uses of the Sea-Bed and the Ocean Floor beyond the Limits of National Jurisdiction, and resolution 2467 A (XXIII) of 1968 made it a standing committee — the forum in which the concept was elaborated.
  • 1969 — resolution 2574 (XXIV) adopted a moratorium pending a régime: States were called upon to refrain from exploiting the resources of the deep seabed, and to claim no rights over it. The moratorium shows that the international community understood non-appropriation before it agreed on sharing.
  • 1970 — resolution 2749 (XXV), the Declaration of Principles Governing the Sea-Bed and the Ocean Floor, and the Subsoil Thereof, beyond the Limits of National Jurisdiction, was adopted by 108 votes to none with 14 abstentions. It declared the seabed and its resources to be "the common heritage of mankind", provided that "no State shall claim or exercise sovereignty or sovereign rights over any part thereof", that its exploration and exploitation "shall be carried out for the benefit of mankind as a whole", taking into particular consideration the interests and needs of the developing countries, that it be reserved exclusively for peaceful purposes, and that it be administered by an international régime to be established. This resolution, not Article 136, is the origin of the concept in positive international law, and it is the instrument to cite if a question asks about the source of the principle.
  • 1973–1982 — the Third United Nations Conference on the Law of the Sea converted the principle into a treaty régime: Part XI of UNCLOS (Articles 133–191) with Annexes III and IV; the régime was, in the words of its negotiators, "the constitutional order of the oceans".

(iii) UNCLOS 1982. The Convention gives the principle a textual home and a whole institutional architecture. The heart of it is a single sentence.

"The Area and its resources are the common heritage of mankind."

UNCLOS, Article 136 — a provision which Article 311(6) puts beyond amendment and beyond derogation

(iv) The crisis and the 1994 Implementation Agreement. The régime of Part XI was opposed by the industrialised States, above all the United States, which objected to the mandatory transfer of technology, the production ceiling designed to protect land-based producers, the financing of the Enterprise, and decision-making rules that allowed the developing-country majority to control the exploitation of resources that only the industrialised States could then extract. The United States voted against the Convention in 1982; several industrialised States stayed out; and between 1982 and 1984 a group of them (the United States, the United Kingdom, France, Germany, Japan and others) concluded reciprocal "mini-treaty" arrangements purporting to govern seabed mining among themselves, with provision for the reciprocal recognition of claims — a direct challenge to the Convention's exclusivity.

The crisis was resolved by negotiation rather than by fragmentation. Between 1990 and 1994 the Secretary-General convened consultations which produced the Agreement relating to the Implementation of Part XI of the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea of 10 December 1982, adopted by General Assembly resolution 48/263 of 28 July 1994 (provisionally applied from 16 November 1994 and in force from 28 July 1996). Its method is important for any answer: it does not rewrite Part XI; it controls it.

  • Single instrument rule — the Agreement and Part XI "shall be interpreted and applied together as a single instrument"; in the event of inconsistency the Agreement prevails (Art. 2(1) and Annex section 1).
  • Market-oriented changes — production ceilings and the "policies" of Article 151 gave way to a market-based régime (Annex section 6); the mandatory transfer of technology was replaced by an obligation to cooperate on fair and reasonable commercial terms (section 5); the Enterprise was not to be established immediately but was to begin with the Secretariat performing its initial functions and to operate through joint ventures (section 2); and States Parties' financial obligations were reduced and channelled through a mandatory Finance Committee.
  • Decision-making — the Assembly and the Council were to take decisions by consensus wherever possible, with carefully weighted voting in the Council, including chambers for the different categories of interest (section 3): the "groups" of the Council (see §5.4).
  • Review — the Review Conference of Article 155 was neutered: the Assembly might not amend the system without the Council's approval, and the Agreement's provisions could not be amended by the Review Conference (Annex section 4).
  • The principle survives — nothing in the 1994 Agreement touches Article 136, and Article 311(6) of the Convention provides that "there shall be no amendments to the basic principle relating to the common heritage of mankind set forth in Article 136" and that States "shall not be party to any agreement in derogation thereof". The common heritage principle is therefore the one element of Part XI which the political compromise of 1994 left untouched — a legal fortress within a negotiated settlement.

C. The content of the principle: eight elements

The common heritage of mankind is not a slogan; it operates through a set of specific rules. The table below sets out the elements with the provisions that carry them, and each element is then explained.

Table 5.19 · The elements of the common heritage of mankind in UNCLOS Part XI
ElementProvisionWhat it means in practice
1. Non-appropriationArt. 137(1); Art. 136; GA res. 2749 (XXV), para. 2No State may claim or exercise sovereignty or sovereign rights over any part of the Area or its resources; no natural or juridical person may appropriate any part; and any such claim or appropriation "shall not be recognized". The prohibition binds the State and the private contractor alike.
2. Peaceful purposesArt. 141; Art. 138; GA res. 2749 (XXV), para. 5The Area is open to use "exclusively for peaceful purposes" by all States, coastal or land-locked, without discrimination — a direct legacy of the Cold War context in which the concept was born.
3. Benefit of mankind as a wholeArt. 140(1)Activities in the Area are to be carried out "for the benefit of mankind as a whole, irrespective of the geographical location of States", taking into particular consideration the interests and needs of developing States and of peoples who have not attained full independence.
4. Equitable sharing of benefitsArt. 140(2); Art. 160(2)(f)(i); Annex III, art. 13; 1994 Agreement, Annex section 7 & 8Financial and other economic benefits derived from activities in the Area are to be shared equitably through an appropriate mechanism, on a non-discriminatory basis — the "distributive" element that distinguishes the principle from mere freedom of use.
5. International management on behalf of mankindArts 137(2), 153, 156–170, 191All rights in the resources are "vested in mankind as a whole, on whose behalf the Authority shall act"; the International Seabed Authority organises and controls activities in the Area, and the Enterprise is its own mining operator.
6. Participation and transfer of technologyArts 143, 144, 148, 268, 269; Annex III, art. 5; 1994 Agreement, Annex section 5Marine scientific research is to be carried out exclusively for peaceful purposes and for the benefit of mankind; the Authority promotes the transfer of technology; developing States, including land-locked and geographically disadvantaged States, are to be able to participate effectively in activities in the Area.
7. Protection of the environment and of lifeArts 145, 146, 147, 209Activities must be conducted with effective protection of the marine environment and of human life; the Authority adopts rules to protect flora and fauna and to prevent pollution — the element through which the principle links to Part XII and to the modern environmental jurisprudence.
8. Benefit of future generations and respect for cultural heritagePreamble; Art. 149; Art. 150The resources are administered as a heritage to be handed on; archaeological and historical objects found in the Area are to be preserved or disposed of "for the benefit of mankind as a whole", with preferential rights for the State or country of origin or of cultural, historical or archaeological origin.
Element 1 — Non-appropriation

"No State shall claim or exercise sovereignty or sovereign rights over any part of the Area or its resources, nor shall any State or natural or juridical person appropriate any part thereof. No such claim or exercise of sovereignty or sovereign rights nor such appropriation shall be recognized."

UNCLOS, Article 137(1)

Three points are worth extracting. First, the prohibition is addressed to States and to private persons: a licence granted by a State over a part of the Area is itself the prohibited claim, and the Convention adds that it "shall not be recognized", so third States are not bound to respect an unlawful title. This is why the unilateral licensing of Area resources by a State party to the Convention (as, for example, in the deep-sea mining controversy of the 2020s, discussed in §5.4) is analysed as a disregard of Article 137 and not merely as a breach of a licence condition. Secondly, Article 137(2) provides that while the resources in situ are not subject to alienation, "the minerals recovered from the Area... may only be alienated in accordance with this Part and the rules, regulations and procedures of the Authority" — the principle governs the origin of the title, not the marketing of the ore. Thirdly, Article 137(3) closes the circle by refusing recognition to any rights over recovered minerals acquired otherwise than under Part XI.

Element 2 — Peaceful purposes

"The Area shall be open to use exclusively for peaceful purposes by all States, whether coastal or land-locked, without discrimination and without prejudice to the other provisions of this Part."

UNCLOS, Article 141

Article 138 supplements this by requiring States to conduct themselves in the Area "in accordance with... the principles embodied in the Charter of the United Nations and other rules of international law in the interests of maintaining peace and security". The prohibition of appropriation is thus reinforced by a prohibition of militarisation; the Area is a zone of peace as well as a zone of common property. The limitation is also practical: installations, artificial islands and safety zones in the Area may not be used as a basis for national sovereignty claims (Art. 147(3) by analogy with Art. 60(8) and Art. 80).

Element 3 — Benefit of mankind as a whole

"Activities in the Area shall, as specifically provided for in this Part, be carried out for the benefit of mankind as a whole, irrespective of the geographical location of States, whether coastal or land-locked, and taking into particular consideration the interests and needs of developing States and of peoples who have not attained full independence or other self-governing status recognized by the United Nations in accordance with General Assembly resolution 1514 (XV) and other relevant General Assembly resolutions."

UNCLOS, Article 140(1)

Note the words "irrespective of the geographical location of States". They mean that the land-locked and the geographically disadvantaged are as entitled as the coastal, and they are the textual bridge between the common heritage principle and the special régime for land-locked States (Part X, §5.1.6 D). Note also the phrase "as specifically provided for in this Part": the benefit is to be measured by the detailed provisions — the sharing mechanism, the participation rights, the technology provisions, the anti-monopoly policy of Article 150 — and not by a generalised claim to equal dividends. This is the textual answer to those who argue that the common heritage principle requires identical outcomes for all States: Article 140 is a standard, and Part XI supplies the machinery.

Element 4 — Equitable sharing of benefits

Article 140(2) requires the Authority to "provide for the equitable sharing of financial and other economic benefits derived from activities in the Area through any appropriate mechanism, on a non-discriminatory basis". The detailed machinery is in Article 160(2)(f)(i) (the Assembly's power to consider and approve the rules on the equitable sharing of benefits), Annex III, article 13 (financial terms of contracts — royalty or a combination of royalty and profit-share, with the rate fixed within specified ranges, and a choice for the contractor), and sections 7 and 8 of the Annex to the 1994 Agreement (economic assistance to developing States suffering adverse effects, and the financing of the Authority). The sharing is of revenue, not of the minerals themselves: the minerals go to the contractor, the payments go to the Authority, and the Authority distributes them among States Parties on the basis of criteria to be adopted — criteria which include the interests of land-locked and geographically disadvantaged States and of the least developed among them. That is why the topic is studied together with land-based producer interests: the 1970s concern was that a flood of seabed nickel, cobalt and manganese would destroy the economies of States such as Zambia, Zaire (now the DRC), Gabon and Botswana, and the original Part XI responded with production ceilings and a compensation mechanism which the 1994 Agreement replaced with a narrower "economic assistance" fund.

Element 5 — International management: the Authority acts "on behalf of mankind"

"All rights in the resources of the Area are vested in mankind as a whole, on whose behalf the Authority shall act."

UNCLOS, Article 137(2)

This sentence contains the institutional core of the principle. Mankind is the owner; the International Seabed Authority is the trustee; and Article 153 provides the system of exploration and exploitation — activities are to be organised, carried out and controlled by the Authority on behalf of mankind, in association with States Parties, State enterprises, natural or juridical persons sponsored by States Parties, and the Enterprise. Article 153(4) makes the Authority's control explicit: it has "the right to take at any time any measures provided for under this Part to ensure compliance with its provisions", including the power to suspend or terminate contracts. Article 154 requires periodic review of the régime; Article 191 entrusts the Seabed Disputes Chamber with advisory jurisdiction over legal questions arising in the Area (see §5.4 and §5.6).

Element 6 — Participation and transfer of technology

Article 143 provides that marine scientific research in the Area shall be carried out "exclusively for peaceful purposes and for the benefit of mankind as a whole"; the Authority may carry out research and enter into contracts; States are to promote international cooperation, disseminate results, and strengthen the research capabilities and the personnel of developing States. Article 144 requires the Authority to promote the transfer of technology relating to activities in the Area so that the Enterprise and all States Parties may benefit, with measures including training programmes and the acquisition of technology on fair and reasonable terms. Article 148 requires the effective participation of developing States, with special attention to land-locked and geographically disadvantaged States, and Article 150 sets out the policies for activities in the Area — orderly development, rational management, expansion of opportunities for participation, avoidance of monopolisation, and protection of the interests of developing States.

Element 7 — Protection of the marine environment and of human life

Article 145 requires the Authority to adopt rules for "the effective protection of the marine environment from harmful effects which may arise from... activities in the Area", including the prevention, reduction and control of pollution, the protection and preservation of the natural resources of the Area, and the prevention of damage to the flora and fauna of the marine environment. Articles 146 and 147 deal with the protection of human life and with the accommodation of activities in the Area alongside other uses of the marine environment — navigation, fishing, scientific research and the laying of cables and pipelines. Article 209 makes the international rules and the rules of coastal States reciprocally reinforcing. This element has become the most litigated: the Seabed Disputes Chamber's advisory opinion of 2011 (below) and the modern controversy over deep-sea mining are largely arguments about the content of Articles 145 and 209 and of the Authority's mining code.

Element 8 — Future generations and cultural heritage

The concept of "heritage" is inter-generational. A heritage is something received and handed on; the Preamble of the Convention, which speaks of realising "the historic significance of this Convention as an important contribution to the maintenance of peace, justice and progress for all peoples of the world", and Article 150's requirement of the "development of the resources of the Area... in a manner... consistent with sound principles of conservation", express the idea. Article 149 gives it a concrete application:

"All objects of an archaeological and historical nature found in the Area shall be preserved or disposed of for the benefit of mankind as a whole, particular regard being paid to the preferential rights of the State or country of origin, or the State of cultural origin, or the State of historical and archaeological origin."

UNCLOS, Article 149 — compare Article 303 (archaeological objects found at sea) and the 2001 UNESCO Convention on the Protection of the Underwater Cultural Heritage

D. The concept in motion: from Pardo to the present

E. What lies within the concept: the Area and its resources

The scope of the principle is defined by two definitions in Article 1 of the Convention. "Area" means "the sea-bed and ocean floor and subsoil thereof, beyond the limits of national jurisdiction" (Art. 1(1)(1)); and "resources" means "all solid, liquid or gaseous mineral resources in situ in the Area at or beneath the sea-bed, including polymetallic nodules" (Art. 133(a)). "Activities in the Area" means all activities of exploration for, and exploitation of, the resources of the Area (Art. 133(b)). Three consequences follow, and they are frequently examined.

  • The principle is spatial as well as material. It attaches to the Area — the seabed and subsoil beyond national jurisdiction — and not to the water column above it, which remains high seas subject to the freedoms of Article 87. The same square metre of ocean can therefore be governed by two different principles: the water column by freedom, the seabed below by common heritage. That is also the structure that produces the "grey area" of the Bay of Bengal award (case 35).
  • It is confined to mineral resources. Article 133 covers minerals in situ; it does not mention living resources. Marine genetic resources of the deep seabed — the biological material of hydrothermal vents, with enormous pharmaceutical potential — were debated for twenty years and are not covered by Part XI as such; the BBNJ Agreement of 2023 addresses marine genetic resources of the high seas and the Area through a benefit-sharing régime of its own (see §5.4 J). The "common heritage" idea has therefore been extended by a separate treaty rather than by reinterpreting Article 133 — a nice illustration of how the principle functions as a source of new régimes.
  • It coexists with the continental shelf beyond 200 nm. Where a State's shelf extends beyond 200 nm, its resources are its own and are not part of the Area, even though the shelf is physically continuous with the Area; the boundary between the two is fixed by the CLCS's recommendations on the outer limits of the shelf (Art. 76(8); §5.1.3). India's extended shelf claims in the Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal are, in effect, claims to remove particular areas from the common heritage régime — which is why neighbouring States and the Commission scrutinise them so closely.

F. The institutional face of the concept: the International Seabed Authority

Articles 156–170 create the Authority, with its seat at Kingston, Jamaica, as the organisation through which States Parties organise and control activities in the Area, "particularly with a view to administering the resources of the Area" (Art. 157(1)). Its principal organs are:

  • The Assembly — all States Parties, one vote each; the supreme organ, which elects the members of the Council and approves the rules on the equitable sharing of benefits (Arts 159–160).
  • The Council — 36 States Parties, the executive organ, with power to approve plans of work and to adopt the rules, regulations and procedures of the mining code (Arts 161–162; the composition and decision-making rules were reshaped by section 3 of the Annex to the 1994 Agreement).
  • The Legal and Technical Commission and the Economic Planning Commission — the subsidiary organs (Arts 163–165; the Economic Planning Commission's functions were largely suspended by the 1994 Agreement).
  • The Secretariat (Arts 166–169) and the Enterprise (Art. 170; Annex IV), the Authority's own operating arm, whose independent establishment was deferred by the 1994 Agreement.
  • The Seabed Disputes Chamber of ITLOS (Arts 186–191; Annex VI, Part XI), which has jurisdiction over disputes between States Parties, between a State Party and the Authority, between parties to a contract, and between the Authority and a contractor, and advisory jurisdiction over legal questions arising in the Area.

The Authority's legislative output — the "mining code" — consists of regulations for prospecting and exploration for polymetallic nodules (2013), polymetallic sulphides (2011) and cobalt-rich ferromanganese crusts (2012), and the still-unfinished regulations for exploitation. Those instruments, and the parallel system of mining by which they are organised, are the subject of §5.4. What belongs to §5.3 is the constitutional point: the Authority is not an owner but a trustee, and every power it exercises is exercised "on behalf of mankind" — the phrase in Article 137(2) that makes the common heritage principle justiciable rather than rhetorical.

G. The jurisprudence: the Seabed Disputes Chamber's advisory opinion of 2011

Responsibilities and Obligations of States Sponsoring Persons and Entities with respect to Activities in the Area (Advisory Opinion)

Seabed Disputes Chamber of the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea, Case No. 17, Advisory Opinion of 1 February 2011 · Requested by the Council of the International Seabed Authority under Article 191 of UNCLOS · Unanimous

(a) Facts and context. Two entities — Nauru Ocean Resources Inc. (sponsored by Nauru) and Tonga Offshore Mining Limited (sponsored by Tonga) — had applied to the Authority for approval of plans of work for exploration in the Area. Both sponsoring States were developing States, and Nauru and Tonga asked the Council how far a sponsoring State was liable for the failures of those it sponsors, given that the contractors' operations are beyond the sponsoring State's direct control. The Council's question was whether the sponsoring State's obligation was one of result (guaranteeing compliance) or of conduct (due diligence), what the extent of liability was, and what the position of developing States was. The Chamber answered on 1 February 2011.

(b) Issues. (i) The content of the obligation "to ensure" compliance under Article 139(1) and Annex III, article 4(4); (ii) the nature and extent of the liability of a sponsoring State for damage caused by a contractor; (iii) whether the obligations applied differently to developing States; (iv) the relationship between the Convention's obligations and other rules of international law, including the precautionary approach; and (v) the applicability of the obligations to developing States which were also geographically disadvantaged.

(c) Decision and principles.

  • Obligation of conduct, not of result. The sponsoring State's obligation "to ensure" is an obligation to deploy adequate means, to do its utmost, and to exercise due diligence — it is not an obligation to guarantee that the contractor will comply. The State must adopt laws, regulations and administrative measures that are reasonably appropriate to secure compliance, and it must take measures within its legal system to ensure that the contractor performs. The standard is a variable one: "due diligence" requires the State to keep regulations up to date and to take account of the risks and the technology in question.
  • Specific legal obligations follow from due diligence. Among them, the Chamber identified the obligation to apply the precautionary approach ("a trend towards making this approach part of customary international law"), the obligation to apply best environmental practices, the obligation to conduct an environmental impact assessment before authorising activity, and the obligation to ensure the contractor's compliance with the Authority's rules; it also identified the duty to provide recourse for damage caused by the contractor, through the contractor's liability under Annex III, article 22, and the sponsoring State's liability under Article 139(2).
  • Obligations erga omnes. The Chamber held that the obligations of States Parties relating to the Area are owed to the international community as a whole: "each State Party may be entitled to seek compensation... on behalf of the victims" — the obligations have an erga omnes character, because the Area and its resources are the common heritage of mankind and the Authority acts on behalf of mankind. The point matters for the topic: the common heritage principle is what gives the obligations their community character.
  • No double standard for developing States. The Chamber held that the obligations apply equally to developed and developing sponsoring States; the special position of developing States may be reflected in the Authority's rules on assistance, but not by lowering the environmental standard, because "the interests of mankind... cannot be served by a lower standard for some States".
  • Advisory jurisdiction. The Chamber held that its advisory jurisdiction under Article 191 extends to legal questions concerning the Area, that the request fell within Article 191, and that it had jurisdiction to answer; and it added a point of permanent importance — the advisory jurisdiction is not limited to disputes but serves the Authority's need for legal certainty in administering the common heritage.

(d) Subsequent practice. The 2011 opinion is now the leading authority on sponsored activity in the Area, and its reasoning has been carried into the Authority's regulations — the sponsoring State's certificate, the obligations of contractors and of sponsoring States, and the requirement of an environmental assessment before a contract is granted. The Chamber's jurisdiction is not confined to that opinion: disputes between the Authority and a contractor, and between parties to a contract, come before it under Article 187, and the Chamber is empowered to give advisory opinions under Article 191. The modern contest over deep-sea mining (§5.4 J) has therefore been conducted before the Authority and, on one occasion in 2026, before the Chamber itself; the details are given in §5.4 so that the two sections are not repeated here.

H. Is the common heritage principle customary international law? The argument both ways

Table 5.20 · The status of the common heritage principle — the competing positions
It is binding beyond the ConventionIt binds only within the Convention
AuthorityGA res. 2749 (XXV) of 1970 adopted by 108 : 0 with 14 abstentions; the moratorium of 1969; the near-universality of UNCLOS (169 States Parties plus the European Union, including every Indian Ocean and South Asian State); Art. 311(6)'s non-derogation clause; the 2011 advisory opinion's erga omnes characterisation.The Convention is a treaty and creates rights mainly for its parties; the 1994 Agreement reshaped the régime in favour of the industrialised States; the "mini-treaty" episode showed that unilateralism can persist; and States outside the Convention are not bound by Part XI.
Practical consequenceThe Area cannot be appropriated by anyone, treaty party or not; unilateral exploitation is unlawful; and the obligations concerning the Area are owed to the international community.Non-parties may argue freedom in the Area; the political contest over unilateral exploitation is settled by diplomacy rather than by law (see §5.4 J).
Examiner's answerThe safe formulation: the core of non-appropriation and the peaceful-purposes rule reflect principles embodied in resolution 2749 (XXV) and are widely accepted; the detailed régime of Part XI binds the parties to the Convention and, as between them, has been modified by the 1994 Agreement. Article 311(6) makes the principle of Article 136 unamendable and non-derogable for the parties, which is the strongest textual statement of its special status.

I. India and the common heritage

India's engagement with the concept is one of the longest in the developing world, and it illustrates both the moral and the practical dimensions of the principle.

  • At UNCLOS III, India was a leading voice of the Group of 77 and of the New International Economic Order: it supported Part XI, the Enterprise, the production ceilings that protected land-based producers and the transfer-of-technology provisions. The New Delhi Declaration of the Non-Aligned Movement of 1983 reaffirmed the common heritage principle as an element of the NIEO.
  • As a pioneer investor, India was registered under resolution II of UNCLOS III and, in 1987, was allotted a pioneer area of 150,000 sq km in the Central Indian Ocean Basin (CIOB); it retained 75,000 sq km after relinquishment, and its contract for exploration was extended in 2017. In 2025 India was awarded a second exploration contract, for polymetallic sulphides on the Carlsberg Ridge — commonly reported as making India the first country with two contracts of this kind — and it has applied for a third, for the Afanasy-Nikitin seamount. India's stakes in the concept are thus both systemic (as a champion of equitable sharing) and concrete (as a contractor whose title derives from the Authority).
  • As a State in the region, India's extended continental shelf submissions are the mirror image of the concept: the greater India's shelf is found to be, the smaller the Area over which the common heritage régime applies. India's submissions of 2009 and 2025 must be seen alongside its support for the Authority's régime — the two positions are consistent only if the shelf entitlements are scientifically established.
  • In the current debate, India has adhered to the Authority's processes, participates in the work on the exploitation regulations, and is among the States whose researchers warn of the effects of nodule mining on benthic ecosystems; the Deep Ocean Mission and the Samudrayaan submersible programme are its national instruments. The Indian position is best described as responsible development within the Authority's framework, rather than either accession to the moratorium campaign or support for unilateral extraction.
Real examples worth writing in an answer
  • Pardo's warning realised: the polymetallic nodule fields of the Clarion–Clipperton Zone in the Pacific — a decade's worth of nickel and cobalt at current demand — is the area in which the Authority has granted most exploration contracts, and the area which the moratorium campaign seeks to protect.
  • The deep-sea treasure that became law: the wreck of the Spanish frigate Nuestra Señora de las Mercedes, with 500,000 gold and silver coins, was recovered by a company and returned by the United States courts to Spain — the practical face of the idea behind Article 149 (though the recovery took place in the territorial sea and the EEZ, not in the Area).
  • India's first contract: the 1987 pioneer registration and the CIOB allocation; the 2025 Carlsberg Ridge contract is the modern update.
  • The 2011 advisory opinion's practical effect: the Nauru and Tonga contracts that led to it are now producing States' reports on environmental data and sponsoring-State certificates — the principle translated into paperwork.

J. The examination angle

Model skeleton — "Discuss the concept of the common heritage of mankind" (15 or 20 marks)
  1. Define and distinguish (2 marks). Res nullius, res communis, common heritage — Table 5.18; the difference is equitable sharing through an institution.
  2. Origin (3 marks). Pardo, 1 November 1967; res. 2340 (XXII), res. 2467 A (XXIII), the 1969 moratorium and res. 2749 (XXV) of 1970; then UNCLOS III and Art. 136.
  3. The content (5 marks). The eight elements of Table 5.19 with provisions: non-appropriation (Art. 137), peaceful purposes (141), benefit of mankind (140(1)), equitable sharing (140(2)), international management (137(2), 153, 156–170), participation and technology (143, 144, 148, 150), environment and life (145–147, 209), future generations and cultural heritage (Preamble, 149).
  4. The institutions (2 marks). The Authority, its organs, the Enterprise, the mining code, and the Seabed Disputes Chamber.
  5. The 1994 modification and the survival of the principle (3 marks). The crisis, the mini-treaties, the Agreement of 1994 — and Art. 311(6).
  6. The jurisprudence (2 marks). The 2011 advisory opinion: due diligence, precautionary approach, EIA, erga omnes, no double standard.
  7. Evaluation and conclusion (3 marks). The principle as the legal form of the NIEO; its successes (non-appropriation, the Authority, benefit-sharing for MGRs under the BBNJ Agreement) and its limits (the 1994 compromise, slow exploitation, the modern moratorium campaign and unilateral licensing). Conclude that the principle has survived the transformation of its own régime: the law of the Area changed in 1994, but the principle of 1967 did not.
Cross-references — where the same material is used
  • §5.1.3 (continental shelf): the outer limit of the shelf is the boundary of the Area — CLCS recommendations determine where the common heritage régime begins.
  • §5.1.5 (high seas): the water column above the Area is high seas; the freedoms of Article 87 apply there and not to the Area.
  • §5.4 (parallel system): the mechanism by which the Authority and States exploit the Area side by side, including the reserved area and the Enterprise's participation.
  • §5.5 (India): India's pioneer-investor history, its contracts and its extended-shelf submissions.
  • §5.6 (ITLOS): the Seabed Disputes Chamber — composition, jurisdiction, and the 2011 and later advisory opinions.

↑ top

5.4 · International Seabed Mining — the Parallel System of Mining

§5.3 explained whose the deep seabed is. This section explains how it is worked. The answer given by Part XI is a compromise of great ingenuity: the Area is not left to the free-for-all of the high seas, and it is not worked by an international monopoly either. The Authority does not mine. It regulates, contracts, sponsors and shares — and the mining is done on two tracks at once, one international and one national, under international control. That is the parallel system.

A. The choice that was made: three models at UNCLOS III

When the Third United Nations Conference took up the deep seabed, three models were on the table, and each corresponded to a different view of what the common heritage principle required.

Table 5.21 · The three models of deep seabed mining and the choice of 1982
ModelHow it worksWho supported itWhy it was rejected
1. The licensing (flag-State) modelThe Authority is a registry: it records claims and co-ordinates the use of the seabed, while the mining State's law governs the operator. Effectively, appropriation through a licence.Industrialised States with the technology — the "first-come" view.Contradicts Article 137's prohibition of appropriation, and gives nothing to the developing majority; the "mini-treaty" arrangements of 1982–84 were an attempt to build it outside the Convention.
2. The single-operator (Enterprise) modelAll mining is carried out by the Enterprise, the Authority's own operating organ; States and companies are excluded, or act only as the Enterprise's contractors.A section of the Group of 77, including some land-based producers, who wanted the benefits to flow directly into the international treasury.Technologically impracticable and financially impossible for the Authority; it would have paralysed the industry rather than internationalised it.
3. The parallel systemBoth tracks at once: the Enterprise mines in its reserved areas and in joint ventures, while States Parties and their sponsored entities mine under contract with the Authority; the Authority controls, co-ordinates and shares in the proceeds of both.The compromise coalition — the United States and other industrialised States on the one side, the Group of 77 on the other, with the Conference's negotiators in the middle.Adopted: Article 153 and Annex III, together with Articles 170 and Annex IV for the Enterprise.

The parallel system is therefore best described as a bargain between the equitable idea and the technical reality. Its logic is: mankind's resources will be extracted by the States and companies that have the capability to extract them, but only with the Authority's permission, on the Authority's terms, and with a share going to mankind. The mechanism that makes sharing possible is the reserved area, and the mechanism that makes control possible is the contract with the Authority.

B. The architecture of the parallel system

"Activities in the Area shall be organized, carried out and controlled by the Authority on behalf of mankind as a whole in accordance with the provisions of this Article and other relevant provisions of this Part, as well as the rules, regulations and procedures of the Authority, and the terms and conditions of the contract..."

UNCLOS, Article 153(1) — the master provision of the parallel system

Article 153(2) then identifies the four categories of actors who may conduct activities in the Area:

  • the Enterprise — the Authority's own organ, "which shall conduct its activities in accordance with Article 170 and Annex IV" (the international track);
  • States Parties and State enterprises;
  • natural or juridical persons possessing the nationality of a State Party, or effectively controlled by it or by its nationals, when sponsored by that State Party; and
  • any combination of the above, including joint arrangements with the Enterprise.

Article 153(4) completes the design by giving the Authority the right to take, at any time, any measures provided for in Part XI to ensure compliance, and by providing that a contract may be suspended or terminated only for a serious and persistent violation. The Authority's control is thus structural, not advisory: no plan of work, no mining.

C. The life-cycle of a deep seabed contract

Annex III of the Convention sets out the "basic conditions of prospecting, exploration and exploitation", and the Authority's regulations fill them in. The sequence below is the skeleton of every answer on the mechanics of the parallel system.

Table 5.22 · From prospecting to exploitation — the stages and their provisions
StageLegal basisWhat the actor may and may not do
1. ProspectingArt. 151 read with Annex III, art. 2Prospecting may be carried out by anyone, after notification to the Authority, without any exclusive rights. It confers no rights over the resources; it is the equivalent of a geological reconnaissance.
2. Application for a plan of workAnnex III, arts 3–7; Regulations on Prospecting and Exploration for Polymetallic Nodules (2013), Sulphides (2011) and Cobalt-rich Crusts (2012)States Parties, State enterprises and sponsored entities apply through the Authority, submitting, among other things, the coordinates of the area, a plan of work in two parts (see stage 4), evidence of financial and technical capability and of compliance with environmental requirements, and a certificate of sponsorship from the sponsoring State.
3. Consideration and approvalArt. 153(2)–(3); Art. 162(2)(j); Annex III, art. 6; 1994 Agreement, Annex section 3The Legal and Technical Commission examines the application, satisfies itself that the applicant is qualified and that the plan complies with the Convention and the regulations, and recommends it to the Council. The Council approves the plan of work (or an approval is deemed where the procedures of the 1994 Agreement so provide); the plan, once approved, is a contract binding the Authority and the contractor.
4. The reserved area — "banking"Annex III, arts 8 and 9Every application for exploitation in the Area must cover an area large enough and of a sufficient estimated commercial value to allow two mining operations. The applicant divides it into two parts of equal estimated commercial value; the Authority designates one part as the reserved area, reserved for the Authority through the Enterprise, or for developing States, or for entities sponsored by them. The other part is the applicant's contract area.
5. ExplorationArt. 153; Annex III, arts 3–4; the exploration regulationsThe contract confers on the contractor an exclusive right to explore for the specified category of resources in the contract area, subject to the Authority's general powers; exploration contracts for polymetallic nodules run for fifteen years (renewable), with obligations of reporting, monitoring, training and the relinquishment of part of the area as work proceeds (the practice which produced India's IOB area of 75,000 sq km from an original 150,000 sq km).
6. ExploitationArts 151, 153(3); Annex III, art. 13; the exploitation regulations (in preparation)A separate plan of work for exploitation is required; the contractor pays the financial contribution to the Authority — under the original Part XI a royalty or a combination of royalty and share of net proceeds, and under the 1994 Agreement and the draft exploitation regulations a market-oriented system of royalties and payments still under negotiation — together with contributions to the Authority's administrative costs and to the fund for developing States.
7. Supervision, suspension, terminationArt. 153(4)–(6); Annex III, arts 5, 18, 19; Art. 162(2)(w)–(z)The Authority monitors compliance, may inspect, and may suspend or terminate a contract for a serious and persistent violation of the Convention or the contract; the contractor's remedies lie before the Seabed Disputes Chamber.
8. Environmental duties throughoutArts 145, 146, 147, 209; 2011 advisory opinionEnvironmental baselines, environmental impact assessment, monitoring programmes, contingency plans, and the precautionary approach; the sponsoring State must exercise due diligence and adopt laws and regulations to secure the contractor's compliance.

D. Sponsorship: the hinge between the State and the contractor

Because a private company cannot sign a contract with the Authority on its own account, the parallel system runs through the institution of sponsorship, and sponsorship is the point at which the law of State responsibility enters the law of the sea.

  • Who may sponsor. A State Party may sponsor an applicant which it or its nationals control, or which has its nationality (Art. 153(2)(b); Annex III, art. 4(3)). The sponsoring State must present a certificate confirming that the applicant is under its effective control, and if the applicant has more than one nationality or more than one sponsor, the sponsors are jointly and severally liable (Annex III, art. 4(3)).
  • What sponsorship entails. The sponsoring State must ensure, within its legal system, that the sponsored contractor carries out its activities in conformity with the Convention and the contract (Art. 139(1); Annex III, art. 4(4)); it must adopt laws, regulations and administrative measures, and it must not allow activity to begin until the plan of work is approved. It remains responsible for the whole period of the activity, not merely at the moment of sponsorship.
  • The standard is due diligence, not guarantee. The 2011 advisory opinion of the Seabed Disputes Chamber (Case No. 17, §5.3 G) settled that the obligation is one of conduct: "no liability for the failure of a contractor to comply arises if the sponsoring State has taken all necessary and appropriate measures". The content of due diligence includes the precautionary approach, best environmental practices and environmental impact assessment, and the obligations are erga omnes — owed to the international community as a whole.
  • An Indian illustration. India's own contracts were originally held by the Government itself (through the Department of Ocean Development and later the Ministry of Earth Sciences) and are now held by its public-sector entity, with the Government as the sponsoring State — the clearest possible example of the State both as sponsor and as operator that the parallel system contemplates.

E. The Enterprise, and the fate of the first track

The Enterprise (Art. 170; Annex IV) was designed to be the Authority's own mining company: a self-financing organ with a governing board, a Director-General and a legal personality distinct from the Authority, entitled to work the reserved areas and to enter into joint ventures. Under the original Part XI, States Parties were to fund one mine site for it, and the Enterprise had rights of first refusal over the reserved areas.

The 1994 Implementation Agreement transformed the first track without abolishing it: the Enterprise's initial functions were to be performed by the Secretariat (Annex, section 2(1)); it was not to be established immediately; it was to conduct its initial operations through joint ventures rather than on its own; and the obligation of States Parties to fund its first mine site was removed. The consequence is that in the first four decades of the Authority's life the Enterprise has been a dormant organ, and the parallel system has operated, in practice, almost entirely through the second track — contracts with States and sponsored entities. Whether the reserved areas can be exploited by others while the Enterprise remains dormant, and on what conditions, is one of the open questions now before the Council. It is a question of real importance: the reserved areas are the substance of the bargain of 1982, and their fate determines whether "parallel" still means two tracks or only one.

F. The financial side: how mankind is paid

  • Annex III, article 13 required the Authority and the contractor to agree financial terms based on a choice between a royalty, a combination of royalty and profit-sharing, or a combination of royalty and a share of the proceeds — with a fixed royalty rate and, for the profit-sharing alternative, a ceiling on the contractor's administrative costs.
  • The 1994 Agreement (Annex, section 8) replaced that structure with a market-based approach: a royalty-only or combined system to be determined, and an annual fixed fee payable from the date of approval of the plan of work for exploration, with the Authority's administrative budget financed by contributions of States Parties until it becomes self-financing from royalty income.
  • The draft exploitation regulations (under negotiation since 2017) continue the debate: a royalty on the value of the metals (variously proposed at rates in the low single figures) and a profit-share ("net proceeds" contribution) once recoupment is complete, together with the "application fee", the "annual contract fee" and the environmental compensation fund. The rates remain the single most contested economic question in the Authority's work.
  • Article 82 — the bridge to the continental shelf. A coastal State exploiting the non-living resources of its continental shelf beyond 200 nm must make payments or contributions to the Authority, which distributes them among the States Parties "taking into account the interests and needs of developing States, particularly the least developed and the land-locked among them" (Art. 82(4)). The payments begin after five years of production and rise to 7 per cent. Article 82 is a remarkable provision: it carries the benefit-sharing idea of the common heritage even into areas of national jurisdiction — the continental shelf outside 200 nm is not the common heritage, but it pays tribute into the common pool.

G. The environmental régime of mining

The environmental obligations of §5.3 remain the substance of the modern dispute, and it is convenient to collect them here in the order in which a miner encounters them.

  1. Baseline data — the contractor must establish an environmental baseline against which change can be measured, in the contract area and in a reference area (the exploration regulations require a monitoring programme and the reporting of data to the Authority and to the public).
  2. Impact assessment — an environmental impact statement and an environmental management plan are conditions for any plan of work for exploitation, with a specified form and content, including an assessment of cumulative impacts.
  3. The precautionary approach and best environmental practice — drawn into the Convention's régime by the 2011 advisory opinion and written into the regulations.
  4. Monitoring, contingency and compensation — annual reports, periodic review by the Commission, response and contingency plans for incidents, and the environmental compensation fund proposed in the draft exploitation regulations.
  5. The sponsoring State's parallel obligations — laws, regulations and administrative measures, due diligence, and liability under Article 139(2) where its failure has caused damage, with liability shared with the contractor only where the State has itself failed to meet its obligations.

H. Evaluation: has the parallel system worked?

Table 5.23 · The parallel system assessed
SuccessesShortcomings
Non-appropriation has held: no State has been able to claim territory in the Area; every exploration title in existence is a contract with the Authority.Exploitation has not begun: in more than four decades, the Authority has regulated prospecting and exploration only; the mining code for exploitation is still unfinished.
Every major technological State and every major developing State is inside the system: China, India, Korea, Japan, France, Germany, Russia, Brazil, Nauru, Tonga, Kiribati, Jamaica, Poland and others hold contracts; the mini-treaties of the 1980s were abandoned.The Enterprise is dormant, so the "parallel" of the parallel system is theoretical; the reserved areas are unexploited, and the fate of that half of the bargain of 1982 is unresolved.
The system produced a usable body of environmental and sponsorship law: the 2011 advisory opinion is one of the most important environmental decisions of the century.The benefit-sharing has not yet produced a rupee of distributed revenue; the financial provisions remain contested and the Authority is financed by State contributions.
It gave land-locked and geographically disadvantaged States a place in the régime (Arts 140, 148) which no other resource régime offers.The land-based producer protection of the original Part XI — production ceilings and compensation — was dismantled in 1994, and the "economic assistance fund" has had little to do.
It created a forum — the Authority and the Chamber — in which the competing claims can be argued as law rather than settled by force or by the flag.Unilateral exploitation outside the Convention (see J below) revives the pre-1967 problem in a new form.

I. The minerals, the technology and the geology — a short factual primer

Answers on this topic are much stronger when the resource is described accurately. The three commercial targets of the Area are:

  • Polymetallic nodules — potato-sized concretions of manganese, iron, nickel, copper and cobalt lying on abyssal plains, the best-known field being the Clarion–Clipperton Zone in the eastern Pacific and the Central Indian Ocean Basin on which India holds its title; they need no drilling, only collection, which is why they are the first target of the industry.
  • Polymetallic sulphides — metal-rich deposits formed at hydrothermal vents on mid-ocean ridges, containing copper, zinc, gold and silver; they are the target of India's second contract, on the Carlsberg Ridge, and the most contested environmentally, because vent ecosystems are unique and fragile.
  • Cobalt-rich ferromanganese crusts — pavements on seamounts and ridges rich in cobalt, platinum and rare earths; the third category in the Authority's regulations, and the least advanced commercially.

The technology is the reason for the delay. Deep-sea mining requires a collector vehicle on the seabed, a riser or lift system to raise ore to the surface, and a surface support vessel able to process or transport it — an industrial chain which has been tested only in prototype and which carries the risk of sediment plumes, noise and the destruction of slow-growing benthic communities. Those risks, and not the legal architecture, are the reason the exploitation regulations remain unfinished, and they are why the debate of the 2020s has been conducted in the language of environmental law as much as in the language of the common heritage.

J. The modern contest, 2021–2026 (illustrative material, not settled law)

Since 2021 the régime of §5.4 has been under the most sustained pressure of its history. The following account is a summary of reported developments, offered to illustrate how the legal framework operates in practice; a candidate should write the law from the Convention and update the facts from the Authority's website.

  • The two-year rule (2021). The "two-year rule" — section 1, paragraph 15 of the Annex to the 1994 Implementation Agreement — provides that if a State Party applies for approval of a plan of work for exploitation while the Council is still completing the rules, the Council must consider the application and the rules together, and if the rules are not completed within two years the Council may nonetheless consider and provisionally approve the plan by the applicable majority. In June 2021 Nauru, the sponsoring State of NORI (a subsidiary of The Metals Company), gave notice under that provision in respect of an exploitation application; the two years expired in July 2023 without the completion of the code, and the Council instead resolved to continue negotiations with a view to adopting the regulations — the deadline has since been repeatedly extended, and the impasse is described as the "mining code crisis".
  • The moratorium campaign. A coalition of States — joined over time by a reported forty or more, including France, Chile, Costa Rica, Fiji, Micronesia, Palau, Panama, New Zealand and others — has called for a precautionary pause or moratorium on deep-sea mining pending adequate scientific knowledge; the coalition's position has framed the Council's debates and has been echoed by the scientists who warn that the ecosystems of the abyssal plains cannot be restored once destroyed.
  • Unilateralism under national law (2025–2026). In April 2025 the United States issued a presidential order reviving the Deep Seabed Hard Mineral Resources Act of 1980 and directing the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration to process applications for domestic licences for deep-sea mining beyond national jurisdiction. The Metals Company's United States subsidiary applied under that scheme; the licence areas were reported as covering some 65,000 sq km and 122,000 sq km respectively, and a decision was expected in early 2027. Because the United States is not a party to UNCLOS, the application raises in acute form the question what legal effect a national licence can have over the Area, whose resources Article 137(1) declares incapable of appropriation and whose minerals may under Article 137(3) be acquired only in accordance with Part XI.
  • The Authority's own proceedings (2026). In July 2026 the Seabed Disputes Chamber made an order on provisional measures in proceedings arising from the Authority's handling of a contractor's exploration contract and the Legal and Technical Commission's inquiry into it; the Chamber declined to suspend the inquiry, called upon the parties to cooperate, and left the substantive question of the contract to the merits (see the Authority's and the Tribunal's publications for the exact terms). Proposals that the Chamber be asked for an advisory opinion on the legal consequences of unilateral mining were debated in the Assembly and the Council; only those two organs may request such an opinion under Article 191, and a request has not been made as at the date of writing. The March 2026 session of the Council ended without agreement on the exploitation regulations.

The legal significance of all this is simple to state and worth stating in an examination: the principle of non-appropriation in Article 137 is the one part of Part XI that no subsequent agreement has altered, and it is the provision on which any argument against unilateral exploitation must rest. The controversy is therefore a test of whether the common heritage principle is law or only aspiration — precisely the question raised at the end of §5.3.

K. The examination angle

Model skeleton — "Explain the parallel system of mining under UNCLOS" (15 or 20 marks)
  1. The problem and the three models (2 marks). Table 5.21: licensing, single-operator, parallel — and why the third was adopted.
  2. The legal architecture (4 marks). Art. 153(1)–(4) with its four categories of actor; the Authority as controller; the contract as the instrument of control. Add the institutional context: the Authority, the Council, the Legal and Technical Commission, the Enterprise, the Seabed Disputes Chamber.
  3. The two tracks (3 marks). Track 1: the Enterprise (Art. 170, Annex IV) and its fate after the 1994 Agreement. Track 2: States Parties, State enterprises and sponsored entities, with the sponsorship certificate and due diligence.
  4. The reserved area (3 marks). Annex III, arts 8–9: two parts of equal estimated commercial value, designation of the reserved area, who may work it, and the current controversy about the dormant Enterprise.
  5. The life-cycle (3 marks). Table 5.22: prospecting → application → approval → exploration (15 years, relinquishment) → exploitation → payments (Annex III, art. 13; 1994 Agreement, Annex section 8) → supervision and termination.
  6. Environment and responsibility (2 marks). Arts 145–147; the 2011 advisory opinion: due diligence, precautionary approach, EIA, erga omnes.
  7. Evaluation (3 marks). Table 5.23 using the modern controversy — the two-year rule, the moratorium coalition, unilateral national licensing — and conclude that the system's architecture has held while its economics and its environmental science are still being negotiated.
Potential questions and where the material is
  • "Discuss the concept of the common heritage of mankind with reference to the resources of the international seabed area." → §5.3 in full, with Tables 5.18–5.20 and Art. 311(6).
  • "Explain the parallel system of mining of the international seabed area." → this section, with Fig. 5.11 and Tables 5.21–5.22.
  • "What is the role of the International Seabed Authority?" → §5.3 F (organs) and §5.4 B–F (functions), plus §5.6 for the Chamber.
  • "Write a note on the reserved area." → §5.4 C, stage 4, and Fig. 5.12.
  • "Write a note on Article 82." → §5.4 F, last bullet, with §5.1.3.
  • "What is India's interest in the Area?" → §5.3 I and §5.5.
