5.5 · Indian Maritime Interests, Policy and Law

India is a maritime State in every sense: a peninsula with a coastline of some 7,500 km (roughly 5,400 km of it mainland), nine coastal States and four coastal Union Territories, more than a thousand islands, an exclusive economic zone of about two million square kilometres — an area nearly two-thirds the size of the land mass — one of the world's most extensive extended continental shelf claims, and a dependence on the sea for the overwhelming share of its trade. This section puts together the law that India has made for the sea — the constitutional foundation, the statutes, the claims, the institutions and the policy — and its disputes, so that the four preceding sections can be answered with Indian material.

A. The stakes: what India draws from the sea

Table 5.24 · India's principal maritime interests, with examples
InterestContent and illustrationLegal basis
Territorial integrity and securityControl of the 12 nm belt, the island territories, the Gulf of Kachchh and Gulf of Mannar, the Andaman and Nicobar Islands commanding the approaches to the Malacca Strait; coastal security after 26/11; the Andaman and Nicobar tri-service Command (the only one of its kind).Arts 2–16 UNCLOS; the Maritime Zones Act 1976, s. 3; the Coast Guard Act 1978 (see Table 5.25).
Resources of the EEZFisheries (the livelihood of millions, and a recurring source of friction with Sri Lanka), hydrocarbons (Bombay High, Krishna–Godavari, Cauvery and the new deep-water finds), polymetallic nodules in the Central Indian Ocean Basin.Arts 55–57, 61–62, 77 UNCLOS; the 1976 Act, ss. 6–7; the Maritime Zones of India (Regulation of Fishing by Foreign Vessels) Act, 1981.
The extended continental shelfClaims to shelf beyond 200 nm in the Arabian Sea (including the Central Arabian Sea, revised in 2025) and the Bay of Bengal, prosecuted through the CLCS; a possible entitlement of the order of a million square kilometres.Art. 76 UNCLOS; India's submissions of 2009 and 2025; the 1976 Act, s. 6.
The Area — deep-sea miningTwo exploration contracts (a 75,000 sq km polymetallic nodule area in the CIOB and the 2025 Carlsberg Ridge sulphide contract), an application for the Afanasy-Nikitin seamount, and the Deep Ocean Mission with the Matsya-6000 submersible programme.Part XI UNCLOS; the Authority's regulations; the common heritage principle (§5.3).
Trade and shippingSome 95 per cent of India's trade by volume and about 70 per cent by value moves by sea; twelve major ports and more than two hundred non-major ports; the Sagarmala programme; a merchant fleet and one of the world's largest supplies of seafarers.The Merchant Shipping Act 1958; the Major Port Authorities Act 2021; the Indian Ports Act 1908; the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act 2017.
Sea lanes and the Indian Ocean orderFreedom of navigation, anti-piracy escort operations (Operation Sankalp; the 2024 missions that recovered the MV Ruen), escort and survey operations; the Indian Ocean Naval Symposium; IORA; the Quad's maritime domain awareness.Arts 87, 100–107, 110–111 UNCLOS; the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act 2022; the SUA Act 2002.
Science, technology and the deep oceanAntarctic, Arctic and Southern Ocean research stations; the tsunami early-warning system of INCOIS; ocean observation; the deep-ocean mission; capacity in marine biotechnology.Arts 143, 238–244 (marine scientific research), 246–255; Part XIII generally; the BBNJ Agreement on marine genetic resources.
Coastal environment and climateCoastal regulation zones, mangrove and coral protection, the Sundarbans and the Gulf of Mannar biosphere reserves, erosion and sea-level rise on the Kerala and Odisha coasts, cyclone preparedness.Arts 192, 194, 207 UNCLOS; the Environment (Protection) Act 1986 and the CRZ notifications; the Biological Diversity Act 2002; the Wetlands (Conservation and Management) Rules 2017.

B. The constitutional basis: Article 297 and the federal division of competence

"All lands, minerals and other things of value underlying the ocean within the territorial waters, or the continental shelf, or the exclusive economic zone, of India shall vest in the Union and be held for the purposes of the Union."

Constitution of India, Article 297 (as amended by the Constitution (Fortieth Amendment) Act, 1976, which inserted the reference to the exclusive economic zone)

Article 297 is the constitutional root of India's maritime claims, and it answers in advance the federal question that arose in Republic of Italy v. Union of India (case 34). Its elements are:

  • Vesting in the Union — the living and non-living resources of the territorial sea, of the continental shelf and of the EEZ belong to the Union and not to the coastal States; a coastal State of the Union cannot, therefore, legislate to control fishing or mineral exploitation in the maritime zones, and any such State law would be beyond its competence.
  • Held for the purposes of the Union — the resources are held in a public trust-like capacity for the whole country, which permits Parliament to regulate, license and share them.
  • Legislative competence — Parliament legislates on "fishing and fisheries beyond territorial waters" (Union List, Entry 21), on "maritime shipping and navigation" (Entry 25), on major ports (Entry 27) and on foreign affairs and treaties (Entries 13–14); the territorial sea, the shelf and the EEZ are thus Union territory in the relevant sense, while fisheries within the territorial sea are within State competence. The Supreme Court's judgment in case 34 relied on this scheme to hold that the Union, and not Kerala, was competent to try the Italian marines.
  • Powers of the Union over a shelf not yet delimited — the vesting under Article 297 is not limited to areas where India's boundary with a neighbour has been settled; the Union may exercise its rights over India's entitlement and must respect the rights of other States under UNCLOS.

Two other constitutional provisions matter. The Territories of India (Art. 1(3)) include India's territorial sea, and the concept of Union territory extends to the zones. And the 40th Amendment (1976), passed at the height of the UNCLOS III negotiations, was the constitutional counterpart of the Maritime Zones Act of the same year — a demonstration of how the law of the sea required constitutional amendment as well as legislation.

C. The legislative framework

(i) The Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and Other Maritime Zones Act, 1976

The 1976 Act is the parent statute. It was enacted three years before the conclusion of UNCLOS, on the basis of the draft convention then under negotiation, and it remains the instrument by which India claims its zones. Its principal provisions are:

  • Territorial waters (s. 3) — the territorial sea extends to 12 nautical miles from the baseline; the baseline is "the low-water line along the coast", with the Central Government empowered to notify straight baselines to be drawn in accordance with international law, including the straight baselines which are appropriate for a coast with "deeply indented and irregular" configuration or with a fringe of islands — the statutory echo of the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case (case 30).
  • Historic waters (s. 4) — the Central Government may declare waters to be historic waters; the Gulf of Kachchh and the Gulf of Mannar have been treated as historic waters, the classification mattering for the Sir Creek dispute with Pakistan.
  • Contiguous zone (s. 5) — the contiguous zone extends to 24 nautical miles, with the Union's rights of enforcement over customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary laws — the Indian version of Article 33.
  • Continental shelf (s. 6) — the shelf extends to 200 nautical miles or to the outer edge of the continental margin where that extends beyond 200 nm, in accordance with Article 76; and it provides that the natural resources of the shelf vest in the Union.
  • Exclusive economic zone (s. 7) — the EEZ extends to 200 nautical miles, and the Union has sovereign rights for the exploration, exploitation, conservation and management of the natural resources, jurisdiction over artificial islands and installations, marine scientific research and the protection of the marine environment, and the other rights recognised by international law.
  • Enforcement and rules (ss. 8–9 and following) — the Act authorises the making of rules and the taking of measures for the enforcement of the Union's rights, and the amendment of the 1976 Act by the Environment (Protection) Act and other statutes has extended its reach (for example, to the regulation of fishing, pollution and the installation of structures).

Two features of the Act deserve emphasis in an examination. First, it is declaratory of India's claims and constitutive of the Union's powers: it is not a grant of rights by the international community but a national assertion which India subsequently brought into conformity with UNCLOS by its ratification in 1995. Secondly, the Act is the source of the Union's legislative competence on which the Supreme Court relied in case 34: because the incident on the Enrica Lexie occurred in the contiguous zone and over the EEZ, and because the Maritime Zones Act and Article 297 vest the relevant powers in the Union, Kerala could not insist on its own prosecution.

(ii) The other statutes
Table 5.25 · India's principal maritime statutes
StatuteSubjectLaw-of-the-sea connection
Maritime Zones Act 1976Territorial sea, contiguous zone, shelf, EEZ, historic waters.Arts 3, 33, 55–57, 76–77 UNCLOS; the statutory basis of India's claims.
Maritime Zones of India (Regulation of Fishing by Foreign Vessels) Act, 1981Prohibits fishing in the Indian maritime zones by foreign vessels except under licence and agreement; enforcement powers and penalties.Arts 61–62 UNCLOS — the coastal State's right to conserve and manage the living resources of the EEZ and to determine the allowable catch and its own capacity to harvest.
Coast Guard Act, 1978Constitution, functions and powers of the Indian Coast Guard: safety, pollution response, anti-smuggling and the enforcement of maritime law.Enforcement of the coastal State's rights in the contiguous zone and EEZ (Art. 73).
Suppression of Unlawful Acts against Safety of Maritime Navigation and Fixed Platforms on Continental Shelf Act, 2002 (as amended)Offences against the safety of ships and of fixed platforms; jurisdiction and prosecution.Implementing the 1988 SUA Convention and Protocol; complements Arts 100–107.
Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022Defines piracy and related offences committed on the high seas and beyond India's jurisdiction; confers jurisdiction on Indian courts over pirates, extends to Indian ships and persons, and provides for punishment up to imprisonment for life; designates special courts.Direct legislative implementation of Arts 100–107 UNCLOS and of the SUA régime; the legal basis of India's naval counter-piracy prosecutions (the 2026 convictions in the Ruen and Al-Kambar cases, §5.1.5).

Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 and the CRZ notificationsStandards and restrictions on coastal activity, including the coastal regulation zone notifications, and the "no development zone".Arts 192, 194, 207–208 UNCLOS (preserve and protect the marine environment; land-based sources of pollution).
Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972; Biological Diversity Act, 2002Protected areas including marine national parks, and access to biological resources and benefit sharing.Arts 194(5), 246(5) UNCLOS (rare or fragile ecosystems); the BBNJ régime on marine genetic resources.
Offshore Areas Mineral (Development and Regulation) Act, 2002Regulates mineral development in India's offshore areas within national jurisdiction (territorial sea, shelf, EEZ), with licensing by the Union.Arts 77, 81 UNCLOS (coastal State's exclusive right to the resources of the shelf and its rights over the EEZ seabed); the domestic counterpart of the Authority's régime, which applies only beyond national jurisdiction.
The Oilfields (Regulation and Development) Act, 1948, and the Petroleum and Natural Gas Rules, 1959Petroleum operations, including in offshore areas, and the licensing of blocks (with the Directorate General of Hydrocarbons).Petroleum operations in the shelf and the EEZ — the Bombay High field, the Krishna–Godavari and Cauvery basins and the recent deep-water discoveries; Arts 77 and 81 UNCLOS.

Three gaps are worth noticing, because they are the stuff of examination conclusions. India has no dedicated domestic deep-sea mining Act for the Area: its contractors act under the Authority's régime and its public entity under the Government's sponsorship rather than under a national licensing statute like the United States' Deep Seabed Hard Mineral Resources Act. India has no comprehensive marine spatial planning or ocean governance statute; the Blue Economy framework has remained a policy document. And India has no integrated maritime security legislation: the coastal security structure has been administrative, with the Marine Police and the Coast Guard sharing functions with the Navy.

D. India's maritime zones and boundaries at a glance

E. The disputes: settled and outstanding

  • Sri Lanka (1974 and 1976) — the agreements fixed the boundary in the Palk Bay, Palk Strait and the Gulf of Mannar and the adjacent waters; the cession of Kachchatheevu (1974) is politically contested and has repeatedly been raised in Parliament and before the Supreme Court, and the practical consequence is the recurring arrest of Indian fishermen by Sri Lanka and of Sri Lankan fishermen by India in the waters around the island (a question of the territorial sea, the historic fishing practices and the 1974/1976 boundary, and not of the EEZ boundary, which the two agreements did not settle in the same instrument).
  • Maldives (1976) — the maritime boundary in the Arabian Sea and the Gulf of Mannar region, concluded by the exchange of letters of 1976 and since implemented without difficulty.
  • Indonesia (1974, 1977) and Thailand (1978) — the boundaries in the Andaman Sea and the Straits approaches, completed by the tripartite arrangements with Thailand and Indonesia concerning the Andaman Sea (the 1978 India–Thailand–Indonesia trijunction).
  • Myanmar (1986) — the maritime boundary in the Andaman Sea and the Bay of Bengal, part of the geometry of the Bay of Bengal arbitration (case 35).
  • Bangladesh (2014) — the settled boundary emerging from the PCA award of 7 July 2014 (case 35), which India accepted; the dispute had lasted four decades and produced the "grey area" in the waters south of the Sundarbans.
  • Pakistan (Sir Creek) — the outstanding dispute: whether the boundary in the Rann of Kachchh and the Sir Creek estuary is the eastern or the western bank of the creek, which determines the starting point of the maritime boundary and hence the ownership of a valuable fishing and hydrocarbon area. The dispute has been before the two Governments since 1969 and is one of the oldest unresolved maritime boundary questions in the world.
  • Extended shelf disputes — India's CLCS submissions of 2009 and 2025 have attracted objections or statements from Pakistan, Myanmar, Sri Lanka and others; under the CLCS's rules, where a land or maritime dispute exists the Commission makes no recommendation on the disputed portion, which is why part of India's Arabian Sea claim has been deferred.
  • The Indian Ocean order — India's disputes with China over research and naval presence in the Indian Ocean, the dispute with Sri Lanka over fishing, and the questions of the Chagos Archipelago, Diego Garcia and the Mauritius–Maldives boundaries are the regional context in which India's maritime policy operates.

F. Policy: the doctrines and the documents

  • SAGAR (2015) — "Security and Growth for All in the Region": India's Indian Ocean doctrine, emphasising cooperation with maritime neighbours, capacity building, respect for international law and the freedom of navigation.
  • The Indo-Pacific Oceans Initiative (2019) — seven pillars: maritime security; maritime ecology; maritime resources; capacity building and resource sharing; disaster risk reduction and management; science, technology and academic cooperation; and trade, connectivity and maritime transport. It is the best Indian statement of a comprehensive maritime policy, and it maps neatly onto the subject-matter of this topic.
  • The Blue Economy framework — the Ministry of Earth Sciences' policy framework for the sustainable use of ocean resources (fisheries, aquaculture, shipping, renewable energy, deep-sea mining, marine biotechnology and tourism) with an emphasis on conservation, and the "Blue Economy" as an element of the national growth strategy.
  • The Deep Ocean Mission (approved 2021) — a mission of the Ministry of Earth Sciences with components including the development of the Matsya-6000 manned submersible, the deep-ocean survey and exploration (including India's programme in the Central Indian Ocean Basin), deep-sea mining technology, ocean climate change advisory services, and the exploration of marine biodiversity; reported expenditure commitments of the order of ₹4,000 crore over its five-year horizon.
  • Maritime transport policy — Maritime India Vision 2030, the Sagarmala programme, the Major Port Authorities Act 2021, the Inland Vessels Act 2021, proposals for a "Maritime Development Fund", and the promotion of India as a shipbuilding and transhipment hub.
  • The BBNJ Agreement — India signed the Agreement on the conservation and sustainable use of marine biological diversity beyond national jurisdiction in September 2023; with its entry into force on 17 January 2026, its régime for marine protected areas, environmental impact assessment, marine genetic resources and capacity building becomes part of the legal framework in which India's Indian Ocean interests will be conducted, and Indian oceanography has a direct interest in the scientific and benefit-sharing provisions.
  • Maritime security architecture — the Indian Navy, the Indian Coast Guard (1978), the Andaman and Nicobar Command, the Information Fusion Centre–Indian Ocean Region (IFC-IOR, 2018), the coastal security scheme after 26/11 (Marine Police, Sagar Prahari Bal), and the regional naval groupings and exercises (IONS, MILAN, Malabar, the Quad's maritime domain awareness).

G. The teaching core: five Indian propositions

  1. India asserted its zones before the Convention was concluded. The 1976 Act, the 40th Amendment and the 1981 fishing legislation preceded ratification in 1995 — India was, in the classical phrase, both a maker and a taker of the law of the sea.
  2. The Union, not the States, holds the maritime zones. Article 297 and the Supreme Court's decision in case 34 make this the constitutional rule; the 1976 Act is the working statute.
  3. India prefers negotiated settlement and adjudication to unilateralism in boundary matters. It settled boundaries with Sri Lanka, the Maldives, Indonesia, Thailand and Myanmar, accepted the Bay of Bengal award even though it lost the bulk of the disputed area, and submitted to the ITLOS provisional measures order in the Enrica Lexie case.
  4. India is a stakeholder in the common heritage. Its pioneer-investor history, its contracts with the Authority, its extended-shelf claims and its participation in the mining-code negotiations make it both a promoter and a user of the régime of Part XI (§§5.3–5.4).
  5. India's maritime policy has become integrated. The progression from the 1976 Act (claims) to the 1981 fishing Act (resources) to SAGAR and the Indo-Pacific Oceans Initiative (strategy) to the Deep Ocean Mission and the BBNJ signature (science and the global commons) shows a State moving from assertion to regulation to stewardship — the arc of the whole topic.

H. The examination angle

Model skeleton — "Discuss India's maritime interests, policy and law" (15 or 20 marks)
  1. Interests (3 marks). Table 5.24: security, EEZ resources, extended shelf, the Area, trade, sea lanes, science, environment — each with one example.
  2. The constitutional basis (2 marks). Art. 297; the 40th Amendment 1976; the Union List entries; the holding in case 34 (Union competent, Kerala not) — with the point that the EEZ is not "territory" but a zone of sovereign rights and jurisdiction.
  3. The legislative framework (4 marks). The Maritime Zones Act 1976 (ss. 3–7); the 1981 Fishing Act; the Coast Guard Act 1978; the SUA Act 2002; the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act 2022; the Merchant Shipping Act 1958; the Admiralty Act 2017; the CRZ notifications; the OAMDR Act 2002 — Table 5.25, plus the three gaps.
  4. The boundaries (3 marks). Refer to Table 5.16 and Fig. 5.13: the settled agreements with Sri Lanka, the Maldives, Indonesia, Thailand and Myanmar; the 2014 award with Bangladesh; the unresolved Sir Creek dispute; the CLCS submissions of 2009 and 2025.
  5. Policy (3 marks). SAGAR 2015; the Indo-Pacific Oceans Initiative 2019 and its seven pillars; the Blue Economy framework; the Deep Ocean Mission; Maritime India Vision 2030; the BBNJ signature and entry into force.
  6. Evaluation (3 marks). India as a maker of the law (1976 Act, pioneer investor, ISA participation) and as a taker (acceptance of the Bay of Bengal award, compliance with the ITLOS order in Enrica Lexie, adherence to CLCS procedure); the unfinished business — Sir Creek, extended shelf, deep-sea mining law, the Sri Lankan fishing question, and the balance between resource exploitation and environmental protection.
  7. Conclusion (2 marks). India's maritime future lies in the Indian Ocean and in the Area, and its legal instruments are increasingly the instruments of the Convention — which is why this section, the smallest in the syllabus, depends on all the others.
Short questions with their points
  • "Write a note on the Maritime Zones Act, 1976." The 12/24/200 nm structure, the baseline provision, historic waters, the rights declared, the Union's vesting, and the fact that it anticipated UNCLOS.
  • "Discuss Article 297 of the Constitution." Vesting of the resources of the territorial sea, shelf and EEZ in the Union; the 40th Amendment; the legislative consequences; and the use made of it in Republic of Italy (case 34).
  • "What is India's position in the international seabed régime?" §5.3 I and §5.4: pioneer investor, CIOB contract of 1987 and the 2017 extension, the 2025 Carlsberg Ridge contract, the Afanasy-Nikitin application, sponsorship by the Government, participation in the ISA.
  • "Write a note on the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022." Definition of piracy with reference to Arts 100–107; jurisdiction over the high seas and beyond; life imprisonment; special courts; and the practical use made of it — the 2026 convictions of 44 persons in the Ruen and Al-Kambar cases after the Navy's 2024 operations (§5.1.5).
  • "Discuss the Sir Creek dispute." The Rann of Kachchh boundary, the question of the eastern or western bank, the 1969 talks, the effect on the maritime boundary and the resources at stake; the difference between a boundary dispute and a delimitation dispute.

5.6 · The International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea

The Convention did not leave the settlement of disputes to the general machinery of international law. It built a court of its own — the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea — and gave it a place in the most elaborate system of compulsory jurisdiction that States have ever accepted: Part XV of UNCLOS. This section is marked in the syllabus as a topic in itself, and rightly so, because ITLOS is where the Convention stops being a text and becomes a decision. It also has a distinctly Indian history: two Indian judges have sat on it, one of them presided over it, and the most famous Indian maritime case of the century — Enrica Lexie — came before it.

A. The place of ITLOS in Part XV

Part XV has two layers. The first (Section 1, Articles 279–285) requires the parties to a dispute concerning the interpretation or application of the Convention to settle it by peaceful means of their own choosing, and to proceed expeditiously to an exchange of views. The second (Section 2, Articles 286–296) creates compulsory procedures entailing binding decisions: if no settlement is reached by the means chosen, the dispute may be submitted at the request of any party to a court or tribunal having jurisdiction.

Where there is no declaration or agreement selecting a forum, arbitration under Annex VII is the default — a point of great practical importance, because it is arbitration and not the Court that has decided most of the modern law-of-the-sea cases, including Enrica Lexie and the Bay of Bengal dispute. Article 287 permits a State to choose, by declaration, one or more of four means: the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea; the International Court of Justice; arbitration under Annex VII; and special arbitration under Annex VIII for particular categories of dispute (fisheries, pollution, navigation and marine scientific research). The choice of forum is thus itself a matter of State policy, and the pattern of declarations tells you where each State's interests lie.

Table 5.26 · The dispute-settlement machinery of UNCLOS and the place of ITLOS
ProvisionWhat it doesITLOS's role
Arts 279–280Obligation to settle disputes peacefully; freedom to choose the means.None directly; the framework.
Art. 281Where the parties have agreed on a peaceful procedure, that procedure applies unless the agreement excludes further procedures.Determines whether recourse to the Tribunal is excluded — a frequent preliminary objection.
Art. 283Duty to exchange views regarding settlement.Compliance with it is a condition of admissibility (considered in Enrica Lexie).
Art. 286The gateway to compulsory jurisdiction where no settlement has been reached.—
Art. 287Choice of forum: ITLOS, ICJ, Annex VII arbitration, Annex VIII special arbitration; the default is Annex VII.The Tribunal is one of the four; its jurisdiction exists where a State has chosen it or where the parties agree.
Art. 288Jurisdiction over disputes concerning the interpretation or application of the Convention, and over questions submitted under other agreements.The scope of the Tribunal's contentious jurisdiction.
Art. 290Provisional measures: the court or tribunal may prescribe them; paragraph 5 allows the Tribunal to act, pending the constitution of an arbitral tribunal, if the urgency so requires.The most-used head of ITLOS jurisdiction in practice — Arctic Sunrise (2013), Enrica Lexie (2015), San Padre Pio (2019).
Art. 292Prompt release of a detained vessel and its crew on the posting of a reasonable bond or other financial security, where the flag State alleges non-compliance; failing agreement between the parties within ten days, the application may be made to ITLOS.A residual compulsory jurisdiction unique to the Tribunal: the flag State gains a forum even without any forum declaration.
Arts 293–296Applicable law (the Convention and other rules of international law not incompatible with it); preliminary proceedings on certain continental shelf claims; exhaustion of local remedies; and the finality of decisions, which "shall be complied with by all the parties to the dispute".The Tribunal applies these in every case; its judgments are final and binding.
Arts 297–299Limitations and optional exceptions: certain disputes (fisheries, marine scientific research, boundary delimitation before the Convention's entry into force, law-enforcement and security matters) may be excluded by declaration under Article 298.Determines the reach of all the compulsory forums, including ITLOS.
Part XI, Section 5 and Annex VI, Part XI (Arts 186–191)The Seabed Disputes Chamber of ITLOS: jurisdiction over disputes relating to the Area and advisory jurisdiction over legal questions arising in the Area.The Chamber is a branch of the Tribunal (see §5.3 F–G and §5.4).
Annex VI, art. 21Advisory jurisdiction: the Tribunal may give an advisory opinion on a legal question where an international agreement related to the purposes of the Convention so provides.The basis of the 2015 fisheries advisory opinion and of the 2024 climate advisory opinion.

B. Composition, election and the chambers

  • Twenty-one independent members (Annex VI, art. 2), elected by the States Parties by secret ballot from persons nominated by States Parties, each of which may nominate not more than two persons. The members must enjoy "the highest reputation for fairness and integrity" and have "recognized competence in the field of the law of the sea" (art. 2(1)); no two members may be nationals of the same State; and the composition must reflect "the representation of the principal legal systems of the world and equitable geographical distribution", with not fewer than three members from each of the geographical groups established by the General Assembly (art. 2(2)).
  • Nine-year terms, renewable (art. 5). The first election in 1996 was staggered so that a third of the membership would be renewed every three years; elections are held at the Meeting of States Parties, usually in New York.
  • President and Vice-President are elected by the members for three years (art. 12), and the Registrar heads the Registry.
  • Incompatible activities (art. 7): a member may not exercise any political or administrative function, or associate actively with or be financially interested in any enterprise concerned with the exploration or exploitation of the sea or its resources, and may not act as agent, counsel or advocate in any case before the Tribunal or before other bodies of the Convention. The rule is stricter than for many other tribunals because the subject-matter of the law of the sea is commercial as well as legal.
  • Ad hoc judges — where the bench does not include a judge of the nationality of a party, that party may choose a person to sit as a judge ad hoc in that case, so that neither party is disadvantaged (the rule which gave Kenya Judge Guillaume in Somalia v. Kenya and India its nominees in the cases in which it appeared).
  • Chambers (Annex VI, arts 14–15): the Tribunal forms a Seabed Disputes Chamber of eleven members and may form a Chamber of Summary Procedure of five members, and special chambers for fisheries, marine environment and maritime delimitation, as well as ad hoc chambers at the request of the parties. The best-known use of the facility is the Special Chamber which decided Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (2017) and Mauritius/Maldives (2023).
  • Seat and constitution. The Tribunal sits at Hamburg and began its work on 1 October 1996; its Rules of the Tribunal were adopted on 28 October 1997 and have since been amended. The Seabed Disputes Chamber may sit, and advise, elsewhere when convenient.
  • Applicable law and finality. The Tribunal applies the Convention and "other rules of international law not incompatible with this Convention" (Art. 293), and its decisions are final and binding on the parties, who are under an obligation to comply with them (Art. 296; Annex VI, art. 33 provides for interpretation).

C. Jurisdiction in outline

  1. Contentious jurisdiction by consent — where the parties have accepted the Tribunal's jurisdiction under Article 287, or where they have agreed to submit a particular dispute to it by special agreement (Art. 288(1) covers disputes concerning the interpretation or application of the Convention; Art. 288(2) covers questions submitted under other agreements conferring jurisdiction on the Tribunal).
  2. Provisional measures pending arbitration (Art. 290(5)) — if a dispute has been submitted to an Annex VII tribunal which has not yet been constituted and the Tribunal considers that prima facie the tribunal would have jurisdiction, the Tribunal may prescribe provisional measures which the parties must comply with (Art. 290(6)). This is the head of jurisdiction that has produced the best-known ITLOS orders, and it is the only remedy available in the interval before the arbitral tribunal exists.
  3. Prompt release (Art. 292) — where the authorities of a State Party detain a vessel flying the flag of another State Party and the flag State alleges a failure to comply with the Convention's provisions for release on bond, the application may be made to ITLOS if the parties have not agreed on another forum within ten days. The object is to protect the shipowner and crew from the coastal State's delays: the Tribunal does not decide the merits of the fisheries offence, only whether the bond demanded was reasonable and the vessel should be released.
  4. Advisory jurisdiction — the full Tribunal under Annex VI, article 21 (where an international agreement related to the purposes of the Convention so provides), and the Seabed Disputes Chamber under Article 191 (legal questions arising in the Area). This jurisdiction has become, in practice, the most influential part of the Tribunal's work.
  5. Seabed disputes — the Chamber's jurisdiction under Article 187 over disputes between States Parties, between a State Party and the Authority, between parties to a contract (including a contractor and the Authority), and over alleged excess of jurisdiction by the Authority. Its decisions are enforceable in the same way as other arbitral awards binding the parties.

D. The case law of the Tribunal and its chambers

Table 5.27 · The leading decisions of ITLOS and its chambers
CaseYear(s)SubjectHolding worth remembering
The M/V "Saiga" (No. 1) St Vincent and the Grenadines v. Guinea1997Prompt release (Art. 292)The bond must be reasonable and must relate to the alleged offence under the Convention; a bond calculated to satisfy customs and tax claims unrelated to the vessel's release is not reasonable. The Tribunal freed the vessel on a bond of US$ 400,000.
The M/V "Saiga" (No. 2) St Vincent and the Grenadines v. Guinea1999Merits: bunkering, hot pursuit, use of forceA coastal State's enforcement in the EEZ is governed by Art. 73, and the law of the sea does not authorise the extension of customs legislation to the EEZ. The use of force in stopping a vessel must be avoided as far as possible and, if unavoidable, must not exceed what is reasonable and necessary; the Tribunal awarded compensation for the unlawful arrest.
"Camouco" Panama v. France2000Prompt releaseSet out the factors for the reasonableness of a bond: gravity of the alleged offence, penalties faced, value of the detained vessel and cargo, and the amount of the bond demanded — not merely the coastal State's fiscal interest.
"Monte Confurco" Seychelles v. France · "Grand Prince" Belize v. France2000 · 2001Prompt release; fishing in the EEZ; proof of flagA coastal State may require a bond in respect of the conservation offence; but the applicant must establish the vessel's nationality — in Grand Prince the Tribunal found that it had no jurisdiction because the applicant had not proved that the vessel was registered in Belize.
"Volga" Russian Federation v. Australia · "Juno Trader" St Vincent v. Guinea-Bissau · "Hoshinmaru" and "Tomimaru" Japan v. Russian Federation2002 · 2004 · 2007Prompt releaseThe consistent practice: the Tribunal reviews the reasonableness of the bond and orders release on payment, while maintaining that it is not an appeal against the coastal State's judgment. In Volga it held that the application does not become moot merely because the vessel has been released by the time of decision, so that the applicant may obtain a ruling on the bond's lawfulness.
"ARA Libertad" Argentina v. Ghana2012Provisional measures; immunity of warshipsThe Tribunal prescribed that Ghana must release the Argentine naval training vessel and enable it to leave; a warship enjoys immunity under general international law and Articles 32 and 95, and a State's domestic court process cannot be used to detain it. The order is also the leading example of a provisional measures order producing immediate practical compliance.
Bangladesh/Myanmar (Case No. 16)2012Maritime delimitation (the first such case before ITLOS)The first international judgment delimiting the territorial sea, the EEZ and the continental shelf, including beyond 200 nm, in a single case; established the equidistance/relevant circumstances method and the cut-off analysis which the Bay of Bengal award (case 35) followed.
"Arctic Sunrise" Netherlands v. Russian Federation2013 (order)Provisional measures under Art. 290(5)The Tribunal ordered Russia to release the vessel and the detainees on the Netherlands' undertaking, holding that the vessel was entitled to navigate in the EEZ; Russia announced that it did not accept the order and did not participate. The subsequent Annex VII award (2015) found Russia's boarding and detention unlawful and, at a later stage, compensation was agreed. The case is the standard illustration of non-appearance and of the difference between a binding order and an enforceable one.
"M/V Virginia G" Panama v. Guinea-Bissau2014Bunkering of fishing vessels in the EEZThe coastal State is competent to regulate the bunkering of foreign vessels fishing in its EEZ as part of its rights over the living resources; but the manner of enforcement — the confiscation of the vessel and the failure to inform the flag State as Article 73(4) requires — violated the flag State's rights, and compensation was awarded.
"Enrica Lexie" Italy v. India (Case No. 24)2015 (order)Provisional measures under Art. 290(5)Order of 24 August 2015, by 15 votes to 6: both parties were directed to suspend all proceedings pending the award of the Annex VII tribunal, with the consequence that the Indian proceedings (including the case before the Supreme Court) were suspended. The Indian angle is set out in full in case 34.
Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (Special Chamber, Case No. 23)2017Delimitation in the Atlantic; Art. 83(3)Fixed the land boundary terminus, delimited the territorial sea, EEZ and shelf, and became the leading authority on the duties of Articles 74(3) and 83(3) in the transitional period, including the duty to seek provisional arrangements and not to jeopardise the final agreement (§5.2 G).
Sub-Regional Fisheries Commission (AO) Case No. 212015Advisory opinion on IUU fishingThe Tribunal held that it had jurisdiction under Annex VI, art. 21, and advised that flag States have an obligation of due diligence to ensure that vessels flying their flags do not engage in illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing in the EEZs of the requesting States; that a flag State is liable for a breach of that obligation where damage results; that the coastal State may take enforcement measures within its EEZ in accordance with the Convention; and that where an international organisation (as the European Union is) is a party, its own responsibility may be engaged.
M/T "Norstar" Panama v. Italy2019Freedom of navigation; criminal jurisdiction over a foreign vesselItaly's seizure and prosecution of a Panamanian-flagged tanker for bunkering offences committed on the high seas was held to violate Panama's rights; the Tribunal confirmed that a State may not extend its criminal jurisdiction over a foreign vessel on the high seas save on the grounds permitted by the Convention, and awarded compensation.
Mauritius/Maldives (Special Chamber, Case No. 28)2021 (preliminary objections) · 2023 (merits)Delimitation in the Indian OceanThe Chamber declined to determine the underlying sovereignty dispute over the Chagos Archipelago but held that it could proceed with the delimitation; on the merits it drew an equidistance line and made the points which now recur in Indian Ocean delimitation, including the treatment of base points and the absence of an "equitable" entitlement independent of geography.
Climate change (AO) Case No. 312024Advisory opinion on climate change and the oceansAnthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions constitute pollution of the marine environment (para. 179); States must take measures to prevent, reduce and control it, under a standard of stringent due diligence (para. 241), using the precautionary approach (para. 242), and the Paris Agreement's commitments do not by themselves discharge the obligations under Part XII (paras 223–224); developed States must "take the lead" in mitigation.

Two features of this table should be noticed. First, the volume of the Tribunal's work is concentrated in the law of vessels and the law of remedies — prompt release, provisional measures, bunkering, navigation — rather than in boundary delimitation, which the parties have preferred to take to the Court or to Annex VII arbitration. Secondly, its most influential decisions are advisory opinions: the 2011 opinion on sponsoring States (from the Seabed Disputes Chamber, §5.3 G), the 2015 fisheries opinion and the 2024 climate opinion have shaped the behaviour of States far more than any of its contentious judgments.

E. The Indian dimension

  • Judge P. Chandrasekhara Rao was a member of the Tribunal from its establishment on 1 October 1996, served as its President from 1999 to 2002, completed a second nine-year term and left the bench in 2017 — a period covering the Saiga cases, Camouco and the first delimitation cases. He was also the co-editor of the standard commentary on the Rules of the Tribunal.
  • Judge Neeru Chadha was elected in June 2017 for a nine-year term and became the second woman to sit on the Tribunal; she has served as its Vice-President since October 2023. Before her election she was India's chief legal adviser in the Ministry of External Affairs and was the agent of India in the Enrica Lexie arbitration and in the Bay of Bengal case — the unusual position of a diplomat-advocate who then sat on the bench of the institution before which India had appeared.
  • Judge Bimal N. Patel, a member of the International Law Commission and Vice-Chancellor of Rashtriya Raksha University, was elected on 19 June 2026 at the 36th Meeting of States Parties, with 115 votes of 168 cast, for the term 2026–2035, and takes office on 1 October 2026, succeeding Judge Chadha. India's representation on the Tribunal is therefore continuous from 1996 to 2035 — a fact worth writing in any answer on India and the law of the sea.
  • India's cases before the Tribunal. India has not made a declaration under Article 287 choosing a forum — a fact expressly recorded in the Enrica Lexie award (Italy, by contrast, declared in favour of ITLOS and the ICJ in 1997) — so India's UNCLOS disputes go to Annex VII arbitration by default. It does hold a declaration under Article 36(2) of the ICJ Statute, but that declaration excludes maritime delimitation disputes, so the Court is not an alternative route. India has nevertheless appeared before ITLOS in the incidental phase on two occasions: as respondent in the Enrica Lexie provisional measures proceedings (Case No. 24, 2015), where the Tribunal's order led the Supreme Court to stay its own proceedings; and in the climate advisory proceedings (Case No. 31, 2024), where it filed a written statement by note verbale of 28 August 2023 that the Tribunal admitted into the case file, although it had been received after the time limit fixed for written statements.
    • The Bangladesh/Myanmar judgment (2012), though between two other States, is the case on which India's own later boundary with Bangladesh was built.
  • India's stake in prompt release. Indian-flagged vessels arrested abroad for fisheries offences in the EEZs of littoral States, and the corresponding Indian arrests of foreign vessels under the 1981 Act, make Article 292 a provision of practical Indian importance — an angle that examiners like, because it connects the Tribunal to the working law of Indian ports.

F. Evaluation: is ITLOS a success?

Table 5.28 · ITLOS assessed
AchievementsLimitations
It created specialised judgeship: members are chosen for competence in the law of the sea, allowing it a technical authority which the ICJ, with its general docket, cannot always match.Its contentious docket is thin. States prefer ad hoc arbitration, where they choose the arbitrators and control the procedure; the great delimitation cases have gone to the Court, to Annex VII or to special chambers.
It gave the Convention two heads of genuinely compulsory jurisdiction — provisional measures pending arbitration (Art. 290(5)) and prompt release (Art. 292) — which have produced real remedies in real time.Compliance is not guaranteed: Russia refused to participate in Arctic Sunrise, and the enforcement of orders depends on politics, not on the Statute.
Its advisory jurisdiction has produced the defining statements of modern ocean law: sponsoring-State obligations (2011), flag-State duties for IUU fishing (2015) and the climate obligations of States (2024).Advisory jurisdiction is available only where a treaty expressly provides for it (Annex VI, art. 21), so the Tribunal cannot act as the general constitutional court of the oceans.
Its special chambers have kept the Tribunal in the mainstream of delimitation (Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire 2017; Mauritius/Maldives 2023) without requiring the full bench.Its judgments, like those of the Court, are binding but not self-enforcing; the gap between judgment and compliance (Somalia v. Kenya, case 36) is a general feature of the system.
Its Seabed Disputes Chamber is the only international judicial organ with jurisdiction over a global commons, and its 2011 opinion is the model for the coming law of the deep sea.The Chamber's workload depends on the mining industry; until exploitation begins its jurisprudence will remain thin, and the modern dispute (2021–2026) has been fought in the Authority's organs rather than in the Chamber.

G. The examination angle

Model skeleton — "Discuss the constitution, jurisdiction and contribution of ITLOS" (15 or 20 marks)
  1. Establishment and place in the system (3 marks). UNCLOS 1982, Part XV and Annex VI; the Tribunal began work on 1 October 1996 at Hamburg; one of the four forums of Article 287; the default forum is Annex VII arbitration — which explains why so much of the law is arbitral.
  2. Composition (3 marks). Twenty-one members; qualifications; no two of the same nationality; equitable geographical distribution with at least three members per UN group; nine-year renewable terms; President and Vice-President; incompatible activities (art. 7); ad hoc judges; the Registrar and the Rules of 1997.
  3. Jurisdiction (5 marks). Contentious jurisdiction under Articles 287–288; special agreements under Article 288(2); the two compulsory heads — provisional measures pending arbitration under Article 290(5) and prompt release under Article 292; advisory jurisdiction under Annex VI, article 21 and Article 191; the Seabed Disputes Chamber under Articles 186–191; limits under Articles 297–298.
  4. The chambers (2 marks). The Seabed Disputes Chamber, the Chamber of Summary Procedure, the environment, fisheries and delimitation chambers, and the ad hoc special chambers — with Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire and Mauritius/Maldives as examples.
  5. Contribution (4 marks). Prompt release jurisprudence (the Saiga line, Camouco, Volga); provisional measures (ARA Libertad, Arctic Sunrise, Enrica Lexie); vessel and navigation law (Saiga No. 2, Virginia G, Norstar); delimitation (Bangladesh/Myanmar, the special chambers); advisory opinions (2011, 2015, 2024).
  6. India (2 marks). Rao (member 1996–2017; President 1999–2002), Chadha (2017–2026, Vice-President since 2023, agent in Enrica Lexie and Bay of Bengal), Patel (elected June 2026 for 2026–2035); India's appearance in Case No. 24; India's absence of an Article 287 declaration and its consequences.
  7. Evaluation (1–2 marks). Table 5.28: specialised judgeship and compulsory incidental jurisdiction on the credit side; thin docket, non-appearance and enforcement gaps on the other; the advisory jurisdiction as its most enduring contribution.
Short questions with their points
  • "Write a note on the Seabed Disputes Chamber." Composition, jurisdiction under Article 187, advisory jurisdiction under Article 191, the 2011 opinion on sponsoring States (due diligence, precautionary approach, environmental impact assessment, erga omnes obligations), and its role in the modern mining controversy.
  • "Explain the prompt release procedure." Article 292 and Article 73(2); the ten-day rule; the residual jurisdiction of ITLOS; reasonableness of the bond; the Saiga (No. 1), Camouco and Volga decisions; and the policy behind it — protecting the shipowner and crew from the coastal State's delays.
  • "Discuss provisional measures under Article 290." The requirement of urgency and irreparable prejudice; paragraph 5 and the prima facie jurisdiction test; binding effect under Article 290(6); and the orders in ARA Libertad, Arctic Sunrise and Enrica Lexie.
  • "What is the advisory jurisdiction of ITLOS?" Annex VI, article 21 (agreement-based) and Article 191 (Area); the 2015 fisheries opinion; the 2024 climate opinion; and the contrast with the ICJ's advisory jurisdiction.
  • "India and ITLOS." The three judges (Rao, Chadha, Patel), the Enrica Lexie order, the climate opinion's written statement, and the absence of an Article 287 declaration.

Past Year Questions (DU) and Model Answers — Topic 5: Law of the Sea

This part collects every law-of-the-sea question that appears in the papers used for LB-205 — the end-semester paper of 2025, which carries the current format, and the older question bank printed with the course material — and answers each of them in the form in which it should be written. The 20-mark answers are written in full; the 10-mark short notes are written as complete notes. Each answer ends with a "where the material is" note so that the remaining detail can be pulled from the relevant section of these notes in the examination hall.
