Answer 1 · Rights and duties of coastal and other States in the territorial sea and contiguous zone; measurement of the territorial sea on a deeply indented coast with a fringe of islands (2025, Q. 6 — 20 marks)

(i) The framework

The 1982 Convention divides the sea into zones of graduated competence: internal waters (full sovereignty, no right of passage), the territorial sea (sovereignty, subject to innocent passage), the contiguous zone (functional enforcement jurisdiction), the exclusive economic zone (sovereign rights over resources, with the freedoms of the high seas preserved for navigation and other uses) and the high seas (no sovereignty, freedom of use). Two ideas order the whole scheme. The first is the principle that "the land dominates the sea": maritime rights are derived from the land territory and are measured from the baseline. The second is the balance of the zonal régime: every right of the coastal State is matched by a right of other States, and the balance is struck differently in each zone.

(ii) Rights and duties of the coastal State in the territorial sea
  • Sovereignty (Art. 2) — the sovereignty of the coastal State extends beyond its land territory and internal waters to the territorial sea, to the air space over it and to its bed and subsoil. It is exercised subject to the Convention and to other rules of international law.
  • Prescriptive power over passage (Art. 21) — the coastal State may adopt laws and regulations on innocent passage, including the safety of navigation, the protection of navigational aids and installations, the protection of cables and pipelines, the conservation of living resources, the prevention of fisheries offences, the preservation of the environment, marine scientific research and the prevention of customs, fiscal, immigration and sanitary offences.
  • Navigation management (Arts 22, 25) — sea lanes and traffic separation schemes for ships, and the temporary suspension of innocent passage in specified areas where it is essential for the protection of its security, provided the suspension is non-discriminatory and does not suspend passage through straits used for international navigation (Art. 25(3); Art. 45).
  • Criminal jurisdiction (Art. 27) — the coastal State should not exercise criminal jurisdiction on board a foreign ship passing through the territorial sea, save in four cases: where the consequences of the crime extend to the coastal State; where the crime disturbs the peace of the country or the good order of the territorial sea; where the master or a diplomatic or consular officer of the flag State has requested the assistance of the local authorities; or where the measures are necessary for the suppression of illicit traffic in narcotic drugs or psychotropic substances. The coastal State must notify the flag State's consular officer where it arrests or investigates, and may not stop a ship passing through the territorial sea for a crime committed before it entered, except in the case of the EEZ exception in Art. 27(5).
  • Civil jurisdiction (Art. 28) — the coastal State should not stop or divert a ship passing through the territorial sea to exercise civil jurisdiction over a person on board, and should not arrest a ship for the purpose of proceedings of a civil nature, except in respect of obligations or liabilities assumed or incurred for the purpose of the ship's voyage, and may not levy execution against a ship passing (or in passage) unless the arrest has already been made for the purpose of a claim not within the exceptions.
  • Charges (Art. 26) — no charge may be levied on foreign ships by reason only of their passage; charges may be levied only for specific services rendered, without discrimination.
  • Duties — not to hamper innocent passage (Art. 24(1)); to give publicity to any danger to navigation within or near the territorial sea (Art. 24(2)); not to discriminate in form or in fact against the ships of any State or against ships carrying cargoes to or from any State (Art. 24(1)(b)); to respect the immunities of warships (Art. 32) and to exercise the discretion under Arts 27–28 as a matter of restraint, not of right.
(iii) Rights and duties of other States in the territorial sea
  • The right of innocent passage (Art. 17) — ships of all States, coastal or land-locked, enjoy the right of innocent passage through the territorial sea. "Passage" means navigation through the territorial sea for the purpose of traversing it without entering internal waters, or of proceeding to or from internal waters (Art. 18(1)); it must be continuous and expeditious, though anchoring, stopping or lying to is permitted where incidental to ordinary navigation or necessary for reasons of force majeure, distress or the saving of life or property (Art. 18(2)).
  • Innocence (Art. 19) — passage is innocent "so long as it is not prejudicial to the peace, good order or security of the coastal State"; the passage of a foreign ship is to be considered prejudicial if it engages in any of twelve listed activities, including the threat or use of force, spying, fishing, research or survey, unauthorised broadcasting, interference with communications, wilful pollution, and the loading or unloading of persons, currency or goods contrary to the laws of the coastal State. The list is exhaustive, and the coastal State bears the burden of establishing that passage is not innocent.
  • Duties of the passing ship — to comply with the coastal State's laws and with the international regulations on the prevention of collisions (Art. 21(4)); submarines must navigate on the surface and show their flag (Art. 20); nuclear-powered ships and ships carrying nuclear or other dangerous substances must carry documents and observe special precautions (Art. 23); and ships in transit must not engage in any of the acts of Article 19(2).
  • The compulsory character of the right — innocent passage cannot be charged for, cannot be taxed, and cannot be suspended generally; it is the price the Convention exacts for the recognition of sovereignty over the belt.
(iv) The contiguous zone: rights and duties

Article 33 allows the coastal State to establish a contiguous zone not extending beyond 24 nautical miles from the baseline. In that zone the coastal State may exercise the control necessary to prevent the infringement of its customs, fiscal, immigration or sanitary laws and regulations within its territory or territorial sea, and to punish the infringement of those laws and regulations committed within its territory or territorial sea. The essential features are:

  • It is not sovereignty but jurisdiction. The contiguous zone is a zone of enforcement over four named classes of law, and the coastal State may not extend it to other subjects: it does not, for example, give general criminal jurisdiction, nor rights over fisheries (which arise separately under Art. 73 in the EEZ), nor rights over the airspace above the zone or the seabed below it.
  • It is "prevent and punish". The two limbs are distinct: prevention within the contiguous zone of an infringement threatened within the territory or territorial sea, and punishment within the contiguous zone of an infringement already committed there (Art. 33(1)(a)–(b)).
  • Customs and immigration in practice. The classical illustrations are the pursuit of a ship that has cleared out of port without paying customs dues (the "customs sweep"), and the "hot pursuit" of a vessel from the territorial sea into the contiguous zone and beyond, which Article 111 embodies — pursuit may be continued outside the territorial sea or the contiguous zone only if it is not interrupted, and it ceases when the pursued ship enters the territorial sea of its own State or of a third State (Art. 111(1)–(4)). Article 111(4) contains the doctrine of constructive presence: a ship outside the territorial sea may be pursued when she herself, or a boat or aircraft operating as a team and using her as a mother ship, has committed an offence within the territorial sea.
  • Duties — to confine enforcement to the four classes of law; to respect the immunities of warships and of State ships (Art. 32, Art. 96); to comply with the safeguards of Article 73 where the enforcement relates to fisheries offences (notification of the flag State, release on bond); and to observe Article 300 (good faith and abuse of right) in every enforcement measure.
  • India's position — section 5 of the Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and Other Maritime Zones Act, 1976 declares a contiguous zone extending to 24 nautical miles and confers the powers of prevention and punishment corresponding to Article 33, including the power to make rules for the protection of the zone; the Act's provisions on hot pursuit appear in section 7 and in the rules on the EEZ.
(v) Measurement of the territorial sea on a deeply indented coast with a fringe of islands

The breadth of the territorial sea is measured from the baseline, and the Convention gives the coastal State the choice, within rules, between the normal baseline and the straight baseline. The relevant provisions are:

  1. The normal baseline (Art. 5) — the low-water line along the coast as marked on large-scale charts officially recognised by the coastal State. In a river mouth, the baseline is a straight line across the mouth between points on the low-water line on each bank (Art. 9). Low-tide elevations (Art. 13) may be used as base points only where they lie wholly within the territorial sea of the mainland or of an island, or where the coastal State has built a lighthouse or similar installation permanently above sea level on them.
  2. Straight baselines (Art. 7) — where the coast is "deeply indented and cut into", or where there is "a fringe of islands along the coast in its immediate vicinity", the method of straight baselines joining appropriate points may be employed. The method is governed by the tests which the International Court laid down in the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case (case 30) and which Article 7 codifies: the drawing of straight baselines must not depart to any appreciable extent from the general direction of the coast, and the sea areas lying within them must be sufficiently closely linked to the land domain to be subject to the régime of internal waters (Art. 7(3)); account may be taken of economic interests peculiar to the region, the reality and importance of which are clearly evidenced by long usage (Art. 7(5)); the system of straight baselines may not be applied so as to cut off the territorial sea of another State from the high seas or the EEZ (Art. 7(6)); and low-tide elevations are generally not to be used as base points unless lighthouses or similar installations have been built on them (Art. 7(4)). The Court's case also produced the rule now in Article 8(2): where straight baselines enclose as internal waters areas which had not previously been considered internal, the right of innocent passage continues to exist in those waters.
  3. Bays (Art. 10) — if the distance between the low-water marks of the natural entrance points of a bay does not exceed 24 nautical miles, a closing line may be drawn and the waters within it are internal waters; the bay must have an area at least equal to that of a semicircle whose diameter is the length of the closing line (the "semicircle test"), and the rule does not apply to "historic" bays.
  4. Islands (Art. 121) — every island (a naturally formed area of land, surrounded by water, above water at high tide) has its own territorial sea, contiguous zone, EEZ and continental shelf; rocks which cannot sustain human habitation or economic life of their own have only a territorial sea and contiguous zone. On a coast fringed with islands, the outer fringe may itself serve as the base line, as Norway's skjærgaard line did, so that the "belt" of sea between the fringe and the mainland becomes internal waters.
  5. Archipelagos (Part IV) — a State which is constituted wholly by one or more archipelagos may draw archipelagic straight baselines under Article 47, enclosing the archipelago and its interconnecting waters as archipelagic waters, but this is a separate régime which does not apply merely because the coast of a continental State is fringed with islands — the fringe rule of Article 7 is its analogue.

Applying these rules to the facts of the question. Where the coastline is deeply indented and cut into and there is a fringe of islands in the immediate vicinity of the mainland, the coastal State may (not must) draw straight baselines joining appropriate points on the coast and on the outer islands, provided that the lines do not depart appreciably from the general direction of the coast, that the enclosed waters are sufficiently closely linked with the land to be treated as internal waters, that economic interests evidenced by long usage may be taken into account, and that the line does not cut off another State's territorial sea from the high seas. The territorial sea is then measured from those lines, and the inner waters become internal waters, subject to the historic right of innocent passage in waters newly enclosed (Art. 8(2)). This is precisely the situation in the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case, where Norway's decree of 1935 drew straight baselines through 48 fixed points along the skjærgaard; the Court upheld the decree, rejected the United Kingdom's ten-mile rule for bays and the theory of "persistent objection" as a condition of the decree's validity, and held that the validity of the delimitation "depends upon international law" and not upon the assent of other States. The case, and Article 7, thus answer the second half of the question directly.

(vi) Conclusion

In the territorial sea the coastal State has sovereignty tempered by the right of innocent passage; in the contiguous zone it has a limited enforcement jurisdiction over four classes of law; and in both zones its rights are matched by duties of non-discrimination, restraint and respect for immunities. Measurement of the belt begins at the baseline, and the baseline may be drawn by straight lines — the technique the Convention borrowed from the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case — where geography has made a coast deeply indented and given it a fringe of islands. Since the baselines generate the outer limits of every subsequent zone, the question of measurement is the most consequential small question in the whole of the law of the sea.

Detail: §§5.1.1–5.1.2 (parts 02–04), Tables 5.3–5.7, Figs 5.2–5.4, and case 30 (full digest).

Answer 2 · Warships and the right of innocent passage (2025, Q. 4(1) — 10 marks)

1. The definition. A "warship" is a ship belonging to the armed forces of a State bearing the external marks distinguishing such ships of its nationality, under the command of an officer duly commissioned by the government of the State whose name appears in the appropriate service list, and manned by a crew under regular armed forces discipline (Art. 29). The definition matters because the Convention gives warships a régime of immunity: complete immunity on the high seas (Art. 95), immunity in the territorial sea — the Convention's provisions on criminal and civil jurisdiction (Arts 27–28) do not apply to warships (Art. 32) — and immunity from any enforcement short of a demand that the ship leave.

2. The right of innocent passage applies to warships. Article 17 confers the right of innocent passage on "ships of all States"; Article 32 assumes that warships exercise it, since it provides that nothing in the Convention affects the immunities of warships and that the only remedy of the coastal State, where a warship does not comply with its laws concerning passage, is to require it to leave the territorial sea immediately (Art. 30). The flag State bears international responsibility for any loss or damage caused by a warship in the territorial sea (Art. 31).

3. The controversy. Before 1982 it was argued that warships require prior authorisation or notification. The 1958 Convention on the Territorial Sea and the Contiguous Zone did not resolve the question, and State practice remains divided: several States require notification or authorisation by their domestic law, while others regard Article 17 as applying to all ships without distinction. The better view, and the one that follows from the text of UNCLOS, is that no prior authorisation is required, but that the coastal State may regulate the passage of warships by reasonable laws under Article 21 so long as the substance of the right is preserved — an approach consistent with the requirement of non-discrimination and with Article 24's prohibition on hampering innocent passage.

4. The leading case. In the Corfu Channel case (case 31) the International Court held that warships may pass through a strait used for international navigation in time of peace without the prior consent of the coastal State, and that the passage of the British squadron on 22 October 1946 was lawful. The Court found that the intention of the United Kingdom had been "not only to test Albania's attitude, but at the same time to demonstrate such force that she would abstain from firing again", but it held that this did not in itself make the passage an abuse: the crews were at action stations and the guns trained because the ships were in waters in which mines were believed to be present, and the burden of proving that a passage was not innocent lay upon the coastal State alleging it. Albania's firing upon the ships was therefore a violation of international law, and the Court's findings on the North Corfu Channel — that the Channel was a strait used for international navigation and that Albania could not prohibit its use — became the foundation of the modern law of transit passage.

5. What the coastal State may do. It may require warships to comply with its laws and with the international regulations on safety; it may designate sea lanes and traffic separation schemes (Art. 22); it may require submarines to navigate on the surface and show their flag (Art. 20); and in the case of non-compliance it may require the warship to leave (Art. 30) — but it may not arrest, detain, board or prosecute. It may not suspend innocent passage through a strait used for international navigation (Art. 45), and it may suspend passage in other areas only temporarily, in specified areas, for security reasons and without discrimination (Art. 25(3)).

6. Conclusion. Warships enjoy the right of innocent passage under UNCLOS, subject to the same requirement of innocence as merchant ships, but they enjoy it with the immunities of the flag State; the coastal State's remedy for abuse is expulsion and, where damage is caused, reparation from the flag State. The Corfu Channel case remains the leading authority that a passage is not converted into an unlawful demonstration merely because it is made by warships, and that the coastal State bears the burden of establishing that the passage was not innocent.

Detail: §5.1.1 F (part 03) — including the tables of warship practice, the analysis of Articles 29–32 and the naval incidents; case 31 (full digest).

Answer 3 · "Write an essay on the Freedom of the Open Sea" (question bank, Q. 9(1) — 20 marks)

I. Introduction. The freedom of the seas is the oldest principle of international law and the one that most directly shaped the modern world. Its classical statement is Grotius's Mare Liberum (1609), written for the Dutch East India Company: the sea, like the air, is incapable of occupation and is therefore res communis — accessible to all nations and belonging to none. Selden's Mare Clausum defended the contrary claims of the English crown, and Bynkershoek, writing a century later, gave the principle its practical settlement: the land's sovereignty ends where the range of its cannon ends, which is why the coastal belt came to be measured in miles while the rest of the ocean remained free. The modern law is the product of that compromise: the high seas are free, the belt is sovereign, and the boundary between them has been pushed outwards steadily for four centuries.

II. The high seas defined. Article 86 of UNCLOS defines the high seas as all parts of the sea that are not included in the exclusive economic zone, in the territorial sea or in the internal waters of a State, or in the archipelagic waters of an archipelagic State. The definition is negative and its effect is to make the high seas the residue of the ocean: what is not zoned is free. The high seas are open to all States, whether coastal or land-locked, and no State may validly purport to subject any part of them to its sovereignty (Art. 89). The high seas are reserved for peaceful purposes (Art. 88).

III. The freedoms. Article 87(1) lists the freedoms of the high seas: navigation; overflight; the laying of submarine cables and pipelines; the construction of artificial islands and other installations permitted by international law; fishing; and scientific research. The list is not exhaustive — it is followed by the words "and other freedoms recognised by international law" — but every freedom must be exercised with due regard to the interests of other States (Art. 87(2)), and the freedoms are qualified by the provisions of the Convention on fisheries (Arts 116–119), on the environment (Part XII), on marine scientific research (Part XIII) and on the Area (Part XI). The 1958 High Seas Convention contained only four freedoms (navigation, fishing, cables and pipelines, overflight) and provided that they were to be exercised with reasonable regard to the interests of other States (Art. 2).

IV. The legal order of the high seas. Freedom is not anarchy, and the high seas are governed by five doctrines which the Convention expresses:

  1. Nationality of ships and the genuine link — ships have the nationality of the State whose flag they are entitled to fly (Art. 91), and there must exist a "genuine link" between the State and the ship; but the genuineness of the link does not affect the ship's nationality for the purpose of the recognition of flag-State rights (the ITLOS judgment in the Saiga (No. 2) case, 1999).
  2. The exclusive jurisdiction of the flag State — a ship on the high seas is subject to the exclusive jurisdiction of its flag State (Art. 92), and every State must effectively exercise its jurisdiction and control over ships flying its flag in administrative, technical and social matters (Art. 94). The classic formulation of the flag-State principle is the Lotus case (1927), where the Permanent Court held that a State may not exercise jurisdiction over a foreign vessel on the high seas; the exception recognised in Lotus for collisions — the flag State and the State of the offender — is now codified in Article 97 for collisions on the high seas.
  3. The exceptions: piracy, slave trade, unauthorised broadcasting, drugs, the right of visit — the freedom of the seas yields to the common interest in suppressing piracy (Arts 100–107: every State may seize a pirate ship on the high seas and try the pirates in its own courts, whatever the flag), the slave trade (Art. 99), unauthorised broadcasting (Art. 109), and the traffic in narcotic drugs (Art. 108). Article 110 confers the right of visit — the boarding of a foreign ship suspected of piracy, slave trading or unauthorised broadcasting, or of being without nationality — which is the most important exception to flag-State exclusivity, and Article 111 provides for hot pursuit.
  4. Warship immunity — warships and government ships operated for non-commercial purposes have complete immunity on the high seas (Arts 95–96), which is why an ITLOS order — in ARA Libertad (2012) — could require the release of a naval training ship detained at the instance of a domestic court.
  5. Duties of the flag State — the freedom carries the duty to protect the marine environment (Arts 192, 194, 209), to conserve living resources (Arts 116–119, including the duty to cooperate in regional fisheries organisations), to render assistance to persons in distress (Art. 98), and to protect cables and pipelines (Art. 113).

V. The erosion of the freedom. The essay should confront the criticism that freedom of the high seas has been hollowed out. The movement of the outer limit from 3 to 12 to 24 to 200 nautical miles; the recognition of the exclusive economic zone, which makes two-thirds of the ocean's productive capacity subject to coastal-State rights; the régime of the Area, which removes the deep seabed from the operation of the freedom altogether; the proliferation of international fisheries regulation and of regional fisheries management organisations; the BBNJ Agreement of 2023, which for the first time authorises the establishment of marine protected areas on the high seas with binding effect; the climate litigation in the ITLOS advisory opinion of 2024; and the increasing regulation of shipping by the International Maritime Organization — all qualify the classical freedom. Two rival readings are possible: that the freedom has been transformed from a right of liberty into a régime of regulated use, or that it has been refined so that each user must respect the interests of the others — freedom with due regard, and not freedom with impunity.

VI. Conclusion. The freedom of the open sea remains the foundational principle of the law of the sea: it is the reason no State has been able to claim sovereignty over the ocean, the reason the flag State is the primary guardian of the law of the sea, and the reason the Convention could be drafted as a single package covering the ocean as a whole. What has changed is its content. The freedom of the present century is a freedom of international cooperation — restrained by the common interest in resources, environment and security — a freedom whose limits are the limits set by the common heritage principle, the environmental duty and the rights of other users. That transformation is precisely the history of the law of the sea.

Detail: §5.1.5 (part 07), Arts 86–120, Tables 5.12, Fig. 5.6; case 31 for Corfu; §5.1.1 for the belt.

Answer 4 · "Discuss the concept of the Continental Shelf with reference to recent developments" (question bank, Q. 9(2) — 20 marks)

I. The physical and legal idea. The continental shelf is the natural seaward extension of the land: geologically it consists of the shelf proper (a gentle slope), the continental slope, and the continental rise, together forming the continental margin, and it is rich in oil, gas and minerals. The legal concept comes from the Truman Proclamation of 28 September 1945, in which the United States claimed the resources of the shelf appurtenant to its coasts on the ground that it was a natural prolongation of the land. The 1958 Geneva Convention on the Continental Shelf adopted both the natural prolongation idea and the exploitability criterion: the shelf extended to a depth of 200 metres or, beyond that limit, to where the depth of the superjacent waters admitted the exploitation of the natural resources (Art. 1). The criterion was indeterminate, and it was replaced by Article 76 of UNCLOS.

II. The modern definition — Article 76. Article 76(1) defines the continental shelf as comprising the seabed and subsoil of the submarine areas that extend beyond the territorial sea throughout the natural prolongation of the land territory to the outer edge of the continental margin, or to a distance of 200 nautical miles from the baselines where the outer edge does not extend that far. The outer edge of the margin beyond 200 nm is measured by the two tests in Article 76(4): the thickness of sedimentary rocks (a point where the distance from the foot of the continental slope to the point where the thickness of sedimentary rocks is at least 1 per cent of the shortest distance to the foot of the slope — the Gardiner formula), or a distance from the foot of the slope not exceeding 60 nautical miles. Those limits are then capped by Article 76(5)–(6): 350 nautical miles from the baseline, or 100 nautical miles beyond the 2,500-metre isobath, and, on submarine ridges, 350 nm. The coastal State establishes the outer limits on the basis of the recommendations of the Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf (Art. 76(8); Annex II) — the Commission being the scientific body of 21 elected members to which submissions are made.

III. The rights of the coastal State. Article 77 vests in the coastal State sovereign rights for the purpose of exploring the shelf and exploiting its natural resources — mineral and other non-living resources of the seabed and subsoil, together with living organisms belonging to sedentary species — and provides that those rights are exclusive in the sense that, if the coastal State does not explore or exploit the shelf, no one else may do so without its express consent. The rights are ipso facto and ab initio, and do not depend upon occupation or proclamation (Art. 77(3)) — the holding of the Court in the North Sea Continental Shelf cases, from which the doctrine derives. Articles 78–81 complete the régime: the legal status of the superjacent waters and airspace is unaffected; the coastal State may not interfere unjustifiably with navigation, cables, pipelines or the other rights of third States; it may construct and operate installations on the shelf and may establish 500-metre safety zones around them; it may authorise drilling for all purposes (Art. 81); it may lay cables and pipelines (Art. 79); and Article 82 requires payments or contributions to the International Seabed Authority for the exploitation of the shelf beyond 200 nm — the only provision that carries the benefit-sharing idea of the common heritage into areas of national jurisdiction.

IV. Delimitation between States. Delimitation is governed by Article 83, which requires an agreement "in order to achieve an equitable solution" and, failing agreement, recourse to the Part XV procedures — the standard which the North Sea cases (case 32) and Tunisia/Libya (case 33) created. The modern method is the three-stage approach discussed at §5.2: a provisional equidistance line, adjusted for relevant circumstances, and then tested for gross disproportion; and the same three stages apply to the shelf beyond 200 nm (Bangladesh/Myanmar 2012, the Bay of Bengal award 2014, Somalia v. Kenya 2021).

V. "Recent developments". Four developments deserve emphasis.

  1. The CLCS and the handling of disputes. The Commission's workload (over eighty submissions) has produced three important practical rules: the ten-year period (decided by the Meeting of States Parties in 2001, which allowed States whose submissions were due by 2009 an extended period), the rule that where a land or maritime dispute exists the Commission makes no recommendation on the disputed portion absent the parties' consent, and the practice of "partial" or amended submissions — as in India's revised Central Arabian Sea submission of 2025, which followed Pakistan's objection to the 2009 submission.
  2. The delimitation of the shelf beyond 200 nm. Bangladesh/Myanmar (ITLOS 2012) and the Bay of Bengal arbitration (2014) established that a court or tribunal may delimit the outer shelf before the CLCS has acted, and the concept of the grey area — where the shelf of one State lies under the EEZ of another — emerged from the 2014 award. Somalia v. Kenya (2021) extended the same line beyond 200 nm. These decisions have given Article 76 a jurisprudential substance which the 1958 Convention never had.
  3. The relationship with the EEZ. Because coastal States now claim an EEZ of 200 nm, and because the shelf beyond 200 nm corresponds to areas of deeper water and lesser present value, the two régimes have converged. The concept of the "single maritime boundary" — one line delimiting the seabed and the water column together — has made the distinction between shelf and EEZ less important in practice than it was in 1969, though it remains decisive in the grey area and in the CLCS procedure.
  4. The Area and the common heritage. The outer limit of the shelf is also the boundary of the common heritage of mankind: everything beyond it is the Area, administered by the Seabed Authority under Part XI (§§5.3–5.4). The extended shelf claim is thus a claim to remove territory from the common heritage.

VI. Conclusion. The continental shelf has moved from a physical description (the 200-metre line) to a legal entitlement (natural prolongation to the outer edge of the margin) to a regime of scientific delimitation conducted through the CLCS; and in the process it has generated the most sophisticated body of delimitation law in international law — the three-stage method, the relevant circumstances and the disproportionality test, all of which were built on the shelf cases.

Detail: §5.1.3 (part 05), Art. 76 in full, Tables 5.10–5.11, Fig. 5.5; cases 32, 33, 35 and 36.

Answer 5 · "Discuss piracy as an international crime" (question bank, Q. 17 — 20 marks)

1. Definition. Piracy is the oldest of the universal crimes. Article 101 of UNCLOS (reproducing Article 15 of the 1958 High Seas Convention) defines it as (a) any illegal act of violence, detention or depredation committed for private ends by the crew or passengers of a private ship or private aircraft, and directed (i) on the high seas against another ship or aircraft, or persons or property on board; or (ii) against a ship, aircraft, persons or property in a place outside the jurisdiction of any State; (b) any act of voluntary participation in the operation of a ship or aircraft with knowledge of facts making it a pirate ship or aircraft; and (c) any act of inciting or of intentionally facilitating an act described in (a) or (b). The definition is narrow and its elements are cumulative.

2. The elements. (i) An illegal act of violence, detention or depredation; (ii) for private ends — the essential element that excludes acts of terrorism or of political violence (hence the need for the SUA Convention of 1988 to cover politically motivated violence at sea); (iii) by the crew or passengers of a private ship — the "two-ship" requirement at customary law, relaxed by Article 101 for acts of complicity and incitement; (iv) on the high seas or outside the jurisdiction of any State — piracy cannot occur in the territorial sea of a State (where it is ordinary crime) nor, as such, in the EEZ (which is within the coastal State's jurisdiction, though many States have extended piracy legislation by treaty); and (v) acts on the high seas must be directed against another ship. The old rule that piracy required actual robbery has been rejected: in In re Piracy Jure Gentium (1934) the Judicial Committee of the Privy Council held, on a reference from Hong Kong, that the offence is complete on a mere attempt — actual robbery is not required.

3. Universal jurisdiction and the duty to cooperate. Piracy engages the universal jurisdiction of every State: Article 105 provides that on the high seas or in any other place outside the jurisdiction of any State, every State may seize a pirate ship and arrest the persons and seize the property on board, and that the courts of the State which carried out the seizure may decide upon the penalties and the action to be taken with regard to the ships or property. Article 107 confines the seizure to warships, military aircraft or other government ships clearly marked and identifiable as being on government service and authorised to that effect. Article 100 imposes a duty on all States to cooperate to the fullest possible extent in the repression of piracy; Articles 103–104 provide for the national character of pirate ships; Article 102 assimilates acts committed by a warship whose crew has mutinied; and Articles 106 and 110 provide for the consequences of seizure without adequate grounds (liability of the seizing State) and the right of visit.

4. Piracy as an international crime. Piracy is a crime under international law — a delictum juris gentium — and not merely under the law of the seizing State: its universal repression is the classical exception to the exclusive jurisdiction of the flag State, and it is the only crime of which the whole community has traditionally been treated as the injured party in the law of the sea. Its modern analogue in the air is hijacking under the Hague Convention of 1970 and the Montreal Convention of 1971.

5. The modern practice. Between 2008 and 2012 piracy off Somalia transformed an academic topic into a live police problem: the Security Council's resolutions, the operations of the European Union (Atalanta), NATO (Ocean Shield) and the Combined Task Forces, and the establishment of the Contact Group on Piracy off the Coast of Somalia, together with the Seychelles and Mauritius prosecutions, created a body of State practice. The legal problems that emerged were the definitional gap for political and land-based attacks, the question of jurisdiction over the suspects, and the question of human rights standards in prosecution. The convention system responded with the SUA Convention and its 2005 Protocol, the IMO's rules on private armed guards, and the use of the "mother ship" (constructive presence) doctrine in enforcement.

6. India and piracy. India is a pivotal case. It has lost seamen and ships to piracy in the Gulf of Aden and off the African coast; its navy has conducted escort operations (Operation Sankalp from 2008 and again from 2023); and it has legislated specifically: the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022 defines piracy in terms of the Convention, extends Indian jurisdiction to acts committed on the high seas and outside India's maritime zones, and provides for punishment up to life imprisonment, with special courts. The statute is the domestic expression of Article 105, and it was used in 2026 to obtain the conviction of 44 persons in the MV Ruen and MV Al-Kambar cases — the first such convictions — following Indian naval operations in 2024.

7. Conclusion. Piracy is an international crime because the community of States, through the Convention, has made it a matter of common concern and has entrusted every State with the power to seize and punish; it is the oldest instance of the proposition that there are acts against the interests of mankind as a whole which no flag can shelter. Its modern difficulty is not the principle but its limits — the private-ends requirement, the high-seas requirement and the capacity of States to prosecute — which the SUA Convention, the Security Council's resolutions and the MAPA 2022 have addressed without entirely removing.

Detail: §5.1.5 (part 07) — Arts 100–107 with the five-element analysis and Fig. 5.6; India's MAPA 2022 at §5.5 C.

Answer 6 · Short notes (question bank, Q. 20 — 10 marks each)

(a) Maritime Belt (the territorial sea)

The "maritime belt" is the older name for the territorial sea — the belt of sea adjacent to the coast over which the coastal State exercises sovereignty. Its legal history is the history of its breadth: Bynkershoek's cannon-shot rule gave it the range of a shore battery; the Scandinavian States claimed four miles; the "three-mile rule" was defended by the United Kingdom and the United States through the nineteenth century; the 1930 Hague Codification Conference failed to agree; the 1958 Geneva Conference settled no breadth; the 1960 Conference failed by one vote on a joint proposal of six and six miles; and UNCLOS settled the matter by allowing every State a territorial sea up to 12 nautical miles from the baseline (Art. 3). The belt is measured from the baseline — the low-water line or straight baselines under Articles 5 and 7 — and includes the airspace above, the water column, the seabed and the subsoil (Art. 2(2)). Sovereignty in the belt is subject to the right of innocent passage (Arts 17–19) and, in straits used for international navigation, to transit passage. India's belt is 12 nm, declared by section 3 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976. The leading cases are the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case on measurement (case 30) and the Corfu Channel case on passage through straits (case 31).

(b) Economic Zone (the exclusive economic zone)

The exclusive economic zone is the most important legal invention of the last century in the law of the sea. It originated in the desires of the developing coastal States of Africa, Latin America and Asia, and was carried into UNCLOS III by, among others, the 1972 Yaoundé conclusions of African States and the work of the Asian–African Legal Consultative Committee; Kenya's proposal of 1972 is often identified as the origin of the concept. Article 55 defines the EEZ as an area beyond and adjacent to the territorial sea, extending up to 200 nautical miles from the baselines, under a specific legal régime: the rights and jurisdiction of the coastal State, and the rights and freedoms of other States, are governed by the Convention. The coastal State has sovereign rights for the purpose of exploring and exploiting, conserving and managing the natural resources, living and non-living, of the waters superjacent to the seabed and of the seabed and subsoil, and rights with regard to other activities for the economic exploitation of the zone, such as the production of energy from the water, currents and winds (Art. 56(1)(a)); it has jurisdiction over the establishment and use of artificial islands, installations and structures, marine scientific research, and the protection and preservation of the marine environment (Art. 56(1)(b)); and it has the duties to conserve the living resources, to determine the allowable catch and its own capacity to harvest, and to give other States access to the surplus (Arts 61–62). Other States enjoy the freedoms of the high seas in the EEZ — navigation, overflight, the laying of cables and pipelines and other internationally lawful uses — together with the duties of Articles 58(3) and 88–115 (Art. 58). The zone is neither territorial sea nor high seas: it is a sui generis zone of functional sovereignty, and the disputes over its military uses and over its "residual rights" (Art. 59) are the unfinished business of the régime. Delimitation is by agreement to achieve an equitable solution (Art. 74), which in practice takes the form of the three-stage equidistance approach. India's EEZ is 200 nm, declared by section 7 of the 1976 Act, and is enforced through the 1981 Fishing Act and the Coast Guard.

(c) Contiguous Zone

The contiguous zone is a belt of sea contiguous to the territorial sea in which the coastal State may exercise the control necessary (i) to prevent the infringement of its customs, fiscal, immigration or sanitary laws and regulations within its territory or territorial sea, and (ii) to punish infringements of those laws and regulations committed within its territory or territorial sea (Art. 33(1)). It may not extend beyond 24 nautical miles from the baselines (Art. 33(2)). It is a zone of enforcement jurisdiction and not of sovereignty: the coastal State cannot claim resources there, cannot prohibit passage, and does not obtain rights in the airspace or in the seabed. Its function is to make the territorial sea's laws effective by giving the coastal State a second belt in which to intercept offenders and to continue the pursuit; the arrest of a ship in the contiguous zone for an offence committed in the territorial sea is the paradigm case (see Art. 111(1) on hot pursuit). The history is instructive: an earlier generation of treaties created customs belts of varying breadths (the "Hovering Acts"), and the codification of 1958 gave the institution its modern form in Article 24 of the Territorial Sea Convention. India's contiguous zone is declared by section 5 of the Maritime Zones Act, 1976, and the prevention and punishment powers correspond to Article 33.

Detail: §§5.1.1–5.1.4 (parts 02–06); Art. 33 and hot pursuit at part 04.

Answer 7 · "State the facts and the decision in: Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries, Corfu Channel, North Sea Continental Shelf" (question bank, Q. 21)

Table 5.30 · The three cases in the compressed form in which they should be recalled
CaseFacts in three linesDecision in three linesThe principle
Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries (UK v. Norway), ICJ Rep. 1951, p. 116Norway's coast is deeply indented and fringed by the skjærgaard; by a decree of 16 July 1935 it delimited its fisheries zone by straight baselines drawn through 48 fixed points on the mainland, islands and rocks; the United Kingdom challenged both the system of straight baselines and the ten-mile rule for bays.The Court upheld the decree: the straight baseline method was lawful for this coast; the lines did not depart appreciably from the general direction of the coast and the enclosed waters were sufficiently linked to the land; the ten-mile rule was not binding on Norway, which had consistently opposed it; the validity of the delimitation depended upon international law and not on the consent of other States.Where the coast is deeply indented or fringed by islands, straight baselines may be drawn — the origin of Article 7 of UNCLOS (with Article 8(2) on historic passage rights).
Corfu Channel (UK v. Albania), ICJ Rep. 1949, p. 4British warships passed through the North Corfu Channel in May and October 1946; on 22 October two destroyers struck mines, with loss of life; Albania had not warned of the mines; the United Kingdom then swept the channel without Albania's consent (Operation Retail).Albania was responsible for the explosions on 22 October (knowledge of the minefield and failure to warn) and for firing upon the ships; the Channel was a strait used for international navigation; the British passage of the warships was not unlawful; Operation Retail was unlawful as a violation of Albanian sovereignty, and the Court's declaration of illegality constituted satisfaction; compensation of £843,947 was awarded.Warships may pass through international straits in peacetime; the coastal State may not prohibit such passage; a court's declaratory judgment may itself be satisfaction for a breach; the case is also the foundation of the modern rules on straits and on the duty to warn of dangers.
North Sea Continental Shelf (Germany v. Denmark; Germany v. Netherlands), ICJ Rep. 1969, p. 3A dispute over the delimitation of the North Sea shelf; Denmark and the Netherlands relied on the equidistance rule of Article 6 of the 1958 Geneva Convention; Germany, which had not ratified the Convention, argued that the rule was not customary and that its concave coast would be cut off by equidistance.Article 6 was not opposable to Germany; the equidistance rule was not customary international law (it lacked a norm-creating character, it was subject to a reservation clause in Article 12 of the Convention, State practice was not uniform, and there was no opinio juris); delimitation must be by agreement on equitable principles, taking account of the coastal geography, and in this configuration produced lines which the parties later implemented by agreement in 1971.Delimitation of the continental shelf is to be effected by agreement in accordance with equitable principles, taking account of all relevant circumstances — the foundation of Articles 74(1) and 83(1) and of the three-stage method.

Full seven-part digests: case 30, case 31, case 32.

Examination technique for Topic 5

Ten rules for answering a law-of-the-sea question
  1. Always open with the zone. State the zone's breadth, its legal character (sovereignty, sovereign rights, enforcement jurisdiction, freedom) and its article — the examiner's first mark is for identifying the régime correctly.
  2. Use the "rights and duties" symmetry. Every question about a zone can be organised as rights of the coastal State, duties of the coastal State, rights of other States, duties of other States — four heads, each with articles. This structure alone can carry a 20-mark answer.
  3. Quote sparingly but exactly. One or two provisions (Art. 2(1), Art. 33(1), Art. 76(1), Art. 87(1), Art. 136) memorised verbatim are worth more than five paraphrases.
  4. Deploy the Indian material. The Maritime Zones Act 1976 (ss. 3, 5, 6, 7), Art. 297 of the Constitution, the 1981 Fishing Act, the MAPA 2022, the CLCS submissions and the 2014 award — Indian examples distinguish a good answer from a general one.
  5. Draw a diagram. Even a rough sketch of the zones with the 12/24/200 nm lines and the baseline earns credit and organises the answer; reproduce Fig. 5.2 or Fig. 5.13 from memory.
  6. Bring in the four DU cases by name — Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries, Corfu Channel, North Sea Continental Shelf, Tunisia/Libya — and Enrica Lexie, which is the highlighted Indian case. A case name must come with a proposition: never cite without a principle.
  7. Note the 1958 Conventions. The four Geneva Conventions and the way UNCLOS replaced them is a favourite preliminary in every question on codification and history.
  8. Mention the neighbouring régime. A question on the territorial sea should mention straits, the contiguous zone and the EEZ; a question on the EEZ should mention the shelf and the Area; a question on the Area should mention the CLCS and the outer shelf.
  9. Answer the second limb. "Discuss X and Y" — most marks are lost by answering the first limb at length and the second in a line.
  10. Conclude with evaluation. Whether the régime is adequate, whether India's law complies with it, what the recent cases have changed — two or three sentences of assessment are the difference between a descriptive and a first-class answer.
Self-test: twenty questions on Topic 5
  1. Define the territorial sea and state the two methods of measuring its breadth.
  2. What are the three tests for straight baselines and where do they come from?
  3. State the twelve acts that make passage non-innocent under Article 19(2).
  4. Summarise Articles 27 and 28 on criminal and civil jurisdiction over foreign ships.
  5. What is the status of internal waters and what is the exception to the rule that there is no right of passage in them?
  6. Explain transit passage and contrast it with innocent passage.
  7. What are the four classes of law to which the contiguous zone extends?
  8. State the elements of hot pursuit and the doctrine of constructive presence.
  9. Give the Article 76 formula for the outer limit of the continental shelf beyond 200 nm.
  10. What is the role of the Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf, and what happens where a dispute exists?
  11. What are the sovereign rights of the coastal State in the EEZ, and what rights remain with other States?
  12. Explain the "residual rights" question under Article 59 with one example.
  13. List the six freedoms of the high seas and the qualifications on them.
  14. State the definition of piracy and its five elements.
  15. What are the exceptions to flag-State jurisdiction on the high seas?
  16. Explain the common heritage of mankind and distinguish it from res communis (use De Soto's formulation).
  17. Describe the parallel system of mining and the reserved area.
  18. What is the three-stage method of maritime delimitation, and where did it come from?
  19. State the facts, holding and principle of the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case.
  20. What did the ITLOS order in the Enrica Lexie case, and what did the Supreme Court of India do after the award?

Appendix D · Glossary

Table 5.35 · Terms used in Topic 5
TermMeaning
Archipelagic StateA State constituted wholly by one or more archipelagos and possibly other islands, which may draw archipelagic straight baselines enclosing archipelagic waters (Part IV, Arts 46–54).
BaselineThe line from which the breadth of every maritime zone is measured: the low-water line (Art. 5) or the straight baselines drawn under Art. 7 (or Art. 47 for an archipelagic State).
BayA well-marked indentation whose area is at least equal to that of a semicircle on the closing line, with the closing line not exceeding 24 nm (Art. 10); historic bays are outside the rule.
Blue economyThe sustainable use of ocean resources for growth and livelihoods; the framework of India's marine policy.
Common heritage of mankindThe legal status of the Area and its resources: no appropriation, use for peaceful purposes, administration on behalf of mankind and equitable sharing of benefits (Art. 136 ff.).
Constructive presenceThe doctrine by which a mother ship outside the territorial sea may be treated as present in it through her boats, so that hot pursuit is lawful (Art. 111(4)).
CLCS (Commission on the Limits of the Continental Shelf)The 21-member scientific body which examines submissions on the outer limits of the shelf beyond 200 nm and makes recommendations (Art. 76(8), Annex II).
Equidistance lineA line every point of which is equidistant from the nearest points on the baselines of the States concerned; the starting point of the three-stage method.
EnterpriseThe Authority's own operating organ (Art. 170, Annex IV), whose initial functions were to be carried out by the Secretariat under the 1994 Agreement.
Grey areaAn area where the continental shelf of one State lies beneath the EEZ of another — an outcome recognised in the Bay of Bengal award of 2014.
Historic watersWaters over which a State claims a title based on long usage and acquiescence, as India does in the Gulf of Kachchh and the Gulf of Mannar (s. 4, Maritime Zones Act 1976).
Hot pursuitThe right of the coastal State to pursue and arrest a foreign ship on the high seas where the pursuit began within its maritime zones and was not interrupted (Art. 111).
Innocent passageNavigation that is continuous, expeditious and "not prejudicial to the peace, good order or security" of the coastal State (Arts 17–19).
Low-tide elevationA naturally formed area of land above water at low tide but submerged at high tide; it has no zones of its own and is only exceptionally used as a base point (Arts 7(4), 13, 47(4)).
Median lineThe line of equidistance between two opposite coasts, the starting point under Art. 15 for the territorial sea.
Natural prolongationThe geological extension of the land mass under the sea, the basis of the shelf entitlement beyond 200 nm (Art. 76(1); the North Sea cases).
Parallel systemThe two-track system of mining in the Area: the Enterprise, and States and their sponsored entities under contract with the Authority (§5.4).
Prompt releaseThe procedure under Art. 292 by which a flag State seeks the release of a detained vessel and crew on the posting of a reasonable bond.
Relevant circumstancesThe geographical and other factors which may justify adjusting a provisional equidistance line (§5.2 C).
Reserved areaThe half of every exploitation area applied for in the Area which is reserved for the Authority, the Enterprise or the developing States (Annex III, arts 8–9).
Res communisA thing incapable of appropriation and open to the use of all — the classical legal status of the high seas, distinguished from the common heritage by the element of joint administration (De Soto).
Single maritime boundaryOne boundary line delimiting the seabed and the water column together — the standard outcome of modern delimitation.
Straight baselinesLines joining appropriate points on a deeply indented coast or the outer edge of a fringe of islands (Art. 7).
Transit passageThe right of the ships and aircraft of all States to continuous and expeditious passage through straits used for international navigation, which cannot be suspended (Arts 37–44).
Zone-locked StateA State whose entitlement to resources within 200 nm is so enclosed by those of its neighbours that it cannot obtain its full entitlement — the situation for which Part XI's special provisions for geographically disadvantaged States were designed, and the argument that succeeded in the Bay of Bengal case.

Appendix F · Note on materials and method

These notes have been prepared for the Topic-5 portion of the DU paper on Public International Law (LB-205, second term), on the basis of the following materials:

  1. The DU course material itself — the second-term case material Public International Law, LB-205 (2022), whose law-of-the-sea block runs from the Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries case through the Somalia v. Kenya judgment, together with the PIL Notes and the Exam Capsule circulated with the course. The case digests at cases 30–34 and 36 follow the case material and the judgments; the course material's own formulations (including the statement that "the land dominates the sea" and De Soto's distinction between the common heritage and res communis) have been retained and attributed.
  2. Verma, S. K., An Introduction to Public International Law, chapter 14 (The Law of the Sea) — used for the Indian position on the zones, the legislative history and the treatment of the 1958 Conventions.
  3. Primary instruments — the text of UNCLOS 1982 and its annexes, the 1958 Geneva Conventions, the 1994 Implementation Agreement, the ISA regulations, the Statute of ITLOS, the Indian statutes (Maritime Zones Act 1976, the 1981 Fishing Act, the MAPA 2022 and others) and the Constitution of India.
  4. Judgments and awards — the judgments of the International Court of Justice, the judgments, orders and advisory opinions of ITLOS and its Seabed Disputes Chamber, and the awards of arbitral tribunals (in particular the Bay of Bengal award of 7 July 2014, whose text, summary and separate opinion are the source for case 35).
  5. Reported developments to mid-2026 — used only for illustration in §§5.3–5.6 (the CLCS submissions and objections, the deep-sea mining controversy and the 2025–2026 developments, the BBNJ Agreement's entry into force on 17 January 2026, and the ITLOS elections of June 2026). Where a development has not been authoritatively concluded — for example the outcome of Kenya's post-judgment position, or the state of the exploitation regulations — these notes say so rather than assert a result. Candidates should verify the current position from the websites of the International Seabed Authority, ITLOS and the Ministry of External Affairs before relying on a factual detail, but the law as stated in these notes is the law of the Convention.

A note on figures and tables. The tables and diagrams in these notes are original teaching aids drawn for this course; no figure reproduces a map or chart from a copyrighted source, and no numerical coordinate has been invented — every coordinate and figure used (the 12/24/200 nm limits, the 48 points of the Norwegian decree, the azimuths, the areas awarded in the Bay of Bengal arbitration, the ratios in Somalia v. Kenya and the Indian agreements) comes from the instruments, the judgments or the awards.

Appendix G · What the teacher highlighted (the syllabus image decoded)

The Topic-5 syllabus image carries two kinds of marking: two entries printed in green, and a set of handwritten annotations. The green entries are the ones the teacher stressed; the handwriting is a student's working notes. Both are set out below, with the place in these notes at which each is answered, and with the caution that a handwritten mark is evidence of emphasis and not of meaning — it has not been over-interpreted.

Table 5.37 · The syllabus image decoded
Marking on the imageWhat it signalsWhere it is answered
Green highlight — case 34, "Republic of Italy v. Union of India (2013) 4 SCC 721"The most important Indian case of the topic and the meeting point of the contiguous zone, the EEZ, the Maritime Zones Act 1976 and Article 297 of the Constitution. Treat it as a full-length answer, not a citation.Case 34, in full (seven-part digest, postscript on the ITLOS order of 2015, the PCA award of 2020 and the Supreme Court's order of 2021, Table 5.17 and Fig. 5.9); §5.1.2 (contiguous zone); §5.1.4 (EEZ); §5.5 B (Article 297).
Green highlight — head 5.6, "International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea"The Tribunal is to be studied as a subject in itself: constitution, jurisdiction, chambers, case law and the Indian dimension.§5.6 in full (Tables 5.26–5.28, Fig. 5.14), with the Seabed Disputes Chamber at §5.3 G and §5.4.
Handwritten: "baseline → low tide line" beside 5.1.1The normal baseline is the low-water line along the coast, from which the breadth of the territorial sea is measured (Art. 5), with the straight-baseline exception (Art. 7).§5.1.1 (Articles 5, 7, 13; Figs 5.2–5.3); case 30; PyQ Answer 1(v).
Handwritten: "Sec-2 Art-3 → …" and "Sec-4 Art-1 → …" style cross-referencesWorking cross-references between the Indian statute and the Convention — i.e. "section 3 of the Maritime Zones Act corresponds to Article 3 UNCLOS". These notes give the mapping in full, article by article, including the 1958 equivalents.§5.5 C (sections 3–7 of the 1976 Act); Table 5.25; Appendix B; Appendix E (article index).
Handwritten: "500 NM E.E.Z"-style marksEmphasis on the breadth of the economic zone. The correct figure is 200 nautical miles for the EEZ (Art. 57), with the continental shelf able to extend further — to the outer edge of the margin and up to 350 nm (Art. 76). The mark is best read as a reminder of the numbers, and the numbers are given in the master grid.§5.1.3 (Art. 76); §5.1.4 (Art. 57); Appendix C (master grid).
Handwritten: "High Seas" underlinedA stressed sub-head of 5.1: the freedoms, flag-State jurisdiction, piracy, the exceptions, and the erosion of the freedoms by the EEZ and the Area.§5.1.5 in full (Arts 86–120, Tables 5.12, Fig. 5.6); PyQ Answers 3 and 5.
Boxed handwriting "Tushar" beside 5.6A name — most probably a classmate's or a teacher's marker, or a note that a particular student was asked to present the topic. It is a personal mark and carries no legal content; nothing in these notes is built on it.Not applicable — recorded here only for completeness.

How to use this appendix. If time is short before the examination, work from the green entries outward: a full answer on Enrica Lexie (case 34) and a full answer on the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea (§5.6) will together carry the topics the teacher stressed, and the annotations then tell you which sub-heads of 5.1 the class spent time on — baselines, the breadth of the zones, and the high seas.
